而他的合伙人则绝望地唾沫四溅地嚷着；因为哈立德非但不帮着往外泵水，反而站在那里盯着水看，仿佛要凭某种奇迹用眼睛或用呼吸把水吸干似的。这位可怜的诗人便叫起来：「泵啊！水要漫过我们了，我们的店要完了。泵啊！」于是这个懒惰、心不在焉的家伙才重新泵起水来，一面却始终怀念着巴勒贝克家中那哗哗滚动的石碾和潺潺滴水的屋檐。有一次在窘境之中——他们正顶着瓢泼大雨苦干——他又照例停下，提醒沙基布阿拉伯的那句俗话：「从滴水的屋顶到流水的石兽。」他再一次被一阵思绪的飓风所裹挟。于是他又问：「你肯定我们在这里过得更好吗？」

而我们这位可怜的录事，在下面水深及膝的水里，破口大骂出一连串诅咒，可哈立德全然不理会。「我受够这活儿了，」他咆哮道，「那石碾子从来没这样耗过我的力气，赶骡子也没这样耗过。唉，再回到我那滴水的屋顶下吧，我们此刻岂不正置身于那流水的石兽之下吗？」说罢他又陷入一种昏沉之中，把整个世界丢给了诗人和水泵。

===Original===
For five years and more they lead such a life in the cellar. And they do not move out of it, lest they excite the envy of their compatriots. But instead of sleeping on the floor, they stretch themselves on the counters. The rising tide teaches them this little wisdom, which keeps the doctor and Azrael away. Their merchandise, however—their crosses, and scapulars and prayer-beads—are beyond hope of recovery. For what the rising tide spares, the rascally flyaway peddlers carry away. That is why they themselves shoulder the box and take to the road. And the pious old dames of the suburbs, we are told, receive them with such exclamations of joy and wonder, and almost tear their coats to get from them a sacred token. For you must remember, they are from the Holy Land. Unlike their goods, they at least are genuine. And every Saturday night, after beating the hoof in the country and making such fabulous profits on their false Holy-Land gewgaws, they return to their cellar happy and content.

“In three years,” writes our Scribe, “Khalid and I acquired what I still consider a handsome fortune. Each of us had a bank account, and a check book which we seldom used.…… In spite of which, we continued to shoulder the peddling box and tramp along.…… And Khalid would say to me, ‘A peddler is superior to a merchant; we travel and earn money; our compatriots the merchants rust in their cellars and lose it.’ To be sure, peddling in the good old days was most attractive. For the exercise, the gain, the experience—these are rich acquirements.”

And both Shakib and Khalid, we apprehend, have been hitherto most moderate in their habits. The fact that they seldom use their check books, testifies to this. They have now a peddleress, Im-Hanna by name, who occupies their cellar in their absence, and keeps what little they have in order. And when they return every Saturday night from their peddling trip, they find the old woman as ready to serve them as a mother. She cooks *mujaddara* for them, and sews the bed-linen on the quilts as is done in the mother country.

“The linen,” says Shakib, “was always as white as a dove’s wing, when Im-Hanna was with us.”

===Chinese===
他们在这样的地下室里一过便是五年有余。他们不肯搬出去，唯恐招惹同胞们的妒忌。不过他们不再睡在地上，而是把身子摊在柜台上。上涨的潮水教会了他们这一点小小的智慧，正是这点智慧挡住了医生和亚兹拉尔（Azrael）的脚步。然而他们的货品——他们的十字架、圣衣（scapulars）和念珠——却是再无指望捞回来了。因为涨潮所留下的，都被那帮无赖的轻浮货郎顺手牵羊带走了。正因如此，他们自己才扛起货郎箱，上路去走街串巷。据说，郊区那些虔诚的老妇人，一见他们便惊喜得连声惊叹，几乎要把他们的衣裳撕破，只为从他们手里求得一件圣物。因为你得记着，他们可是从圣地来的。不像他们的货品，他们本人至少是货真价实的。于是每个星期六晚上，他们在乡间徒步奔波，靠那些假充圣地的廉价小玩意儿赚了那样一笔惊人的厚利之后，便回到他们的地下室里，心满意足，快乐无边。

「三年之间，」我们的录事写道，「哈立德和我挣下了一笔我至今仍认为相当可观的财产。我们每人都有了一个银行账户，还有一本支票簿，不过我们很少动用它。……尽管如此，我们仍继续肩扛货郎箱，徒步奔走。……哈立德常对我说：『一个货郎胜过一个商人；我们四处旅行又能赚钱；我们的商人同胞却在地下室里生了锈，还把钱赔掉。』诚然，在往昔的好时光里，货郎这行当是最诱人的。因为那其中的锻炼、进项、阅历——这些都是丰厚的所得。」

我们想，沙基布与哈立德二人至今在生活习惯上都极为节制。他们很少动用支票簿这一事实，便可为证。如今他们有了一位女货郎，名叫伊姆-汉娜（Im-Hanna），在他们外出时住在他们的地下室里，替他们照管那一点点家当。而每个星期六晚上他们从货郎之旅归来时，都发现这位老妇人像一位母亲一样，随时准备服侍他们。她给他们做穆贾达拉（mujaddara），并像在故国那样把床单缝在被子上。

「只要有伊姆-汉娜在，」沙基布说，「那亚麻布总是白得像鸽子的翅膀。」

===Original===
And in the Khedivial Library Manuscript we find this curious note upon that popular Syrian dish of lentils and olive oil.

“*Mujaddara*,” writes Khalid, “has a marvellous effect upon my humour and nerves. There are certain dishes, I confess, which give me the blues. Of these, fried eggplants and cabbage boiled with corn-beef on the American system of boiling, that is to say, cooking, I abominate the most. But *mujaddara* has such a soothing effect on the nerves; it conduces to cheerfulness, especially when the raw onion or the leek is taken with it. After a good round pewter platter of this delicious dish and a dozen leeks, I feel as if I could do the work of all mankind. And I am then in such a beatific state of mind that I would share with all mankind my sack of lentils and my pipkin of olive oil. I wonder not at Esau’s extravagance, when he saw a steaming mess of it. For what is a birthright in comparison?”

That Shakib also shared this beatific mood, the following quaint picture of their Saturday nights in the cellar, will show.

“A bank account,” he writes, “a good round dish of *mujaddara*, the lute for Khalid, al-Mutanabbi for me—neither of us could forego his hobby—and Im-Hanna, affectionate, devoted as our mothers—these were the joys of our Saturday nights in our underground diggings. We were absolutely happy. And we never tried to measure our happiness in those days, or gauge it, or flay it to see if it be dead or alive, false or real. Ah, the blessedness of that supreme unconsciousness which wrapped us as a mother would her babe, warming and caressing our hearts. We did not know then that happiness was a thing to be sought. We only knew that peddling is a pleasure, that a bank account is a supreme joy, that a dish of *mujaddara* cooked by Im-Hanna is a royal delight, that our dour dark cellar is a palace of its kind, and that happiness, like a bride, issues from all these, and, touching the strings of Khalid’s lute, mantles us with song.”

===Chinese===
而在赫迪夫图书馆手稿里，我们发现了这则关于那道叙利亚人喜闻乐见的扁豆橄榄油菜的怪诞笔记。

「穆贾达拉，」哈立德写道，「对我的脾气和神经有着奇效。我承认，有那么几样菜，吃了叫我心情阴郁。其中，我最厌恶的莫过于油炸茄子，以及按美国人的煮法——说白了就是烧法——和咸牛肉一同煮烂的卷心菜。可穆贾达拉却对神经有那样一种抚慰之效；它能令人心神开朗，尤其是就着生葱或韭葱一同吃下的时候。美美地吃上一大锡盘这道佳肴，再嚼上一打韭葱之后，我便觉得自己仿佛能干完全人类的活儿。那时我的心境是那样一种蒙福的状态，以至我情愿与全人类共享我那一袋扁豆和我那一小罐橄榄油。我一点也不奇怪以扫（Esau）见到一盘热气腾腾的此物时所生的那种奢念。两相比较，长子名分又算得了什么呢？」

沙基布同样身在这种蒙福的心境之中，下面这幅关于他们在地下室度过星期六夜晚的古拙画面，便可为证。

「一个银行账户，」他写道，「一大盘穆贾达拉，给哈立德的琵琶，给我的穆太奈比（al-Mutanabbi）——我们俩谁也少不了各自的癖好——还有伊姆-汉娜，像我们的母亲一样慈爱、忠心——这些便是我们在那地下穴居中度过的星期六夜晚的种种快乐。我们那时是绝对幸福的。在那些日子里，我们从不曾试着去丈量我们的幸福，去勘测它，去剥它的皮看看它是死是活、是假是真。啊，那种至高无上的浑然不觉所赐的福分，它像一位母亲包裹她的婴孩那样把我们裹住，把我们的心焐暖、抚摸。那时我们并不知道幸福是一种需要去追寻的东西。我们只晓得，做货郎是一件乐事，一个银行账户是一种至高的喜悦，一盘由伊姆-汉娜所做的穆贾达拉是一种王者般的享受，我们那阴沉黑暗的地下室也算是它那种类里的一座宫殿，而幸福，就像一位新娘，从这一切之中款款走出，拨动哈立德琵琶的琴弦，以歌声将我们披覆。」

## V / V

The Cellar of the Soul / 灵魂的地窖

===Original===
Heretofore, Khalid and Shakib have been inseparable as the Pointers. They always appeared together, went the rounds of their peddling orbit together, and together were subject to the same conditions and restraints. Which restraints are a sort of sacrifice they make on the altar of friendship. One, for instance, would never permit himself an advantage which the other could not enjoy, or a pleasure in which the other could not share. They even slept under the same blanket, we learn, ate from the same plate, puffed at the same narghile, which Shakib brought with him from Baalbek, and collaborated in writing to one ladylove! A condition of unexampled friendship this, of complete oneness. They had both cut themselves garments from the same cloth, as the Arabic saying goes. And on Sunday afternoon, in garments spick and span, they would take the air in Battery Park, where the one would invoke the Statue of Liberty for a thought, or the gilded domes of Broadway for a metaphor, while the other would be scouring the horizon for the Nothingness, which is called, in the recondite cant of the sophisticated, a vague something.

In the Khedivial Library MS. we find nothing which this Battery Park might have inspired. And yet, we can not believe that Khalid here was only attracted by that vague something which, in his spiritual enceinteship, he seemed to relish. Nothing? Not even the does and kangaroos that adorn the Park distracted or detained him? We doubt it; and Khalid’s lute sustains us in our doubt. Ay, and so does our Scribe; for in his *Histoire Intime* we read the following, which we faithfully transcribe.

===Chinese===
此前，哈立德与沙基布一如指极双星（the Pointers），形影不离。他们总是一同现身，一同走完那货郎巡回的轨道，也一同受制于相同的境遇与约束。而这些约束，正是他们在友谊的祭坛上所作的一种献祭。比方说，其中一人绝不容许自己独享那另一人无从染指的好处，也不肯沉溺于那另一人无从分尝的欢愉。我们得知，他们甚至同盖一条毯子，同食一盘，同抽一杆水烟壶（narghile）——那是沙基布从巴勒贝克带来的——甚至还合起伙来给同一个情人写信！这是一种空前绝后的友谊，一种浑然合一的境地。用阿拉伯人的话说，他们俩是从同一匹布上栽下衣裳来的。而在礼拜日下午，两人身着光鲜齐整的衣裳，到巴特利公园（Battery Park）去散步透气；其中一位会向自由女神像（Statue of Liberty）祈一个念头，或向百老汇那些镀金的穹顶讨一个比喻，而另一位则在搜索地平线，寻觅那「无」——在世故者那套晦涩的行话里，这被称作一种模糊的「有」。

在赫迪夫图书馆手稿里，我们找不到任何由这座巴特利公园所激发出来的东西。然而我们无法相信，哈立德在此地竟只被那种模糊的「有」所吸引——在他那灵性孕育的状态中，他似乎颇为享受这种「有」。一无所得？难道连点缀着这座公园的雌鹿与袋鼠，都不曾分他的心、不曾留住他的脚步？我们深表怀疑；而哈立德的诗琴也印证了我们的疑心。是啊，我们的这位书记者亦复如此；因为在《私密史》（Histoire Intime）中我们读到下面这一段，我们忠实地迻录于此。

===Original===
“Of the many attractions of Battery Park, the girls and the sea were my favourite. For the girls in a crowd have for me a fascination which only the girls at the bath can surpass. I love to lose myself in a crowd, to buffet, so to speak, its waves, to nestle under their feathery crests. For the rolling waves of life, the tumbling waves of the sea, and the fiery waves of al-Mutanabbi’s poetry have always been my delight. In Battery Park I took especial pleasure in reading aloud my verses to Khalid, or in fact to the sea, for Khalid never would listen.

“Once I composed a few stanzas to the Milkmaid who stood in her wagon near the lawn, rattling out milk-punches to the boys. A winsome lass she was, fresh in her sororiation, with fair blue eyes, a celestial flow of auburn hair, and cheeks that suggested the milk and cherry in the glass she rattled out to me. I was reading aloud the stanzas which she inspired, when Khalid, who was not listening, pointed out to me a woman whose figure and the curves thereof were remarkable. ‘Is it not strange,’ said he, ‘how the women here indraw their stomachs and outdraw their hips? And is not this the opposite of the shape which our women cultivate?’

“Yes, with the Lebanon women, the convex curve beneath the waist is frontward, not hindward. But that is a matter of taste, I thought, and man is partly responsible for either convexity. I have often wondered, however, why the women of my country cultivate that shape. And why do they in America cultivate the reverse of it? Needless to say that both are pruriently titillating﻿—both distentions are damnably suggestive, quite killing. The American woman, from a fine sense of modesty, I am told, never or seldom ventures abroad, when big with child. But in the kangaroo figure, the burden is slightly shifted and naught is amiss. Ah, such haunches as are here exhibited suggest the *aliats* of our Asiatic sheep.”

===Chinese===
「在巴特利公园那许多诱人之处当中，姑娘们与大海是我的最爱。因为成群的姑娘对于我有一种魅力，唯有浴中的姑娘才能胜过。我爱在人群中迷失自己，可以说，去和它的波浪搏击，去依偎在它们如羽的浪尖之下。因为那翻滚的生命之浪、那跌宕的大海之浪、以及穆太奈比（al-Mutanabbi）诗中那炽烈的浪，一向是我的欢愉。在巴特利公园，我尤其乐于把我的诗句朗诵给哈立德听——其实不如说是朗诵给大海听，因为哈立德从不肯听。

「有一次，我为那位卖奶姑娘作了几个诗节，她站在草坪旁自己的篷车里，把一杯杯奶露丁当作响地递给小伙子们。她是个标致的姑娘，初入姐妹会般的鲜活气，有一双明媚的蓝眼睛，一头如天泉般倾泻的赭色秀发，两颊则让人联想到她摇给我的那杯中牛奶与樱桃的颜色。我正高声朗诵她所激发的那些诗节，哈立德却并不在听，只指着一位身形与曲线都颇惹人注目的妇人给我看。『岂不奇怪，』他说，『这里的女人怎么都把肚子往里收、把臀部往外挺？这岂不与我们那边女人所培养的体态恰好相反？』

「是啊，在黎巴嫩女人身上，腰下那道凸起的曲线是朝前，不是朝后的。可我想，这不过是口味的问题罢了，而两种凸起，男人都得负一部分责任。不过我常纳闷，我们那里的女人何以培养出那种体态，而美国的女人又何以培养出与之相反的体态？不必说，两种都带着撩人的肉欲之痒——两般鼓胀都该死地惹人遐思，简直要命。我听说，美国女人出于一种细腻的羞耻心，在身怀六甲时极少或从不出门。可在这袋鼠般的体态里，重负稍稍挪了位，便一切无恙。啊，此地所展露的这般臀，倒叫人想起我们亚细亚绵羊的肥尾（aliat）来。」

===Original===
And what he says about the pruriently titillating convexities, whether frontward or hindward, suggests a little prudery. For in his rhymes he betrays both his comrade and himself. Battery Park and the attractions thereof prove fatal. Elsewhere, therefore, they must go, and begin to draw on their bank accounts. Which does not mean, however, that they are far from the snare. No; for when a young man begins to suffer from what the doctors call hebephrenia, the farther he draws away from such snares the nearer he gets to them. And these lusty Syrians could not repel the magnetic attraction of the polypiosis of what Shakib likens to the *aliat* (fattail) of our Asiatic sheep. Surely, there be more devils under such an *aliat* than under the hat of a Jesuit. And Khalid is the first to discover this. Both have been ensnared, however, and both, when in the snare, have been infernally inspired. What Khalid wrote, when he was under the influence of feminine curves, was preserved by Shakib, who remarks that one evening, after returning from the Park, Khalid said to him, “I am going to write a poem.” A fortnight later, he hands him the following, which he jealously kept among his papers.

===Chinese===
他这一番关于撩人凸起——无论朝前还是朝后——的议论，多少透出几分假道学的气息。因为在他的韵文里，他既出卖了他的同伴，也出卖了他自己。巴特利公园及其种种诱惑，果然是致命的。于是他们只好另投他处，并开始动用银行里的存款。然而这并不意味着他们已离罗网很远。不；因为当一个年轻人开始患上医生们所谓「青春型癫狂」（hebephrenia）的病症时，他愈是远离这类罗网，便愈是接近它们。而这两个精力充沛的叙利亚人，也抵挡不住沙基布所比作我们亚细亚绵羊肥尾（aliat，即 fattail）的那股多息肉般赘肉的磁性吸引。诚然，这样一条肥尾底下藏着的魔鬼，比一顶耶稣会士（Jesuits）的帽子底下藏着的还要多。而哈立德头一个发现了这一点。不过两人都已坠入罗网，而两人一旦入网，都受到了地狱般的灵感眷顾。哈立德在那女性曲线的影响下所写下的东西，由沙基布保存了下来；沙基布说，一日傍晚从公园回来后，哈立德对他说：「我要写一首诗。」半月之后，他便把下面这首交给了他，沙基布把它珍而重之地夹在自己的文稿里。

===Original===
I dreamt I was a donkey-boy again.

Out on the sun-swept roads of Baalbek, I tramp behind my burro, trolling my *mulayiah*.

At noon, I pass by a garden redolent of mystic scents and tarry awhile.

Under an orange tree, on the soft green grass, I stretch my limbs.

The daisies, the anemones, and the cyclamens are round me pressing:

The anemone buds hold out to me their precious rubies; the daisies kiss me in the eyes and lips; and the cyclamens shake their powder in my hair.

On the wall, the roses are nodding, smiling; above me the orange blossoms surrender themselves to the wooing breeze; and on yonder rock the salamander sits, complacent and serene.

I take a daisy, and, boy as boys go, question its petals:

Married man or monk, I ask, plucking them off one by one,

And the last petal says, Monk.

I perfume my fingers with crumpled cyclamens, cover my face with the dark-eyed anemones, and fall asleep.

And my burro sleeps beneath the wall, in the shadow of nodding roses.

And the blackbirds too are dozing, and the bulbuls flitting by whisper with their wings, ‘salaam.’

Peace and salaam!

The bulbul, the blackbird, the salamander, the burro, and the burro-boy, are to each other shades of noonday sun:

Happy, loving, generous, and free;﻿—

As happy as each other, and as free.

We do what we please in Nature’s realm, go where we please;

No one’s offended, no one ever wronged.

No sentinels hath Nature, no police.

But lo, a goblin as I sleep comes forth;﻿—

A goblin taller than the tallest poplar, who carries me upon his neck to the Park in far New York.

Here women, light-heeled, heavy-haunched, pace up and down the flags in graceful gait.

My roses these, I cry, and my orange blossoms.

But the goblin placed his hand upon my mouth, and I was dumb.

The cyclamens, the anemones, the daisies, I saw them, but I could not speak to them.

The goblin placed his hand upon my mouth, and I was dumb.

O take me back to my own groves, I cried, or let me speak.

But he threw me off his shoulders in a huff, among the daisies and the cyclamens.

Alone among them, but I could not speak.

He had tied my tongue, the goblin, and left me there alone.

And in front of me, and towards me, and beside me,

Walked Allah’s fairest cyclamens and anemones.

I smell them, and the tears flow down my cheeks;

I can not even like the noonday bulbul

Whisper with my wings, salaam!

I sit me on a bench and weep.

And in my heart I sing

O, let me be a burro-boy again;

O, let me sleep among the cyclamens

Of my own land.

===Chinese===
我梦见我又成了赶驴娃。

在巴勒贝克阳光漫洒的道路上，我紧跟我的小毛驴，一面走一面哼唱我的《穆拉伊亚》（mulayiah）。

正午时分，我经过一座氤氲着神秘香气的花园，逗留片刻。

在一棵橙子树下，柔软的青草地上，我舒展开四肢。

雏菊、银莲花和仙客来团团将我围住：

银莲花蕾向我捧出它们珍贵的红宝石；雏菊亲吻我的眼睛和嘴唇；仙客来把它们的粉末摇落在我发间。

墙上，玫瑰颔首微笑；我头顶上方，橙花向着求爱的微风缴械投降；远处的岩石上，坐着一只火蜥蜴（salamander），怡然自得、安详宁静。

我摘下一朵雏菊，像个男孩该有的样子，去问它的花瓣：

娶妻的人，还是修道者？我问道，把花瓣一片一片摘下，

而最后一片花瓣说，修道者。

我用揉皱的仙客来给指尖熏香，用黑眼的银莲花遮住脸庞，沉沉睡去。

我的小毛驴也在墙下睡去，在颔首的玫瑰的阴影里。

乌鸫们也在打盹，掠过的夜莺（bulbuls）用翅膀低语：「色兰（salaam）。」

平安，与色兰！

夜莺、乌鸫、火蜥蜴、小毛驴，还有那赶驴娃，彼此都是正午阳光下的荫凉：

幸福、相爱、慷慨而自由；——

彼此一样幸福，彼此一样自由。

在自然的国度里我们为所欲为，去我们所欲去之处；

谁也不冒犯谁，谁也从未受过委屈。

自然没有哨兵，没有警察。

可看哪，我睡着时一个小妖精走了出来；——

一个比最高的白杨还要高的小妖精，把我驮在他脖颈上，带到遥远纽约的那座公园。

这里的女人，步履轻盈、臀丰沉重，在旗石上以优雅的步态走来走去。

这些是我的玫瑰，我喊道，还有我的橙花。

可小妖精把手捂在我嘴上，我便哑了。

仙客来、银莲花、雏菊，我看见了它们，却不能对它们说话。

小妖精把手捂在我嘴上，我便哑了。

啊，把我带回我自己的树林去吧，我喊道，要么让我开口说话。

可他气鼓鼓地把我从他肩上摔下来，摔进雏菊与仙客来之间。

独自在它们之间，但我却不能说话。

他绑住了我的舌头，那个小妖精，把我独自留在那里。

在我面前，朝着我，在我身旁，

走着真主（Allah）最美的仙客来和银莲花。

我闻着她们，泪水顺着面颊流淌；

我甚至不能像正午的夜莺那样

用我的翅膀低语，色兰！

我坐在一条长椅上哭泣。

而在我心里我歌唱

啊，让我再做一个赶驴娃吧；

啊，让我睡在仙客来之间，

在我自己的土地上。

===Original===
Shades of Whitman! But Whitman, thou Donkey, never weeps. Whitman, if that goblin tried to silence him, would have wrung his neck, after he had ridden upon it. The above, nevertheless, deserves the space we give it here, as it shadows forth one of the essential elements of Khalid’s spiritual makeup. But this slight symptom of that disease we named, this morbidness incident to adolescence, is eventually overcome by a dictionary and a grammar. Ay, Khalid henceforth shall cease to scour the horizon for that vague something of his dreams; he has become farsighted enough by the process to see the necessity of pursuing in America something more spiritual than peddling crosses and scapulars. Especially in this America, where the alphabet is spread broadcast, and free of charge. And so, he sets himself to the task of self-education. He feels the embryo stir within him, and in the squeamishness of enceinteship, he asks but for a few of the fruits of knowledge. Ah, but he becomes voracious of a sudden, and the little pocket dictionary is devoured entirely in three sittings. Hence his folly of treating his thoughts and fancies, as he was treated by the goblin. For do not words often rob a fancy of its tongue, or a thought of its soul? Many of the pieces Khalid wrote when he was devouring dictionaries were finally disposed of in a most picturesque manner, as we shall relate. And a few were given to Shakib, of which that Dream of Cyclamens was preserved.

And Khalid’s motto was, “One book at a time.” He would not encumber himself with books any more than he would with shoes. But that the mind might not go barefoot, he always bought a new book before destroying the one in hand. Destroying? Yes; for after reading or studying a book, he warms his hands upon its flames, this Khalid, or makes it serve to cook a pot of *mujaddara*. In this extraordinary and outrageous manner, barbarously capricious, he would baptise the ideal in the fire of the real. And thus, glowing with health and confidence and conceit, he enters another Park from which he escapes in the end, sad and wan and bankrupt. Of a truth, many attractions and distractions are here; else he could not forget the peddling-box and the light-heeled, heavy-haunched women of Battery Park. Here are swings for the mind; toboggan-chutes for the soul; merry-go-rounds for the fancy; and many devious and alluring paths where one can lose himself for years. A sanitarium this for the hebephreniac. And like all sanitariums, you go into it with one disease and come out of it with ten. Had Shakib been forewarned of Khalid’s mind, had he even seen him at the gate before he entered, he would have given him a few hints about the cross-signs and barbed-cordons therein. But should he not have divined that Khalid soon or late was coming? Did *he* not call enough to him, and aloud? “Get thee behind me on this dromedary,” our Scribe, reading his al-Mutanabbi, would often say to his comrade, “and come from this desert of barren gold, if but for a day﻿—come out with me to the oasis of poesy.”

===Chinese===
惠特曼（Whitman）的幽魂啊！可是惠特曼，你这头驴子，是从来不哭的。换了惠特曼，若那小妖精想叫他噤声，待骑过他的脖颈之后，定要把它那脖子拧断。不过，上面这首诗仍值得我们在书中给它这方篇幅，因为它影影绰绰地勾勒出哈立德性灵构造中一个不可或缺的要素。然而我们所点名的那种病症的这一轻微症状——这种青春期所伴发的病态——终归被一本字典和一部语法书所克服。是啊，哈立德从此再也不去搜索地平线、寻觅他梦中那模糊的「有」了；经过这一番磨砺，他的目力已远到足以看清：在美国，他必须去追求一些比沿街兜售十字架与圣衣（scapular）更富于精神性的东西。尤其在这片美利坚，字母是免费广播、人人可得的。于是他着手从事自我教育这一桩工程。他觉出胚胎在体内蠕动，在那种孕育之身的娇怯之中，他不过讨要几枚知识之果罢了。啊，可他忽然变得饕餮起来，那本小小的袖珍字典，分三次便被他吃得一干二净。正因如此，他才像那小妖精对付他一般，去对付自己的心思与遐想——这便是他的痴处。难道不是常常如此吗：言语夺去了一个遐想的舌头，或者夺去了一个念头的灵魂？哈立德在吞食字典的那些日子里所写的许多篇什，最终都以一种极富画面感的方式被处置掉了，这一点我们后文再述。其中少数几篇给了沙基布，那首《仙客来之梦》便这样被保存了下来。

而哈立德的箴言是：「一次只读一本书。」他不肯让书本成为自己的累赘，一如他不肯让鞋子成为自己的累赘。但为使心智不至于赤足行走，他总要在毁去手头那本之前，先买一本新的。毁去？是的；因为每读完或研毕一本书，这个哈立德便要用它的火焰烘手，或者拿它去煮一锅穆贾达拉（mujaddara）。他以这般离经叛道、荒蛮任性的方式，把理想之物投入现实之火中受洗。就这样，他通身焕发着健康、自信与自满，走进了另一座公园——可到末了，他却是形容憔悴、面色苍白、囊空如洗地逃出来的。诚然，此处诱人分心之物甚多；否则他也不会忘掉那货郎箱，忘掉巴特利公园里那些步履轻盈、臀丰沉重的女人。此处有为心智而设的秋千，有为灵魂而设的雪橇滑道，有为遐想而设的旋转木马，还有许许多多曲折而迷人的小径，一脚踏进去便能迷失数年。这是青春型癫狂者的疗养院。而所有的疗养院莫不如此：你带着一种病进去，却带着十种病出来。倘若沙基布事先窥见哈立德的心思，哪怕只是在门口看见他进门之前，本可以给他几句关于园中那些十字路标与铁丝围栏的提醒。可他难道不该早已料到，哈立德迟早是要走进去的吗？他岂不是已经向他呼唤够多了，而且声嘶力竭？「跨上我这匹单峰驼，坐到我身后来，」我们的这位书记者，读着他的穆太奈比，常常这样对他的同伴说，「从这片荒芜金沙的沙漠里出来吧，哪怕只有一日——随我一同出去，到诗的绿洲里来。」

===Original===
That is why, although writing in Arabic, Khalid prefers English. For the Arabic verb is confined to three tenses, the primary ones only; and to break through any of these in any degree, requires such crowbars as only auxiliaries and other verbs can furnish. For this and many other reasons Khalid stops short in the mazes of Sibawayh, runs out of them exasperated, depressed, and never for a long time after looks in that direction. He is now curious to know if the English language have its Sibawayhs and Niftawayhs. And so, he buys him a grammar, and there finds the way somewhat devious, too, but not enough to constitute a maze. The men who wrote these grammars must have had plenty of time to do a little useful work. They do not seem to have walked leisurely in flowing robes disserting a lifelong dissertation on the origin and descent of a preposition. One day Shakib is amazed by finding the grammars page by page tacked on the walls of the cellar and Khalid pacing around leisurely lingering a moment before each page, as if he were in an art gallery. That is how he tackled his subject. And that is why he and Shakib begin to quarrel. The idea! That a fledgling should presume to pick flaws. To Shakib, who is textual to a hair, this is intolerable. And that state of oneness between them shall be subject hereafter to “the corrosive action of various unfriendly agents.” For Khalid, who has never yet been snaffled, turns restively from the bit which his friend, for his own sake, would put in his mouth. The rupture follows. The two for a while wend their way in opposite directions. Shakib still cherishing and cultivating his bank account, shoulders his peddling-box and jogs along with his inspiring demon, under whose auspices, he tells us, he continues to write verse and gull with his brummagems the pious dames of the suburbs. And Khalid sits on his peddling-box for hours pondering on the necessity of disposing of it somehow. For now he scarcely makes more than a few peddling-trips each month, and when he returns, he does not go to the bank to add to his balance, but to draw from it. That is why the accounts of the two Syrians do not fare alike; Shakib’s is gaining in weight, Khalid’s is wasting away.

Yes, the strenuous spirit is a long time dead in Khalid. He is gradually reverting to the Oriental instinct. And when he is not loafing in Battery Park, carving his name on the bench, he is burrowing in the shelves of some secondhand bookshop or dreaming in the dome of some Broadway skyscraper. Does not this seem inevitable, however, considering the palingenetic burden within him? And is not loafing a necessary prelude to the travail? Khalid, of course, felt the necessity of this, not knowing the why and wherefor. And from the vast world of paper-bound souls, for he relished but pamphlets at the start﻿—they do not make much smoke in the fire, he would say﻿—from that vast world he could command the greatest of the great to help him support the loafing while. And as by a miracle, he came out of that chaos of contending spirits without a scratch. He enjoyed the belligerency of pamphleteers as an American would enjoy a prize fight. But he sided with no one; he took from everyone his best and consigned him to Im-Hanna’s kitchen. Torquemada could not have done better; but Khalid, it is hoped, will yet atone for his crimes.

===Chinese===
正因如此，哈立德虽用阿拉伯语写作，却更偏爱英语。因为阿拉伯语的动词局限于三种时态，且仅是那几种基本时态；要从其中任何一种稍有突破，都需要只有助动词及其他动词才能提供的撬棍。由于这个以及其它许多缘故，哈立德在西伯威（Sibawayh）的迷宫里半途刹住，愤懑而沮丧地跑了出来，此后很长一段时间都不再朝那个方向瞧上一眼。如今他好奇起来：英语有没有它自己的西伯威与尼夫塔威（Niftawayh）呢？于是他给自己买了一部语法书，发现那里的路也有些曲折，却还不至于构成一座迷宫。写这些语法书的人，想必还有大把闲暇来做一点有用的事。他们似乎不曾穿着长袍、踱着方步，花一辈子去作一篇关于某个介词起源与流变的论文。一日，沙基布惊讶地发现：那些语法书被一页一页地钉在地窖的墙上，而哈立德正悠闲地踱来踱去，在每一页前驻足片刻，仿佛置身于一座美术馆。他便是这样攻取他的科目的。也正因如此，他和沙基布开始争吵起来。岂有此理！一个乳臭未干的小子，竟敢公然挑刺。对于字斟句酌、一丝不苟的沙基布而言，这简直无法忍受。于是两人之间那种浑然合一的境地，此后便要遭受「种种不友善因素的腐蚀作用」了。因为哈立德从未被人套上嚼子，如今朋友为着他好，要把嚼子塞进他嘴里，他便焦躁地别过脸去。裂痕随之而来。两人一度各走各的相反方向。沙基布仍一边珍摄培植着他的银行存款，一边挑起货郎箱，与他那赐灵的魔鬼一同踽踽前行；他说，在这魔鬼的庇佑之下，他继续写着诗，并用他那批廉价劣货（brummagems）去哄骗郊外那些虔诚的太太。而哈立德则一连几个钟头坐在自己的货郎箱上，苦思着该如何把它脱手。因为如今他每月不过跑几趟货郎买卖，回来时也不去银行添他的存款，反而去提款。正因如此，这两个叙利亚人的账目命运迥异：沙基布的在涨分量，哈立德的则在日渐消瘦。

是的，那奋发的精神在哈立德身上早已死了许久。他正在一点点地回归那东方的本能。当他不在巴特利公园闲荡、在长椅上刻自己的名字时，他便在某家旧书店的书架间打洞钻营，或在百老汇某座摩天楼的穹顶之下出神。然而，想想他体内那轮回再生的重负，这一切岂不是势所必然？而闲荡，岂不正是那场产难之前必不可少的序曲？哈立德当然感到了这一必要性，却说不出个中缘由。于是他从那浩瀚的、纸面装订的灵魂世界——起初他只读得起小册子，它们在火里烧不出多少烟，他如是说——从那个浩瀚的世界里，他能随意调遣最最伟大的人物，来帮他撑过这段闲荡的光阴。而如同奇迹一般，他从那群相互厮杀的灵魂的混沌之中全身而退，毫发无伤。他享受小册子作者们的相互攻讦，就像一个美国人享受一场拳击赛。可他不偏袒任何一方；他从每个人身上取其精华，然后把他打发到伊姆-汉娜（Im-Hanna）的厨房里去。托尔克马达（Torquemada）也不能做得比他更好了；不过我们希望，哈立德终将为他的罪孽作出补偿。

===Original===
Monsieur Pascal, with whom he quarrels before he burns, had a particular influence upon him. He could not rest after reading his *Thoughts* until he read the Bible. And of the Prophets of the Old Testament he had an especial liking for Jeremiah and Isaiah. And once he bought a cheap print of Jeremiah which he tacked on the wall of his cellar. From the Khedivial Library MS. we give two excerpts relating to Pascal and this Prophet.

“O Monsieur Pascal,

“I tried hard to hate and detest myself, as you advise, and I found that I could not by so doing love God. ’Tis in loving the divine in Man, in me, in you, that we rise to the love of our Maker. And in giving your proofs of the true religion, you speak of the surprising measures of the Christian Faith, enjoining man to acknowledge himself vile, base, abominable, and obliging him at the same time to aspire towards a resemblance of his Maker. Now, I see in this a foreshadowing of the theory of evolution, nay a divine warrant for it. Nor is it the Christian religion alone which unfolds to man the twofold mystery of his nature; others are as dark and as bright on either side of the pole. And Philosophy conspiring with Biology will not consent to the apotheosis of Man, unless he wear on his breast a symbol of his tail.﻿ ﻿… Au-revoir, Monsieur Pascal, Remember me to St. Augustine.”

===Chinese===
帕斯卡先生（Monsieur Pascal），这个他在付之一炬之前先与之一番争吵的人，对他有过特别的影响。他读了帕斯卡的《思想录》（*Thoughts*）之后便不得安宁，非要去读《圣经》不可。而在《旧约》众先知之中，他格外偏爱耶利米（Jeremiah）与以赛亚（Isaiah）。有一次他买了一张便宜的耶利米画像，把它钉在自己地窖的墙上。我们从赫迪夫图书馆手稿中迻录两段，一关乎帕斯卡，一关乎这位先知。

「啊，帕斯卡先生，

「我竭力照你的劝告去憎恶、厌弃我自己，却发现我无法借由这样做来爱真主。唯有借着爱人之中、我之中、你之中的那一点神性，我们方能升腾到对造物主的爱。而你在为真正的宗教提供证据时，论到基督教信仰那些令人惊诧的章程：一方面命人承认自己卑劣、下贱、可憎，另一方面又责成他同时去追求与他造物主的肖似。如今我在其中所见，不啻是进化论的先声——不，简直就是它的一纸神授凭据。而向人揭示其本性这一双重奥秘的，也并非只有基督教；其它宗教在两极的任一端，也同样地一面幽暗、一面明亮。哲学若与生物学联手，便不会同意将人神化——除非他在胸前佩上他那条尾巴的标志。……再会了，帕斯卡先生，替我向圣奥古斯丁（St. Augustine）问好。」

===Original===
“O Jeremiah,

“Thy picture, sitting among the ruins of the City of Zion, appeals to my soul. Why, I know not. It may be because I myself once sat in that posture among the ruins of my native City of Baal. But the ruins did not grieve me as did the uncle who slammed the door in my face that night. True, I wept in the ruins, but not over them. Something else had punctured the bladderets of my tears. And who knows who punctured thine, O Jeremiah? Perhaps a daughter of Tamar had stuck a bodkin in thine eye, and in lamenting thine own fate﻿—Pardon me, O Jeremiah. Melikes not all these tears of thine. Nor did Zion and her children in Jahannam, I am sure.﻿ ﻿… Instead of a scroll in thy hand, I would have thee hold a harp. Since King David, Allah has not thought of endowing his prophets with musical talent. Why, think what an honest prophet could accomplish if his message were put into music. And withal, if he himself could sing it. Yes, our modern Jeremiahs should all take music lessons; for no matter how deep and poignant our sorrows, we can always rise from them, harp in hand, to an ecstasy, joyous and divine.”

Now, connect with this the following from the *Histoire Intime*, and you have the complete history of this Prophet in Khalid’s cellar. For Khalid himself never gives us the facts in the case. Our Scribe, however, comes not short in this.

===Chinese===
「啊，耶利米，

「你那坐在锡安城（City of Zion）废墟之中的画像，深深触动我的灵魂。缘由为何，我不知道。也许是因为我自己也曾以那副姿态，坐在我故土巴力之城（City of Baal）的废墟之间。然而使我悲伤的并非废墟，而是那晚当着我面砰然关上门的舅舅。不错，我是在废墟中哭泣，却非为废墟而哭。是别的东西戳破了我的泪囊。又有谁知道，啊，耶利米，戳破你泪囊的又是谁呢？也许是一位他玛（Tamar）之女，把一根长针扎进了你的眼里，于是你便哀叹起自己的命运来——宽恕我吧，啊，耶利米。我这人不喜欢你这一切眼泪。我确信，锡安和她的儿女们在火狱（Jahannam）里，也不会喜欢。……我宁愿你手中拿的不是一卷书，而是一架琴。自大卫王（King David）以来，真主（Allah）就不曾想过要赐给他众先知什么音乐才华。可不是么，你想想看，一个诚实的先知，若把他的训诲谱成乐曲，能成就怎样一番事业。况且，若他自己还能引吭唱出来。是的，我们当代的耶利米们都该去上上音乐课；因为无论我们的悲伤多么深沉、多么锥心，我们总能手持竖琴，从其中升华到一种欢愉而神圣的狂喜。」

如今，再把《私密史》中下面这一段与之勾连起来，你便得到了这位先知在哈立德地窖里的完整掌故。因为哈立德本人从不向我们提供这件事的实情。然而我们的书记者，在这方面却并不短缺。

===Original===
“The picture of the Prophet Jeremiah,” writes he, “Khalid hung on the wall, above his bed. And every night he would look up to it invokingly, muttering I know not what. One evening, while in this posture, he took up his lute and trolled a favourite ditty. For three days and three nights that picture hung on the wall. And on the morning of the fourth day﻿—it was a cold December morning, I remember﻿—he took it down and lighted the fire with it. The Pamphlet he had read a few days since, he also threw into the fire, and thereupon called to me saying, ‘Come, Shakib, and warm yourself.’ ”

And the Pamphlet, we learn, which was thus baptised in the same fire with the Prophet’s picture, was Tom Paine’s *Age of Reason*.

===Chinese===
「先知耶利米的画像，」他写道，「哈立德把它挂在墙上，就在他床铺的上方。每夜他都仰望它，神情如有所祈，口中喃喃念叨着些什么，我听不真切。一日傍晚，正当这副姿态，他抄起诗琴，哼起一支心爱的小调。那张画像在墙上挂了三日三夜。到了第四日清晨——我记得，那是一个寒冷的十二月清晨——他把它取了下来，用它生了火。几天前他读过的那本小册子，他也一并扔进了火里，随即唤我道：『来呀，沙基布，来烤烤火。』」

而我们得知，那本与先知画像在同一团火中一并受洗的小册子，便是汤姆·潘恩（Tom Paine）的《理性时代》（*Age of Reason*）。

## VI / VI

The Summer Afternoon of a Sham / 一个骗子的夏日午后

===Original===
For two years and more Khalid’s young mind went leaping from one swing to another, from one carousel or toboggan-chute to the next, without having any special object in view, without knowing why and wherefor. He even entered such mazes of philosophy, such labyrinths of mysticism as put those of the Arabian grammaticasters in the shade. To him, education was a sport, pursued in a free spirit after his own fancy, without method or discipline. For two years and more he did little but ramble thus, drawing meanwhile on his account in the bank, and burning pamphlets.

One day he passes by a secondhand bookshop, which is in the financial hive of the city, hard by a church and within a stone’s throw from the Stock Exchange. The owner, a shabby venerable, standing there, pipe in mouth, between piles of pamphlets and little pyramids of books, attracts Khalid. He too occupies a cellar. And withal he resembles the Prophet in the picture which was burned with Tom Paine’s *Age of Reason*. Nothing in the face at least is amiss. A flowing, serrated, milky beard, with a touch of gold around the mouth; an aquiline nose; deep set blue eyes canopied with shaggy brows; a forehead broad and high; a dome a little frowsy but not guilty of a hair﻿—the Prophet Jeremiah! Only one thing, a clay pipe which he seldom took out of his mouth except to empty and refill, seemed to take from the prophetic solemnity of the face. Otherwise, he is as grim and sullen as the Prophet. In his voice, however, there is a supple sweetness which the hard lines in his face do not express. Khalid nicknames him secondhand Jerry, makes to him professions of friendship, and for many months comes every day to see him. He comes with his bucket, as he would say, to Jerry’s well. For the two, the young man and the old man of the cellar, the neophite and the master, would chat about literature and the makers of it for hours. And what a sea of information is therein under that frowsy dome. Withal, secondhand Jerry is a man of ideals and abstractions, exhibiting now and then an heretical twist which is as agreeable as the vermiculations in a mahogany. “We moderns,” said he once to Khalid, “are absolutely one-sided. Here, for instance, is my bookshop, there is the Church, and yonder is the Stock Exchange. Now, the men who frequent them, and though their elbows touch, are as foreign to each other as is a jerboa to a polar bear. Those who go to Church do not go to the Stock Exchange; those who spend their days on the Stock Exchange seldom go to Church; and those who frequent my cellar go neither to the one nor the other. That is why our civilisation produces so many bigots, so many philistines, so many pedants and prigs. The Stock Exchange is as necessary to Society as the Church, and the Church is as vital, as essential to its spiritual well-being as my bookshop. And not until man develops his mental, spiritual and physical faculties to what Matthew Arnold calls ‘a harmonious perfection,’ will he be able to reach the heights from which Idealism is waving to him.”

Thus would the master discourse, and the neophite, sitting on the steps of the cellar, smoking his cigarette, listens, admiring, pondering. And every time he comes with his bucket, Jerry would be standing there, between his little pyramids of books, pipe in mouth, hands in pockets, ready for the discourse. He would also conduct through his underworld anyone who had the leisure and inclination. But fortunately for Khalid, the people of this district are either too rich to buy secondhand books, or too snobbish to stop before this curiosity shop of literature. Hence the master is never too busy; he is always ready to deliver the discourse.

===Chinese===
两年多的光景，哈立德那颗年轻的心灵，从这一架秋千跃向那一架秋千，从这一座旋转木马或滑雪槽奔向下一座，眼前并无什么特别的目标，也不知其所以然、不知其何所为。他甚至闯入了那样幽深的哲学迷宫、那样盘曲的神秘主义回廊，相形之下，连那些阿拉伯咬文嚼字之徒的迷阵也要黯然失色。在他看来，求学问是一桩游戏，以自由的精神、依着自己一时的兴致去追逐，既无法度，也无约束。两年多的光景，他几乎别无所为，只这样四处漫游，一面支取他在银行里的存款，一面焚毁小册子。

一日，他路过一家旧书店，那店正开在城中的金融蜂巢里，紧挨着一座教堂，距证券交易所不过一掷之遥。店主是一位衣衫寒素的耆宿，口中衔着烟斗，站在一摞摞小册子与一座座小小的书山之间——这副模样吸引了哈立德。此人同样栖身于一间地下室。况且他酷肖那幅曾与汤姆·潘恩（Tom Paine）的《理性时代》（*Age of Reason*）一同被焚毁的画里的那位先知。至少在面容上，没有什么不妥帖之处：一部飘逸、参差、乳白的长须，唇边略染一抹金黄；一只鹰钩鼻；两只深陷的蓝眼睛，上覆蓬乱的浓眉；额头宽阔高耸；头顶那圆穹虽略显蓬乱，却并不曾短缺一根毛发——正是先知耶利米（Jeremiah）！唯有一件物事——一只黏土烟斗，他极少从口中取下，除非是为了倒尽烟灰、重新装填——似乎夺去了那张脸上先知般的庄严。除此之外，他正如那位先知一般冷峻阴郁。然而他的嗓音里，却有一种柔韧的甘美，是他脸上那几道硬朗的线条所不曾流露的。哈立德给他起了个绰号叫「二手杰瑞」（secondhand Jerry），向他殷勤致意、结为朋友，一连数月每日都来探望他。他每每拎着水桶前来——用他自己的话说——到杰瑞的井边打水。因为这一老一少、一长一幼，这地下室的老者与年轻的后生，这初入道者与大师，一谈文学及其造就者们便是好几个钟头。而在那蓬乱的圆穹之下，藏着一整片信息的汪洋。况且，二手杰瑞是个心怀理想与抽象之物的人，时不时会显出一股异端的曲折来，一如桃花心木上那蜿蜒的花纹，颇惹人喜。「我们这些现代人，」他有一次对哈立德说，「是彻头彻尾地偏于一端的。譬如，这里是我的书店，那边是教堂，再那边是证券交易所。而常常出入这些地方的人，虽说手肘相触，彼此却陌生得如同跳鼠之于白熊。上教堂的人不上证券交易所；整日在证券交易所里消磨的人很少上教堂；而常来我这地下室的人，两处都不去。正因如此，我们的文明才造出这许多的狂信徒、这许多的庸人、这许多的学究与道学先生。证券交易所之于社会，其必要一如教堂；而教堂之于社会精神上的福祉，其重要、其不可缺，又一如我的书店。唯有待人将其心智、精神、体魄诸般官能，都发展到马修·阿诺德（Matthew Arnold）所谓『一种和谐之完美』的境界，他方能登临那理想主义向他招手的高处。」

大师每每如此高谈阔论，而那初入道者，坐在地下室的台阶上，衔着纸烟，一边听，一边赞叹，一边沉吟。每次他提着桶前来，杰瑞总已立在那里，站在他那几座小小的书山之间，口中衔烟斗，双手插在口袋里，预备开讲。凡有闲暇又有此雅兴的人，他也愿领其穿行于他那地下世界。不过于哈立德倒是幸事：这一区的人要么太阔气，不屑买旧书；要么太势利，不肯在这家文学的奇货铺前驻足。故而大师从不致太忙；他总是随时准备开讲。

===Original===
One day Khalid is conducted into the labyrinthine gloom and mould of the cellar. Through the narrow isles, under a low ceiling, papered, as it were, with pamphlets, between ramparts and mounds of books, old Jerry, his head bowed, his lighted taper in hand, proceeds. And Khalid follows directly behind, listening to his guide who points out the objects and places of interest. And thus, through the alleys and byways, through the nooks and labyrinths of these underground temple-ruins, we get to the rear, where the ramparts and mounds crumble to a mighty heap, rising pell-mell to the ceiling. Here, one is likely to get a glimpse into such enchanted worlds as the name of a Dickens or a Balzac might suggest. Here, too, is Shakespeare in lamentable state; there is Carlyle in rags, still crying, as it were, against the filth and beastliness of this underworld. And look at my lord Tennyson shivering in his nakedness and doomed to keep company with the meanest of poetasters. Observe how Emerson is wriggled and ruffled in this crushing crowd. Does he not seem to be still sighing for a little solitude? But here, too, are spots of the rarest literary interest. Close to the vilest of dime novels is an autograph copy of a book which you might not find at Brentano’s. Indeed, the rarities here stand side by side with the superfluities﻿—the abominations with the blessings of literature﻿—cluttered together, reduced to a common level. And all in a condition which bespeaks the time when they were held in the affection of someone. Now, they lie a-mouldering in these mounds, and on these shelves, awaiting a curious eye, a kindly hand.

===Chinese===
一日，哈立德被引进了那地下室如迷宫般的幽暗与霉气之中。老杰瑞在前引路，低着头，手执一支点燃的细烛，穿过狭窄的甬道，行过低矮的天花板——那天花板仿佛用小册子裱糊而成——行经一道道由书垒成的壁垒与土丘。哈立德紧随其后，听他的向导一一指点值得驻足的物件与去处。就这样，沿着巷陌与小径，穿过这些地下神殿废墟的角落与迷阵，我们来到了最里头：那里的壁垒与土丘崩塌成一巨大书堆，乱糟糟地一直堆到天花板。在这里，人仿佛能窥见如狄更斯（Dickens）或巴尔扎克（Balzac）之名所暗示的那般魔幻世界。这里，莎士比亚（Shakespeare）正处在可悲的境地；那里，卡莱尔（Carlyle）衣衫褴褛，仿佛仍在抗议这地下世界的污秽与兽性。再看丁尼生（Tennyson）大人，赤条条地瑟瑟发抖，注定要与最末流的打油诗人终日为伍。瞧爱默生（Emerson）在这拥挤的人堆里被挤得皱皱巴巴、狼狈不堪——他岂不像是仍在叹息，只求得一隅清静？可这里也有文学旨趣最为珍稀的所在。紧挨着最不堪入目的地摊小说的，或许是一部亲笔签名的孤本，而你在布伦塔诺书店（Brentano’s）未必能觅得。诚然，此处的珍本与冗物并陈——文学的精粹与糟粕杂处——堆叠一处，拉平为同一水准。而它们又都带着一种痕迹，诉说着曾几何时它们也曾为人所珍爱。如今，它们却在这书堆里、在这书架上腐朽发霉，等候着一只好奇的眼睛、一只温存的手。

===Original===
“To me,” writes Khalid in the K.L. MS., “there is always something pathetic in a secondhand book offered again for sale. Why did its first owner part with it? Was it out of disgust or surfeit or penury? Did he throw it away, or give it away, or sell it? Alas, and is this how to treat a friend? Were it not better burned, than sold or thrown away? After coming out of the press, how many have handled this tattered volume? How many has it entertained, enlightened, or perverted? Look at its pages, which evidence the hardship of the journey it has made. Here still is a pressed flower, more convincing in its shrouded eloquence than the philosophy of the pages in which it lies buried. On the flyleaf are the names of three successive owners, and on the margin are lead pencil notes in which the reader criticises the author. Their spirits are now shrouded together and entombed in this pile, where the mould never fails and the moths never die. They too are fallen a prey to the worms of the earth. A secondhand bookshop always reminds me of a Necropolis. It is a kind of Serapeum where lies buried the kings and princes with the helots and underlings of literature. Ay, every book is a mortuary chamber containing the remains of some poor literary wretch, or some mighty genius.﻿ ﻿… A book is a friend, my brothers, and when it ceases to entertain or instruct or inspire, it is dead. And would you sell a dead friend, would you throw him away? If you can not keep him embalmed on your shelf, is it not the wiser part, and the kinder, to cremate him?”

===Chinese===
「在我，」哈立德在赫迪夫图书馆手稿（K.L. MS.）中写道，「一本再度出售的旧书，总带有某种可悲的意味。它的第一位主人为何要与它分手？是出于厌弃，是出于餍足，还是出于拮据？他是把它扔了，是把它赠了人，还是把它卖了？唉，这便是待朋友之道吗？与其被卖、被弃，倒不如烧了岂不更好？自它走出印刷机以来，有多少人经手过这本残破的书？它曾娱悦过多少人、启悟过多少人、又教坏过多少人？看看它的书页吧，那上面印着它一路风尘的痕迹。这里还夹着一朵压干的花，它那缄默的雄辩，比夹着它的那些书页里的哲学更有说服力。扉页上写着先后三位主人的名字，而页边的铅笔批注里，读者对作者横加挑剔。他们的魂灵如今一同被裹挟、被埋葬在这堆书里，这里的霉永远不绝，蛀虫永不死亡。他们也一样，沦为了地下蛀虫的猎物。一家旧书店总叫我想起一座亡灵之城（Necropolis）。它是一座塞拉皮姆（Serapeum），其中并葬着文学的王公贵胄与希洛人（helots）和喽啰。是啊，每一本书都是一间停灵之室，盛殓着某个可怜的文学可怜虫、或某个伟大天才的遗骸。……一本书，我的兄弟们，乃是一位朋友；当它不再娱悦人、不再教诲人、不再鼓舞人时，它便已死去。你难道要出卖一位死去的朋友，难道要把他丢弃？你既不能让他以防腐之躯供奉在你书架上，那么将他火化，岂不是更明智、也更仁厚的办法？」

===Original===
And Khalid tells old Jerry, that if everyone buying and reading books, disposed of them in the end as he himself does, secondhand bookshops would no longer exist. But old Jerry never despairs of business. And the idea of turning his Serapeum into a kiln does not appeal to him. Howbeit, Khalid has other ideas which the old man admires, and which he would carry out if the police would not interfere. “If I were the owner of this shop,” thus the neophite to the master, “I would advertise it with a bonfire of pamphlets. I would take a few hundreds from that mound there and give them the match right in front of that Church, or better still before the Stock Exchange. And I would have two sandwich-men stand about the bonfire, as high priests of the Temple, and chant the praises of secondhand Jerry and his secondhand bookshop. This will be the sacrifice which you will have offered to the god of Trade right in front of his sanctuary that he might soften the induration in the breasts of these worthy citizens, your rich neighbours. And if he does not, why, shut up shop or burn it up, and let us go out peddling together.”

We do not know, however, whether old Jerry ever adopted Khalid’s idea. He himself is an Oriental in this sense; and the business is good enough to keep up, so long as Khalid comes. He is supremely content. Indeed, Shakib asseverates in round Arabic, that the old man of the cellar got a good portion of Khalid’s balance, while balancing Khalid’s mind. Nay, firing it with free-thought literature. Are we then to consider this cellar as Khalid’s source of spiritual illumination? And is this genial old heretic an American avatar of the monk Bahira? For Khalid is gradually becoming a man of ideas and crotchets. He is beginning to see a purpose in all his literary and spiritual rambles. His mental nebulosity is resolving itself into something concrete, which shall weigh upon him for a while and propel him in the direction of Atheism and Demagogy. For old Jerry once visits Khalid in his cellar, and after partaking of a dish of *mujaddara*, takes him to a political meeting to hear the popular orators of the day.

===Chinese===
哈立德又告诉老杰瑞，倘若所有买书读书的人，最终都像他自己那样处置书籍，旧书店便不复存在了。然而老杰瑞从不为生意发愁。至于把他的塞拉皮姆改作一座焚书的窑，他更是毫无兴趣。不过，哈立德另有几番主意，倒颇得老人赏识，若不是怕警察干涉，他真要照办。「我若是这家店的主人，」初入道者对大师如此说，「我便要用一场小册子的篝火来替它做广告。我要从那边那堆书里取它几百本，干脆就在那座教堂门前划根火柴；或者更好，就在证券交易所门前。我还要请两个夹广告牌的人，立在篝火四周，作这神殿的祭司，高声颂扬二手杰瑞和他的二手书店。这便是你在他的圣所门前献给贸易之神的祭品，好让他软化这些体面公民——你那些阔邻居——胸中的那块硬疙瘩。倘若他偏不软化，那好，关了店也罢、烧了店也罢，咱们一同出去做货郎便是。」

然而我们却不知老杰瑞究竟曾否采纳哈立德的主意。就这一点而言，他自己也算是个东方人；况且只要哈立德肯来，生意便足以维持。他心满意足。真的，沙基布用斩钉截铁的阿拉伯语断言：这地下室的老者，在为哈立德的心思谋求平衡之时，也顺带捞到了哈立德存款里可观的一份。不，他还用自由思想的读物，给那心思点了一把火。那么，我们是否该把这间地下室视作哈立德精神启蒙的源泉？这位和蔼的异端老者，难道是修士贝希拉（Bahira）在美国的一个化身？因为哈立德正渐渐变成一个满脑子主张与怪念头的人。他开始在自己一切文学与精神的漫游中，看出一个目的来。他心智上那片混沌的星云，正在凝聚为某种具体之物，将要压在他身上一时，并将他推向无神论与煽动家的方向。因为老杰瑞有一次到地下室来访哈立德，吃了一盘穆贾达拉（mujaddara）之后，便带他去参加一个政治集会，去听当日走红的那几位演说家。

===Original===
And in this is ineffable joy for Khalid. Like every young mind he is spellbound by one of those masters of spread-eagle oratory, and for some time he does not miss a single political meeting in his district. We even see him among the crowd before the corner groggery, cheering one of the political spouters of the day.

And once he accompanies Jerry to the Temple of Atheism to behold its high Priest and hear him chant hallelujah to the Nebular Hypothesis. This is wonderful. How easy it is to dereligionise the human race and banish God from the Universe! But after the High Priest had done this, after he had proven to the satisfaction of every atheist that God is a myth, old Jerry turns around and gives Khalid this warning: “Don’t believe all he says, for I know that atheist well. He is as eloquent as he is insincere.”

And so are all atheists. For at bottom, atheism is either a fad or a trade or a fatuity. And whether the one or the other, it is a sham more pernicious than the worst. To the young mind, it is a shibboleth of cheap culture; to the shrewd and calculating mind, to such orators as Khalid heard, it is a trade most remunerative; and to the scientists, or rather monists, it is the aliment with which they nourish the perversity of their preconceptions. Secondhand Jerry did not say these things to our young philosopher; for had he done so, Khalid, now become edacious, would not have experienced those dyspeptic pangs which almost crushed the soul-fetus in him. For we are told that he is as sedulous in attending these atheistic lectures as he is in flocking with his fellow citizens to hear and cheer the idols of the stump. Once he took Shakib to the Temple of Atheism, but the Poet seems to prefer his *Al-Mutanabbi*. In relating of Khalid’s waywardness he says:

===Chinese===
这其中对哈立德而言，有一种难以言传的欢愉。一如每一颗年轻的心灵，他被一位雄辩滔滔、辞藻夸饰的演说大师所迷住，有一阵子，他区里没有一场政治集会他是缺席的。我们甚至看见他挤在街角那家小酒馆前的人群里，为当日某位政客叫好。

有一次，他陪杰瑞到无神论之殿，去瞻仰那位大祭司，去听他向星云假说（Nebular Hypothesis）唱哈利路亚。这真奇妙。把宗教从人类身上剥除、把上帝逐出宇宙，竟是何等容易！可当那位大祭司做完了这一切、令每位无神论者都满意地证明了上帝不过是一个神话之后，老杰瑞却回过头来，给哈立德这样一句告诫：「别全信他说的，因为我太了解那位无神论者了。他能言善辩，可他同样地言不由衷。」

所有的无神论者无不如此。因为说到底，无神论要么是一种癖好，要么是一桩买卖，要么是一种愚蠢。无论是哪一种，它都是比最坏的更坏的一种伪饰。对于年轻的心灵，它是廉价教养的一句口头禅；对于精明而工于心计之人，对于哈立德所听到的那一类演说家，它是一桩获利极丰的买卖；而对于科学家们——或者不如说一元论者们——它是他们用来滋养自己那种先入为主的偏执的养料。二手杰瑞并未把这些话告诉我们的这位年轻哲人；因为若是他告诉了，哈立德这个如今已变得贪吃的人，便不会经历那几阵几乎把体内灵魂的胎儿压碎的消化不良之痛了。因为我们听说，他赴这些无神论的演讲，与他随同市民们拥去聆听、欢呼那些树桩演说偶像一样，从不懈怠。有一次他带沙基布去无神论之殿，可这位诗人似乎更爱他的穆太奈比（al-Mutanabbi）。在讲述哈立德的任性时，诗人说道：

===Original===
“Ever since we quarrelled about Sibawayh, Khalid and I have seldom been together. And he had become so opinionated that I was glad it was so. Even on Sunday I would leave him alone with Im-Hanna, and returning in the evening, I would find him either reading or burning a pamphlet. Once I consented to accompany him to one of the lectures he was so fond of attending. And I was really surprised that one had to pay money for such masquerades of eloquence as were exhibited that night on the platform. Yes, it occurred to me that if one had not a dollar one could not become an atheist. *Billah!* I was scandalized. For no matter how irreverent one likes to pose, one ought to reverence at least his Maker. I am a Christian by the grace of Allah, and my ancestors are counted among the martyrs of the Church. And thanks to my parents, I have been duly baptized and confirmed. For which I respect them the more, and love them. Now, is it not absurd that I should come here and pay a hard dollar to hear this heretical speechifier insult my parents and my God? Better the ring of al-Mutanabbi’s scimitars and spears than the clatter of these atheistical bones!”

===Chinese===
「自从我们为西伯威（Sibawayh）争吵过一回之后，哈立德和我便很少在一起了。他变得那样固执己见，我倒庆幸彼此疏远了。即便礼拜天，我也由他独自和伊姆-汉娜（Im-Hanna）待在一起，晚上回来时，我总看见他不是在读小册子，便是在烧小册子。有一次我答应陪他去听一场他最爱赴的那种演讲。而我当真大为诧异：那一夜讲坛上所表演的那番雄辩的化装舞会，竟要人掏钱去看。是的，我忽然想到：人若没有一块钱，是当不成无神论者的。凭真主起誓（billah）！我简直骇然。因为不论人爱怎样摆出大不敬的姿态，至少也当敬重造他的主。我托真主（Allah）之恩是一名基督徒，我的先祖也算在教会的殉道者之列。而多亏了我的双亲，我曾 duly 受洗，也曾行过坚振礼。正因如此，我才格外敬重他们、爱他们。那么，我岂不是荒唐至极——竟跑到这里来，掏一块辛辛苦苦挣来的钱，听这个异端的鼓簧之徒辱骂我的父母、辱骂我的上帝？穆太奈比的弯刀与长枪相击的铿锵，胜过这些无神论的枯骨相磕的咔嚓！」

===Original===
From which we infer that Shakib was not open to reason on the subject. He would draw his friend away from the verge of the abyss at any cost. “And this,” continues he, “did not require much effort. For Khalid like myself is constitutionally incapable of denying God. We are from the land in which God has always spoken to our ancestors.”

And the argument between the shrewd verse-maker and the foolish philosopher finally hinges on this: namely, that these atheists are not honest investigators, that in their sweeping generalisations, as in their speciosity and hypocrisy, they are commercially perverse. And Khalid is not long in deciding about the matter. He meets with an accident﻿—and accidents have always been his touchstones of success﻿—which saves his soul and seals the fate of atheism.

One evening, returning from a ramble in the Park, he passes by the Hall where his favourite Mountebank was to lecture on the Gospel of Soap. But not having the price of admittance that evening, and being anxious to hear the orator whom he had idolised, Khalid bravely appeals to his generosity in this quaint and touching note: “My pocket,” he wrote, “is empty and my mind is hungry. Might I come to your Table tonight as a beggar?” And the man at the stage door, who carries the note to the orator, returns in a trice, and tells Khalid to lift himself off. Khalid hesitates, misunderstands; and a heavy hand is of a sudden upon him, to say nothing of the heavy boot.

===Chinese===
由此我们推断，沙基布在这件事上是不肯讲理的。他要不惜一切代价，把朋友从深渊的边缘拉回来。「而这，」他接着说，「并不费多大气力。因为哈立德和我一样，天生就否认不了上帝。我们来自那片真主向来向我们的先祖说话的土地。」

这位精明的诗匠与那位愚蠢的哲人之间的辩论，最终落在这上面：即这些无神论者并非诚实的研究者，在他们那些一概而论的论断里，一如在他们的似是而非与虚伪里，他们是为了逐利而存心偏颇的。而哈立德不久便对此事下了决断。他遭遇了一场意外——而意外向来是他成功的试金石——这场意外救了他的灵魂，也封死了无神论的命数。

一天傍晚，他从公园里散步归来，路过那座大厅，他最崇拜的那位江湖骗子原定要在那儿演讲《肥皂福音》。可那一晚他偏偏没有买票的钱，而又急欲一聆他所奉若神明的演说家，哈立德便大着胆子，以这封古雅而动人的短笺向那人的慷慨求助：「我的口袋，」他写道，「空空如也；而我的头脑，饥肠辘辘。今夜我可否作为一个乞丐，到您的席前来？」那个守后台门的人把短笺送呈演说家，转眼便回来了，叫哈立德快滚。哈立德迟疑着，会错了意；而一只重手忽然落在他身上，更不消说那只重重的靴子了。

===Original===
Ay, and that boot decided him. Atheism, bald, bold, niggardly, brutal, pretending withal, Khalid turns from its door never to look again in that direction, Shakib is right. “These people,” he growled, “are not free thinkers, but free stinkards. They do need soap to wash their hearts and souls.”

An idea did not come to Khalid, as it were, by instalments. In his puerperal pains of mind he was subject to such crises, shaken by such downrushes of light, as only the few among mortals experience. (We are quoting our Scribe, remember.) And in certain moments he had more faith in his instincts than in his reason. “Our instincts,” says he, “never lie. They are honest, and though they be sometimes blind.” And here, he seems to have struck the truth. He can be practical too. Honesty in thought, in word, in deed﻿—this he would have as the cornerstone of his truth. Moral rectitude he places above all the cardinal virtues, natural and theological. “Better keep away from the truth, O Khalid,” he writes, “better remain a stranger to it all thy life, if thou must sully it with the slimy fingers of a mercenary juggler.” Now, these brave words, we can not in conscience criticise. But we venture to observe that Khalid must have had in mind that Gospel of Soap and the incident at the stage door.

===Chinese===
是啊，那只靴子让他下了决断。无神论——赤裸、狂妄、悭吝、粗野、而又虚矫做作——哈立德从它的门前转过身去，再也不朝那个方向看一眼，沙基布是对的。「这些人，」他咆哮道，「不是自由思想家，而是自由臭皮囊。他们确乎需要肥皂，来洗一洗他们的心与灵魂。」

一个念头之降临于哈立德，并非是分期付款式地一点一点来的。在他心智的产褥之痛中，他常经受那样的危机，被那样的一股股光之急流所震撼——而这，唯有凡人中的少数方能经历。（切记，我们此处是援引我们那位书记的话。）在某些时刻，他之信自己的本能，甚于信自己的理性。「我们的本能，」他说，「从不说谎。它们是诚实的，尽管有时是盲目的。」在这一点上，他似乎撞见了真理。他也能务实。思想上的诚实、言语上的诚实、行动上的诚实——这便是他要立为他真理基石之物。他将道德的正直，置于一切根本德性之上，无论是自然的还是神学的德性。「宁可远离真理，哈立德啊，」他写道，「宁可终生与它形同陌路，倘若你必得用一个唯利是图的戏法师那黏腻的手指去玷污它。」这番豪语，我们凭良心说，无从指摘。但我们敢进一言：哈立德写下这话时，心中所想的，必是那部《肥皂福音》，以及后台门口的那场遭遇。

===Original===
And in this, we, too, rejoice. We, too, forgetting the dignity of our position, participate of the revelry in the cellar on this occasion. For our editorialship, dear Reader, is neither American nor English. We are not bound, therefore, to maintain in any degree the algidity and indifference of our confrères’ sublime attitude. We rejoice in the spiritual safety of Khalid. We rejoice that he and Shakib are now reconciled. For the reclaimed runagate is now even permitted to draw on the poet’s balance at the banker. Ay, even Khalid can dissimulate when he needs the cash. For with the assistance of secondhand Jerry and the box-office of the atheistical jugglers, he had exhausted his little saving. He would not even go out peddling any more. And when Shakib asks him one morning to shoulder the box and come out, he replies: “I have a little business with it here.” For after having impeached the High Priests of Atheism he seems to have turned upon himself. We translate from the K.L. MS.

===Chinese===
对此，我们亦复欣然。我们也忘却自己身份的尊严，参与到此刻地下室里的这番欢庆之中。因为亲爱的读者，我们这位编者，既非美利坚人，也非英吉利人。因此我们并无义务，要去维系我们那些同人以那种高高在上的姿态所保持的几分冷峻与淡漠。我们为哈立德精神上的平安而欣喜。我们为他与沙基布如今重修旧好而欣喜。因为这浪子回头之后，如今甚至获准可以支取那位诗人在银行里的存款了。是啊，哈立德一旦需要钱用，也是会做作伪饰的。因为在二手杰瑞的襄助下，在那些无神论戏法师的售票口前，他那一点小小的积蓄早已花光。他甚至连出去做货郎也不肯了。一日清晨沙基布叫他扛上货箱出门去，他答道：「我在这儿跟它还有一点小小的事要料理。」因为在弹劾了无神论的大祭司之后，他似乎转而把自己也弹劾了起来。以下我们译自赫迪夫图书馆手稿——

===Original===
“When I was disenchanted with atheism, when I saw somewhat of the meanness and selfishness of its protagonists, I began to doubt in the honesty of men. If these, our supposed teachers, are so vile, so mercenary, so false﻿—why, welcome Jahannam! But the more I doubted in the honesty of men, the more did I believe that honesty should be the cardinal virtue of the soul. I go so far in this, that an honest thief in my eyes is more worthy of esteem than a canting materialist or a hypocritical free thinker. Still, the voice within me asked if Shakib were honest in his dealings, if I were honest in my peddling? Have I not misrepresented my gewgaws as the atheist misrepresents the truth? ‘This is made in the Holy Land,’﻿—‘This is from the Holy Sepulchre’﻿—these lies, O Khalid, are upon you. And what is the difference between the jewellery you passed off for gold and the arguments of the atheist-preacher? Are they not both instruments of deception, both designed to catch the dollar? Yes, you have been, O Khalid, as mean, as mercenary, as dishonest as those canting infidels.

“And what are you going to do about it? Will you continue, while in the quagmires yourself, to point contemptuously at those standing in the gutter? Will you, in your dishonesty, dare impeach the honesty of men? Are you not going to make a resolution now, either to keep silent or to go out of the quagmires and rise to the mountain-heights? Be pure yourself first, O Khalid; then try to spread this purity around you at any cost.

“Yes; that is why, when Shakib asked me to go out peddling one day, I hesitated and finally refused. For atheism, in whose false dry light I walked a parasang or two, did not only betray itself to me as a sham, but also turned my mind and soul to the sham I had shouldered for years. From the peddling-box, therefore, I turned even as I did from atheism. Praised be Allah, who, in his providential care, seemed to kick me away from the door of its temple. The sham, although effulgent and alluring, was as brief as a summer afternoon.”

As for the peddling-box, our Scribe will tell of its fate in the following Chapter.

===Chinese===
「当我对无神论幻灭之后，当我多少看出了它的几位主角的卑劣与自私之后，我便开始怀疑起人的诚实来。如果这些我们假定的师表都如此卑鄙、如此唯利是图、如此虚伪——那么，就让火狱（Jahannam）来吧！可是我越怀疑人的诚实，便越坚信诚实应当是灵魂的根本德性。在这一点上我走得很远——在我眼中，一个诚实的小偷，也比一个满口仁义的唯物论者、或一个虚伪的自由思想家更值得尊敬。然而，我里面的声音却问我：沙基布在买卖上诚实吗？我自己在做货郎时诚实吗？我难道不曾像那无神论者曲解真理那样，曲解过我那些花里胡哨的零碎？『这是圣地里造的』——『这是从圣墓那儿来的』——哈立德啊，这些谎话都要算在你头上。而你冒充金子推销出去的首饰，与那无神论说教者的论据，又有什么分别？它们岂不都是欺骗的工具，岂不都是为着骗取那块美元而设的？是的，哈立德，你一向与那些满口仁义的不信者一样卑鄙、一样唯利是图、一样不诚实。

「那你又打算如何是好？你自己尚陷在泥沼里，还要不要轻蔑地去指那些站在阴沟里的人？你自己既不诚实，还敢不敢去弹劾人的诚实？你现在难道不该立下一个决心——要么缄口不言，要么从泥沼里拔身出来，登临那山巅的高处？先求你自身的纯洁吧，哈立德啊；然后，再不惜一切代价，把这种纯洁向你四周播散开去。

「是的；正因如此，那一日沙基布叫我出去做货郎时，我迟疑了，终于拒绝了。因为无神论——在它那虚假干硬的光里，我曾走了一两个帕拉桑（parasang）的路——它不仅向我自暴其为一个骗局，更把我的心与灵魂，转向了我多年来肩负着的那个骗局。于是，我转过身背对那只货箱，一如我背对无神论。赞美真主（Allah），他在那眷顾之中，似乎一脚把我从它神殿的门口踢开了。那个骗局，虽曾光芒四射、诱人沉醉，却如一个夏日午后般短暂。」

至于那只货箱，我们的书记将在下一章里讲述它的下场。

## VII / VII

In the Twilight of an Idea / 在一个念头的暮色中

===Original===
It is Voltaire, we believe, who says something to the effect that one’s mind should be in accordance with one’s years. That is why an academic education nowadays often fails of its purpose. For whether one’s mind runs ahead of one’s years, or one’s years ahead of one’s mind, the result is much the same; it always goes ill with the mind. True, knowledge is power; but in order to feel at home with it, we must be constitutionally qualified. And if we are not, it is likely to give the soul such a wrenching as to deform it forever. Indeed, how many of us go through life with a fatal spiritual or intellectual twist which could have been avoided in our youth, were we a little less wise. The young philosophes, the products of the University Machine of today, who go about with a nosegay of ‑isms, as it were, in their lapels, and perfume their speech with the bottled logic of the College Professor—are not most of them incapable of honestly and bravely grappling with the real problems of life? And does not a systematic education mean this, that a young man must go through life dragging behind him his heavy chains of set ideas and stock systems, political, social, or religious? (Remember, we are translating from the Khedivial Library MS.) The author continues:

“Whether one devour the knowledge of the world in four years or four nights, the process of assimilation is equally hindered, if the mind is sealed at the start with the seal of authority. Ay, we can not be too careful of dogmatic science in our youth; for dogmas often dam certain channels of the soul through which we might have reached greater treasures and ascended to purer heights. A young man, therefore, ought to be let alone. There is an infinite possibility of soul-power in every one of us, if it can be developed freely, spontaneously, without discipline or restraint. There is, too, an infinite possibility of beauty in every soul, if it can be evoked at an auspicious moment by the proper word, the proper voice, the proper touch. That is why I say, Go thy way, O my Brother. Be simple, natural, spontaneous, courageous, free. Neither anticipate your years, nor lag childlike behind them. For verily, it is as ridiculous to dye the hair white as to dye it black. Ah, be foolish while thou art young; it is never too late to be wise. Indulge thy fancy, follow the bent of thy mind; for in so doing thou canst not possibly do thyself more harm than the disciplinarians can do thee. Live thine own life; think thine own thoughts; keep developing and changing until thou arrive at the truth thyself. An ounce of it found by thee were better than a ton given to thee gratis by one who would enslave thee. Go thy way, O my Brother. And if my words lead thee to Jahannam, why, there will be a great surprise for thee. There thou wilt behold our Maker sitting on a flaming glacier waiting for the like of thee. And he will take thee into his arms and poke thee in the ribs, and together you will laugh and laugh, until that glacier become a garden and thou a flower therein. Go thy way, therefore; be not afraid. And no matter how many tears thou sheddest on this side, thou wilt surely be poked in the ribs on the other. Go—thy—but—let Nature be thy guide; acquaint thyself with one or two of her laws ere thou runnest wild.”

===Chinese===
伏尔泰（Voltaire），我们想，曾说过这样意思的话：一个人的心智应当与他的年岁相称。这正是当今学院教育屡屡不能达成其目的的缘故。因为无论是一个人的心智跑在年岁之前，还是年岁跑在心智之前，结局大体相同——受苦的总是心智。诚然，知识即力量；但要与之安然相处，我们须有与之相配的天赋体魄。倘若没有，它便很可能把灵魂狠狠扭上一把，致使其永远畸形。的确，我们之中有多少人带着某种致命的精神或智识上的扭曲走完一生，而这本可在我们年少时加以避免——只要我们当初别那么「聪明」。今日那些「大学机器」所出产的年轻哲士们，胸前别着一束形形色色的「主义」，仿佛插着一束胸花，言语之间用学院教授瓶装的逻辑来熏香自己——他们之中，岂非大多数都无力诚实而勇敢地去与人生真正的问题角力？而所谓系统的教育，岂非正意味着：一个青年必须拖着他那一串沉甸甸的既成观念与现成体系——政治的、社会的、宗教的——身后拖着，走完一生？（切记，我们是从赫迪夫图书馆手稿中迻译此段。）作者接着写道：

「一个人无论是用四年还是四个夜晚吞下世间全部学识，只要他的心智从一开始便被权威之印封住，那同化的过程便同样受阻。诚然，我们在年少时对教条式的学问再怎么小心也不为过；因为教条往往会堵死灵魂的某些通路，而正是经由那些通路，我们本可触及更宏大的宝藏、攀上更纯净的高处。所以，青年人应当被放手任其自然。我们每一个人身上都有无穷的心灵之力，只要它能自由地、自发地、不受管束与拘束地开展。每一个灵魂里也有无穷的美之可能，只要它能在恰当时机被恰当的话语、恰当的声音、恰当的触碰所唤起。正因如此我说：走你的路罢，我的兄弟。要质朴、自然、自发、勇敢、自由。既不要抢在你的年岁之前，也不要如孩童一般落在其后。因为，把头发染白与把头发染黑，同样可笑。啊，趁你年少，尽管荒唐；行善向智，永不嫌迟。纵任你的奇想，顺着你心智的取向；如此行事，你所加诸自身的损害，断不至于超过那些管束者所能加之于你的。过你自己的日子，想你自己心里的念头；不断发展、不断改变，直到你自己抵达真理。你自己寻得的一两真理，胜过他人免费塞给你的一吨——而那人，不过是想奴役你罢了。走你的路罢，我的兄弟。倘若我的话把你引入火狱（Jahannam），嘿，那里头可有一桩大大的惊喜等着你。在那里你将看见我们的造物主（Maker）坐在一座燃烧的冰川之上，等候着你这般的人。他会把你搂进怀里，戳戳你的肋骨，于是你们一道大笑、大笑，直到那座冰川化作一座花园，而你是其中的一朵花。所以走你的路罢，不必害怕。无论你在这边洒下多少眼泪，到了那边，你的肋骨照旧要被人戳上一下。走——你——的——罢——但让大自然做你的向导；在你撒野之前，先去熟悉她的一两条法则。」

===Original===
And to what extent did this fantastic mystic son of a Phoenician acquaint himself with Nature’s laws, we do not know. But truly, he was already running wild in the great cosmopolis of New York. From his stivy cellar he issues forth into the plashing, plangent currents of city life. Before he does this, however, he rids himself of all the encumbrances of peddlery which hitherto have been his sole means of support. His little stock of crosses, rosaries, scapulars, false jewellery, mother-of-pearl gewgaws, and suchlike, which he has on the little shelf in the cellar, he takes down one morning—but we will let our Scribe tell the story.

“My love for Khalid,” he writes, “has been severely tried. We could no longer agree about anything. He had become such a dissenter that often would he take the wrong side of a question if only for the sake of bucking. True, he ceased to frequent the cellar of secondhand Jerry, and the lectures of the infidels he no longer attended. We were in accord about atheism, therefore, but in riotous discord about many other things, chief among which was the propriety, the necessity, of doing something to replenish his balance at the banker. For he was now impecunious, and withal importunate. Of a truth, what I had I was always ready to share with him; but for his own good I advised him to take up the peddling-box again. I reminded him of his saying once, ‘Peddling is a healthy and profitable business.’ ‘Come out,’ I insisted, ‘and though it be for the exercise. Walking is the whetstone of thought.’

“One evening we quarrelled about this, and Im-Hanna sided with me. She rated Khalid, saying, ‘You’re a good-for-nothing loafer; you don’t deserve the *mujaddara* you eat.’ And I remember how she took me aside that evening and whispered something about books, and Khalid’s head, and Mar-Kizhayiah.1 Indeed, Im-Hanna seriously believed that Khalid should be taken to Mar-Kizhayiah. She did not know that New York was full of such institutions.2 Her scolding, however, seemed to have more effect on Khalid than my reasoning. And consenting to go out with me, he got up the following morning, took down his stock from the shelf, every little article of it—he left nothing there—and packed all into his peddling-box. He then squeezed into the bottom drawer, which he had filled with scapulars, the bottle with a little of the Stuff in it. For we were in accord about this, that in New York whiskey is better than arak. And we both took a nip now and then. So I thought the bottle was in order. But why he placed his bank book, which was no longer worth a straw, into that bottom drawer, I could not guess. With these preparations, however, we shouldered our boxes, and in an hour we were in the suburbs. We foot it along then, until we reach a row of cottages not far from the railway station. ‘Will you knock at one of these doors,’ I asked. And he, ‘I do not feel like chaffering and bargaining this morning.’ ‘Why then did you come out,’ I urged. And he, in an air of nonchalance, ‘Only for the walk.’ And so, we pursued our way in the Bronx, until we reached one of our favourite spots, where a sycamore tree seemed to invite us to its ample shade.

===Chinese===
这位腓尼基人（Phoenicians）的奇幻神秘主义之子，究竟在何等程度上熟悉了大自然的法则，我们不得而知。但确实，他早已在纽约这座大都会里撒起了野。他从他那闷窒的地窖里钻出，投身于城市生活那奔涌、回荡的人潮之中。然而在这样做之前，他先卸下了所有货郎什物的累赘——那些东西向来是他赖以为生的唯一手段。他那一点小小的存货——十字架、念珠、圣衣（scapulars）、假首饰、珠贝小玩意儿以及诸如此类，原本摆在地窖里的小架上——某天早晨他把它们一一取下——但我们还是让我们的抄写人来讲这个故事罢。

「我对哈立德的爱，」他写道，「受到了严峻的考验。我们在任何事情上都无法再达成一致。他已变成了一个如此好唱反调的人，常常明明一个问题错在他那边，他偏要站过去，只为跟我抬杠。诚然，他不再去光顾那个二道贩子杰里的地窖了，那些不信者的讲座他也不再出席。所以在无神论这一点上我们倒是投契，但在许多别的事情上却闹得不可开交，其中首要的一桩，便是：该不该、有没有必要做点什么，去补一补他在银行家那里的户头结余。因为他这时已是身无分文，却又缠人不已。说真的，我所有的我向来乐意与他分享；但为着他好，我还是劝他重拾货郎箱。我提醒他，他曾说过：『货郎这营生，既养身，又得利。』『出来罢，』我坚持道，『哪怕只是为了活动活动筋骨。走路，是思想的磨刀石。』

「某天傍晚我们就这事吵了一架，伊姆-汉娜（Im-Hanna）站在了我这边。她把哈立德数落了一顿，说道：『你这个没出息的懒汉，你吃的那口穆贾达拉（mujaddara）都不配。』我记得那天傍晚她怎样把我拉到一旁，悄悄说了些关于书、关于哈立德的脑袋、关于马尔-基扎亚（Mar-Kizhayiah）的话。原来，伊姆-汉娜当真相信哈立德该被送去马尔-基扎亚。她不知道纽约到处都是这类机构。不过，她的责骂似乎比我的讲理对哈立德更奏效。他答应同我一起出去，于是第二天一早起身，从架上把他的存货全部取下——每一件小物什，一件不留——统统塞进他的货郎箱。接着他又把那个瓶子里尚余一点「那东西」的酒瓶，硬塞进那已装满圣衣的底层抽屉。因为在这点上我们倒是一致：在纽约，威士忌胜过阿拉克酒（arak）。我们俩都时不时抿上一小口。所以我以为那瓶子原也合情合理。但他为什么把自己那本早已一文不值的银行存折也放进那只底层抽屉，我却猜不透。就这样，一切准备停当，我们扛起各自的箱子，一个钟头后便到了郊外。我们一路步行，直到一排离火车站不远的小屋前。『你去敲其中一家的门罢，』我说。他却道：『今早我没有讨价还价的兴致。』『那你出来做什么？』我催他。他一脸淡然地答道：『只为散步。』于是我们继续在布朗克斯（the Bronx）前行，直到一处我们钟爱的所在，那里一棵梧桐树仿佛在邀请我们到它浓密的荫凉下去。

===Original===
“Here, Khalid, absentminded, laid down his box and sat upon it, and I stretched my limbs on the grass. But of a sudden, he jumped up, opened the bottom drawer of his case, and drew from it the bottle. It is quite in order now, I mused; but ere I had enjoyed the thought, Khalid had placed his box at a little distance, and, standing there beside it, bottle in hand, delivered himself in a semi-solemn, semi-mocking manner of the following: ‘This is the oil,’ I remember him saying, ‘with which I anoint thee—the extreme unction I apply to thy soul.’ And he poured the contents of the bottle into the bottom drawer and over the box, and applied to it a match. The bottle was filled with kerosene, and in a jiffy the box was covered with the flame. Yes; and so quickly, so neatly it was done, that I could not do aught to prevent it. The match was applied to what I thought at first was whiskey, and I was left in speechless amazement. He would not even help me to save a few things from the fire. I conjured him in the name of Allah, but in vain. I clamoured and remonstrated, but to no purpose. And when I asked him why he had done this, he asked me in reply, ‘And why have you not done the same? Now, methinks I deserve my *mujaddara*. And not until you do likewise, will you deserve yours, O Shakib. Here are the lies, now turned to ashes, which brought me my bread and are still bringing you yours. Here are our instruments of deception, our poisoned sources of lucre. I am most happy now, O Shakib. And I shall endeavour to keep my blood in circulation by better, purer means.’ And he took me thereupon by the shoulders, looked into my face, then pushed me away, laughing the laugh of the hashish-smokers.

“Indeed, Im-Hanna was right. Khalid had become too odd, too queer to be sane. Needless to say, I was not prone to follow his example at that time. Nor am I now. *Mashallah!* Lacking the power and madness to set fire to the whole world, it were folly, indeed, to begin with one’s self. I believe I had as much right to exaggerate in peddling as I had in writing verse. My license to heighten the facts holds good in either case. And to some extent, everyone, a poet be he or a cobbler, enjoys such a license. I told Khalid that the logical and most effective course to pursue, in view of his rigorous morality, would be to pour a gallon of kerosene over his own head and fire himself out of existence. For the instruments of deception and debasement are not in the peddling-box, but rather in his heart. No; I did not think peddling was as bad as other trades. Here at least, the means of deception were reduced to a minimum. And of a truth, if everybody were to judge themselves as strictly as Khalid, who would escape burning? So I turned from him that day fully convinced that my little stock of holy goods was innocent, and my balance at the banker’s was as pure as my rich neighbour’s. And he turned from me fully convinced, I believe, that I was an unregenerate rogue. Ay, and when I was knocking at the door of one of my customers, he was walking away briskly, his hands clasped behind his back, and his eyes, as usual, scouring the horizon.”

===Chinese===
「到了这里，哈立德心不在焉地把箱子放下，一屁股坐在上面，我则把四肢摊在草地上。可突然之间，他跳了起来，打开箱子的底层抽屉，从里头抽出那只瓶子。这下倒当真合情合理了，我心想；可我还没来得及把这一念头咂摸够，哈立德已把他的箱子搁到稍远处，立在它旁边，一手执瓶，以半庄严、半讥诮的神气发表了如下一番话：『这就是油，』我记得他说，『我用以膏抹你的油——我施给你灵魂的终傅圣事。』说着，他把瓶中之物倒进底层抽屉、又淋遍整只箱子，划了根火柴凑上去。那瓶里装的是煤油，眨眼之间整只箱子便被火焰吞没。是的；这一切干得如此之快、如此利落，我竟无从阻挡。那火柴点着了我起初以为是威士忌的东西，而我只能瞠目结舌地怔在原地。他甚至不肯帮我从火里抢出几件东西。我奉真主（Allah）之名求他，却徒劳无功。我又嚷又谏，全然无用。待我问他为何这样做，他反倒问我：『你又为何不照做？如今，我想我总算配得上我那口穆贾达拉了。而你，沙基布，除非你也照此办理，否则你不配你的那一份。这些谎言，如今已化作灰烬——它们曾给我带来面包，如今仍在给你带来你的面包。这些，就是我们行骗的家什，我们那些被毒化的财源。我现在无比快乐，沙基布。我将努力用更善、更纯的法子，让我的血继续流起来。』说罢他抓住我的双肩，直视我的脸，然后一把将我推开，发出了哈希什（hashish）烟客们那种笑。

「确实，伊姆-汉娜说得对。哈立德已变得太古怪、太乖张，不能算神志健全了。不消说，那时我并不打算效法他。如今也不。*Mashallah*（真主所欲）！既无放火烧掉整个世界的气力与疯癫，那么从自己烧起，实在未免愚蠢。我相信，我在货郎营生里夸大其词的权利，与我在写诗时夸大其词的权利，原是一样的。我那把事实加以抬高的特许权，在两种场合都一概有效。而且在某种程度上，每一个人，无论他是诗人还是皮匠，都享有这种特许。我对哈立德说，以他那套严厉的道德观而论，最合乎逻辑、也最见成效的做法，莫过于往自己头上浇一加仑煤油，把自己一把火烧出这世上。因为行骗与堕落的工具并不在货郎箱里，倒是在他的心里。不；我并不以为货郎这一行比别行更糟。在这里，欺骗的手段至少已被压到最低。说真的，倘若人人都像哈立德那样严苛地审判自己，谁能逃过被烧的下场？于是那天我从他身边转过身去，深信我那一小份圣物是无辜的，我在银行家那里的结余也像我那位阔邻居的结余一样清白。而他，我相信，也从我身边转过身去，深信我是一个不可救药的恶棍。是啊，当我正在敲一位主顾的门时，他却正快步走开，双手背在身后，那双眼睛，一如往常，扫视着地平线。」

===Original===
And on that horizon are the gilded domes and smoking chimneys of the seething city. Leaving his last friend and his last burden behind, he will give civilised life another trial. Loafer and tramp that he is! For even the comforts of the grand cable-railway he spurns, and foots it from the Bronx down to his cellar near Battery Park, thus cutting the city in half and giving one portion to Azrael and the other to Iblis. But not being quite ready himself for either of these winged Furies, he keeps to his cellar. He would tarry here a while, if but to carry out a resolution he has made. True, Khalid very seldom resolves upon anything; but when he does make a resolution, he is even willing to be carried off by the effort to carry it out. And now, he would solve this problem of earning a living in the great city by honest means. For in the city, at least, success well deserves the compliments which those who fail bestow upon it. What Montaigne said of greatness, therefore, Khalid must have said of success. If we can not attain it, let us denounce it. And in what terms does he this, O merciful Allah! We translate a portion of the apostrophe in the K.L. MS., and not the bitterest, by any means.

“O Success,” the infuriated failure exclaims, “how like the Gorgon of the *Arabian Nights* thou art! For does not everyone whom thou favorest undergo a pitiful transformation even from the first bedding with thee? Does not everything suffer from thy look, thy touch, thy breath? The rose loses its perfume, the grapevine its clusters, the bulbul its wings, the dawn its light and glamour. O Success, our lords of power today are thy slaves, thy helots, our kings of wealth. Everyone grinds for thee, everyone for thee lives and dies.… Thy palaces of silver and gold are reared on the souls of men. Thy throne is mortised with their bones, cemented with their blood. Thou ravenous Gorgon, on what bankruptcies thou art fed, on what failures, on what sorrows! The railroads sweeping across the continents and the steamers ploughing through the seas, are laden with sacrifices to thee. Ay, and millions of innocent children are torn from their homes and from their schools to be offered to thee at the sacrificial-stone of the Factories and Mills. The cultured, too, and the wise, are counted among thy slaves. Even the righteous surrender themselves to thee and are willing to undergo that hideous transformation. O Success, what an infernal litany thy votaries and high-priests are chanting to thee.… Thou ruthless Gorgon, what crimes thou art committing, and what crimes are being committed in thy name!”

===Chinese===
而在那道地平线上的，是这座沸腾之城的镀金穹顶与冒着烟的烟囱。抛下最后一位朋友、抛下最后一件负累，他要对文明生活再试上一试。好一个游荡汉、流浪汉！因为连那气派的有缆铁道的舒适他也嗤之以鼻，硬是从布朗克斯一路步行，回到他靠近巴特利公园（Battery Park）的地窖，如此把这座城市拦腰切成两半，一半献给亚兹拉尔（Azrael），一半献给伊布力斯（Iblis）。但他自己既尚未准备好去见这两位生翼的复仇女神，便仍躲在他的地窖里。他要在这里盘桓一阵，哪怕只是为了实行他所立下的一个决心。诚然，哈立德极少对任何事下决心；可一旦下了决心，他甚至情愿为着把它贯彻到底而被那番努力裹挟了去。如今，他要用正当的法子，解决在这座大城市里谋生这一问题。因为至少在城市里，成功确当之无愧地享受那些失败者对它的恭维。所以蒙田（Montaigne）论及伟大所说的那番话，哈立德想必也用在了成功之上。我们若不能得到它，便让我们去诋毁它罢。而他是用怎样的言辞来诋毁的啊，仁慈的真主！我们从赫迪夫图书馆手稿中迻译了他那段呼告的一部分——而且无论如何，还不是最刻毒的那部分。

「啊，成功，」这位怒火中烧的失败者呼喊道，「你多么像《一千零一夜》（Arabian Nights）里那个戈耳工（Gorgon）！因为凡蒙你青眼的，岂不是从与你同枕的第一夜起便经历了一场可悲的变形？万物岂不都因你的目光、你的触碰、你的呼吸而受害？玫瑰失去它的芬芳，葡萄藤失去它的果穗，夜莺（bulbul）失去它的翅膀，黎明失去它的光与魅。啊，成功，我们今日的权贵老爷们是你的奴仆、你的希洛人（helots），我们的财富之王。人人都为你卖命，人人都为你而生、为你而死。……你那些金银的宫殿，是建立在人的灵魂之上的。你的宝座以他们的骨头榫接，以他们的鲜血胶合。你这贪婪的戈耳工，你以多少破产、多少失败、多少哀愁为食！横扫诸大陆的铁路，犁穿大海的轮船，满载着献与你的祭品。唉，还有数以百万计的无辜孩童被从家中、从学校里生生夺走，在工厂与磨坊那块祭石之上献与了你。那有教养的人、那有智慧的人，也算在你的奴仆之中。连义人也向你降服，甘愿承受那可怖的变形。啊，成功，你的信徒与大祭司正向你吟诵着何等地狱般的祷文。……你这无情的戈耳工，你犯下了怎样的罪行，又有着怎样的罪行正以你的名义犯下！」

===Original===
From which it is evident that Khalid does not wish for success. Khalid is satisfied if he can maintain his hold on the few spare feet he has in the cellar, and continue to replenish his little store of lentils and olive oil. For he would as lief be a victim of success, he assures us, as to forego his *mujaddara*. And still having this, which he considers a luxury, he is willing to turn his hand at anything, if he can but preserve inviolate the integrity of his soul and the freedom of his mind. These are a few of the pet terms of Khalid. And in as much as he can continue to repeat them to himself, he is supremely content. He can be a menial, if while cringing before his superiors, he were permitted to chew on his pet illusions. A few days before he burned his peddling-box, he had read Epictetus. And the thought that such a great soul maintained its purity, its integrity, even in bonds, encouraged and consoled him. “How can they hurt me,” he asks, “if spiritually I am far from them, far above them? They can do no more than place gilt buttons on my coat and give me a cap to replace this slouch. Therefore, I will serve. I will be a slave, even like Epictetus.”

And here we must interpose a little of our skepticism, if but to gratify an habitual craving in us. We do not doubt that Khalid’s self-sufficiency is remarkable; that his courage—on paper—is quite above the common; that the grit and stay he shows are wonderful; that his lofty aspirations, so indomitable in their onwardness, are great: but we only ask, having thus fortified his soul, how is he to fortify his stomach? He is going to work, to be a menial, to earn a living by honest means? Ah, Khalid, Khalid! Did you not often bestow a furtive glance on someone else’s check-book? Did you not even exercise therein your skill in calculation? If the bank, where Shakib deposits his little saving, failed, would you be so indomitable, so dogged in your resolution? Would you not soften a trifle, loosen a whit, if only for the sake of your blood-circulation?

===Chinese===
由此可见，哈立德并不希求成功。哈立德只要能保住他在地窖里那区区几尺地盘，能继续添补他那点小扁豆和橄榄油的存粮，便心满意足了。因为他对我们保证，他宁愿做成功的牺牲品，也不愿舍弃他的穆贾达拉。既然仍拥有这道他视为珍馐的吃食，他便愿意动手做任何事，只要能保全他那灵魂的完整与心智的自由，使之不受玷污。这几个，便是哈立德的口头禅。而只要他能继续把它们向自己念诵，他便至为满足。他能做一个仆役，只要在向上司卑躬屈膝之际，仍获准咀嚼他那些钟爱的幻觉。在烧掉货郎箱的几天之前，他读过爱比克泰德（Epictetus）。想到这样一颗伟大的灵魂即使在枷锁之中仍能保住它的纯净、它的完整，他便受到鼓舞与慰藉。「他们怎能伤得了我，」他问，「倘若我在精神上远远离开了他们、远远高过了他们？他们至多不过是在我的外套上钉上几颗镀金纽扣，再给我一顶帽子来替换我这顶旧软帽罢了。所以，我愿意服役。我愿意做一个奴隶，哪怕像爱比克泰德那样。」

这里我们不得不插进一点自己的怀疑，哪怕只为满足我们自己一项积习难改的嗜好。我们并不怀疑哈立德的自足堪称非凡；他的勇气——写在纸上的勇气——远在常人之上；他所显示的坚毅与定力令人惊叹；他那些崇高的抱负，在那股一往无前之中如此不可折挠，确乎伟大：我们只想问，他既如此武装了他的灵魂，又将如何武装他的胃？他要去做工、去做仆役、去靠正当手段谋生？唉，哈立德，哈立德！你难道不曾屡屡对别人的支票簿偷去一瞥？你难道不曾甚至在其中演练过你那计算的技巧？倘若沙基布存着那点小积蓄的银行倒闭了，你的决心还能如此不可摧、如此顽强吗？难道你不会软上一丝、松上一毫，哪怕只是为了你那血液循环的缘故？

===Original===
Indeed, Shakib has become a patron to Khalid. Shakib the poet, who himself should have a patron, is always ready to share his last dollar with his loving, though cantankerous friend. And this, in spite of all the disagreeable features of a friendship which in the Syrian Colony was become proverbial. But Khalid now takes up the newspapers and scans the Want Columns for hours. The result being a clerkship in a lawyer’s office. Nay, an apprenticeship; for the legal profession, it seems, had for a while engaged his serious thoughts.

And this of all the professions is the one on which he would graft his scion of lofty morality? Surely, there be plenty of fuel for a conflagration in a lawyer’s office. Such rows of half-calf tomes, such piles of legal documents, all designed to combat dishonesty and fraud, “and all immersed in them, and nourished and maintained by them.” In what a sorry condition will your Morality issue out of these bogs! A lawyer’s clerk, we are informed, can not maintain his hold on his clerkship, if he does not learn to blink. That is why Khalid is not long in serving papers, copying summonses, and searching title-deeds. In this lawyer’s office he develops traits altogether foreign to his nature. He even becomes a quidnunc, prying now and then into the personal affairs of his superiors. Ay, and he dares once to suggest to his employer a new method of dealing with the criminals among his clients. Withal, Khalid is slow, slower than the law itself. If he goes out to serve a summons he does not return for a day. If he is sent to search title-deeds, he does not show up in the office for a week. And often he would lose himself in the Park surrounding the Register’s Office, pondering on his theory of immanent morality. He would sit down on one of those benches, which are the anchors of loafers of another type, his batch of papers beside him, and watch the mad crowds coming and going, running, as it were, between two fires. These puckered people are the living, moving chambers of sleeping souls.

===Chinese===
的确，沙基布已成了哈立德的恩主。诗人沙基布，本该自己有位恩主的，却总是乐意把自己最后一块钱分给这位既爱他、又爱找茬的朋友。而且这还是不顾这段友谊里种种不快之处——这段友谊在叙利亚区（Syrian Quarter）早已成了有口皆碑的笑谈。可哈立德如今却拿起报纸，一连数小时细细浏览「招聘栏」。结果便是：在一家律师事务所谋得一个书记的差事。不，应当说是一段学徒生涯；因为看来法律这一行，曾有一阵子确实占去了他严肃的思虑。

而在所有职业之中，他偏要把自己那一枝崇高道德的幼苗，嫁接到这一行上？一家律师事务所里，引燃一场大火的燃料当然是充足的。那一排排半小牛皮的对开巨册，那一堆堆的法律文书，统统是为对付欺瞒与诈伪而设，「然而又统统浸淫其中，靠它们滋养、维系。」你的道德观从这些泥沼里钻出来时，将是何等狼狈的模样！据说，一个律师事务所的书记若不学会视而不见，便不能保住他的差事。正因如此，哈立德没过多久，便送传票、抄传票、查地契，都做起来了。在这家律师事务所里，他养出了种种与他本性全然相悖的习气。他甚至成了一个爱打听是非的人，时不时去窥探上司们的私事。是啊，他竟敢有一次向雇主进言，提出一种处置当事人中那些罪犯的新法子。此外，哈立德做事之慢，比法律本身还慢。他若出去送一张传票，一日不回。他若被派去查地契，一周不在事务所里露面。他又常常把自己丢失在登记处（Register’s Office）周围的那座公园里，琢磨着他那套内在道德论。他会坐在其中一条长椅上——那种长椅是另一类游荡汉们的锚——身边放着一沓文件，看着那疯狂的众人来来去去，仿佛在两重烈火之间奔走。这些眉头紧锁的人，便是一个个活生生的、会移动的、装着沉睡灵魂的小室。

===Original===
Khalid was always glad to come to this Register’s Office. For though the searching of title-deeds be a mortal process, the loafing margin of the working hour could be extended imperceptibly, and without hazarding his or his employer’s interest. The following piece of speculative fantasy and insight must have been thought out when he should have been searching title-deeds.

“This Register’s Office,” it is written in the K.L. MS., “is the very bulwark of Society. It is the foundation on which the Trust Companies, the Courts, and the Prisons are reared. Your codes are blind without the miraculous torches which this Office can light. Your judges can not propound the ‘laur’—I beg your pardon, the law—without the aid of these musty, smelling, dilapidated tomes. Ay, these are the very constables of the realm, and without them there can be no realm, no legislators, and no judges. Strong, club-bearing constables, these Liebers, standing on the boundary lines, keeping peace between brothers and neighbours.

“Here, in these Liebers is an authority which never fails, never dies—an authority which willy-nilly we obey and in which we place unbounded trust. In any one of these Registers is a potentiality which can always worst the quibbles and quiddities of lawyers and ward off the miserable technicalities of the law. Any of them, when called upon, can go into court and dictate to the litigants and the attorneys, the jury and the judge. They are the deceased witnesses come to life. And without them, the judges are helpless, the marshals and sheriffs too. Ay, and what without them would be the state of our real-estate interests? Abolish your constabulary force, and your police force, and with these muniments of power, these dumb but farseeing agents of authority and intelligence, you could still maintain peace and order. But burn you this Register’s Office, and before the last Lieber turn to ashes, ere the last flame of the conflagration die out, you will have to call forth, not only your fire squads, but your police force and even your soldiery, to extinguish other fires different in nature, but more devouring—and as many of them as there are boundary lines in the land.”

===Chinese===
哈立德一向乐于到这座登记处来。因为查地契虽是一桩要命的差事，可工作时间的「闲荡余地」却能在这里不知不觉地拉长，而于他本人或他雇主的利益都无妨害。下面这一段思辨的奇想与洞见，想必正是他本该查地契的时候想出来的。

「这座登记处，」赫迪夫图书馆手稿中写道，「正是社会的中流砥柱。它是托拉斯公司、法院与监狱赖以耸立的根基。你们的法典若无此处的神奇火把照亮，便全是盲目的。你们的法官若无这些发霉、发臭、残破不堪的对开巨册相助，便无法宣讲『洛』——请恕我，我是说法律。是的，这些才是这国度真正的治安吏，没有它们便没有国度，没有立法者，也没有法官。这些利伯氏（Liebers），是手执警棍、强壮有力的治安吏，立于地界之上，在兄弟与邻里之间维持着和平。
