「在这些利伯之中，是一种永不失效、永不枯竭的权威——一种我们不问情愿与否都得服从、并对之寄予无限信任的权威。在随便哪一册登记簿（Registers）里，都潜藏着一种力量，它总能胜过律师们的诡辞与玄谈，挡开法律那套可悲的技术细节。它们之中随便哪一册，一旦被援引，便能上堂去对诉讼两造与律师、对陪审团与法官发号施令。它们便是死而复生的证人。没有它们，法官们一筹莫展，法警与县治安官亦然。唉，若没有它们，我们的不动产利益又将处于何等境地？即便废除了你们的治安队，废除了你们的警察队，只要凭借这些权力的凭证、这些哑口却目光深远的权威与智识的代理人，你们仍可维持和平与秩序。可你若把这座登记处付之一炬，那么，在最后一册利伯化作灰烬之前，在这场大火的最后一簇火焰熄灭之前，你便不得不调动的，不仅是你的消防队，还有你的警察队，甚至你的军队，去扑灭另一些性质不同、却更加贪噬的火焰——其数目之多，正如这国土地界之数。」

===Original===
And we now come to the gist of the matter.

“What wealth of moral truth,” he continues, “do we find in these greasy, musty pages. When one deeds a piece of property, he deeds with it something more valuable, more enduring. He deeds with it an undying human intelligence which goes down to posterity, saying, Respect my will; believe in me; and convey this respect and this belief to your offspring. Ay, the immortal soul breathes in a deed as in a great book. And the implicit trust we place in a musty parchment, is the mystic outcome of the blind faith, or rather the farseeing faith which our ancestors had in the morality and intelligence of coming generations. For what avails their deeds if they are not respected?… We are indebted to our forbears, therefore, not for the miserable piece of property they bequeath us, but for the confidence and trust, the faith and hope they had in our innate or immanent morality and intelligence. The will of the dead is law for the living.”

Are we then to look upon Khalid as having come out of that Office with soiled fingers only? Or has the young philosopher abated in his clerkship the intensity of his moral views? Has he not assisted his employer in the legal game of quieting titles? Has he not acquired a little of the delusive plausibilities of lawyers? Shakib throws no light on these questions. We only know that the clerkship or rather apprenticeship was only held for a season. Indeed, Khalid must have recoiled from the practice. Or in his recklessness, not to say obtrusion, he must have been outrageous enough to express in the office of the honourable attorney, or in the neighbourhood thereof, his views about pettifogging and suchlike, that the said honourable attorney was under the painful necessity of asking him to stay home. Nay, the young Syrian was discharged. Or to put it in a term adequate to the manner in which this was done, he was “fired.” Now, Khalid betakes him back to his cellar, and thrumming his lute-strings, lights up the oppressive gloom with Arabic song and music.

===Chinese===
至此，我们来到了事情的关节。

「我们在这些油污、发霉的书页里，」他接着写道，「所见到的道德真理，何等丰饶。当一个人立契让渡一块地产时，他连同它一道让渡了某种更可贵、更经久的东西。他连同它一道让渡了一种不朽的人类智识，它传诸后世，说道：尊重我的意志；信我；并把这份尊重、这份信，也转达给你的子孙。是的，不朽的灵魂在一纸契约里呼吸，一如在一本伟大的书里。而我们置于一张发霉羊皮纸上的那份默然之信，正是我们的祖先对后代道德与智识所抱的那种盲目之信——不如说，远见之信——所结出的神秘果实。因为他们的契约若不受尊重，又有何用？……所以，我们之所以受惠于先人，并非为了他们遗下给我们的那可怜的一块地产，而是为了他们对我们内在的、或说内在固有的道德与智识所怀的那份信赖与信托、那份信心与希望。逝者之意志，即生者之法律。」

那么，我们难道要据此把哈立德看作只是带着一双沾污的手指，从那处 Office 里走出来？抑或，这位年轻的哲士在他的书记差事中，已缓和了他那套道德观的峻烈？他难道不曾协助他的雇主，玩过那套「了结产权」的法律把戏？他难道不曾沾上一点律师们那骗人的圆滑？这些问题，沙基布并未提供任何线索。我们只知道，那段书记生涯——或者毋宁说学徒生涯——只维持了一季。的确，哈立德想必是对这一行径退缩了的。否则，以他那股孟浪——不说僭妄了——他必是在那位可敬的律师的事务所里、或其邻近之处，放肆地就钻营打官司之类的事发表了一番见解，以至于那位可敬的律师不得不痛苦地请他留在家里别来了。不，应当说，这位年轻的叙利亚人被解雇了。或者用一种足以与此事发生的情形相配的词来说，他被「炒了」（fired）。如今，哈立德回到了他的地窖，一边拨弄着他的鲁特琴弦，一边以阿拉伯的歌与乐，照亮那压人的阴翳。

## VIII / VIII

With the Houris / 与呼丽们在一起

===Original===
From the house of law the dervish Khalid wends his way to that of science, and from the house of science he passes on to that of metaphysics. His staff in hand, his wallet hung on his shoulder, his silver cigarette case in his pocket, patient, confident, content, he makes his way from one place to another. Unlike his brother dervishes, he is clean and proud of it, too. He knocks at this or that door, makes his wish known to the servant or the mistress, takes the crumbs given him, and not infrequently gives his prod to the dogs. In the vestibule of one of the houses of spiritism, he tarries a spell and parleys with the servant. The Mistress, a fair-looking, fair-spoken dame of seven lustrums or more, issues suddenly from her studio, in a curiously designed black velvet dressing-gown; she is drawn to the door by the accent of the foreigner’s speech and the peculiar cadence of his voice. They meet: and magnetic currents from his dark eyes and her eyes of blue, flow and fuse. They speak: and the lady asks the stranger if he would not serve instead of begging. And he protests, “I am a Dervish at the door of Allah.” “And I am a Spirit in Allah’s house,” she rejoins. They enter: and the parley in the vestibule is followed by a tête-à-tête in the parlour and another in the dining-room. They agree: and the stranger is made a member of the Spiritual Household, which now consists of her and him, the Medium and the Dervish.

===Chinese===
德尔维希（dervish）哈立德，从律法之舍走向科学之舍，又从科学之舍转赴玄学之舍。手中拄着行杖，肩上挂着褡裢，口袋里揣着他的银烟盒，耐心、自信、知足，他从一处踅到另一处。与他的德尔维希弟兄们不同，他是洁净的，且以此为傲。他敲敲这家的门，又敲敲那家的门，向仆人或是主妇道明来意，接下施舍给他的那点残屑，并不时给那些恶狗一点颜色看看。在一处招魂之家的门廊里，他盘桓了片刻，与仆人攀谈起来。那位主妇——一位相貌端好、言语婉转、年约七个五年期有余的夫人——忽地从她的工作室里走了出来，身披一件式样奇异的黑丝绒晨衣；引她到门前来的，是那异乡人话语里的口音，与他嗓音中那种奇特的抑扬。两人相会：他那双黑眼睛与她那双蓝眼睛之间，磁性的电流奔涌交融。两人交谈：夫人问这位异乡人，可愿意去做工，而不去乞讨。他抗声道：「我是立于真主（Allah）门前的一名德尔维希。」「而我，」她应道，「是真主宅中的一灵。」两人入内：门廊里的那番攀谈之后，是客厅里的一席密语，又是饭厅里的一席。两人达成默契：这位异乡人便成了那「灵性之家」的一员——此家如今只由她与他构成，灵媒（the Medium）与德尔维希。

===Original===
Now, this fair-spoken dame, who dotes on the occult and exotic, delights in the aroma of Khalid’s cigarettes and Khalid’s fancy. And that he might feel at ease, she begins by assuring him that they have met and communed many times ere now, that they have been friends under a preceding and long vanished embodiment. Which vagary Khalid seems to countenance by referring to the infinite power of Allah, in the compass of which nothing is impossible. And with these mystical circumlocutions of ceremony, they plunge into an intimacy which is bordered by the metaphysical on one side, and the physical on the other. For though the Medium is at the threshold of her climacteric, Khalid afterwards tells Shakib that there be something in her eyes and limbs which always seem to be waxing young. And of a truth, the American woman, of all others, knows best how to preserve her beauty from the ravages of sorrow and the years. That is why, we presume, in calling him, “child,” she does not permit him to call her, “mother.” Indeed, the Medium and the Dervish often jest, and somewhiles mix the frivolous with the mysterious.

===Chinese===
这位言语婉转的夫人，素来钟情于玄秘与异域之物，哈立德那烟卷的香、哈立德那奇思的香，都教她心喜。为着让他自在，她一上来便向他保证：他们在此前早已相会、相谈过许多次，在前一番业已消逝的久远化身之中，他们曾为友。这番奇想，哈立德似乎也予以首肯——他把一切归于真主那无穷的大能，在大能所及的范围之内，没有什么是不可能的。就在这套玄秘的客套与迂回之间，他们陷入了一段亲密：其一侧以玄学为界，另一侧以肉身为界。因为灵媒虽已站在她那更年之候的门槛上，哈立德后来却告诉沙基布，她的眼与她的肢体之中，总有某种东西，仿佛永远在向年轻处滋长。说真的，美国女人，比之任何别的女人，最懂得如何护住自己的美，使之免受哀愁与岁月的摧残。正因如此，我们揣想，她唤他作「孩子」，却不许他唤她作「母亲」。确实，灵媒与德尔维希时常戏谑，有时也将轻佻与玄秘搅在一处。

===Original===
We would still follow our Scribe here, were it not that his pruriency often reaches the edge. He speaks of “the liaison” with all the rude simplicity and frankness of the *Arabian Nights*. And though, as the Mohammedans say, “To the pure everything is pure,” and again, “Who quotes a heresy is not guilty of it”; nevertheless, we do not feel warranted in rending the veil of the reader’s prudery, no matter how transparent it might be. We believe, however, that the pruriency of Orientals, like the prudery of Occidentals, is in fact only an appearance. On both sides there is a display of what might be called verbal virtue and verbal vice. And on both sides, the exaggerations are configured in a harmless pose. Be this as it may, we at least, shall withhold from Shakib’s lasciviousness the English dress it seeks at our hand.

===Chinese===
若不是我们这位抄写人的好色之辞每每逼近那条边线，我们原还要在此跟着他往下走的。他谈及「那番私情」，带着《一千零一夜》（Arabian Nights）那种粗犷的质朴与坦白。虽然如穆斯林所言，「于纯洁者，万物皆洁」，又如他们所言，「引用异端之人，并不为异端之罪」；然而我们仍觉得，无权去撕开读者那层端庄的帷幕——无论那帷幕多么透明。不过我们相信，东方人的好色，正似西方人的端庄，其实不过是一种表象。两边所展示的，都可称作「言辞上的德行」与「言辞上的罪过」。而两边的夸张，也都摆出一种于人无害的姿态。无论如何，至少我们，决不替沙基布那番淫辞，穿上它向我们讨要的那身英装。

===Original===
We note, however, that Khalid now visits him in the cellar only when he craves a dish of *mujaddara*; that he and the Medium are absorbed in the contemplation of the Unseen, though not, perhaps, of the Impalpable; that they gallivant in the Parks, attend Bohemian dinners, and frequent the Don’t Worry Circles of Metaphysical Societies; that they make long expeditions together to the Platonic North-pole and back to the torrid regions of Swinburne; and that together they perform their *zikr* and drink at the same fountain of ecstasy and devotion. Withal, the Dervish, who now wears his hair long and grows his finger nails like a Brahmin, is beginning to have some manners.

===Chinese===
然而我们留意到：哈立德如今只有在馋一碗穆贾达拉（mujaddara）时，才会下到地窖里去看他；他与灵媒潜心凝视着那「不可见」，虽然未必是那「不可触」；他们一道在公园里招摇，赴波希米亚（Bohemian）的宴席，常去出入那些玄学学会的「莫愁圈」；他们一同远征，远至柏拉图式的北极，又折回斯温伯恩（Swinburne）的炎热之境；他们又一同行他们的齐克尔（zikr），同饮那一脉狂喜与虔信之泉。此外，这位如今蓄起长发、留起指甲、状若婆罗门的德尔维希，也总算开始有了几分规矩。

===Original===
The Medium, nevertheless, withholds from him the secret of her art. If he desires, he can attend the séances like every other stranger. Once Khalid, who would not leave anything unprobed, insisted, importuned; he could not see any reason for her conduct. Why should they not work together in Tiptology, as in Physiology and Metaphysics? And one morning, dervish-like, he wraps himself in his aba, and, calling upon Allah to witness, takes a rose from the vase on the table, angrily plucks its petals, and strews them on the carpet. Which portentous sign the Medium understands and hastens to minister her palliatives.

===Chinese===
然而，灵媒却向他守着她那一门技艺的秘密。他若愿意，可以像任何一个陌生客那样出席降神会。哈立德一向凡事都要探个究竟，有一回便执意追问、苦苦纠缠；他实在看不出她那番举止有何道理。为何他们不能在招魂术（tiptology，即 spirit-table-tipping）上一同用功，一如他们在生理学与玄学上一同用功？于是一天早晨，他一副德尔维希做派，把自己裹进他的阿巴（aba，阿拉伯斗篷），唤真主作证，从桌上的花瓶里取出一朵玫瑰，愤愤地扯下花瓣，撒在地毯上。这一不祥之兆，灵媒一看便懂，赶忙施以她的缓兵之辞。

===Original===
“No, Child, you shall not go,” she begs and supplicates; “listen to me, are we not together all the time? Why not leave me alone then with the spirits? One day you shall know all, believe me. Come, sit here,” stroking her palm on her lap, “and listen. I shall give up this tiptology business very soon; you and I shall overturn the table. Yes, Child, I am on the point of succumbing under an awful something. So, don’t ask me about the spooks any more. Promise not to torment me thus any more. And one day we shall travel together in the Orient; we shall visit the ruins of vanished kingdoms and creeds. Ah, to be in Palmyra with you! Do you know, Child, I am destined to be a Bedouin queen. The throne of Zenobia is mine, and yours too, if you will be good. We shall resuscitate the glory of the kingdom of the desert.”

===Chinese===
「不，孩子，你不许走，」她又是求、又是告；「听我说，我们岂不是时时刻刻都在一起？那何不把我一个人留给那些精灵呢？终有一日你会尽知一切的，信我。来，坐这里，」她在自己膝上摩挲着手心，「听着。我很快便会丢开这套招魂术的营生；你和我，要一同把那张桌子掀翻。是的，孩子，我已濒于在某样可怕之物下屈服。所以，别再问我那些鬼魂的事了。答应我，别再这样折磨我。有朝一日，我们要一同去东方游历；我们要去看那些消逝了的王国与信仰的废墟。啊，能与你同在帕尔米拉（Palmyra）！你可知道，孩子，我命里注定要做一位贝都因（Bedouin）女王。芝诺比娅（Zenobia）的宝座是我的，也是你的——只要你乖。我们要复兴那沙漠王国的荣光。」

===Original===
To all of which Khalid acquiesces by referring as is his wont to the infinite wisdom of Allah, in whose all-seeing eye nothing is impossible.

===Chinese===
对这一切，哈立德概然默许——一如他的习惯，把话头归于真主那无穷的智慧，在真主那无所不见的眼中，没有什么是不可能的。

===Original===
And thus, apparently satisfied, he takes the cigarette which she had lighted for him, and lights for her another from his own. But the smoke of two cigarettes dispels not the threatening cloud; it only conceals it from view. For they dine together at a Bohemian Club that evening, where Khalid meets a woman of rare charms. And she invites him to her studio. The Medium, who is at first indifferent, finally warns her callow child. “That woman is a writer,” she explains, “and writers are always in search of what they call ‘copy.’ She in particular is a huntress of male curiosities, *originales*, whom she takes into her favour and ultimately surrenders them to the reading public. So be careful.” But Khalid hearkens not. For the writer, whom he afterwards calls a flighter, since she, too, “like the van of the brewer only skims the surface of things,” is, in fact, younger than the Medium. Ay, this woman is even beautiful﻿—to behold, at least. So the Dervish, a captive of her charms, knocks at the door of her studio one evening and enters. Ah, this then is a studio! “I am destined to know everything, and to see everything,” he says to himself, smiling in his heart.

===Chinese===
于是，他一副显然心足意满的样子，接过她替他点燃的烟卷，又从自己的烟卷上为她点燃另一支。然而两支烟卷的烟雾，并不能驱散那团逼人之云；它只是把它遮了起来，使之不入眼罢了。因为那天傍晚，他们一同在一家波希米亚俱乐部用饭，在那里，哈立德遇见了一位风韵罕有的女子。她邀他到她的工作室去。灵媒起先不以为意，末了才警告她这个不更事的稚子。「那女人是个作家，」她解释道，「而作家们，总是在搜寻他们所谓的『素材』。她尤其是一位猎取男性奇珍——那些『怪人』（originales）——的猎手，先把他们宠幸一番，最终再把他们献出去，交给读书的大众。所以你要当心。」可哈立德偏不听。因为那位作家——他后来称她作一个「浮掠者」，因为她「也像酿酒桶口那层浮沫，只掠事物的表皮」——事实上比灵媒年轻。是啊，这女子甚至可以说是美的——至少看起来是。于是这位做了她魅力之囚的德尔维希，某天傍晚敲响了她工作室的门，走了进去。啊，原来这便是一间工作室！「我命中注定要尽知一切、尽见一切，」他心里微笑着，对自己说。

===Original===
The charming hostess, in a Japanese kimono receives him somewhat orientally, offering him the divan, which he occupies alone for a spell. He is then laden with a huge scrapbook containing press notices and reviews of her many novels. These, he is asked to go through while she prepares the tea. Which is a mortal task for the Dervish in the presence of the Enchantress. Alas, the tea is long in the making, and when the scrapbook is laid aside, she reinforces him with a lot of magazines adorned with stories of the short and long and middling size, from her fertile pen. “These are beautiful,” says he, in glancing over a few pages, “but no matter how you try, you can not with your pen surpass your own beauty. The charm of your literary style can not hold a candle to the charm of your﻿—permit me to read your hand.” And laying down the magazine, he takes up her hand and presses it to his lips. In like manner, he tries to read somewhat in the face, but the Enchantress protests and smiles. In which case the smile renders the protest null and void.

===Chinese===
那位迷人的女主人，身着一袭日本和服，颇有几分东方风味地迎接了他，请他在长榻上坐，他独自在那榻上占了一阵。随后，一只硕大的剪贴簿被压到了他手里，里头尽是报界对她那许多部小说的评介与书评。她请他翻阅，自己则去沏茶。这对在女妖（the Enchantress）跟前的德尔维希而言，实在是一桩要命的苦差。哎，茶迟迟沓沓，那剪贴簿被搁到一边之后，她又拿一大摞杂志来增补他的负担——那上头尽出自她那支多产的笔，点缀着长长短短、不长不短的故事。「这些真美，」他随意翻了几页说道，「可无论你怎样努力，你那支笔总也胜不过你自己的美。你那文笔的风韵，给你那——请许我一读你的手——的风韵提鞋也不配。」说罢他把杂志放下，拉起她的手，按到自己唇上。他又照此办理，要在那张脸上读出一点什么，女妖却一边抗议、一边微笑。在这一情形下，那微笑便使抗议归于无效。

===Original===
Henceforth, the situation shall be trying even to the Dervish who can eat live coals. He oscillates for some while between the Medium and the Enchantress, but finds the effort rather straining. The first climax, however, is reached, and our Scribe thinks it too sad for words. He himself sheds a few rheums with the fair-looking, fair-spoken Dame, and dedicates to her a few rhymes. Her magnanimity, he tells us, is unexampled, and her fatalism pathetic. For when Khalid severs himself from the Spiritual Household, she kisses him thrice, saying, “Go, Child; Allah brought you to me, and Allah will bring you again.” Khalid refers, as usual, to the infinite wisdom of the Almighty, and, taking his handkerchief from his pocket, wipes the tears that fell﻿—from her eyes over his. He passes out of the vestibule, silent and sad, musing on the time he first stood there as a beggar.

===Chinese===
从此往后，这局面便连那吞得下炭火的德尔维希也要为之犯难了。他在灵媒与女妖之间来回摇摆了一阵，却觉得这番周旋颇为吃力。然而第一个高潮终于到来，我们的抄写人觉得它悲伤得难以言表。他自己也陪着那位相貌端好、言语婉转的夫人洒下几点老泪，并献给她几行诗句。她的宽宏大量，他告诉我们，可谓前所未有；她的宿命之念，则令人心恻。因为当哈立德的身子从那「灵性之家」割断之时，她吻了他三次，说道：「去罢，孩子；真主将你带到我这里，真主也会再把你带回来。」哈立德一如往常，把话头归于全能者那无穷的智慧，又从口袋里取出手帕，擦去那落下来的泪——那是从她的眼里落到他眼里的泪。他默然、伤感地穿过那道门廊，回想着当初自己第一次作为一名乞者立在那里的光景。

===Original===
Now, the horizon of the Enchantress is unobstructed. Khalid is there alone; and her free love can freely pass on from him to another. And such messages they exchange! Such evaporations of the insipidities of free love! Khalid again takes up with Shakib, from whom he does not conceal anything. The epistles are read by both, and sometimes replied to by both! And she, in an effort to seem Oriental, calls the Dervish, “My Syrian Rose,” “My Desert Flower,” “My Bedouin Boy,” et cetera, always closing her message with either a strip of Syrian sky or a camel load of the narcissus. Ah, but not thus will the play close. True, Khalid alone adorns her studio for a time, or rather adores in it; he alone accompanies her to Bohemia. But the Dervish, who was always going wrong in Bohemia﻿—always at the door of the Devil﻿—ventures one night to escort another woman to her studio. Ah, those studios! The Enchantress on hearing of the crime lights the fire under her cauldron. “Double, double, toil and trouble!” She then goes to the telephone﻿—g‑r‑r‑r‑r you swine﻿—you Phoenician murex﻿—she hangs up the receiver, and stirs the cauldron. “Double, double, toil and trouble!” But the Dervish writes her an extraordinary letter, in which we suspect the pen of our Scribe, and from which we can but transcribe the following:

===Chinese===
如今，女妖那方地平线再无遮拦。哈立德独自在那里；她的自由之爱，大可从他身上自由地转向另一个。瞧他们交换的那些书信！瞧那些自由之爱的寡淡无味，如何蒸发而出！哈立德重又与沙基布搅在一起，对他什么都不隐瞒。那些情书两人都读，有时还两人都回！而她，为着装出一副东方做派，唤那德尔维希作「我的叙利亚玫瑰」、「我的沙漠之花」、「我的贝都因少年」云云，每封信末总少不了一绺叙利亚的天，或一骆驼的水仙。啊，可这出戏不会就此收场。诚然，哈立德曾一度独自点缀她的工作室——或者毋宁说，独自在其中膜拜；曾一度独自伴她去波希米亚。可这位在波希米亚总要走错路——总站在魔鬼门前的德尔维希，竟有一晚斗胆护送了另一个女子去她工作室。啊，那些工作室！女妖一听见这桩罪状，便在她那口大釜底下点起了火。「不惮不劳，鼎沸镬熬！」（Double, double, toil and trouble!）她接着走到电话机前——咕——噜——噜——噜——你这不洁之物——你这腓尼基（Phoenician）的紫贝——她挂上话筒，去搅那口大釜。「不惮不劳，鼎沸镬熬！」可那德尔维希给她写了一封非同寻常的信，我们疑心其中有我们这位抄写人的笔意，从中我们只能迻译如下一段：

===Original===
“You found in me a vacant heart,” he pleads, “and you occupied it. The divan therein is yours, yours alone. Nor shall I ever permit a chance caller, an intruder, to exasperate you.﻿ ﻿… My breast is a stronghold in which you are well fortified. How then can anyone disturb you?﻿ ﻿… How can I turn from myself against myself? Somewhat of you, the best of you, circulates with my blood; you are my breath of life. How can I then overcome you? How can I turn to another for the sustenance which you alone can give?﻿ ﻿… If I be thirst personified, you are the living, flowing brook, the everlasting fountain. O for a drink﻿—”

===Chinese===
「你在我身上寻得一颗空着的心，」他恳道，「你占据了它。其中的长榻是你的，独属你一人。我也绝不容许任何一个不速之客、任何一个擅入者，来激恼你。……我的胸膛是一座堡垒，你在其中设防森严。那人又怎能搅扰你？……我又怎能背着自己、攻伐自己？你的一部分——你那最好的一部分——与我的血一同周流；你是我生命之息。我又怎能胜过你？我又怎能转向另一个人，去求那唯独你能赐予的滋养？……我若为渴的化身，你便是那活水奔流的小溪，那永不枯竭的泉。啊，求一饮——」

===Original===
And here follows a hectic uprush about pearly breasts, and honey-sources, and musk-scented arbours, closing with “Your Bedouin Boy shall come tonight.”

===Chinese===
紧接其后的，是一段气喘吁吁的奔涌之辞，关于珠贝似的胸脯、关于蜜之源、关于麝香之气氤氲的凉亭，末了以「你的贝都因少年今夜就来」作结。

===Original===
Notwithstanding which, the Enchantress abandons the Syrian Dwelling: she no longer fancies the vacant Divan of which Khalid speaks. Fortress or no fortress, she gives up occupation and withdraws from the foreigner her favour. Not only that; but the fire is crackling under the cauldron, and the typewriter begins to click. Ay, these modern witches can make even a typewriter dance around the fire and join in the chorus. “Double, double, toil and trouble, Fire burn, and cauldron bubble!” and the performance was transformed from the studio to the magazine supplement of one of the Sunday newspapers. There, the Dervish is thrown into the cauldron along with the magic herbs. Bubble﻿—bubble. The fire-eating Dervish, how can he now swallow this double-tongued flame of hate and love? The Enchantress had wrought her spell, had ministered her poison. Now, where can he find an antidote, who can teach him a healing formula? Bruno D’Ast was once bewitched by a sorceress, and by causing her to be burned he was immediately cured. Ah, that Khalid could do this! Like an ordinary pamphlet he would consign the Enchantress to the flames, and her scrapbooks and novels to boot. He does well, however, to return to his benevolent friend, the Medium. The spell can be counteracted by another, though less potent. Ay, even witchcraft has its homeopathic remedies.

===Chinese===
尽管有此一信，女妖仍弃了那处叙利亚人的居所：她不再垂青哈立德所说的那张空着的长榻。无论那是堡垒与否，她放弃了占领，把她的恩宠从那异乡人身上收回。不仅如此；釜下的火正噼啪作响，打字机也开始嗒嗒作响。是的，这些现代女巫，竟能使一台打字机也围着火跳舞、加入那合唱。「不惮不劳，鼎沸镬熬，烈火燃兮，大釜冒泡！」（Fire burn, and cauldron bubble!）于是那场演出，便从工作室搬到了一家星期日报纸的杂志增刊之上。在那里，德尔维希被连同那些魔草一道，扔进了大釜。噗——噗。这位吞火之德尔维希，如今怎能再吞下这道又恨又爱、两面三刀的火焰？女妖已施了她的咒，下了她的毒。如今，他到何处去寻一味解药，又有谁能教他一道疗伤的咒诀？布鲁诺·达斯特（Bruno D’Ast）曾被一个女巫迷住，而使她被活活烧死之后，他便霍然痊愈了。啊，但愿哈立德也能如此！他会像处理一本寻常小册子那样，把女妖付之一炬，连同她的剪贴簿与小说一道。不过，他回到他那仁慈的朋友灵媒那里，倒也算做得不错。一道咒可以用另一道咒去解——虽然效力较弱。是的，即便妖术，也有它顺势疗法的药剂。

===Original===
And the Medium, Shakib tells us, is delighted to welcome back her prodigal child. She opens to him her arms, and her heart; she slays the fatted calf. “I knew that Allah will bring you back to me,” she ejaculates; “my prevision is seldom wrong.” And kissing her hand, Khalid falters, “Forgiveness is for the sinner, and the good are for forgiveness.” Whereupon, they plunge again into the Unseen, and thence to Bohemia. The aftermath, however, does not come up to the expectations of the good Medium. For the rigmarole of the Enchantress about the Dervish in New York had already done its evil work. And﻿—double﻿—double﻿—wherever the Dervish goes. Especially in Bohemia, where many of its daughters set their caps for him.

===Chinese===
而灵媒——沙基布告诉我们——欢天喜地地迎回她这个浪子。她向他敞开双臂，敞开心扉；她宰了那头肥牛犊。「我就知道真主会把你带回我这里，」她脱口而出；「我的预感鲜有落空之时。」哈立德吻了她的手，嗫嚅道：「宽恕是为罪人而设，而善人是为宽恕而设。」于是他们又重投那「不可见」，又从那里去到波希米亚。然而，那余波并未如愿遂这位好心肠的灵媒之意。因为女妖关于这位德尔维希在纽约的那番信口胡扯，早已完成了它那桩恶业。而且——噗——噗——那德尔维希走到哪里，它便跟到哪里。尤其是在波希米亚，那里的许多女儿家都把帽子朝他抛去。

===Original===
And here, he is neither shy nor slow nor visionary. Nor shall his theory of immanent morality trouble him for the while. Reality is met with reality on solid, though sometimes slippery, ground. His animalism, long leashed and starved, is eager for prey. His Phoenician passion is awake. And fortunately, Khalid finds himself in Bohemia where the poison and the antidote are frequently offered together. Here the spell of one sorceress can straightway be offset by that of her sister. And we have our Scribe’s word for it, that the Dervish went as far and as deep with the houris, as the doctors eventually would permit him. That is why, we believe, in commenting upon his adventures there, he often quotes the couplet,

===Chinese===
在这里，他既不羞怯、也不迟钝、更不好玄想。他那套内在道德论，暂时也不会来为难他。现实与真身在坚实的——虽然有时也打滑的——地面上短兵相接。他那久被拴着、久被饿着的兽性，正急不可耐地要攫取猎物。他那腓尼基式的欲火已经醒来。而所幸的是，哈立德发觉自己身处波希米亚，在这里，毒与解毒之剂常常一并奉上。在这里，一个女巫的咒，可以马上由她姊妹的咒来抵消。而我们这位抄写人担保，德尔维希与那些天园美女（houris）之间，走得之远、之深，直到了大夫们最终所容许的极限。正因如此，我们相信，他在评述自己在那里的艳遇时，才常常援引那两句诗：

===Original===
“In my sublunar paradise

					There’s plenty of honey﻿—and plenty of flies.”

===Chinese===
「在这月下的乐园里，
						
						蜜丰盈——蝇亦丰盈。」

===Original===
The flies in his cup, however, can not be detected with the naked eye. They are microbes rather﻿—microbes which even the physicians can not manage with satisfaction. For it must be acknowledged that Khalid’s immanent morality and intellectualism suffered an interregnum with the houris. Reckless, thoughtless, heartless, he plunges headlong again. It is said in al-Hadith that he who guards himself against the three cardinal evils, namely, of the tongue (*laklaka*), of the stomach (*kabkaba*), and of the sex (*zabzaba*), will have guarded himself against all evil. But Khalid reads not in the Hadith of the Prophet. And that he became audacious, edacious, and loquacious, is evident from such wit and flippancy as he here likes to display. “Some women,” says he, “might be likened to whiskey, others to seltzer water; and many are those who, like myself, care neither for the soda or the whiskey straight. A ‘highball’ I will have.”

===Chinese===
然而他杯中的那些蝇，肉眼却看不出来。它们毋宁说是些微菌——是连大夫们也不能满意地加以对付的微菌。因为必须承认，哈立德那套内在道德论与智识主义，在与天园美女周旋之时，曾经历了一段中断。他又不顾前后、不思不想、不长心肝地一头扎了下去。《圣训》（al-Hadith）中说，凡能守住自己、远避那三桩根本之恶——即舌之恶（laklaka）、腹之恶（kabkaba）、色之恶（zabzaba）——者，便已守住了自己免于一切恶。可哈立德并不读先知的《圣训》（Hadith）。至于他变得胆大、饕餮、且多嘴，则从他在此爱卖弄的那番机锋与轻薄便可见一斑。「有些女子，」他说，「可以比作威士忌，另一些可以比作苏打水；而有许多人——比如我——既不爱单喝苏打，也不爱单喝威士忌。我要的是一杯兑了水的『海波』（highball）。」

===Original===
Nay, he even takes to punch; for in his cup of amour there is a subtle and multifarious mixture. With him, he himself avows, one woman complemented another. What the svelte brunette, for instance, lacked, the steatopygous blonde amply supplied. Delicacy and intensity, effervescence and depth, these he would have in a woman, or a harem, as in anything else. But these excellences, though found in a harem, will not fuse, as in a poem or a picture. Even thy bones, thou scented high-lacquered Dervish, are likely to melt away before they melt into one.

===Chinese===
不，他甚至还喝起潘趣酒来；因为在他那杯爱之酒里，原本是一道微妙而驳杂的配方。据他自己供认，在他这里，一个女人补足的是另一个女人。比如，那身段窈窕的褐发女子所欠缺的，那臀肥丰腴的金发女子便充裕地补上了。精致与浓烈、沸腾与深沉——这些他在一个女人身上、或说一个内闱里所要的，一如在别的事物上所要的一样。然而这些好处，虽可在一个内闱中寻得，却不能像在一首诗、或一幅画里那样融为一炉。哪怕是你，你这香气袭人、漆光锃亮的德尔维希，你的骨头怕也要等到化尽了，方能化而为一。

===Original===
It is written in the K.L. MS. that women either bore, or inspire, or excite. “The first and the last are to be met with anywhere; but the second? Ah, well you have heard the story of Diogenes. So take up your lamp and come along. But remember, when you do meet the woman that inspires, you will begin to yearn for the woman that excites.”

===Chinese===
赫迪夫图书馆手稿中写道：女人要么使人厌倦，要么使人感发，要么使人激荡。「头一种与末一种，随处可遇；可是第二种呢？啊，你当然听过第欧根尼（Diogenes）的故事。所以，提上你的灯，跟我来罢。但要记住，当你当真遇见了那能使你感发的女子，你便又会开始渴念那能使你激荡的女子了。」

===Original===
And here, the hospitality of the Dervish does not belie his Arab blood. In Bohemia, the bonfire of his heart was never extinguished, and the wayfarers stopping before his tent, be they of those who bored, or excited, or inspired, were welcome guests for at least three days and nights. And in this he follows the rule of hospitality among his people.

===Chinese===
在这里，这位德尔维希的好客之道，并不曾辜负他的阿拉伯血统。在波希米亚，他心底那堆篝火从未熄灭；而那些在他帐篷前驻足的过客——无论其属于那使人厌倦的、使人激荡的、还是使人感发的一类——至少三日三夜之内，都是受欢迎的上宾。而在这点上，他遵行的，正是他族人待客的古礼。

## Book the Second / 第二卷

### In the Temple / 在神殿

### To Nature / 致自然

===Original===
O Mother eternal, divine, satanic, all encompassing, all-nourishing, all-absorbing, O star-diademed, pearl-sandaled Goddess, I am thine forever and ever: whether as a child of thy womb, or an embodiment of a spirit-wave of thy light, or a dumb blind personification of thy smiles and tears, or an ignis-fatuus of the intelligence that is in thee or beyond thee, I am thine forever and ever: I come to thee, I prostrate my face before thee, I surrender myself wholly to thee. O touch me with thy wand divine again; stir me once more in thy mysterious alembics; remake me to suit the majestic silence of thy hills, the supernal purity of thy sky, the mystic austerity of thy groves, the modesty of thy slow-swelling, soft-rolling streams, the imperious pride of thy pines, the wild beauty and constancy of thy mountain rivulets. Take me in thine arms, and whisper to me of thy secrets; fill my senses with thy breath divine; show me the bottom of thy terrible spirit; buffet me in thy storms, infusing in me of thy ruggedness and strength, thy power and grandeur; lull me in thine autumn sundowns to teach me in the arts that enrapture, exalt, supernaturalise. Sing me a lullaby, O Mother eternal! Give me to drink of thy love, divine and diabolic; thy cruelty and thy kindness, I accept both, if thou wilt but whisper to me the secret of both. Anoint me with the chrism of spontaneity that I may be ever worthy of thee.﻿—Withdraw not from me thy hand, lest universal love and sympathy die in my breast.﻿—I implore thee, O Mother eternal, O sea-throned, heaven-canopied Goddess, I prostrate my face before thee, I surrender myself wholly to thee. And whether I be tomorrow the censer in the hand of thy High Priest, or the incense in the censer﻿—whether I become a star-gem in thy cestus or a sun in thy diadem or even a firefly in thy fane, I am content. For I am certain that it shall be for the best.

===Chinese===
永恒之母啊，神圣而又魔祟的母亲——你无所不包、无所不养、无所不纳；星辰为冠、珍珠为履的女神啊，我永永远远属于你：或作你子宫中所生的孩子，或作你光中一缕灵波的化身，或作你笑与泪的无言无目的化身，或作你之内、你之外那智慧的鬼火一闪——我永永远远属于你。我来到你前，伏面于你之前，将我自己全然交托于你。啊，求你再以神圣之杖触我；再在你奥秘的蒸馏瓶中搅动我；重新造我，使我合于你群山庄严的寂静、你穹苍绝高的纯净、你林间神秘的清苦、你那缓缓涨起、轻轻滚流的溪水的羞怯、你松树的骄矜、你山涧的野性之美与恒久。将我拥入你怀，向我低语你的奥秘；以你神圣的气息充满我的感官；向我显示你那可畏性灵的底蕴；在你的风暴中击打我，把你那份粗粝与刚强、权能与崇高灌注入我；在秋日的夕暮里抚我入眠，授我以令人迷醉、令人飞升、令人超凡入化的技艺。为我唱一支摇篮曲吧，永恒之母啊！让我饮你的爱——神圣而又魔祟的爱；你的残忍与你的仁慈，我两皆领受，只要你肯向我低语二者共通的奥秘。请以自发性之圣膏膏我，使我永远配得上你。——莫从我这儿撤回你的手，免得博爱与共情在我胸中死去。——我恳求你，永恒之母啊，以海为宝座、以天为华盖的女神，我伏面于你之前，将我自己全然交托于你。明日我无论是你大祭司手中的香炉，还是炉中焚的香烟——无论我化作你腰带上一颗星宝，还是你冠冕上的一轮太阳，甚至只是你圣殿中一只萤火——我都心甘。因为我确知，这一切终归于至善。

> 哈立德

## I / I

The Dowry of Democracy / 民主的嫁妆

===Original===
Old Arabic books, printed in Bulaq, generally have a broad margin wherein a separate work, independent of the text, adds gloom to the page. We have before us one of these tomes in which the text treats of the ethics of life and religion, and the margins are darkened with certain adventures which Shahrazad might have added to her famous *Nights*. The similarity between Khalid’s life in its present stage and some such book, is evident. Nay, he has been so assiduous in writing the marginal Work, that ever since he set fire to his peddling-box, we have had little in the Text worth transcribing. Nothing, in fact; for many pages back are as blank as the evil genius of Bohemia could wish them. And how could one with that mara upon him, write of the ethics of life and religion?

Al-Hamazani used to say that in Jorajan the man from Khorasan must open thrice his purse: first, to pay for the rent; second, for the food; and third, for his coffin. And so, in Khalid’s case, at least, is Bohemia. For though the purse be not his own, he was paying dear, and even in advance, in what is dearer than gold, for his experience. “O, that the Devil did not take such interest in the marginal work of our life! Why should we write it then, and for whom? And how will it fare with us when, chapfallen in the end and mortified, we stand before the great Taskmaster like delinquent school boys with a blank text in our hands?” (Thus Shakib, who has caught the moralising evil from his Master.) And that we must stand, and fall, for thus standing, he is quite certain. At least, Khalid is. For he would not return to the Text to make up for the blank pages therein, if he were not.

===Chinese===
在布拉格（Bulaq）印行的老阿拉伯古籍，大都留有宽阔的页边，其中另有一部与正文无关的著作，为书页添上一层阴翳。我们面前便有这样一部对开巨册，正文论的是人生与宗教的伦理，而页边的空白处却被某些奇遇染得晦暗——那些奇遇，山鲁佐德（Shahrazad）原也可以补进她那部著名的《一千零一夜》（Arabian Nights）里去。哈立德此时阶段的人生与这样一部书相似，不言自明。不，应该说，他写那部「页边之作」写得如此勤勉，以至于自从他一把火烧了自己的货郎箱之后，我们几乎再无可堪抄录的「正文」。事实上，一无所有；往前连翻数页，皆空白如也，正合波希米亚（Bohemia）那邪灵之所愿。一个身负玛拉（mara，即梦魇之魔）的人，又怎能写得下人生与宗教的伦理呢？

哈马扎尼（al-Hamazani）常说，在戈尔甘（Jorajan），一个呼罗珊（Khorasan）人非得打开三次钱袋不可：头一次付房租，第二次付饭钱，第三次付棺材本。波希米亚之于哈立德，至少，也是如此。因为钱袋虽非他自己所有，他却付出惨重——甚至预付——以比黄金更昂贵的东西，去换他的阅历。「啊，愿魔鬼莫要对我们人生的『页边之作』如此关切！那我们又何必去写它，又为谁而写？待到末了，垂头丧气、羞愧难当，我们如负疚的学童一般，两手空空地捧着一册空白正文，站在那位大监工面前时，又将如何是好？」（沙基布之语，他已从其师那里染上了好发议论的痼疾。）而我们必须如此站着，又因如此站着而跌倒——这一点他是深信不疑的。至少，哈立德深信不疑。否则，他便不会回到「正文」之中，去补那里头的空白页了。

===Original===
“When he returned from his last sojourn in Bohemia,” writes our Scribe, “Khalid was pitiful to behold. Even Sindbad, had he seen him, would have been struck with wonder. The tears rushed to my eyes when we embraced; for instead of Khalid I had in my arms a phantom. And I could not but repeat the lines of al-Mutanabbi,

“So phantom-like I am, and though so near,

				If I spoke not, thou wouldst not know I’m here.”

“No more voyages, I trust, O thou Sindbad.” And he replied, “Yes, one more; but to our dear native land this time.” In fact, I, too, was beginning to suffer from nostalgia, and was much desirous of returning home. But Shakib is in such a business tangle that he could not extricate himself in a day. So, they tarry another year in New York, the one meanwhile unravelling his affairs, settling with his creditors and collecting what few debts he had, the other brooding over the few blank pages in his Text.

===Chinese===
「当他从波希米亚上一次归来时，」我们的抄写人写道，「哈立德的模样叫人见了心酸。便是辛巴达（Sindbad）见了，也要惊诧不已。我们相拥时，泪水涌上了我的双眼；因为落在我怀里的并非哈立德，而是一道幻影。我不禁吟诵起穆太奈比（al-Mutanabbi）的诗句——

『我竟已形如幻影，虽近在咫尺，
　　　　若不开口，你便不知我尚在此。』

『但愿你此后再无航程了，辛巴达啊。』他答道：『是，还有一次；可这一回，是回我们亲爱的故土。』事实上，我自己也开始为乡愁所苦，颇为思归。但沙基布缠在一桩生意纠葛之中，一日之间难以脱身。于是，他们又在纽约盘桓了一年，一个去理清他自己的事务，与债主结算、把欠他的一点债收回来；另一个则在那里闷闷地对着他「正文」里那几页空白出神。」

===Original===
One day he receives a letter from a fellow traveller, a distinguished citizen of Tammany Land, whom he had met and befriended in Bohemia, relating to an enterprise of great pith and moment. It was election time, we learn, and the high post of political canvasser of the Syrian District was offered to Khalid for a consideration of—but the letter which Shakib happily preserved, we give in full.

“Dear Khalid:

“I have succeeded in getting Mr. O’Donohue to appoint you a canvasser of the Syrian District. You must stir yourself, therefore, and try to do some good work, among the Syrian voters, for Democracy’s Candidate this campaign. Here is a chance which, with a little hustling on your part, will materialise. And I see no reason why you should not try to cash your influence among your people. This is no mean position, mind you. And if you will come up to the Wigwam tomorrow, I’ll give you a few suggestions on the business of manipulating votes.

“Yours truly,

					“**Patrick Hoolihan**.”

===Chinese===
某日，他收到一位同路旅人的来信，此人是塔马尼之地（Tammany Land）一位显赫的公民，他在波希米亚与之相识、且曾加以照应，信中所谈乃是一桩「事关重大」的事业。原来正值选举时节，叙利亚区（Syrian District）政治游说人这一要职被许给哈立德，所论代价为何——但沙基布有幸把那封信保存了下来，我们不妨全文照录：

「亲爱的哈立德：

「我已促成奥多诺休先生（Mr. O'Donohue）委任你为叙利亚区游说人。故而你须振奋精神，在这一役中为民主党的候选人，在叙利亚裔选民中间多做些好事。这是一个机会，只要你稍稍奔走一番，便可化为实利。我看不出你有何理由不该把你在同胞中的影响兑现。切莫小看这个职位，记住了。明日你若肯到会所（Wigwam）来一趟，我可以在『操纵选票』这门生意上给你出几个主意。

「你忠诚的，

「**帕特里克·胡利汉（Patrick Hoolihan）**。」

===Original===
And the said Mr. Hoolihan, the letter shows, is Secretary to Mr. O’Donohue, who is first henchman to the Boss. Such a letter, if luckily misunderstood, will fire for a while the youthful imagination. No; not his Shamrag Majesty’s Tammany Agent to Syria, this Canvassership, you poor phantom-like zany! A high post, indeed, you fond and pitiful dreamer, on which you must hang the higher aspirations of your soul, together with your theory of immanent morality. You would not know this at first. You would still kiss the official notification of Mr. Hoolihan, and hug it fondly to your breast. Very well. At last—and the gods will not damn thee for musing—you will stand in the bandwagon before the corner groggery and be the object of the admiration of your fellow citizens—perhaps of missiles, too. Very well, Khalid; but you must shear that noddle of thine, and straightway, for the poets are potted in Tammany Land. We say this for your sake.

The orator-dream of youth, ye gods, shall it be realised in this heaven of a dray-cart with its kerosene torch and its drum, smelling and sounding rather of Jahannam? Surely, from the Table of Bohemia to the Stump in Tammany Land, is a far cry. But believe us, O Khalid, you will wish you were again in the gardens of Proserpine, when the silence and darkness extinguish the torch and the drum and the echoes of the shouting crowds. The headaches are certain to follow this inebriation. You did not believe Shakib; you would not be admonished; you would go to the Wigwam for your portfolio. “High post,” “political canvasser,” “manipulation of votes,” you will know the exact meaning of these esoteric terms, when, alas, you meet Mr. Hoolihan. For you must know that not everyone you meet in Bohemia is not a Philistine. Indeed, many helots are there, who come from Philistia to spy out the Land.

===Chinese===
据信可知，这位胡利汉先生，乃是奥多诺休先生的秘书，而奥多诺休先生又是老板（the Boss）手下的头号死党。这样一封信，倘若碰巧被人误解了，便能在一时之间点燃年轻人的想象。不；并非他沙姆拉格陛下（Shamrag Majesty）派驻叙利亚的塔马尼代理官——你这个形如幻影的可怜滑头，这游说人之职！好一个要职啊，你这个痴心妄想、可悲可叹的梦者，你竟要把灵魂里更高的抱负、连同你那一套「内在道德」（immanent morality）论，一并挂到这职位上去！这一点你起初是不会明白的。你甚至会亲一亲胡利汉先生那张官式通知，把它珍重地抱在胸前。很好。到了末了——诸神不会因你这番冥想而降罪于你——你便会站在街角小酒馆（groggery）门前那辆竞选大车（bandwagon）上，成为你同胞们艳羡的对象——或许，也是飞掷之物的对象。很好，哈立德；可你得把你那个脑袋剪一剪，立刻就剪，因为诗人在塔马尼之地是要被装进罐子里的。我们这番话，是为你着想。

青春的演说家之梦，诸神啊，难道竟要在这座由大板车、煤油火把与鼓构成的天堂里成真吗——这天堂闻起来、听起来倒更像是火狱（Jahannam）？诚然，从波希米亚的餐桌到塔马尼之地的树桩（Stump），相去不可以道里计。但是信我们罢，哈立德，等到寂静与黑暗把火把、把鼓、把那喧嚣人群的回声一一扑灭之时，你便会盼望自己重又置身于普罗瑟皮娜的花园（gardens of Proserpine）了。这场酒醉之后，头痛是必定要接踵而至的。你当初不信沙基布；你不听劝；你非要跑到会所去领你的公文包。「要职」、「政治游说人」、「操纵选票」——这些秘传术语的精确含义，唉，等你见着胡利汉先生时便会明白。因为你须知道，在波希米亚你所遇见的人，并非个个都不是非利士人（Philistine）。事实上，那里头有不少希洛人（helots），是从非利士（Philistia）派来窥探这地的。

===Original===
We read in the *Histoire Intime* of Shakib that Khalid did become a Tammany citizen, that is to say, a Tammany dray-horse; that he was much esteemed by the Honourable Henchmen, and once in the Wigwam he was particularly noticed by his Shamrag Majesty Boss O’Graft; that he was Tammany’s Agent to the Editors of the Syrian newspapers of New York, whom he enrolled in the service of the Noble Cause for a consideration which no eloquence or shrewdness could reduce to a minimum; that he also took to the stump and dispensed to his fellow citizens, with rhetorical gestures at least, of the cut-and-dried logic which the Committee of Buncombe on such occasions furnishes its squad of talented spouters; and that—the most important this—he was subject in the end to the ignominy of waiting in the lobby with tuft-hunters and political stockjobbers, until it pleased the Committee of Buncombe and the Honourable Treasurer thereof to give him—a card of dismissal!

But what virtue is there in waiting, our cynical friend would ask. Why not go home and sleep? Because, O cynical friend, the Wigwam now is Khalid’s home. For was he not, in creaking boots and a slouch hat, ceremoniously married to Democracy? Ay, and after spending their honeymoon on the Stump and living another month or two with his troll among her People, he returns to his cellar to brood, not over the blank pages in his Text, nor over the disastrous results of the Campaign, but on the weightier matter of divorce. For although Politics and Romance, in the History of Human Intrigue, have often known and enjoyed the same yoke, with Khalid they refused to pull at the plough. They were not sensible even to the goad. Either the yoke in his case was too loose, or the new yoke-fellow too thick-skinned and stubborn.

Moreover, the promise of a handsome dowry, made by the Shamrag Father-in-Law or his Brokers materialised only in the rotten eggs and tomatoes with which the Orator was cordially received on his honeymoon trip. Such a marriage, O Mohammad, and such a honeymoon, and such a dowry!—is not this enough to shake the very sides of the Kaaba with laughter? And yet, in the Wigwam this not uncommon affair was indifferently considered; for the good and honourable Tammanyites marry off their Daughters every day to foreigners and natives alike, and with like extraordinary picturesque results.

===Chinese===
我们在沙基布的《私密史》（Histoire Intime）中读到：哈立德确乎成了一名塔马尼公民——也就是说，一匹塔马尼的拉车马；他颇受那群可敬的死党们（Honourable Henchmen）的器重，有一次在会所里还特别为他那位沙姆拉格陛下奥格拉夫老板（Boss O'Graft）所注目；他又曾充当塔马尼驻纽约叙利亚文各报编辑的代理人，把他们一一招进那桩「崇高事业」（Noble Cause）之中，所付出的代价，任凭怎样的口才与精明都压不到最低限度；他也曾登上演说台，至少以种种动听的手势，向他的同胞们分发那套由「班康委员会」（Committee of Buncombe）在这种场合配给它那一队天才鼓手们的现成逻辑；而最重要的是——他末了竟要蒙受这样的羞辱：与一帮攀附权贵者（tuft-hunters）和政治投机客一同在大堂里候着，直候到班康委员会及其可敬的司库大人高兴为止，才发给他——一张解雇卡！

可守候之中又有何德行可言呢？我们那位愤世的朋友便会这样问。为什么不回家睡觉？因为，愤世的朋友啊，这会所如今便是哈立德的家了。难道他不是穿着那双咯吱作响的靴子、戴着那顶软檐帽，与「民主」举行了隆重的婚礼吗？是啊，在树桩上度完蜜月，又与他的妖妇（troll）在她的子民里再过上那么一两个月之后，他才回到自己的地窖里去出神——他所出的神，不是对着他「正文」里那几页空白，也不是对着这场战役灾难性的结局，而是对着更重大的事——离婚。因为尽管在「人类心机史」上，「政治」与「浪漫」常常相识、并共受同一副轭而乐在其中，可到了哈立德这里，它们却都拒绝拉那张犁。甚至连刺棍也驱它们不动。要么是他这副轭太松，要么就是这新同伴皮太厚、性太拗。

再者，那位沙姆拉格岳丈大人或其经纪人事先许下的丰厚嫁妆，到头来兑现的，不过是在这位演说家蜜月之旅中，人们「热诚」迎接他所投掷的臭鸡蛋与烂番茄罢了。这样的婚姻，穆罕默德（Mohammad）啊，这样的蜜月，这样的嫁妆！——这岂不足以叫克尔白（Kaaba）笑得四壁震动？然而在会所里，这种司空见惯的勾当却被视为等闲；因为那些善良而可敬的塔马尼人天天把他们的女儿嫁给外人或本乡人，所得的结果同样离奇、同样多彩多姿。

===Original===
Were it not wiser, therefore, O Khalid, had you consulted your friend the Dictionary before you saw exact meaning of canvass and manipulation, before you put on your squeaking boots and slouch hat and gave your hand and heart to Tammany’s Daughter and her Father-in-Law O’Graft? But the Dictionary, too, often falls short of human experience; and even Mr. O’Donohue could at best but hint at the meaning of the esoteric terms of Tammany’s political creed. These you must define for yourself as you go along; and change and revise your definitions as you rise or descend in the Sacred Order. For canvass here might mean eloquence; there it might mean shrewdness; lower down, intimidation and coercion; and further depthward, human sloth and misery. It is but a common deal in horses. Ay, in Tammany Land it is essentially a trade honestly conducted on the known principle of supply and demand. These truths you had to discover for yourself, you say; for neither the Dictionary, nor your friend and fellow traveller in Bohemia, Mr. Hoolihan, could stretch their knowledge or their conscience to such a compass. And you are not sorry to have made such a discovery? Can you think of the Dowry and say that? We are, indeed, sorry for you. And we would fain insert in letter *D* of the Dictionary a new definition: namely, Dowry, n. (Tammany Land Slang). The odoriferous missiles, such as eggs and tomatoes, which are showered on an Orator-Groom by the people.

But see what big profits Khalid draws from these small shares in the Reality Stock Company. You remember, good Reader, how he was kicked away from the door of the Temple of Atheism. The stogies of that inspired Doorkeeper were divine, according to his way of viewing things, for they were at that particular moment God’s own boots. Ay, it was God, he often repeats, who kicked him away from the Temple of his enemies. And now, he finds the Dowry of Democracy, with all its wonderful revelations, as profitable in its results, as divine in its purpose. And in proof of this, we give here a copy of his letter to Boss O’Graft, written in that downright manner of his contemporaries, the English original of which we find in the *Histoire Intime*.

===Chinese===
那么，哈立德啊，倘若你在弄清「游说」与「操纵」的确切含义之前，在穿上那双吱吱作响的靴子、戴上软檐帽、把手与心一并许给塔马尼之女及其岳丈奥格拉夫之前，先去请教一下你那位字典朋友，岂不更明智？然而字典也常常不能尽人情之万一；便是奥多诺休先生，至多也只能对塔马尼政治信条里这些秘传术语的含义略作暗示罢了。这些含义，你须得一边走下去、一边自行下定义；并且随着你在那「神圣等级」中或升或降，不断加以变更与修订。因为「游说」在此处也许意味着口才，在彼处也许意味着精明；再往下，是恐吓与胁迫；再往深处，则是人的怠惰与苦楚。这不过是马市上一桩寻常买卖。是啊，在塔马尼之地，这本质上就是一桩买卖，老老实实地照着那条众所周知的供需原则在做。这些道理——你说——你不得不亲自去发现；因为无论是字典，还是你那位在波希米亚的同路旅人胡利汉先生，都既不能把他们的知识、也不能把他们的良心，拉伸到那样的范围之内。而你竟不后悔有此一发现？你能想着那「嫁妆」、再说出这话吗？我们倒真替你难过。我们很想在字典的字母 D 之下，添上一条新释义：即 嫁妆（dowry），名词。（塔马尼之地俚语。）那些芳香的飞弹——诸如鸡蛋与番茄——由民众雨点般投掷在一位演说家新郎身上的东西。

但是且看，哈立德从「现实股份有限公司」（Reality Stock Company）这几股小小的股份里，抽出了多大的红利。好读者，你该记得，他是怎样从无神论之庙（Temple of Atheism）的门口被一脚踢开的。按那位受灵感的守门人的看法，他脚上那双廉价雪茄式的靴子（stogies）原是神圣的，因为在那特定的一刻，那正是真主自家的靴子。是啊——哈立德常常重复——正是真主，把他从仇敌的庙门口踢开的。如今，他又发觉这「民主的嫁妆」，连同其一切奇妙的启示，其结局之有利、其用意之神圣，亦与之相去无几。为证此言不虚，我们在此附上他写给奥格拉夫老板的信件一份——此信以他那一代人特有的直率口吻写成，其英文原件，我们是从《私密史》中找出。

===Original===
“From Khalid to Boss O’Graft.

“Right *Dis*honourable Boss:

“I have just received a check from your Treasurer, which by no right whatever is due me, having been paid for my services by Him who knows better than you and your Treasurer what I deserve. The voice of the people, and their eggs and tomatoes, too, are, indeed, God’s. And you should know this, you who dare to remunerate me in what is not half as clean as those missiles. I return not your insult of a check, however; but I have tried to do your state some service in purchasing the few boxes of soap which I am now dispatching to the Wigwam. You need more, I know, you and your Honourable Henchmen or Hashmen. And instead of canvassing and orating for Democracy’s illustrious Candidate and the Noble Cause, *mashallah!* one ought to do a little canvassing for Honesty and Truth among Democracy’s leaders, tuft-hunters, political stockjobbers, and suchlike. O, for a higher stump, my Boss, to preach to those who are supporting and degrading the stumps and the stump-orators of the Republic!”

===Chinese===
「哈立德致奥格拉夫老板。

「『不』可敬的老板大鉴：

「我刚刚收到贵司库一张支票，这笔钱我绝无半点权利可领，因为我的效劳早已由那一位付过报酬了——他比我应得什么、比你和贵司库所知的更清楚。民众的声音，连同民众的鸡蛋和番茄，诚然，都是真主的。这一点你理应知晓，你竟敢用连那些飞弹一半都不如的东西来酬报我。然而，我并不把那张具侮辱性的支票退还给你；我只是设法为贵邦略尽绵薄，购下数箱肥皂，现已发往会所。我知道，你和你的可敬死党们——或可敬剁肉党们——还需要更多。再者，与其为民主党的辉煌候选人与那崇高事业去游说、去演说，*Mashallah*（真主所欲）！倒不如在民主党的领袖、攀附权贵者、政治投机客以及诸如此类的人物中间，稍微为「诚实」与「真理」做一点游说。啊，但愿有一座更高的树桩，我的老板，好让我向那些正在支持又正在糟蹋这共和国内的树桩与树桩演说家的人，宣讲一番！」

===Original===
And is it come to this, you poor phantom-like dreamer? Think you a Tammany Boss is like your atheists and attorneys and women of the studio, at whom you could vent your ire without let or hindrance? These harmless humans have no constables at their command. But his Shamrag Majesty—O wretched Khalid, must we bring one of his myrmidons to your cellar to prove to you that, even in this Tammany Land, you can not with immunity give free and honest expression to your thoughts? Now, were you not summoned to the Shamrag’s presence to answer for the crime of lèse-majesté? And were you not, for your audacity, left to brood ten days and nights in gaol? And what tedium we have in Shakib’s History about the charge on which he was arrested. It is unconscionable that Khalid should misappropriate Party funds. Indeed, he never even touched or saw any of it, excepting, of course, that check which he returned. But the Boss was still in power. And what could Shakib do to exonerate his friend? He did much, and he tells as much about it. With check-boot in his pocket, he makes his way through aldermen, placemen, henchmen, and other questionable political species of humanity, up to the Seat of Justice—but such detail, though of the veracity of the writer nothing doubting, we gladly set aside, since we believe with Khalid that his ten days in gaol were akin to the Boots and the Dowry in their motive and effect.

But our Scribe, though never remiss when Khalid is in a pickle, finds much amiss in Khalid’s thoughts and sentiments. And as a further illustration of the limpid shallows of the one and the often opaque depths of the other, we give space to the following:

===Chinese===
事情竟至如此地步了么，你这个形如幻影的可怜梦者？你以为一位塔马尼老板，也像你所识的那些无神论者、律师、画室里的女人们一样，你可以肆无忌惮地对他们发泄怒火？这些无害的人手下并没有警察听其调遣。可那位沙姆拉格陛下——唉，苦命的哈立德，难道非得把他的一名爪牙带到你地窖里来，才能向你证明：便是在这塔马尼之地，你也休想安然无恙地把自己心里的话自由而诚实地讲出来么？话说回来，你难道不是被传到沙姆拉格陛下面前，去为大不敬之罪（lèse-majesté）听候发落的吗？你难道不是因你那番放肆，被关在监牢里闷坐了十个日夜么？而沙基布的史著里，关于他被捕的罪名所写的那一段，又是何等冗繁。说哈立德挪用党款，未免太不讲理。事实上，他根本连碰都没碰、见都没见过那笔款子——当然，除了那张被他退回去的支票。可老板仍然大权在握。沙基布又能做什么来替朋友洗刷呢？他做了许多，也讲了同样多。他口袋里揣着那张支票的靴子，从市参议员、有禄位之人、死党以及其他形形色色可疑的政治人种之间穿过，一路走到那正义之座前——但这类细节，虽我们对执笔者的诚实毫无怀疑，仍乐于搁置一旁；因为我们与哈立德所见一致：他那十天的牢狱之灾，在其动机与效果上，与「靴子」、与「嫁妆」原是一脉相通的。

然而我们的抄写人，虽在哈立德落难之时从不怠慢，却在他这回的心思与情感里发觉许多不对头之处。为了进一步说明前者那清澈见底的浅滩、与后者那往往不透明的深潭，我们留出篇幅，抄录如下：

===Original===
“When Khalid was ordered to appear before the Boss,” writes Shakib, “such curiosity and anxiety as I felt at that time made me accompany him. For I was anxious about Khalid, and curious to see this great Leader of men. We set out, therefore, together, I musing on an incident in Baalbek when we went out to meet the Pasha of the Lebanons and a droll old peasant, having seen him for the first time, cried out, ‘I thought the Pasha to be a Pasha, but he’s but a man.’ And I am sorry, after having seen the Boss, I can not say as much for him.”

Here follows a little philosophising, unbecoming of our Scribe, on men and names and how they act and react upon each other. Also, a page about his misgivings and the effort he made to persuade Khalid not to appear before the Boss. But skipping over these, “we reach the Tammany Wigwam and are conducted by a thickset, heavy-jowled, heavy-booted citizen through the long corridor into a little square room occupied by a little square-faced clerk. Here we wait a half hour and more, during which the young gentleman, with his bell before him and his orders to minor clerks who come and go, poses as somebody of some importance. We are then asked to follow him from one room into another, until we reach the one adjoining the private office of the Boss. A knock or two are executed on the door of Greatness with a nauseous sense of awe, and ‘Come in,’ Greatness within huskily replies. The square-faced clerk enters, shuts the door after him, returns in a trice, and conducts us into the awful Presence. Ye gods of Baalbek, the like of this I never saw before. Here is a room sumptuously furnished with sofas and fauteuils, and rugs from Isfahan. On the walls are pictures of Washington, Jefferson, and the great Boss Tweed; and right under the last named, behind that preciously carved mahogany desk, in that soft rolling mahogany chair, is the squat figure of the big Boss. On the desk before him, besides a plethora of documents, lay many things pell-mell, among which I noticed a box of cigars, the Criminal Code, and, most prominent of all, the Boss’ feet, raised there either to bid us welcome, or to remind us of his power. And the rich Isfahan rug, the cuspidor being small and overfull, receives the richly coloured matter which he spurts forth every time he takes the cigar out of his mouth. O, the vulgarity, the bestiality of it! Think of those poor patient Persian weavers who weave the tissues of their hearts into such beautiful work, and of this proud and paltry Boss, whose office should have been furnished with straw. Yes, with straw; and the souls of those poor artist-weavers will sleep in peace. O, the ignominy of having such precious pieces of workmanship under the feet and spittle of such vulgar specimens of humanity. But if the Boss had purchased these rugs himself, with money earned by his own brow-sweat, I am sure he would appreciate them better. He would then know, if not their intrinsic worth, at least their market value. Yes, and they were presented to him by someone *needing, I suppose, police connivance and protection*. The first half of this statement I had from the Boss himself; the second, I base on Khalid’s knowingness and suspicion. Be this, however, as it may.

===Chinese===
「哈立德被传到老板面前听训时，」沙基布写道，「我当时所感到的那份好奇与焦虑，使我决意陪他同去。因为我既为哈立德担心，又好奇想一睹这位伟人的领袖风采。于是我们一道动身，我一路上想着一桩发生在巴勒贝克（Baalbek）的旧事——当年我们出去迎见黎巴嫩的帕夏（pasha），一个滑稽的老农头一回见着他，便嚷了起来：『我原以为帕夏是帕夏，可他也不过是个人哪。』遗憾的是，见了这位老板之后，我连这句话都不能照样奉送给它。」

接下来是我们的抄写人一段不合身份的、关于「人」与「名」如何相互作用的玄谈；又有一页讲他心里的种种不安，以及他怎样力劝哈立德不要到老板面前去。但这些且略过不提——「我们到了塔马尼会所，由一个敦实、阔腮、穿着厚重靴子的公民领着，穿过那条长廊，进了一间小小的四方形房间，里头坐着一个身材矮小、长着一张方脸的小书记。我们在这里候了半个钟头有余，其间那位年轻先生，面前的铃，对着进进出出的下级书记们发号施令，装出一副是个什么要人的派头。随后他让我们跟着他，从一间房走到另一间房，最后到了紧邻老板私室的那一间。在『伟大』的门外，怀着一种令人作呕的敬畏，叩上一两下，里头那位『伟大』便沙哑着嗓子应一声：『进来。』方脸书记进去，随手带上门，转眼又出来，把我们引到那可畏的『御前』。巴勒贝克的诸神啊，这等场面我可是头一回见。这是一间陈设豪华的屋子——沙发、安乐椅、还有来自伊斯法罕（Isfahan）的地毯。墙上挂着华盛顿、杰斐逊（Jefferson），以及大老板特威德（Boss Tweed）的画像；就在最后这一位的画像正下方，那张雕工考究的桃花心木写字台后面，那张柔软的、卷沿的桃花心木椅上，坐着大老板那矮墩墩的身子。他面前的桌上，除了一堆文书之外，还乱七八糟摊着许多东西，我留意到其中有一匣雪茄、一本《刑法典》，而最为显眼的，是老板那一双脚——抬起来搁在桌上，要么是表示欢迎我们，要么是提醒我们别忘了他的权势。那块贵重的伊斯法罕地毯，因为痰盂太小又太满，便承接了他每一次把雪茄从嘴里抽出时所喷出来的、五颜六色的东西。啊，这等粗鄙，这等兽性！想想那些可怜而耐心的波斯织工，他们把心头的纤维织成了如此美丽的成品；再想想这位傲慢而鄙陋的老板——他的办公室本该用稻草来布置才对。是的，用稻草；那样，那些可怜的织工艺术家们的灵魂才能安息。啊，这样珍贵的工艺品，竟被踩在一个如此粗鄙的人物的脚下、唾沫之下，是何等的耻辱。但倘若这位老板是亲手挣来血汗钱、自己去买下这些地毯的，我相信他一定会更懂得欣赏。那样他至少会知道它们的市场价值，哪怕仍不知其内在价值。是的，而且——这些地毯还是别人『想必是需要警察的纵容与保护』才送上门来的。这句话的前半截，是我从老板本人那里听来的；后半截，则是我根据哈立德的精明与疑心所作的揣测。然而，姑作如是观罢。

===Original===
“When we entered this sumptuously furnished office, the squat figure in the chair under the picture of Boss Tweed, remained as immobile as a fixture and did not as much as reply to our salaam. But he pointed disdainfully to seats in the corner of the room, saying, ‘Sit down there,’ in a manner quite in keeping with his stogies raised on the desk directly in our face. Such freedom, nay, such bestiality, I could never tolerate. Indeed, I prefer the suavity and palaver of Turkish officials, no matter how crafty and corrupt, to the puffing, spitting manners of these come-up-from-the-shamble men. But Khalid could sit there as immobile as the Boss himself, and he did so, *billah*! For he was thinking all the while, as he told me when we came out, not of such matters as grate on the susceptibilities of a poet, but on the one sole idea of how such a bad titman could lead by the nose so many good people.”

Shakib then proceeds to give us a verbatim report of the interview. It begins with the Boss’ question, “What do you mean by writing such a letter?” and ends with this other, “What do you mean by immanent morality?” The reader, given the head and tail of the matter, can supply the missing parts. Or, given its two bases, he can construct this triangle of Politics, Ethics, and the Constable, with Khalid’s letter, offended Majesty, and a prison cell, as its three turning points. We extract from the report, however, the concluding advice of the Boss. For when he asked Khalid again what he meant by immanent morality, he continued in a crescendo of indignation: “You mean the morality of hayseeds, and priests, and philosophical fools? That sort of morality will not as much as secure a vote during the campaign, nor even help to keep the lowest clerk in office. That sort of morality is good for your mountain peasants or other barbarous tribes. But the free and progressive people of the United States must have something better, nobler, more practical. You’d do well, therefore, to get you a pair of rings, hang them in your ears, and go preach, your immanent morality to the South African Pappoos. But before you go, you shall taste of the rigour of our law, you insolent, brazen-faced, unmannerly scoundrel!”

===Chinese===
「我们走进这间陈设豪华的办公室时，坐在椅子上、特威德老板画像之下那个矮墩墩的身形，纹丝不动，一如墙上的固定物件，连对我们的色兰（salaam）也不屑一答。他只是鄙夷地朝屋子角落里的几把椅子指了指，说道：『坐那儿去。』那副神气，与他那双直接对着我们脸、高搁在桌上的廉价靴子，倒十分相称。这种放肆——不，这种兽性——我实在不能忍受。说真的，比起这些『刚从屠宰场里爬出来』的家伙们那种喷着烟、吐着唾沫的作派，我倒宁愿要土耳其官吏那套和气与虚文——无论他们多么狡诈、多么腐败。但哈立德却能像老板本人一样坐在那里纹丝不动，他居然坐住了，*billah*（凭真主起誓）！因为他自始至终——他出来后对我说——根本没在想那些会刺痛诗人敏感处的种种，而只在想一个唯一的念头：这样一个卑劣的提特人（titman，即小爬虫），竟能牵着那么多好人的鼻子走。」

接下来，沙基布为我们给出这次会见的逐字实录。它以老板的一句问话开场——「你写这样一封信，是何用意？」——又以另一句问话收束——「你那个『内在道德』又是何用意？」读者诸君，得了这头尾两端，中间所缺的部分自可补全。或者，得了它的两条底边，便可构造出这「政治、伦理与警察」的三角形来——以哈立德的信、被冒犯的陛下、与一间牢房，作为它的三个顶点。不过我们只从那份实录中摘出老板临了的训话。因为当他再次追问哈立德所谓「内在道德」究竟何所指时，他带着愈来愈烈的怒气接着说道：「你说的是乡巴佬、神父、和哲学家式的傻瓜的道德罢？那种道德在竞选之中连一票都换不来，连最低微的书记都保不住位置。那种道德正合你的山里农夫或其他蛮族之用。但自由而进步的合众国人民必须有更善、更高、更切实的东西。因此，你最好去弄一副耳环，挂在你耳朵上，再到南非的帕普斯（Pappoos）中间去宣讲你那『内在道德』。但在你走之前，你可得先尝尝我们法律的厉害，你这个放肆、厚颜、不懂规矩的无赖！」

===Original===
And we are assured that the Boss did not remain immobile as be spurted forth this mixture of wrath and wisdom, nor did the stogies; for moved by his own words, he rose promptly to his feet. “And what of it,” exclaims our Scribe. “Surely, I had rather see those boots perform any office, high or low, as to behold their soles raised like mirrors to my face.” But how high an office they performed when the Boss came forward, we are not told. All that our Scribe gives out about the matter amounts to this: namely, that he walked out of the room, and as he looked back to see if Khalid was following, he saw him brushing with his hands—his hips! And on that very day Khalid was summoned to appear before the Court and give answer to the charge of misappropriation of public funds. The orator-dream of youth—what a realisation! He comes to Court, and after the legal formalities are performed, he is delivered unto an officer who escorts him across the Bridge of Sighs to gaol. There, for ten days and nights—and it might have been ten months were it not for his devoted and steadfast friend—we leave Khalid to brood on Democracy and the Dowry of Democracy. A few extracts from the Chapter in the K.L. MS. entitled “In Prison,” are, therefore, appropriate.

“So long as one has faith,” he writes, “in the general moral summation of the experience of mankind, as the philosophy of reason assures us, one should not despair. But the material fact of the Present, the dark moment of no-morality, consider that, my suffering Brothers. And reflect further that in this great City of New York the majority of citizens consider it a blessing to have a *rojail* (titman) for their boss and leader.… How often have I mused that if Ponce de Leon sought the Fountain of Youth in the New World, I, Khalid, sought the Fountain of Truth, and both of us have been equally successful!

===Chinese===
而且我们确有把握：老板在喷出这通怒火与高论混杂的言语之际，并没有保持不动；那双靴子也没有。因为被自己的话所激，他霍地立起身来。「那又怎样，」我们的抄写人叹道，「我当然宁愿看那双靴子做任何高低贵贱的事，也不愿再见它的鞋底如镜子一般抬到我的脸前。」至于老板向前逼近时那双靴子做了何等高贵的事，我们却不得而知。我们的抄写人关于此事的全部交代，归结起来不过是：他走出了房间，回头一望，看看哈立德是否跟在身后，却看见哈立德正用手在——拍自己的屁股！就在那一天，哈立德被传到法庭，就「挪用公款」之罪答辩。青春的演说家之梦——好一个成全！他到了法庭，循例履行法定手续之后，便被交给一名警官，由他押解着越过叹息桥（Bridge of Sighs），押进监牢。在那里，十个日夜——倘若没有那位忠诚不渝的朋友，本可能是十个月——我们把哈立德留下，让他对着「民主」、对着「民主的嫁妆」出神。所以，从赫迪夫图书馆手稿（K.L. MS.）题为《在狱中》那一章里摘出几段，正属合宜。

「只要一个人仍对人类经验的总体道德总结怀有信心——正如理性哲学所保证我们的那样——他便不应绝望。但是且想一想当前的物质事实罢，我的受苦的兄弟们，这段全无道德可言的黑暗时刻。再进一步反思一下：在这座伟大的纽约城里，大多数公民竟以有一位『罗杰尔』（rojail，即提特人、小爬虫）做他们的老板与领袖为幸事。……我时常这样冥想：倘若庞塞·德莱昂（Ponce de Leon）当年到新大陆来寻觅『青春之泉』，那么我哈立德所寻觅的便是『真理之泉』，而我们两人，竟同样的得成功！

===Original===
“But the Americans are neither Pagans—which is consoling—nor fetish-worshipping heathens: they are all true and honest votaries of Mammon, their great God, their one and only God. And is it not natural that the Demiurgic Dollar should be the national Deity of America? Have not deities been always conceived after man’s needs and aspirations? Thus in Egypt, in a locality where the manufacture of pottery was the chief industry, God was represented as a potter; in agricultural districts, as a god of harvest; among warring tribes as an avenger, a Jehovah. And the more needs, the more deities; the higher the aspirations, the better the gods. Hence the ugly fetish of a savage tribe, and the beautiful mythology of a Greek Civilisation. Change the needs and aspirations of the Americans, therefore, and you will have changed their worship, their national Deity, and even their Government. And believe me, this change is coming; people get tired of their gods as of everything else. Ay, the time will come, when man in this America shall not suffer for not being a seeker and lover and defender of the Dollar.…

“Obedience, like faith, is a divine gift; but only when it comes from the heart: only when prompted by love and sincerity is it divine. If you can not, however, reverence what you obey, then, I say, withhold your obedience. And if you prefer to barter your identity or ego for a counterfeit coin of ideology, that right is yours. For under a liberal Constitution and in a free Government, you are also at liberty to sell your soul, to open a bank account for your conscience. But don’t blame God, or Destiny, or Society, when you find yourself, after doing this, a brother to the ox. Herein, we Orientals differ from Europeans and Americans; we are never bribed into obedience. We obey either from reverence and love, or from fear. We are either power-worshippers or cowards but never, never traders. It might be said that the masses in the East are blind slaves, while in Europe and America they are become blind rebels. And which is the better part of valour, when one is blind—submission or revolt?…

===Chinese===
「但美国人既非异教徒——这倒令人宽慰——亦非崇拜物神的蛮族：他们全是玛门（Mammon）那位真正而诚实的信徒，玛门是他们伟大的神、他们唯一的神。而那『造化美元』（Demiurgic Dollar）成为合众国的国神，岂不自然之至？诸神岂不是一向依照人的需要与抱负而被构想出来的么？故而在埃及，在一个以制陶为主要手工业的地方，神便被描绘为一位陶工；在农作之区，神是丰收之神；在好战之部族中，神是一位复仇者——一位耶和华（Jehovah）。需要愈多，神祇愈多；抱负愈高，神祇愈善。故而有野蛮部族的丑陋物神，也有希腊文明的优美神话。所以，改变美国人的需要与抱负，你便也改变了他们的崇拜、他们的国神、甚至他们的政府。并且信我罢，这变化必将来临；人会厌倦他们的神，一如厌倦其他一切。是啊，时候将到，在这片美洲大地上，人将不再因不肯去寻觅、热爱、捍卫那美元而受苦。……

「服从，一如信仰，乃是一种属天的恩赐；但只有发自内心、只有由爱与真诚所唤起之时，它才是属天的。然而，倘若你不能对你所服从者心存敬畏，那么我说，你便收回你的服从罢。倘若你宁愿把你的本真或自我，换取一枚意识形态的假硬币，那也是你的权利。因为在一部宽宏的宪法之下、在一个自由的政府之中，你同样有自由出卖你的灵魂、为你的良心开一个银行户头。但是当你如此行之后，发觉自己已沦为牛的兄弟，那时可别去怪真主、怪命运、怪社会。正是在这一点上，我们东方人有别于欧美人；我们绝不受贿而服从。我们服从，要么出于敬畏与爱，要么出于恐惧。我们要么是权力的崇拜者，要么是懦夫，但绝不是、绝不是生意人。或许可以说，东方的群众是盲目的奴隶，而欧美的群众则成了盲目的叛逆者。而当一个人盲目之时，服从与反叛——何者才是勇之上策？……

===Original===
“No; popular suffrage helps not the suffering individual; nor does it conduce to a better and higher morality. Why, my Masters, it can not as much as purge its own channels. For what is the ballot box, I ask again, but a modern vehicle of corruption and debasement? The ballot box, believe me, can not add a cubit to your frame, nor can it shed a modicum of light on the deeper problems of life. Of course, it is the exponent of the will of the majority, that is to say, the will of the Party that has more money at its disposal. The majority, and Iblis, and Jahannam—ah, come out with me to the new gods!…”

But we must make allowance for these girds and gibes at Democracy, of which we have given a specimen. Khalid’s irony bites so deep at times as to get at the very bone of truth. And here is the marrow of it. We translate the following prophecy with which he closes his Chapter “In Prison,” and with it, too, we close ours.

“But my faith in man,” he swears, “is as strong as my faith in God. And as strong, too, perhaps, is my faith in the future world-ruling destiny of America. To these United States shall the Nations of the World turn one day for the best model of good Government; in these United States the wellsprings of the higher aspirations of the soul shall quench the thirst of every race-traveller on the highway of emancipation; and from these United States the sun and moon of a great Faith and a great Art shall rise upon mankind. I believe this, *billah*! and I am willing to go on the witness stand to swear to it. Ay, in this New World, the higher Superman shall rise. And he shall not be of the tribe of Overmen of the present age, of the beautiful blond beast of Zarathustra, who would riddle mankind as they would riddle wheat or flour; nor of those political moralists who would reform the world as they would a parish.

===Chinese===
「不；普选并不能救助受苦的个人；亦不能促成一种更高更善的道德。我的主人们啊，何以见得？它连自己的渠道都清不动。我再问一遍——那投票箱，除了是一辆现代的腐化与堕落的载具之外，还能是什么？请相信我，投票箱不能给你的身量添上一肘，也不能为人生的更深层问题照亮一毫。当然，它是多数意志的体现——也就是说，是那个手中钱更多的党派的意志的体现。多数、伊布力斯（Iblis），与火狱（Jahannam）——啊，随我一同出来，去见见这批新神罢！……」

不过，对于哈立德这些讥刺民主的话——我们已摘出一种样品——我们必须略加宽待。哈立德的反讽有时咬得深得触及真理的骨头。而此处便是其中的骨髓。我们把他结束《在狱中》那一章时所写下的预言迻译如下，并以此结束我们这一章。

「但我对人的信心，」他发誓道，「一如我对真主（God）的信心。或许，我对合众国未来那个统治世界的命运，也同样深信不疑。世上万国，终有一日要转向这些合众国，来寻觅最佳良政的典范；在这些合众国里，灵魂更高抱负的诸泉源，将为每一个在解放的大道上跋涉的旅人解渴；从这些合众国里，一种大信仰、一种大艺术的日月，将升起照耀人类。我相信这一点，*billah*！我愿走上证人席，对此发誓。是啊，在这片新大陆之上，那位更高的『超人』（Superman）将兴起。他并不属于当今这个超人们（Overmen）的部族——不属于查拉图斯特拉（Zarathustra）那头美丽的金发野兽，那要把人类像麦子或面粉一样放在筛子里筛的人；也不属于那些要把世界当作一个教区来改良的政治道德家。

===Original===
“From his transcendental height, the Superman of America shall ray forth in every direction the divine light, which shall mellow and purify the spirit of Nations and strengthen and sweeten the spirit of men, in this New World, I tell you, he shall be born, but he shall not be an American in the Democratic sense. He shall be nor of the Old World nor of the New; he shall be, my Brothers, of both. In him shall be reincarnated the Asiatic spirit of origination, of Poesy and Prophecy, and the European spirit of Art, and the American spirit of Invention. Ay, the Nation that leads the world today in material progress shall lead it, too, in the future, in the higher things of the mind and soul. And when you reach that height, O beloved America, you will be far from the majority-rule, and Iblis, and Jahannam. And you will then conquer those ‘enormous mud Megatheriums’ of which Carlyle makes loud mention.”

===Chinese===
「从他那超凡的高度之上，这位美国的超人将向四面八方射出神圣之光，这光将使万国的精神变得醇厚而纯净，使人类的精神变得坚强而甘美。我告诉你们，在这片新世界，他将降生——但他绝不会是民主意义上的那个『美国人』。他既不属旧世界，也不属新世界；我的兄弟们，他乃是两者之合。在他身上，将转世那亚洲的原创精神——诗与预言的精神、那欧洲的艺术精神，以及那美国的发明精神。是啊，今日这个在物质进步上领先世界的国度，将来也必在心智与灵魂那些更高的事物上领先世界。等到你抵达那一高度之时，亲爱的美洲啊，你便远离了多数人之治，远离了伊布力斯，远离了火狱。那时，你也将征服那些卡莱尔（Carlyle）大声提到过的『庞大的泥沼巨懒兽（Megatheriums）』。」

## II / II

Subtranscendental / 亚超验

===Original===
Deficiencies in individuals, as in States, have their value and import. Indeed, that sublime impulse of perfectibility, always vivacious, always working under various forms and with one underlying purpose, would be futile without them, and fatuous. And what were life without this incessant striving of the spirit? What were life without its angles of difficulty and defeat, and its apices of triumph and power? A banality this, you will say. But need we not be reminded of these wholesome truths, when the striving after originality nowadays is productive of so much quackery? The impulse of perfectibility, we repeat, whether at work in a Studio, or in a Factory, or in a Prison Cell, is the most noble of all human impulses, the most divine.

Of that Chapter, In Prison, we have given what might be called the exogenous bark of the Soul, or that which environment creates. And now we shall endeavour to show the reader somewhat of the ludigenous process, by which the Soul, thrumming its own strings or eating its own guts, develops and increases its numbers. For Khalid in these gaol-days is much like Hamlet’s player, or even like Hamlet himself—always soliloquising, tearing a passion to rags. And what mean these outbursts and objurgations of his, you will ask; these suggestions, fugitive, rhapsodical, mystical; this furibund allegro about Money, Mediums, and Bohemia; these sobs and tears and asseverations, in which our Lady of the Studio and Shakib are both expunged with great *billahs*;—the force and significance of these subliminal uprushes, dear Reader, we confess we are, like yourself, unable to understand, without the aid of our Interpreter. We shall, therefore, let him speak.

===Chinese===
个人如国家，其缺陷亦自有其价值与意义。诚然，那种臻于完美的崇高冲动——它永远生气勃勃，永远以千般面目运作、而怀着一贯的宗旨——倘若没有这些缺陷，便会徒劳无功，甚至流于愚妄。而生命倘若没有这种精神上无休无止的奋争，又将若何？倘若没有它那些艰难与挫败的棱角，那些胜利与权势的尖峰，又将若何？你必定要说，这是老生常谈。然而当今之世，世人追逐独创性竟产出如许江湖骗术，我们难道不需要有人来提醒这些有益的真理么？我们再说一遍：那臻于完美的冲动，无论它运作于画室、工厂，还是囚室之中，都是人类一切冲动里最崇高、最神圣的一种。

关于《在狱中》那一章，我们所给的，不妨说是灵魂的外生（exogenous）之皮壳——亦即环境所造就的那一层。如今我们将勉力向读者略略展示那内衍（ludigenous）的历程：灵魂正是借此，拨弄自身的琴弦、抑或啃噬自身的脏腑，从而发育滋长、繁衍其数。因为哈立德在狱中的这些日子，颇像哈姆雷特（Hamlet）剧中的伶人，甚至像哈姆雷特本人——总在独白，总把一腔激情撕成碎片。你要问，他这些迸发与咒詈意味着什么？这些忽隐忽现、狂想式的、神秘的暗示？这些围绕着金钱、灵媒（Mediums）与波希米亚（Bohemia）的狂暴快板？这些抽泣、眼泪与激切之誓——其中我们画室的夫人与沙基布皆被几声重重的「凭真主起誓（billah）」一笔勾销？——至于这些潜意识涌升之力与义，亲爱的读者，我们承认，与你一样，若无我们的诠释者相助，我们也茫然不解。因此，我们且让他自己来说。

===Original===
“When in prison,” writes Shakib, “Khalid was subject to spasms and strange hallucinations. One day, when I was sweating in the effort to get him out of gaol, he sends me word to come and see him. I go; and after waiting a while at the Iron gate, I behold Khalid rushing down the isle like an angry lion. ‘What do you want,’ he growled, ‘why are you here?’ And I, amazed, ‘Did you not send for me?’ And he snapped up, ‘I did; but you should not have come. You should withhold from me your favours.’ Life of Allah, I was stunned. I feared lest his mind, too, had gone in the direction of his health, which was already sorrily undermined. I looked at him with dim, tearful eyes, and assured him that soon he shall be free. ‘And what is the use of freedom,’ he exclaimed, ‘when it drags us to lower and darker depths? Don’t think I am miserable in prison. No; I am not—I am happy. I have had strange visions, marvellous. O my Brother, if you could behold the sloughs, deeper and darker than any prison-cell, into which *you* have thrown me. Yes, *you*—and another. O, I hate you both. I hate my best lovers. I hate You—no—no, no, no.’ And he falls on me, embraces me, and bathes my cheeks with his tears. After which he falters out beseechingly, ‘Promise, promise that you will not give me any more money, and though starving and in rags you find me crouching at your door, promise.’ And of a truth, I acquiesced in all he said, seeing how shaken in body and mind he was. But not until I had made a promise under oath would he be tranquillised. And so, after our farewell embrace, he asked me to come again the following day and bring him some books to read. This I did, fetching with me Rousseau’s *Emile* and Carlyle’s *Hero-Worship*, the only two books he had in the cellar. And when he saw them, he exclaimed with joy, ‘The very books I want! I read them twice already, and I shall read them again. O, let me kiss you for the thought.’ And in an ecstasy he overwhelms me again with suffusing sobs and embraces.

“What a difference, I thought, between Khalid of yesterday and Khalid of today. What a transformation! Even I who know the turn and temper of his nature had much this time to fear. Surely, an alienist would have made a case of him. But I began to get an inkling into his cue of passion, when he told me that he was going to start a little business again, if I lend him the necessary capital. But I reminded him that we shall soon be returning home. ‘No, not I,’ he swore; ‘not until I can pay my own passage, at least. I told you yesterday I’ll accept no more money from you, except, of course, the sum I need to start the little business I am contemplating.’ ‘And suppose you lose this money,’ I asked.—‘Why, then *you* lose *me*. But no, you shall not. For I know, I believe, I am sure, I swear that my scheme this time will not be a failure in any sense of the word. I have heavenly testimony on that.’—‘And what was the matter with you yesterday? Why were you so queer?’ ‘O, I had nightmares and visions the night before, and you came too early in the morning. See this.’ And he holds down his head to show me the back of his neck. ‘Is there no swelling here? I feel it. Oh, it pains me yet. But I shall tell you about it and about the vision when I am out.’—And at this, the gaoler comes to inform us that Khalid’s minutes are spent and he must return to his cell.”

===Chinese===
「『哈立德在狱中时，』沙基布写道，『动辄发作痉挛，生种种奇异的幻觉。某日，我正为着把他弄出监狱而到处奔走、汗流浃背，他却捎话叫我去见他。我便去了；在铁门外候了一阵，便见哈立德如一头怒狮般沿着过道冲将下来。『你来做什么，』他咆哮，『你为何在此？』我大惊，答道：『不是你叫人传话叫我来么？』他厉声道：『是我叫的；可你本不该来。你本该把你的恩惠从我身上收回。』凭真主的生命起誓，我怔住了。我生怕他的心智也已朝着他身体的那个方向垮了下去——他的身体早已可悲地衰败。我以朦胧含泪的眼睛望着他，向他保证他很快便会重获自由。『自由又有何用，』他叫道，『倘若它把我们拖到更低、更暗的深渊？别以为我在狱中受苦。不；我并非如此——我是快乐的。我见过种种异象，奇妙无比。啊，我的兄弟，你若能瞧一瞧你把我投入的那些泥淖——它们比任何囚室都更深、更暗！是你——是的，是你——还有另一个人。啊，我恨你们两个。我恨最爱我的人。我恨你——不——不，不，不。』说着他扑到我身上，搂住我，用他的泪水沐浴我的双颊。随后他颤声恳求道：『答应我，答应我你不再给我钱；哪怕你见我饿到将死、衣衫褴褛地蜷在你门前，也要答应我。』说真的，见他身心震骇至此，他所说的一切我无不依从。可非等我发了誓、立了诺，他总不肯安宁。于是，作别拥抱之后，他请我次日再来，并带几本书给他解闷。我照办了，给他捎去卢梭（Rousseau）的《爱弥儿》（Emile）与卡莱尔（Carlyle）的《英雄崇拜》（Hero-Worship）——这两本也是他在地窖里仅有之书。他一见便欣喜叫道：『正合我心意！它们我已读过两遍，还要再读。啊，为了这份心思，让我亲你一下。』说罢一阵狂喜，又以盈眶的抽泣与拥抱将我淹没。』」

「『昨日之哈立德与今日之哈立德，差别何其之大，我心想。何等的一场蜕变！便是我这般深知他性情的转折与脾气的人，这一回也着实忧惧。诚然，换作精神病学家，必得拿他立一个病症。可当他告诉我，他打算再做一点小生意——只要我借他那必要的本钱——我便开始隐约窥见他那一腔激情的线索了。但我提醒他，我们不久便要返乡了。『不，我不返，』他起誓；『至少要等我自己付得起船资再说。我昨日告诉过你，你的钱我一分也不再收，当然，我做这桩小本生意所需的那笔款子除外。』『万一你蚀了本呢？』我问。——『哎呀，那你就失去我了。但不，你不会的。因为我知道，我相信，我确信，我起誓——我这一回的谋划，无论从何种意义上讲都不会失败。此事我有天上的见证。』——『那你昨日是怎么了？为何那般古怪？』『啊，前一夜我做了噩梦、见了异象，而你一大早就来了。你瞧这个。』说着他低下头，让我看他后颈。『这里没肿么？我摸得着。唉，至今还疼。但等我出去之后，再细细告诉你——连同那个异象。』——正说着，狱卒过来告诉我们：哈立德的时刻已尽，他必须回到自己的囚室了。』」

===Original===
All of which from our Interpreter is as clear as God Save the King. And from which we hope our Reader will infer that those outbursts and tears and rhapsodies of Khalid did mean somewhat. They did mean, even when we first approached his cell, that something was going on in him—a revolution, a coup d’état, so to speak, of the spirit. For a Prince in Rags, but not in Debts and Dishonour, will throttle the Harpy which has hitherto ruled and degraded his soul.

But the dwelling, too, of that soul is sorely undermined. And so, his leal and loving friend Shakib takes him later to the best physician in the City, who after the tapping and auscultation, shakes his head, writes his prescriptions, and advises Khalid to keep in the open air as much as possible, or better still, to return to his native country. The last portion of the advice, however, Khalid can not follow at present. For he will either return home on his own account or die in New York. “If I can not in time save enough money for the Steamship Company,” he said to Shakib, “I can at least leave enough to settle the undertaker’s bill. And in either case, I shall have paid my own passage out of this New World. And I shall stand before my Maker in a shroud, at least, which I can call my own.”

===Chinese===
我们的诠释者所说这一切，清楚得如同《天佑国王》（God Save the King）。我们也希望读者由此推知，哈立德那些迸发、那些眼泪、那些狂想，确乎有所指。它们所指的——甚至当我们初近他的囚室之时——是他内心正有所进行：一场革命，一场精神上的政变（coup d'état），可以这样说。因为一位衣衫褴褛——却不负债、不失节——的王子，终将扼死那只至今统治并辱没他灵魂的鹰身女妖（Harpy）。

然而，那灵魂的居所也已可悲地颓败。于是，他那忠诚而多情的朋友沙基布后来便带他去见城中最好的医师。医师一番叩诊听诊之后，摇了摇头，开了方子，劝哈立德尽量待在户外，更好的则是回到他的祖国去。然而这后半段劝告，哈立德此时却无法听从。因为：要么他靠自己的盘缠返乡，要么便死在纽约。『万一我不能及时攒够给轮船公司的船资，』他对沙基布说，『至少我能留下足够的钱去结清殡葬人的账。无论哪一种情形，我都已自付船资，离开了这个新世界。而我立在我的造物主（Maker）面前时，至少能裹着一件我称得起我自己的寿衣。』

===Original===
To which Shakib replies by going to the druggist with the prescriptions. And when he returns to the cellar with a package of four or five medicine bottles for rubbing and smelling and drinking, he finds Khalid sitting near the stove—we are now in the last month of Winter—warming his hands on the flames of the two last books he read. *Emile* and *Hero-Worship* go the way of all the rest. And there he sits, meditating over Carlyle’s crepitating fire and Rousseau’s writhing, sibilating flame. And it may be he thought of neither. Perhaps he was brooding over the resolution he had made, and the ominous shaking of the doctor’s head. Ah, but his tutelar deities are better physicians, he thought. And having made his choice, he will pitch the medicine bottles into the street, and only follow the doctor’s advice by keeping in the open air.

Behold him, therefore, with a note in hand, applying to Shakib, in a formal and businesslike manner, for a loan; and see that noble benefactor and friend, after gladly giving the money, throw the note into the fire. And now, Khalid is neither dervish nor philosopher, but a man of business with a capital of twenty-five dollars in his pocket. And with one-fifth of this capital he buys a secondhand pushcart from his Greek neighbour, wends his way with it to the marketplace, makes a purchase there of a few boxes of oranges, sorts them in his cart into three classes—“there is no equality in nature,” he says, while doing this—sticks a price card at the head of each class, and starts, in the name of Allah, his business. That is how he will keep in the open air twelve hours a day.

===Chinese===
对此，沙基布的回答是拿着方子去了药铺。待他带着一包四五瓶外擦、嗅吸、内服的药水回到地窖，却见哈立德坐在炉子旁——此时已是冬季最后一个月——正用他读过的最后两本书的火焰烘手。《爱弥儿》与《英雄崇拜》亦步了其余一切的后尘。他便坐在那里，对着卡莱尔那毕毕剥剥的火、卢梭那扭曲嘶啸的焰，冥然凝思。也许他两者都未想及。也许他正琢磨着自己所下的决心，与医师那不祥的摇头。啊，但他的守护神祇是更好的医者，他想。既已作出抉择，他便把那几只药瓶尽数掷到街上，只遵从医师一项劝告：待在户外。

那么且看他罢：手执一张借据，一本正经、公事公办地向沙基布求贷；再看那位高尚的恩主与朋友，慷慨解囊之后，把那张借据掷入炉火。至此，哈立德既非德尔维希（dervish），亦非哲士，而是一个口袋里揣着二十五块本钱的生意人。他以这本钱的五分之一，向他的希腊邻居买了一辆二手手推车，推着它走向集市，在那里购入几箱橘子，把它们在车里分成三等——『天地间原无平等，』他一边分一边说——在每一等前面插上标价的纸牌，便奉真主（Allah）之名，开了张。这便是他如何一天在户外待上十二个钟头的法子。

===Original===
But in the district where he is known he does not long remain. The sympathy of his compatriots is to him worse than the doctor’s medicines, and those who had often heard him speechifying exchanged significant looks when he passed. Moreover, the police would not let him set up his stand anywhere. “There comes the pushcart orator,” they would say to each other; and before our poor Syrian stops to breathe, one of them grumpishly cries out, “Move on there! Move on!” Once Khalid ventures to ask, “But why are others allowed to set up their stands here?” And the “copper” (we beg the Critic’s pardon again) coming forward twirling his club, lays his hand on Khalid’s shoulder and calmly this: “Don’t you think I know you? Move on, I say.” O Khalid, have you forgotten that these “coppers” are the minions of Tammany? Why tarry, therefore, and ask questions? Yes, make a big move at once—out of the district entirely.

Now, to the East Side, into the Jewish Quarter, Khalid directs his cart. And there, he falls in with Jewish fellow pushcart peddlers and puts up with them in a cellar similar to his in the Syrian Quarter. But only for a month could he suffer what the Jew has suffered for centuries. Why? There is this difference between the cellar of the Semite Syrian and that of the Semite Jew: in the first we eat *mujaddara*, in the second, kosher but stinking flesh; in the first we read poetry and play the lute, in the second we fight about the rent and the division of the profits of the day; in the first we sleep in linen “as white as the wings of the dove,” in the second on pieces of smelly blankets; the first is redolent of attar of roses, Shakib’s favourite perfume, the second is especially made insufferable by that stench which is peculiar to every Hebrew hive. For these and other reasons, Khalid separates himself from his Semite fellow peddlers, and makes this time a bigger move than the first.

===Chinese===
但在他为人所知的那个区里，他待不长。同胞们的同情，对他而言比医师的药还难熬；往日常听他演说的人们，在他经过时彼此交换着意味深长的眼色。况且，警察也不准他在任何地方摆摊。他们会彼此说道：『那个手推车演说家来了。』于是，不等我们可怜的叙利亚人停下喘口气，其中之一便恶声吆喝：『往前走！往前走！』哈立德有一回壮胆问：『可为何别人可以在这里摆摊？』那「铜徽」（copper，我们再次请批评家见谅）上前旋着警棍，一手按在哈立德肩上，从容说道：『你以为我不认得你？往前走，我说。』啊，哈立德，你莫非忘了这些「铜徽」是坦慕尼（Tammany）的爪牙？那你又何必逗留、又何必发问？是的，赶紧来一个大挪步——完全挪出这个区。

于是，哈立德把他的车推向东区，推入犹太区（Jewish Quarter）。在那里，他与犹太籍的手推车同行们搭上了伙，与他们一同挤在一间与他在叙利亚区（Syrian Quarter）的地窖相仿的地窖里。可犹太人受了几百年的苦，他连一个月也忍不下来。缘故何在？闪族叙利亚人的地窖与闪族犹太人的地窖，有此一别：在第一类地窖里我们吃穆贾达拉（mujaddara），在第二类里吃的是合于教规（kosher）却发臭的肉；在第一类里我们读诗、弹鲁特琴，在第二类里我们为房租、为当日盈利的分割而争吵；在第一类里我们睡在『白如鸽翼』的亚麻布上，在第二类里睡在一片片发臭的毯子碎块上；第一类地窖洋溢着沙基布心爱的玫瑰精油（attar of roses）之香，第二类则因那种每一个希伯来人聚居处所特有的恶臭，而尤其叫人难以忍受。因这些以及别样缘故，哈立德脱离了他的闪族同行，这一次挪了一个比头一回更大的步子。

===Original===
Ay, even to the Bronx, where often in former days, shouldering the peddling-box, he tramped, will he now push his orange-cart and his hopes. There, between City and Country, nearer to Nature, and not far from the traffic of life, he fares better both in health and purse. It is much to his liking, this upper end of the City. Here the atmosphere is more peaceful and soothing, and the police are more agreeable. No, they do not nickname and bully him in the Bronx. And never was he ordered to move on, even though he set up his stand for months at the same corner. “Ah, how much kinder and more humane people become,” he says, “even when they are not altogether out of the City, but only on the outskirts of the country expanse.”

Khalid passes the Spring and Summer in the Bronx and keeps in the open air, not only in the day, but also in the night. How he does this, is told in a letter which he writes to Shakib. But does he sleep at all, you ask, and how, and where? Reader, we thank you for your anxiety about Khalid’s health. And we would fain show you the Magic Carpet which he carries in the lockbox of his pushcart. But see for yourself, here be neither Magic Carpet, nor Magic Ring. Only his papers, a few towels, a blanket, some underwear, and his coffee utensils, are here. For Khalid could forego his *mujaddara*, but never his coffee, the Arab that he is. But an Arab on the wayfare, if he finds himself at night far from the camp, will dig him a ditch in the sands and lie there to sleep under the living stars. Khalid could not do thus, neither in the City nor out of it. And yet, he did not lodge within doors. He hired a place only for his pushcart; and this, a small padlock-booth where he deposits his stock in trade. But how he lived in the Bronx is described in the following letter:

===Chinese===
是啊，哪怕到了布朗克斯（the Bronx）——往日他常扛着货郎箱在那儿长途跋涉——他如今也要推着他的橘子车、推着他的希望前去。在那里，在城市与乡野之间，更近自然，又离人间的往来不远，他的身体与钱袋都过得更好些。这座城市的这一上端，甚合他的意。这里的空气更为宁静、更为宜人，警察也较为和气。不，他们在布朗克斯并不给他起绰号、并不欺侮他。即便他在同一个街角一连摆了几个月的摊，他也从未被勒令『往前走』。『啊，人是变得多么更仁慈、更人道，』他说，『哪怕他们并非完全出了城，只不过是在辽阔乡野的边上。』

哈立德在布朗克斯度过了春夏两季，且始终待在户外——不但白日里，夜里也是。他怎样做到的，记在他写给沙基布的一封信里。可他到底睡不睡觉呢，你要问，又怎么睡，睡在哪里？读者啊，我们感谢你对哈立德健康的关切。我们也乐意把他在手推车锁箱里随身带着的那张魔毯（Magic Carpet）指给你看。可你自己瞧罢，这里既无魔毯，也无魔戒（Magic Ring）。只有他的文件、几条毛巾、一条毯子、一些内衣，以及他的煮咖啡器具。因为哈立德可以舍弃他的穆贾达拉，却绝不能舍弃他的咖啡——他到底是个阿拉伯人。可一个阿拉伯人行路之人，若夜里发觉自己远离营地，便会在沙里挖一道沟，躺下去在活生生的星辰之下安眠。哈立德却做不到这般——无论在城里还是城外。然而，他也并不下榻于户内。他只为他的手推车租了一个安身处：那是一间小小的挂锁棚屋，他在那里存放他那点买卖家什。至于他在布朗克斯是怎样过活的，详见下面这封信：

===Original===
“My loving Brother Shakib,

“I have been two months here, in a neighbourhood familiar to you. Not far from the place where I sleep is the sycamore tree under which I burned my peddling-box. And perhaps I shall yet burn there my pushcart too. But for the present, all’s well. My business is good and my health is improving. The money-order I am enclosing with this, will cancel the note, but not the many debts, I owe you. And I hope to be able to join you again soon, to make the voyage to our native land together. Meanwhile I am working, and laying up a little something. I make from two to three dollars a day, of which I never spend more than one.
