===Original===
And while the many-ruffled sky-blue dress is being made, Khalid, inspired by Najma’s remarks on his hair, rhapsodises on flounces and ruffles. Of this striking piece of fantasy, in which are scintillations of the great Truth, we note the following:

“What can you do without your flounces? How can you live without your ruffles? Ay, how can you, without them, think, speak, or work? How can you eat, drink, walk, sleep, pray, worship, moralise, sentimentalise, or love, without them? Are you not ruffled and flounced when you first see the light, ruffled and flounced when you last see the darkness? The cradle and the tomb, are they not the first and last ruffles of Man? And between them what a panoramic display of flounces! What clean and attractive visible Edges of unclean invisible common Skirts! Look at your huge elaborate monuments, your fancy sepulchers, what are they but the ruffles of your triumphs and defeats? The marble flounces, these, of your cemeteries, your Pantheons and Westminster Abbeys. And what are your belfries and spires and chimes, your altars and reredoses and suchlike, but the sanctified flounces of your churches. No, these are not wholly adventitious sanctities; not empty, superfluous growths. They are incorporated into Life by Time, and they grow in importance as our Aesthetics become more inutile, as our Religions begin to exude gum and pitch for commerce, instead of bearing fruits of Faith and Love and Magnanimity.

“The first church was the forest; the first dome, the welkin; the first altar, the sun. But that was, when man went forth in native buff, brother to the lion, not the ox, without ruffles and without faith. His spirit, in the course of time, was born; it grew and developed zenithward and nadirward, as the cycles rolled on. And in spiritual pride, and pride of power and wealth as well, it took to ruffling and flouncing to such an extent that at certain epochs it disappeared, dwindled into nothingness, and only the appendages remained. These were significant appendages, to be sure; not altogether adscititious. Ruffles these, indeed, endowed, as it were, with life, and growing on the dead Spirit, as the grass on the grave.

“And is it not noteworthy that our life terrene at certain epochs seems to be made up wholly of these? That as the great Pine falls, the noxious weeds, the brambles and thorny bushes around it, grow quicker, lustier, luxuriating on the vital stores in the earth that were its own—is not this striking and perplexing, my rational friends? Surely, Man is neither the featherless biped of the Greek Philosopher, nor the tool-using animal of the Sage of Chelsea. For animals, too, have their tools, and man, in his visible flounces, has feathers enough to make even a peacock gape. Both my Philosophers have hit wide of the mark this time. And Man, to my way of thinking, is a flounce-wearing Spirit. Indeed, flounces alone, the invisible ones in particular, distinguish us from the beasts. For like ourselves they have their fashions in clothes; their peculiar speech; their own hidden means of intellection, and, to some extent, of imagination: but flounces they have not, they know not. These are luxuries, which Man alone enjoys.

===Chinese===
那件缀满褶裥的天蓝袍子正在赶制之际，哈立德受娜吉玛对他那一头秀发的议论所触动，竟对「荷边与褶裥」大发了一段狂想。这段奇崛的幻想之作，其间时有伟大真理的火花闪现，我们记下如下几则：

「离了你的荷边，你能做什么？离了你的褶裥，你又怎能活？哎，离了它们，你怎能思考、说话，或做工？离了它们，你怎能吃、喝、走、睡、祈祷、礼拜、说教、伤感，或谈情说爱？你初次见光之时，岂不已被荷边褶裥所裹缠？你末次见暗之时，岂不也仍被荷边褶裥所裹缠？摇篮与坟墓，岂不正是人的第一道褶裥与最末一道褶裥？而在此二者之间，又是何等一幅琳琅满目的褶裥长卷！那些又洁净又惹眼的、可见的『边』，所缀的又正是那不洁净、不可见、俗不可耐的『裙』！看看你那些精心堆砌的巍峨纪念碑、你那些花俏的陵寝罢，它们除了是你胜利与失败的褶裥之外，还能是什么？这些大理石的褶裥，属于你的墓园、你的万神庙（Pantheons）、你的威斯敏斯特修道院（Westminster Abbeys）。而你那些钟楼、尖塔、钟乐，你那些祭坛、祭坛后屏诸如此类，除了是你教堂里被奉为圣的褶裥之外，又算得了什么？不，这些并非全是外加的神圣；并非空洞而多余的赘疣。它们已被时间织入生命之中，并且随着我们的美学愈发无用、随着我们的宗教开始为生意而渗出树胶与沥青（不再结出信仰、爱与宽宏的果实），它们也愈发显得举足轻重。

「头一座教堂是森林；头一座穹顶是苍穹；头一座祭坛是太阳。可那是当人赤身露体走出丛林、与狮而非与牛同列、既无褶裥亦无信仰之时的事。在岁月流转之间，他的灵魂诞生了；它一面向天顶、一面向深渊地生长发展，随着旋回的世纪层层推进。于是在属灵的骄傲里——也在权势与财富的骄傲里——它一味地加褶加边，加到了这样的地步：在某个时期，它竟隐没不见、萎缩成无，只剩下那些附赘悬疣还留着。这些附赘自然是有意义的附赘，并非全然是外加之物。可以说，它们是些仿佛禀有生命的褶裥，长在那已死的灵魂之上，一如野草长在坟头。

「而难道不值得我们一提的是：在若干时期，我们这尘世的生命似乎便全然由这些东西构成？大松一倒，它周遭那些恶草、荆棘与有刺的灌木，反倒长得更快、更壮，在它原所独占的地里那些生气蕴藏之上肆意繁茂——我这些讲理性的朋友们，这难道不既惊人、又叫人困惑么？诚然，人既不是那位希腊哲学家所谓的『无毛两足』，也不是那位切尔西圣人（Sage of Chelsea）所谓『会用工具的动物』。因为禽兽也有它们的工具，而人呢，在他那些可见的褶裥里，所有的羽毛便足以叫一只孔雀也瞠目结舌。这一回，我的两位哲学家都射偏了靶心。而照我看来，人乃是一个『穿褶裥的灵魂』。诚然，唯有褶裥——尤其是那些看不见的褶裥——将我们与禽兽分别开来。因为禽兽也像我们一样，有它们的衣着时样；有它们独特的言语；有它们自己隐秘的思维手段，甚至在某种程度上也有想象的手段：唯独褶裥，它们是没有的，也是它们所不知的。这些乃是唯有人才享用的奢侈品。

===Original===
“Ah, Man—thou son and slave of Allah, according to my Oriental Prophets of Heaven; thou exalted, apotheosised ape, according to my Occidental Prophets of Science;—how much thou canst suffer, how much thou canst endure, under what pressure and in what Jahannam depths thou canst live; but thy flounces thou canst not dispense with for a day, nor for a single one-twelfth part of a day. Even in thy suffering and pain, the agonised spirit is wrapped, bandaged, swathed in ruffles. It is assuaged with the flounces of thy lady’s caresses, and the scalloped intonations of her soft and soothing voice. It is humbugged into health by the malodorous flounces of the apothecary and the medicinal ruffles of the doctor.

“Ay, we live in a phantasmagoric, cycloramic economy of flounces and ruffles. The human Spirit shirks nudity as it shirks pain. Even your modern preacher of the Simple Life is at best suggesting the moderate use of ruffles.… Indeed, we can suffer anything, everything, but the naked and ugly reality. Alas, have I not listened for years to what I mistook to be the strong, pure voice of the naked Truth? And have I not discovered, to my astonishment, that the supposed scientific Nudity is but an indurated thick Crust under which the Lie lies hidden. Why strip Man of his fancy appendages, his adventitious sanctities, if you are going to give him instead only a few yards of shoddy? No, I tell you; this can not be done. Your brambles and thorn hedges will continue to grow and luxuriate, will even shut from your view the Temple in the Grove, until the great Pine rises again to stunt, and ultimately extirpate, them.

===Chinese===
「啊，人啊——照我东方那些属天的先知所言，你是真主之子、真主之奴；照我西方那些科学的先知所言，你是一只被抬举、被神化了的猿猴；——你能受多少苦，又能忍多少痛，能在怎样的重压之下、在火狱（Jahannam）何等的深处活着；可你的褶裥，你却一日不可缺少，便是一日的十二分之一，也缺它不得。便在你受苦受痛之时，那受煎熬的灵魂也是被褶裥层层裹缠、层层绷扎、层层包裹的。它被你那位夫人爱抚的荷边所抚平，被她那柔和抚慰之声的、如波浪般起伏的语调所抚平。它被药剂师那臭气熏人的荷边、被医生那药味的褶裥，一路哄骗，竟哄到了康健。

「是啊，我们活在一个变幻如走马灯、如全景画一般的、由荷边与褶裥构成的经济里。人的灵魂躲避赤裸，一如它躲避痛苦。便是你那位现代的『简朴生活』布道师，充其量也不过是在劝人『适度地使用褶裥』罢了。……的确，我们什么都能受，一切都能受，唯独受不住那赤裸而丑陋的现实。唉，我岂不是多年以来一直在听那被我误认为赤裸真理之强劲、纯粹的声音？而我岂不是终于惊讶地发觉：那号称科学的赤裸，不过是一层硬化了的厚壳，谎言之物就藏在那壳下。倘若你给他的不过是几码劣等的再生毛织物，那你又何必剥去人那些花哨的附赘、那些外加的神圣呢？不，我告诉你；这是办不到的。你的荆棘与刺篱还要继续生长、继续繁茂，甚至要把那林中神庙也从你眼前遮没，直等到那棵大松重新耸起，把它们先压制、终铲除。

===Original===
“Behold, meanwhile, how the world parades in ruffles before us. What a bewildering phantasmagoria this: a very Dress Ball of the human race. See them pass: the Pope of Christendom, in his three hats and heavy trailing gowns, blessing the air of heaven; the priest, in his alb and chasuble, dispensing of the blessings of the Pope; the judge, in his wig and bombazine, endeavouring to reconcile divine justice with the law’s mundane majesty; the college doctor, in cap and gown, anointing the young princes of knowledge; the buffoon, in his cap and bells, dancing to the god of laughter; mylady of the pink-tea circle, in her huffing, puffing gasoline-car, fleeing the monster of ennui; the bride and bridegroom at the altar or before the mayor putting on their already heavy-ruffled garments the sacred ruffle of law or religion; the babe brought to church by his mother and kindred to have the priest-tailor sew on his new garment the ruffle of baptism; the soldier in his gaudy uniform; the king in his ermine with a crown and sceptre appended; the Nabob of Ind in his gorgeous and multicolored robes; and the Papuan with horns in his nostrils and rings in his ears: see them all pass.

“And wilt thou still add to the bewildering variety of the pageant? Or wilt have another of the higher things of the mind? Lo, the artist this, wearing his ruffles of hair over his shoulders; and here, too, is the man of the sombrero and red flannel, which are the latest flounces of a certain set of New World poets. Directly behind them is Dame Religion with her heavy ruffled robes, her beribboned and belaced bodices, her ornaments and sacred gewgaws. And *billah*, she has stuffings and paddings, too. And false teeth and foul breath! Never mind. Pass on, and let her pass. But tarry thou a moment here. Behold this pyrotechnic display, these buntings and flags; hear thou this music and these shouts and cheers; on yonder stump is an orator dispensing to his fellow citizens spread-eagle rhetoric as empty as yonder drum: these are the elaborate and attractive ruffles of politics. And among the crowd are genial and honest citizens who have their own way of ruffling your temper with their coarse flounces of linsey-woolsey freedom. Wilt thou have more?”

===Chinese===
「且看这当儿，这世界如何在我们眼前穿着褶裥招摇过市。这是一幅何等令人眼花缭乱、变幻莫测的图卷：简直就是一场人类的化装舞会。看他们鱼贯而过：基督教世界的教皇，戴着三重冠、拖着沉重的长袍后摆，对着天上的空气降福；神父，穿着白长衣与祭披，分发教皇的祝福；法官，戴着假发、穿着蚕驼毛绸袍，竭力把神圣的正义与法律那世俗的威严调和起来；学院的博士，戴着方帽、穿着学袍，为那些年轻的求知王子们涂膏授职；小丑，戴着系铃的滑稽帽，向着那位笑之神舞蹈；那位午后茶会圈里的贵妇，坐在她那辆哼哧哼哧、喷着汽油味的汽车里，逃避那名叫『无聊』的怪物；新郎新娘在圣坛前、或是在市长面前，在他们那身已缀满褶裥的衣裳上，再添上一道法律或宗教的神圣褶裥；婴孩被母亲与亲族抱到教堂里来，好让那位『神父裁缝』在他那件新衣上，缝上那道洗礼的褶裥；军人穿着他光鲜的制服；国王穿着貂皮袍，外加王冠与权杖；印度（Ind）的那位富翁，穿着他绚烂多彩的袍服；还有那巴布亚人，鼻孔里插着角、耳朵上挂着环：看他们一一走过。

「你还要给这场令人目眩神迷的游行再添上几种花样么？或者你还要再看一样心智上更高的事物？瞧，这边是那位艺术家，把他的头发当作褶裥披在肩上；这边又是那位戴宽边帽、穿红法兰绒衬衫的人，这正是一班新大陆诗人最新的褶裥。紧跟在他们身后的，是宗教夫人（Dame Religion），拖着她那重重褶裥的袍子，她那镶满缎带与花边的胸衣，她那些饰物与神圣的小玩意儿。而且 *billah*（凭真主起誓），她还有塞垫与衬垫呢。还有假牙，还有口臭！不要紧。往前走罢，让她自过去。可你且在此处逗留片刻。看这场焰火般的排场，这些彩旗与旌旗；听这乐曲，这阵阵欢呼与喝彩；那边树桩上有一位演说家，正向他的同胞们发放着那种与那边一面鼓同样空洞的、张牙舞爪的辞藻：这些便是政治那精心堆砌而引人注目的褶裥。而人群之中，又有些和善而诚实的公民，自有他们那一套，用他们那种粗糙的、亚麻羊毛交织的『自由』褶裥，来撩拨你的脾气。你还要不要再看？」

===Original===
Decidedly not, we reply. For how can we even keep company with Khalid, who has become such a maniac on flounces? And was this fantastic, phantasmagoric rhapsody all inspired by Najma’s simple remark on his hair? Fruitful is thy word, O woman!

But being so far away now from the Hermitage in the Bronx, what has the “cherry in the cocktail” and “the olive in the oyster patty” to do with all this? Howbeit, the following deserves a place as the tail-flounce of his Fantasy.

---

			

“Your superman and superwoman,” says he, with philosophic calm, “may go Adam-and-Eve like if they choose. But can they, even in that chaste and splendid nudity, dispense with ruffles and flounces? Pray, tell me, did not our first parents spoon and sentimentalise in the Paradise, before the Serpent appeared? And would they not often whisper unto each other, ‘Ah, Adam, ah, Eve!’ sighing likewise for sweeter things? And what about those fatal Apples, those two sour fruits of their Love?—I tell thee every newborn babe is the magnificent flesh-flounce of a shivering, trembling, nudity. And I Khalid, what am I but the visible ruffle of an invisible skirt? Verily, I am; and thou, too, my Brother. Yea, and this aquaterrestrial globe and these sidereal heavens are the divine flounces of the Vesture of Allah.”

===Chinese===
坚决不要了，我们答道。因为哈立德既已成了一个为褶裥发狂的狂人，我们怎能还与他同行？而这一段奇幻莫测、如走马灯一般的狂想，竟全是被娜吉玛对他头发的一句随口议论所引出的么？女人啊，你的话何其多产！

可如今既然离 Bronx 那座「隐庐」已如此之远，那「鸡尾酒里的樱桃」、「蚝馅饼里的橄榄」，与此事又有何相干？然而，下面这一段，作为他那篇《狂想》的收尾荷边，倒也值得一录。

---

			

「你们的超人与超女，」他以一种哲人的恬静说道，「尽可以随他们的便，像亚当夏娃那样赤身露体。可是，便在那贞洁而辉煌的赤裸之中，他们便能免去褶裥与荷边么？请问告诉我，在毒蛇出现之前，我们那两位始祖不曾在乐园里卿卿我我、伤春感怀么？他们岂不也常常彼此低语：『啊，亚当，啊，夏娃！』也同样地为更甜蜜之物而叹息？那么，那两颗致命的苹果、那两颗他们的爱情的酸果，又当作何论？——我告诉你，每一个初生的婴孩，便是一道堂皇的、血肉做成的褶裥，缝在一袭战栗、颤抖的赤裸之上。而我哈立德，我又算得什么，岂不正是一道看不见的裙所翻出的、看得见的褶裥？诚然，我是；你也是，我的兄弟。是啊，而这水陆两栖的地球、这些星辰密布的天穹，便是真主那件衣裳上属神的荷边。」

## VII / VII

The Howdaj of Falsehood / 虚妄的驼轿

===Original===
"Humanity is so feeble in mind," says Renan, "that the purest thing has need of the cooperation of some impure agent." And this, we think, is the gist of Khalid's rhapsody on flounces and ruffles. But how is he to reconcile the fact with the truth in his case? For a single sanctified ruffle—a line of type in the canon law—is likely to upset all his plans. Yes, a priest in alb and chasuble not only can dispense with the blessings of his Pope, but—and here is the rub—he can also withhold such blessings from Khalid. And now, do what he may, say what he might, he must either revise his creed, or behave, at least, like a Christian.

===Chinese===
「人性在心智上如此孱弱，」勒南（Renan）说道，「以至最纯粹之物，亦需某种不洁之物加以襄助。」而我们以为，这正是哈立德那一通关于褶边与荷叶边的狂想之要旨。然而他怎能将这一事实，与他自身处境之中的真相调和一致呢？因为单单一道奉为圣物的褶边——教会法典里的一行铅字——便足以推翻他全部的计划。是的，一位身披白长袍与祭披的神父，不但能豁免其教宗的祝福，而且——难就难在这里——他还能把这祝福从哈立德身上扣下。事到如今，他无论做什么、说什么，都必须要么修正自己的信条，要么至少表现得像个基督徒。

===Original===
Everything is ready, you say? The sky-blue, many-ruffled wedding gown; the set-out for the wayfare; the camel and donkeys; the little stock of books; the coffee utensils; the lentils and sweet oil;—all ready? Very well; but you can not set forth tomorrow, nor three weeks from tomorrow. Indeed, before the priest can give you his blessings—and what at this juncture can you do without them?—the dispensations of the ban must be performed. In other words, your case must now be laid before the community. Every Sunday, for three such to come, the intended marriage of Khalid to Najma will be published in the Church, and whoso hath any objection to make can come forth and make it. Moreover, there is that little knot of consanguinity to be considered. And your priest is good enough to come and explain this to you. Understand him well. "An alm of a few gold pieces," says he, "will remove the obstacle; the unlawfulness of your marriage resulting from consanguinity will cease on payment of five hundred piasters."

===Chinese===
你都预备好了，是么？那天蓝色的、缀满荷叶边的婚纱；那上路的一应行装；那骆驼和毛驴；那一小箱书；那煮咖啡的家什；那扁豆和清油——全都齐备了？很好；可是你明日动身不了，自明日起三周之内也动身不了。因为在这位神父把他的祝福赐予你之前——而在此关头，离了它你又能怎样呢？——必须先履行那道禁婚令的种种豁免手续。换言之，你这一桩案子如今须得提交整个教团公议。今后连续三个礼拜日，哈立德与娜吉玛即将成婚之事，将在教堂里当众公布，凡有异议者皆可上前陈述。况且，还有那一层血亲的小疙瘩须得考虑。于是你们那位神父竟肯亲自登门，向你把这番道理讲明。且听他说得何等透彻。「几枚金币的布施，」他说，「便能除去此障；因血亲而生的婚姻之不法，一经缴纳五百皮阿斯特（piasters），即告消除。」

===Original===
All of which startles Khalid, stupefies him. He had not, heretofore, thought of such a matter. Indeed, he was totally ignorant of these forms, these prohibitions and exemptions of the Church. And the father of Najma, though assenting, remarks nevertheless that the alms demanded are much. "Why," exclaims Khalid, "I can build a house for five hundred piasters."

The priest sits down cross-legged on the divan, lights the cigarette which Najma had offered with the coffee, and tries to explain.

===Chinese===
这一切把哈立德惊得目瞪口呆。他迄今从未想到过这样一桩事。事实上，他对教会的这些形式、这些禁制与豁免，全然无知。便是娜吉玛的父亲，虽已应允，却仍嫌所索的布施过重。「哎呀，」哈立德嚷道，「五百皮阿斯特足够我盖一所房子了。」

神父盘腿在长榻上坐下，点起娜吉玛随咖啡奉上的那一支烟卷，设法加以解释。

===Original===
"And where have you this, O Reverend, about consanguinity, prohibition, and alms!" Khalid asks.

"Why, my child, in the Canons of our Church, Catholic and Apostolic. Everyone knows that a marriage between cousins can not be effected, without the sanction of the Bishop."

"But can we not obtain this sanction without paying for it?"

"You are not paying for it, my child; you are only contributing some alms to the Church."

"You come to us, therefore, as a beggar, not as a spiritual father and guide."

"That is not good speaking. You misunderstand my purpose."

"And pray, tell me, what is the purpose of prohibiting a marriage between cousins; what chief good is there in such a ban?"

"Much good for the community."

"But I have nothing to do with the community. I'm going to live with my wife in the desert."

"The good of your souls is chiefly concerned."

"Ah, the good of our souls!"

"And there are other reasons which can not be freely spoken of here."

"You mean the restriction and prohibition of sexual knowledge between relatives. That is very well. But let us return to what concerns us properly: the good of my soul, and the spiritual well-being of the community—what becomes of these, when I pay the prescribed alms and obtain the sanction of the Bishop?"

"No harm then can come to them—they'll be secure."

"Secure, you say? Are they not hazarded, sold by your Church for five hundred piasters? If my marriage to my cousin be wrong, unlawful, your Bishop in sanctioning same is guilty of perpetuating this wrong, this unlawfulness, is he not?"

"But what the Church binds only the Church can loosen."

"And what is the use of binding, O Reverend Father, when a little sum of money can loosen anything you bind? It seems to me that these prohibitions of the Church are only made for the purpose of collecting alms. In other words, you bind for the sake of loosening, when a good bait is on the hook, do you not? Pardon, O my Reverend Father, pardon. I can not, to save my soul and yours, reconcile these contradictions. For if Mother Church be certain that my marriage to my cousin is contrary to the Law of God, is destructive of my spiritual well-being, then let her by all means prohibit it. Let her restrain me, compel me to obey. Ay, and the police ought to interfere in case of disobedience. In her behalf, in my behalf, in the behalf of my cousin's soul and mine, the police ought to do the will of God, if the Church knows what it is, and is certain and honest about it. Compel me to stop, I conjure you, if you know I am going in the way of damnation. O my Father, what sort of a mother is she who would sell two of her children to the devil for a few hundred piasters? No, *billah*! no. What is unlawful by virtue of the Divine Law the wealth of all the Trust-Kings of America can not make lawful. And what is so by virtue of your Canon Law concerns not me. You may angle, you and your Church, as long as you please in the murky, muddy waters of Bind-and-Loosen, I have nothing to do with you."…

===Chinese===
「那么，神父，关于血亲、禁婚与布施这些规矩，您是从何处得来的？」哈立德问。

「哎呀，我的孩子，是在我们那大公而使徒的教会之法典里。谁都知道，表兄妹之间的婚事，若无主教的核准，便不能成就。」

「但我们难道不能不花钱就取得这核准么？」

「你并非在花钱买它，我的孩子；你不过是向教会捐一点布施罢了。」

「那么您到我们这里来，是以乞儿的身份，而非以灵性之父与向导的身份了。」

「这话未免不中听。你误会了我的来意。」

「那么请问，禁止表兄妹通婚究竟有何用意；这样一道禁令之中，到底有何大好处？」

「于公众大有好处。」

「可我与公众毫无干系。我要和妻子到沙漠里去过活。」

「所关切的，主要是你们灵魂的好处。」

「啊，我们灵魂的好处！」

「此外还有种种缘由，在此不便明言。」

「您指的是限制并禁止亲属之间互通性知识。这倒很好。可还是让我们回到与我们真正相关的事上来：我灵魂的好处，以及公众的灵性福祉——待我缴纳了规定的布施、取得了主教的核准之后，它们又当如何？」

「那时它们便不会受损害了——它们将安然无恙。」

「安然无恙，您说？它们岂不是已遭危害——被你们的教会以五百皮阿斯特出卖了？若与我表妹成婚乃是错事、乃是不法，那么你们的主教一加核准，便等于使这错事、这不法绵延下去，他岂非有罪？」

「然而教会所绑的，唯有教会能解。」

「那么捆绑又有何用，神父大人，既然一点小钱便能解开你们所绑的一切？在我看来，教会的这些禁制，其用意不过是为了收取布施罢了。换言之，你们之所以捆绑，原是为了解开——只要钩上挂着好饵，不是么？宽恕我罢，神父大人，宽恕我。我纵为救我灵魂与您灵魂之故，也不能把这些矛盾调和起来。因为倘若慈母教会确信我与表妹之婚事有悖于上帝之律法、有损于我灵性之福祉，那么就让她竭力加以禁止好了。让她约束我，强制我顺从。是啊，倘若我不顺从，连警察也该出面干涉才对。为着她，为着我，为着我表妹与我两人灵魂之故，警察就该去行上帝之意——只要教会知道那是什么，并且确信无疑、诚实行事。倘若您知道我正走向沉沦之路，我求您，强令我停下脚步罢。我的父啊，这是怎样一位母亲——竟为区区数百皮阿斯特，把自己的两个孩子卖给魔鬼？不，凭真主起誓（billah）！不。那依神法而为不法的，纵有全美利坚所有的托拉斯大王之财，也不能使之成为合法。而那依你们教会法典而为不法的，则与我无涉。你和你的教会，尽可在那『捆绑与解开』的浑水浊流里，随心所欲地垂钓多久，我都与你们无干。」……

===Original===
But the priests, O Khalid, have yet a little to do with you. Such arguments about the Divine Law and the Canon Law, about alms and spiritual beggars, might cut the Gordian knot with your uncle, but—and whether it be good or bad English, we say it—they cut no ice with the Church. Yes, Mother Church, under whose wings you and your cousin were born and bred, and under whose wings you and your cousin would be married, can not take off for the sweet sake of your black eyes the ruffles and flounces of twenty centuries. Think well on it, you who have so extravagantly and not unwisely delivered yourself on flounces and ruffles. But to think, when in love, were, indeed, disastrous. O Love, Love, what Camels of wisdom thou canst force to pass through the needle's eye! What miracles divine are thine! Khalid himself says that to be truly, deeply, piously in love, one must needs hate himself. How true, how inexorably true! For would he be always inviting trouble and courting affliction, would he be always bucking against the dead wall of a Democracy or a Church, if he did not sincerely hate himself—if he were not religiously, fanatically in love—in love with Najma, if not with Truth?

===Chinese===
可是，哈立德啊，那些神父与你之间，还有那么一点未了之缘呢。这一番关于神法与教会法、关于布施与灵性乞丐的辩难，或许能斩断你与你叔父之间的戈尔迪之结（Gordian knot），然而——管它英文通与不通，我们不妨这么说——它对教会却「切不动半块冰」（cut no ice）。是的，慈母教会——你与你表妹生于其翼下、育于其翼下，又将婚于其翼下的那位慈母——绝不会为了你那双黑眼睛的甜蜜缘故，便卸下那二十个世纪的褶边与荷叶边。你且三思罢，你这个曾经就褶边与荷叶边大发宏论——大发得铺张、却也不无道理的人。然而恋爱之中还能思考，那确是灾难。啊，爱情，爱情，你能迫使多少头智慧之驼穿过针眼（needle's eye）！你的神迹何等辉煌！哈立德自己便说过：要真正地、深深地、虔诚地恋爱，一个人非得恨他自己不可。何其真切，何其不留情面地真切！因为，倘若他不是真心地恨自己——倘若他不是宗教般地、狂热地恋爱（倘若不是恋着娜吉玛，便是恋着真理）——他岂会总是自招麻烦、自寻苦恼？他岂会总是把头撞向那名为民主、或名为教会的高墙？

===Original===
Now, on the following Sunday, instead of publishing the intended marriage of Khalid and Najma, the parish priest places a ban upon it. And in this, ye people of Baalbek, is food enough for tattle, and cause enough for persecution. Potent are the ruffles of the Church! But why, we can almost hear the anxious Reader asking, if the camels are ready, why the deuce don't they get on and get them gone? But did we not say once that Khalid is slow, even slower than the law itself? Nevertheless, if this were a Novel, an elopement would be in order, but we must repeat, it is not. We are faithful transcribers of the truth as we find it set down in Shakib's *Histoire Intime*.

===Chinese===
于是，下一个礼拜日，本堂神父非但没有宣布哈立德与娜吉玛即将成婚之事，反而对这桩婚事下了禁令。而这一下，巴勒贝克（Baalbek）的乡邻们啊，可够你们嚼一阵子舌根、也够你们兴一场迫害的了。教会的褶边，何其威力赫赫！可是——我们几乎听得见那位焦心的读者在问——既然骆驼都备好了，他们到底见什么鬼还不赶紧上路走人？可是我们不是曾说过，哈立德慢吞吞，比法律本身还要慢么？然而，倘若这是一部小说，私奔倒正合时宜；可我们必须重申——它不是。我们只是忠实的誊录者，照沙基布《私密史》（Histoire Intime）里所记之事如实抄录。

===Original===
True, Khalid did ask Najma to throw with him the handful of dust, to steal out of Baalbek and get married on the way, say in Damascus. But poor Najma goes over to his mother instead, and mingling their tears and prayers, they beseech the Virgin to enlighten the soul and mind of Khalid. "Yes, we must be married here, before we go to the desert," says she, "for think, O my mother, how far away we shall be from the world and the Church if anything happens to us."

===Chinese===
诚然，哈立德确实求过娜吉玛与他一同撒下那一撮黄土（叙利亚婚仪之俗）——悄悄溜出巴勒贝克，在路上成婚，比如到大马士革（Damascus）。可苦命的娜吉玛却转身去了他母亲那里，母女二人泪下如雨、祷告连连，恳求圣母照亮哈立德的灵魂与心智。「是的，我们须得先在此地成婚，再去沙漠，」她说，「因为我的母亲啊，你想，万一我们有了什么事，离这尘世、离这教会，将何其遥远。」

===Original===
And they would have succeeded, the mother and cousin of Khalid, in persuading the parish priest to accept from them the prescribed alms and perform the wedding ceremony, had not the Jesuits, in the interest of the Faith and the Church, been dogging Khalid still. For if they have failed in sending him to the Bosphorus, they will succeed in sending him elsewhither. And observe how this is done.

After communicating with the Papal Legate in Mt. Lebanon about that fatal Latter Day Pamphlet of Thomas Carlyle, the Adjutant-General, or Adjutant-Bird, stalks up there one night in person and lays before the Rt. Rev. Mgr. his devil's brief in Khalid's case. It has already been explained that this Pamphlet was fathered on Khalid by the Jesuits. For if they can not punish the Voice which is still pursuing them—and in their heart of hearts they must have recognised its thunder, even in a Translation—they will make the man smart for it who first mentioned Carlyle in this connection.

"And besides this pernicious booklet," says the Adjutant-Bird, "the young man's heretical opinions are notorious. He was banished from home on that account. And now, after corrupting and deluding his cousin, he is going to marry her despite the ban of the Church. Something, Monseigneur, ought to be done, and quickly, to protect the community against the poison of this wretch." And Monseigneur, nodding his accord, orders his Secretary to write a note to the Patriarch, enclosing the aforesaid devil's brief, and showing the propriety, nay, the necessity of excommunicating Khalid the Baalbekian. The Adjutant-Bird, with the Legate's letter in his pocket, skips over to the Patriarch on the other hilltop below, and after a brief interview—our dear good Ancient of the Maronites must willy-nilly obey Rome—the fate of Khalid the Baalbekian is sealed.

===Chinese===
哈立德的母亲与表妹本可劝说本堂神父收下规定的布施、并主持婚礼，若不是那些耶稣会士（Jesuits）——为着信仰与教会的利益——仍在对哈立德穷追不舍。因为既然他们未能把他发配到博斯普鲁斯（Bosphorus），便要设法把他发配到别处去。且看这是如何做成的。

那位副官长（Adjutant-General）——或曰副官鸟（Adjutant-Bird）——与黎巴嫩山的教廷使节就托马斯·卡莱尔（Thomas Carlyle）那本要命的小册子《末日小册》（Latter Day Pamphlet）通过信之后，于某夜亲自踱步上山，把一纸针对哈立德的「魔鬼诉状」呈到那位可敬的大人（Rt. Rev. Mgr.）面前。前文已经说过，这本小册子是被耶稣会士栽到哈立德头上的。因为倘若他们惩治不了那仍在追逐他们的「声音」——而他们在心底深处，纵是透过一部译本，也必已听出了那声音的雷霆——那么他们便要让那个最先在这种场合提到卡莱尔的人，为此吃一番苦头。

「除了这本恶毒的小册子，」副官鸟说，「这年轻人的异端之言也尽人皆知。他当年正是为此被逐出家门。如今他在诱骗并玷污了他的表妹之后，竟要顶着教会的禁令与她成婚。大人，为保护公众免受这恶棍之毒害，应当速速有所举措才是。」那位大人点头称是，随即吩咐他的书记官致书宗主教（Patriarch），随函附上前述那份「魔鬼诉状」，并陈明将巴勒贝克人哈立德逐出教门之得当——乃至必要。副官鸟怀揣着教廷使节的书信，一蹦一跳地翻到下面那座山头上的宗主教府；一番简短会面之后——我们那位亲爱的、善良的马龙派老族长不得不俯首听命于罗马——巴勒贝克人哈立德的命运，便就此盖棺定论。

===Original===
Indeed, the upshot of these Jesuitic machinations is this: on the very day when Khalid's mother and cousin are pleading before the parish priest for justice, for mercy—offering the prescribed alms, beseeching that the ban be revoked, the marriage solemnised—a messenger from the Bishop of the Diocese enters, kisses his Reverence's hand, and delivers an imposing envelope. The priest unseals it, unfolds the heavy foolscap sheet therein, reads it with a knitting of the brow, a shaking of the beard, and, clapping one hand upon the other, tells the poor pleaders to go home.

"It is all finished. There is no more hope for you and your cousin." And he shows the Patriarchal Bull, and explains.

Whereupon, Najma and Khalid's mother go out weeping, wailing, beating their breasts and cheeks, calling upon Allah to witness their sorrow and the outrageous tyranny of the priests.

===Chinese===
果然，这些耶稣会士的阴谋所结之果，是这样的：正当哈立德的母亲与表妹在本堂神父面前求个公道、求个开恩——奉上规定的布施、恳求撤销禁令、主持婚礼——的那一天，教区主教的一名差役走了进来，吻了神父大人的手，递上一只气派非凡的大信封。神父拆开它，展开里面那张厚实的大页纸（foolscap），皱着眉、抖着胡须读了一遍，继而一手拍在另一手之上，叫那两个可怜的哀求者回家去。

「一切都完了。你和你表妹再无指望了。」说着，他把那张宗主教谕令（Patriarchal Bull）给她们看，并加以解说。

于是娜吉玛与哈立德的母亲哭着出去了，哀号不已，捶胸拍颊，呼求真主（Allah）见证她们的悲伤，见证神父们那骇人听闻的暴虐。

===Original===
"What has my son done to be excommunicated? Hear it, ye people, hear it. And be just to me and my son. What has he done to deserve the anathema of the Church? What has he done?" And thus frantic, mad, she runs through the main street of the town, making wild gestures and clamours—publishing, as it were, the Patriarchal Bull, before it was read by the priest on the following day, and tacked on the door of the Church.

Of this Bull, tricked with the stock phrases of the Church of the Middle Ages, such as "anathema be he," or "banned be he," who speaks with, deals with, and so forth, we have a copy before us. But our readers will not pardon us, we fear, if further space and consideration be here given to its contents. Suffice it to say, however, that Khalid comes to church on that fatal day, takes the foolscap sheet down from the door, and, going with it to the town-square, burns it there before the multitudes.

===Chinese===
「我的儿子到底做了什么，竟被逐出教门？众人哪，你们听，你们听啊。要对我公正，对我的儿子公正。他到底做了什么，竟招教会这般咒诅？他到底做了什么？」她就这样疯了一般，沿着城里的大街狂奔，做出种种狂乱的手势、发出种种喧嚷——仿佛抢在翌日神父当众宣读、并把它钉在教堂门上之前，便已自行把那道宗主教谕令公告于众了。

这道谕令，满缀着中世纪教会的套话——诸如「当受咒诅」、「当受禁逐」，以及「凡与之交谈者、与之交易者」云云——其抄本，我们手边正有一份。然而倘若在此再费笔墨与篇幅去论它的内容，我们恐读者不能见谅。只消说这一点便够了：在那个要命的日子，哈立德来到教堂，从门上揭下那张大页纸，拿着它走到城中广场，当着众人之面，将它付之一炬。

===Original===
And it came to pass, when the Bull is burned in the town-square of Baalbek, in the last year of the reign of Abdul Hamid, some among the multitudes shout loud shouts of joy, and some cast stones.

Then, foul, vehement speaking falleth between the friends and the enemies of him who wrought evil in the sight of the Lord;

And everyone thereupon brandisheth a stick or taketh up a stone and the battle ensueth.

Now, the mighty troops of the Sultan of the Ottomans come forth like the Yaman wind and stand in the town-square like rocks;

And the battle rageth still, and the troops who are come forth to part the fighting multitudes, having gorged themselves at the last meal, can not as much as speak their part:

And it came to pass, when the clubs and spades are veiled and the battle subsideth of itself, the good people return to their respective callings and trades;

But the perverse recalcitrants which remain—and Khalid the Baalbekian is among them—are taken by the aforesaid overfed troops to the City Hall and thence to the *vilayet* prison in Damascus.

And here endeth our stichometrics of the Battle of the Bull.

===Chinese===
当那道谕令在巴勒贝克的城中广场被焚之时——时值阿卜杜勒·哈米德（Abdul Hamid）在位之末年——众人之中，有人放声欢呼，有人投掷石块。

于是那在耶和华眼中行恶者之友与众人之敌之间，恶言烈语纷起；

众人遂各挥一杖、或拾一石，战斗于是乎起。

此时，奥斯曼苏丹之雄兵如也门之风（Yaman wind）席卷而出，立于城中广场，巍然如磐石；

战火仍烈；而那些被遣来分开鏖战之众的兵卒，因在末一顿饭上吃得过饱，竟连自己分内的话也说不出口：

及至棍棒与铁锹一旦藏匿不挥、战事自行平息，众善民各归其业、各复其工；

唯有那顽劣不驯之徒——巴勒贝克人哈立德亦在其内——被前述那些饱食过度的兵卒押往市政府，再由那里押往大马士革的维拉耶特（vilayet，行省）监狱。

我们这段《谕令之战》（Battle of the Bull）的诗体编年，到此终结。

===Original===
Now, Shakib may wear out his shoes this time, his tongue, too, and his purse, but to no purpose. Behold, your friend the *kaimakam* is gloomy and impassive as a camel; what can you do? Whisper in his ear? The Padres have done that before you. Slip a purse into his pocket? They have done that, too, and overdone it long since. Yes, the City Hall of every city in the Empire is an epitome of Yildiz Kiosk. And your *kaimakams*, and *valis*, and viziers, have all been taught in the same Textbook, at the same Political School, and by the same Professor. Let Khalid rest, therefore and ponder these matters in silence. For in the City Hall and during the month he passes in the prison of Damascus, we are told, he does not utter a word. His partisans in prison ask to be taught his creed, and among these are some Mohammadans: "We'll burn the priests and their church yet and follow you. By our Prophet Mohammad we will…" Khalid makes no reply. Even Shakib, when he comes to visit him, finds him dumb as a stone, slain by adversity and disease. Nothing can be done now. The giant excommunicated, incommunicative soul, struggling in a prison of sore flesh, we must leave, alas, with his friends and partisans to pass his thirty days and nights in the second prison of stone.

===Chinese===
这一回，沙基布就是把鞋底磨穿、把舌头说烂、把钱袋掏空，也是枉然。瞧瞧，你那位卡伊马卡姆（kaimakam，县长）朋友阴沉着脸，冷若冰霜，像一头骆驼；你能拿他怎样？在他耳边吹风？那些神父早你之前便吹过了。往他兜里塞钱袋？他们也早塞过了，而且早就塞得过了头。是的，帝国之内每一座城市的市政府，都是伊尔迪兹宫（Yildiz Kiosk）的缩影。而你们那些卡伊马卡姆、瓦利（vali，省督）、维齐尔（vizier，大臣），都是在同一本教科书里、同一所政治学堂之中、受教于同一位教授门下。所以，且让哈立德静静地待着，在沉默之中思量这些事罢。因为据说在市政府里、在他路过大马士革监狱那整整一个月里，他一语不发。狱中他的同道求他传授他的信条，其中也有一些穆罕默德之民（Mohammadans）：「我们要把这些神父连同他们的教堂烧个干净，然后追随你。凭我们的先知穆罕默德（Mohammad）起誓，我们必……」哈立德一言不发。便是沙基布前来探望时，也见他哑如顽石——已被厄难与疾病所击杀。如今再无可为了。这颗被逐出教门、封闭自我的伟大灵魂，在一具病肉牢狱之中挣扎——唉，我们只能把他留给他的朋友与同道，让他在那第二座石头监狱里熬过他的三十个日夜。

===Original===
Now, let us return to the Jesuits, who, having worsted Khalid, or the Devil in Khalid, as they charitably put it, will also endeavour to do somewhat in the interest of his intended bride. For the Padres, in addition to their many crafts and trades, are matrimonial brokers of honourable repute. And in their meddling and making, their baiting and mating, they are as serviceable as the Column Personal of an American newspaper. Whoso is matrimonially disposed shall whisper his mind at the Confessional or drop his advertisement in the pocket of the visiting Columns of their Bride-Dealer, and he shall prosper. She as well as he shall prosper.

Now, Father Farouche is commissioned to come all the way from Zahleh to visit the brother of Abu-Khalid their porter, and bespeak him in the interest of his daughter. All their faculties of persuasion shall be exerted in behalf of Najma. She must be saved at any cost. Hence they volunteer their services. And while Khalid is lingering in prison at Damascus, they avail themselves of the opportunity to further the suit of their pickle-herring candidate for Najma's love.

===Chinese===
现在，让我们回到那些耶稣会士身上。他们既已击败了哈立德——或者如他们仁慈所言，击败了哈立德体内的魔鬼——便还要为他那位准新娘稍尽一番心力。因为那些神父，除了他们种种手艺行当之外，还是声名清白的做媒经纪人。在他们这般穿插撮合、这般张罗配对之间，其功用之广，不下于一张美国报纸的征婚专栏。凡有结缡之意者，只须在告解室里低声吐露心意，或把广告一则投进他们那位「新娘经纪」的拜访栏口袋里，便可称心如意。男称心，女亦如意。

于是，法鲁什神父（Father Farouche）奉差从扎赫莱（Zahleh）远道而来，拜访他们那位看门人阿布·哈立德（Abu-Khalid）的兄弟，为着他女儿的事进言。他们要竭尽一切说服之能事，为娜吉玛效劳。务必不计代价把她救出来。所以他们自告奋勇。趁着哈立德还滞留在大马士革狱中，他们便利用这个机会，为那个跳梁小丑式的（pickle-herring）求婚人——那位觊觎娜吉玛之爱的人——推波助澜。

===Original===
The Reverend Farouche, therefore, holds a secret conference with her father.

"No," says he, "God would never have forgiven you for giving your daughter to one utterly destitute of morality, religion, money, and health. But praise Allah! the Church has come to her rescue. She shall be saved, wrested from the hands of Iblis. Yes, Holy Church, through us, will guide her to find a god-fearing life-companion; one worthy of her charms, her virtues, her fine qualities of heart and mind. The young man we recommend is rich, respected in the community; is an official of the Government with a third-class Medjidi decoration and the title of Bey; and is free from all diseases. Moreover, he is a good Catholic. Consider these advantages. A relation this, which no father would reject, if he loves his daughter and is solicitous of her future well-being. Speak to her, therefore, and let us know soon your mind."

===Chinese===
于是，法鲁什神父与她父亲密谈。

「不，」他说，「你若把女儿给了一个全无道德、全无信仰、全无钱财、又全无健康之人，上帝永远不会宽恕你。可是赞美真主罢！教会已来搭救她了。她必被救出，从伊布力斯（Iblis）的手中夺回来。是的，圣教会将借我们之手，引导她寻得一位敬畏上帝的终身伴侣；一位配得上她的姿容、她的美德、她那心地与心智之良质的君子。我们所推荐的这位青年，家道殷实，在乡里颇受人敬重；他是政府里的一名官员，领有三级梅吉迪勋章（Medjidi decoration），并享有贝伊（Bey）的头衔；并且，他百病不染。再者，他是个虔诚的天主教徒。请权衡这些好处罢。这样一桩亲事，凡是爱自己的女儿、又关心她将来福祉的父亲，没有会拒绝的。所以请你去跟她谈谈，早日把你的意思告知我们。」

===Original===
And our Scribe, in relating of this, loses his temper.—"An Official of the Government, a Bey with a third-class Medjidi decoration from the Sultan! As if Officialdom could not boast of a single scoundrel—as if any rogue in the Empire, with a few gold coins in his purse, were not eligible to the Hamidian decorations! And a third-class decoration! Why, I have it on good authority that these Medjidi Orders were given to a certain Patriarch in a bushel to distribute among his minions.…"

But to our subject. Abu-Najma does not look upon it in this light. A decorated and titled son-in-law were a great honour devoutly to be wished. And some days after the first conference, the Padre Farouche comes again, bringing along his Excellency the third-class Medjidi Bey; but Najma, as they enter and salaam, goes out on the terrace roof to weep. The third time the third-class Medjidi Dodo comes alone. And Najma, as soon as she catches a glimpse of him, takes up her earthen jar and hies her to the spring.

===Chinese===
我们的抄写人述及此事，不禁动了肝火。——「政府里的一名官员，从苏丹那里领了一枚三级梅吉迪勋章的贝伊！仿佛官场之中连一个无赖都拿不出手似的——仿佛帝国之内任何一个兜里有几枚金币的流氓，都不够资格领取哈米德式的勋章似的！况且还是一枚三级勋章！嗳，据我所确知，这些梅吉迪勋章（Medjidi Orders）曾一蒲式耳一蒲式耳地赏给某位宗主教，好让他分赏给手下的那些喽啰。……」

可话说回来。阿布·娜吉玛（Abu-Najma）却不作如是观。一位挂着勋章、顶着头衔的女婿，乃是一份梦寐以求的大荣耀。第一次密谈之后数日，法鲁什神父再次登门，还把那位三级梅吉迪贝伊阁下一并领了来；可娜吉玛一见他们进来行色兰之礼（salaam），便走到屋顶平台上去落泪。到了第三次，那位三级梅吉迪的呆头鹅（Dodo）独自登门。娜吉玛刚一瞥见他的身影，便抄起陶罐，径往泉水那边去了。

===Original===
"O the hinny! I'll rope noose her (hang her) tonight," murmurs the father. But here is his Excellency with his Sultan's green button in his lapel. Abu-Najma bows low, rubs his hands well, offers a large cushion, brings a *masnad* (leaning pillow), and blubbers out many unnecessary apologies.

"This honour is great, your Excellency—overlook our shortcomings—our *beit* (one room house) can not contain our shame—it is not becoming your Excellency's high rank—overlook—you have condescended to honour us, condescend too to be indulgent.—My daughter? yes, presently. She is gone to church, to mass, but she'll return soon."

===Chinese===
「这丫头！我今夜就给她上吊索（绞死她），」父亲咕哝道。可眼前这位阁下，襟头上别着苏丹所赐的绿扣子。阿布·娜吉玛深深鞠躬，把两手搓了又搓，端上一只厚实的坐垫，又奉上一只马斯纳德（masnad，倚枕），嘴里冒出一连串本不必要的赔罪话。

「阁下，这是莫大的恩光——多多包涵我们的简慢罢——我们这单间小屋（beit，一室之居）装不下我们的羞愧——这等不配阁下之高位——包涵包涵——您既肯屈尊光临我们，也请屈尊宽宏大量罢。——我女儿么？是，这就来。她上教堂去了，望弥撒，可她马上就回来。」

===Original===
But Najma is long gone; returns not; and the third-class Dodo will call again tomorrow. Now, Abu-Najma brings out his rope, soaps it well, nooses and suspends it from the rafter in the ceiling. And when his daughter returns from the spring, he takes her by the arm, shows her the rope, and tells her laconically to choose between his Excellency and this. Poor Najma has not the courage to die, and so soon. Her cousin Khalid is in prison, is excommunicated—what can she do? Run away? The Church will follow her—punish her. There's something satanic in Khalid—the Church said so—the Church knows. Najma rolls these things in her mind, looks at her father beseechingly. Her father points to the noose. Najma falls to weeping. The noose serves well its purpose.

===Chinese===
可娜吉玛早走远了，再不回来；那三级呆头鹅明日再来。于是阿布·娜吉玛拿出他的绳子，抹上好皂，打好活结，把它吊在天花板的椽子上。等女儿从泉边回来，他便一把抓住她的胳膊，指给她看那根绳子，言简意赅地叫她在阁下与此物之间作一个选择。苦命的娜吉玛没有去死的勇气，至少这么快没有。她的表哥哈立德身陷囹圄，又被逐出教门——她还能怎样？私奔么？教会会追着她——惩罚她。哈立德身上有几分邪魔之气——教会是这么说的——教会懂得。娜吉玛把这些事在心里翻来覆去，哀求地望着父亲。父亲指向那个绳套。娜吉玛失声痛哭。那绳套把它的差使办得很好。

===Original===
For hereafter, when the Dodo comes decorated, **she** has to offer him the cushion, bring him the *masnad*, make for him the coffee. And eventually, as the visits accumulate, she goes with him to the dressmaker in Beirut. The bridal gown shall be of the conventional silk this time; for his Excellency is travelled, and knows and reverences the fashion. But why prolong these painful details?

===Chinese===
因为此后，每逢那位呆头鹅戴着勋章登门，是她得给他端坐垫、奉马斯纳德、为他煮咖啡。渐渐地，随着来往之次数越积越多，她竟随他一同到贝鲁特（Beirut）的裁缝铺里去了。这一回的婚纱，须得是那规规矩矩的绸缎；因为阁下见过世面，深知时尚、且敬重时尚。可又何必把这些痛切的细枝末节再往下拖延呢？

===Original===
"Allah, in the mysterious working of his Providence," says Shakib, "preordained it thus: Khalid, having served his turn in prison, Najma begins her own; for a few days after he was set free, she was placed in bonds forged for her by the Jesuits. Now, when Khalid returned from Damascus, he came straightway to me and asked that we go to see Najma and try to prevail upon her, to persuade her to go with him, to run away. They would leave on the night-train to Hama this time, and thence set forth towards Palmyra. I myself did not know what had happened, and so I approved of his plan. But alas! as we were coming down the main Street to Najma's house, we heard the sound of tomtoms in the distance and the shrill ulluluing of women. We continued apace until we reached the byway through which we had to pass, and lo, we find it choked by the *zeffah* (wedding procession) of none but she and the third-class Medjidi.…"

===Chinese===
「真主以其冥冥之中的神旨，」沙基布说，「早已如此前定：哈立德在狱中服满了他的刑期，娜吉玛便开始服她自己的刑期；因为自他获释之后不出几日，她便被耶稣会士为她锻成的锁链锁了起来。哈立德从大马士革回来时，径直来找我，要我们一同去看娜吉玛，试着打动她、劝她随他走、与他私奔。这一回他们要乘夜车去哈马（Hama），再从那里动身往巴尔米拉（Palmyra）。我当时还不知发生了什么事，便赞成他的计划。可叹啊！我们正顺着大街往娜吉玛家走，便远远听见手鼓之声，和女人们那尖厉的颤舌欢呼（ulluluing）。我们加快脚步，一直走到那条必经的小巷口，一看——竟被迎亲的泽法（zeffah，婚礼游行）堵得水泄不通；那不是别人，正是她与那位三级梅吉迪的婚礼。……」

===Original===
But we'll no more of this! Too tragic, too much like fiction it sounds, that here abruptly we must end this Chapter.

===Chinese===
可这件事我们不再往下讲了！听来太惨、太像小说了——所以我们只好在此戛然结束本章。

## VIII / VIII

The Kaaba of Solitude / 孤寂之克尔白

===Original===
Disappointed, distraught, diseased—worsted by the Jesuits, excommunicated, crossed in love—but with an eternal glint of sunshine in his breast to open and light up new paths before him, Khalid, after the fatal episode, makes away from Baalbek. He suddenly disappears. But where he lays his staff, where he spends his months of solitude, neither Shakib nor our old friend the sandomancer can say. Somewhither he still is, indeed; for though he fell in a swoon as he saw Najma on her caparisoned palfrey and the decorated Excellency coming up along side of her, he was revived soon after and persuaded to return home. But on the following morning, our Scribe tells us, coming up to the booth, he finds neither Khalid there, nor any of his few worldly belongings. We, however, have formed a theory of our own, based on certain of his writings in the K.L. MS., about his mysterious levitation; and we believe he is now somewhither whittling arrows for a coming combat. In the Lebanon mountains perhaps. But we must not dog him like the Jesuits. Rather let us reverence the privacy of man, the sacredness of his religious retreat. For no matter where he is in the flesh, we are metaphysically certain of his existence. And instead of filling up this Chapter with the bitter bickerings of life and the wickedness and machination of those in power, let us consecrate it to the divine peace and beauty of Nature. Of a number of Chapters in the Book of Khalid on this subject, we choose the one entitled, My Native Terraces, or Spring in Syria, symbolising the natural succession to Khalid’s Winter of destiny. In it are signal manifestations of the triumph of the soul over the diseases and adversities and sorrows of mortal life. Indeed, here is an example of faith and power and love which we reckon sublime.

===Chinese===
失望，烦乱，抱病——被耶稣会士（Jesuits）所败，遭绝罚，爱情又受挫——然而胸中却永有一点不灭的阳光，为他开辟、照亮新的前路；那场致命的变故之后，哈立德离开了巴勒贝克（Baalbek）。他忽然销声匿迹。然而他把行杖搁在了何处，他那数月的孤寂又是在哪里打发的，沙基布说不上来，我们那位老朋友沙占师（sandomancer）也说不上来。他确乎仍在于某处；因为虽说他在看见娜吉玛（Najma）骑着那匹披挂华美的鞍马、那位勋章满胸的阁下挨到她身旁之时，当场昏厥倒地，可不久便被救醒，并被劝回了家。然而翌日清晨——据我们的抄写人所言——他走到那间茅棚一看，既不见哈立德在那里，也不见他那寥寥几件随身之物。不过，关于他这一桩神秘的「飞升」，我们依据赫迪夫图书馆手稿（K.L. MS.）中他的若干文字，自成了一套说法；我们相信，他此刻正在某处，为一场即将到来的争斗削制箭矢。或许就在黎巴嫩（Lebanon）群山之中。但我们不可像耶稣会士那样跟踪盯梢他。与其如此，不如去敬重一个人的隐私、敬重他宗教退修的圣洁。因为无论他的肉身身在何方，我们在形而上学的意义上确知他的存在。本章我们不愿再充斥人生的尖酸龃龉、当权者的邪恶与诡谋，而宁愿把它奉献给大自然那神圣的宁静与美。在《哈立德之书》论此主题的若干章节中，我们选取题为《我乡的梯田，或叙利亚之春》的一章，象征着哈立德命运的严冬之后自然的更迭。其中有灵魂战胜尘世疾病的灾厄与悲愁的显著见证。诚然，这里有一例信心、力量与爱，我们奉之为崇高。

===Original===
“The inhabitants of my terraces and terrace walls,” we translate, “dressed in their Sunday best, are in the doorways lounging or peeping idly through their windows. And why not? It is Spring, and to these delicate, sweet little creatures, Spring is the one Sunday of the year. Have they not hugged the damp, dark earth long enough? Hidden from the wrath of Winter, have they not squatted patiently round the primitive, smokeless fire of the mystic depths? And now, the rain having partly extinguished the inner, hidden flame, they come out to bask in the sun, and drink deeply of the ambrosial air. They come, almost slain with thirst, to the Mother Fountain. They come out to worship at the shrine of the sweet-souled, God-absorbed Rabia of Attar. In their bright, glowing faces what a delectable message from the under world of romance and enchantment! Their lips are red with the kisses of love, in whose alembics, intangible, unseen, the dark and damp of the earth are translated into warmth and colour and shade. Ay, these dear little children, unfolding their soft green scrolls and reading aloud such odes on Modesty and Beauty, are as inspiring as the star-crowned night. And every chink in my terrace walls seems to breathe a message of sweetness and light and love.

“Know you not the anecdote about the enchanting Goddess Rabia, as related by Attar in his *Biographies of Sufi Mystics and Saints*? Here it is. Rabia was asked if she hated the devil, and she replied, ‘No.’ Asked again why, she said, ‘Being absorbed in love, I have no time to hate.’ Now, all the inhabitants of my terraces and fields seem to echo this sublime sentiment of their Goddess. The air and sunshine, nay, the very rocks are imbued with it. See, how the fissures in the boulders yonder seem to sympathise with the gaps in the terrace walls: the cyclamen leaves in the one are salaaming the cyclamen flowers in the other. O, these terraces would have delighted the heart of the American naturalist Thoreau. He could not have desired stone walls with more gaps in them. But mind you, these are not dark, ugly, hollow, hopeless chinks. Behind every one of them lurks a mystery. Far back in the niches I can see the busts of the poets who wrote the poems which these beautiful wild flowers are reading to me. Yes, the authors are dead, and what I behold now are the flowers of their amours. These are the offspring of their embraces, the crystallised dew of their love. Yes, this one single, simple act of love brings forth an infinite variety of flowers to celebrate the death of the finite outward shape and the eternal essence of life perennial. In complete surrender lies the divineness of things eternal. This is the keynote of the Oriental mystic poets. And I incline to the belief that they of all bards have sung best the song of love. In rambling through the fields with these beautiful children of the terraces, I know not what draws me to al-Farid, the one erotic-mystic poet of Arabia, whose interminable rhymes have a perennial charm. Perhaps such lines as these—

‘All that is fair is fairer when she rises,

					All that is sweet is sweeter when she is here;

					And every form of beauty she surprises

					With one brief word she whispers in its ear:

‘Thy wondrous charms, O let them not deceive thee;

					They are but borrowed from her for a while;

					Thine outward guise and loveliness would grieve thee,

					If in thine inmost soul she did not smile.

‘All colours, forms, into each other merging,

					Are woven on her Loom of Unity;

					For she alone is One in All diverging,

					And she alone is absolute and free.’

“Now, I will bring you to a scene most curiously suggestive. Behold that little knot of daisies pressing around the alone anemone beneath the spreading leaves of the colocasia. Here is a rout at the Countess Casiacole’s, and these are the debutantes crowding around the Celebrity of the day. But would they do so if they were sensible of their own worth, if they knew that their idol, flaunting the crimson crown of popularity, had no more, and perhaps less, of the pure essence of life than any of them? But let Celebrity stand there and enjoy her hour; tomorrow the Ploughman will come.

===Chinese===
「我那梯田与梯田石墙上的居民们，」我们迻译道，「穿着他们最讲究的礼拜日盛装，倚在门口闲坐，或从窗子里懒懒地向外窥望。这有何不可呢？春日已至，对这些娇嫩甘美的小生灵而言，春天便是一年里唯一的礼拜日。他们拥抱那潮湿幽暗的泥土，难道还不够长久么？为躲避冬日的烈怒，他们耐着性子蹲踞在那幽秘深处的原始无烟之火周围，难道还不够久么？如今，雨水已将那内里隐藏的火焰灭去了一半，他们便出来晒晒太阳，痛饮那芳醇的空气。他们几乎渴得要死地来到那「母亲之泉」（Mother Fountain）。他们出来，在阿塔尔（Attar）笔下那位灵魂甘美、全心归主的女神拉比娅（Rabia）的神龛前朝拜。他们光彩焕发的脸庞上，传递着何等动人的消息——来自那浪漫与迷人的地下世界！他们的双唇因爱的亲吻而殷红；在那无形的、看不见的爱之蒸馏器里，大地的阴暗与潮湿被转化成了温暖、色彩与光影。是啊，这些亲爱的小孩儿们，展开他们柔软的绿色卷轴，高声诵读着关于谦逊与美的颂歌，其鼓舞人心，一如那群星为冠的夜。而我梯田石墙上的每一条裂缝，似乎都在呼吸着一条甘美、光明与爱的消息。

「你们可知道那段关于迷人的女神拉比娅的轶事么？那是阿塔尔在他的《苏菲神秘主义者与圣徒列传》（*Biographies of Sufi Mystics and Saints*）里所讲述的。便是这一段：有人问拉比娅是否恨魔鬼，她答道：『不恨。』又问她为何不恨，她说：『我既已沉醉于爱，便无暇去恨。』如今，我那梯田与田野上的一切居民，似乎都在回应他们女神这句崇高的情思。空气与阳光——不，连岩石本身——都浸透了它。看哪，那边巨石上的裂缝，似乎正与我梯田石墙上的缺口同病相怜：这一处的仙客来叶子，正向着那一处的仙客来花朵行色兰（salaam）之礼。啊，这些梯田定能叫美国博物学家梭罗（Thoreau）满心欢喜。他再不能指望有比这缺口更多的石墙了。可是请留意，这些都不是阴暗、丑陋、空洞、无望的裂缝。它们每一个的背后都潜藏着一个秘密。在那些壁龛的深处，我能看见那些诗人的半身像——正是他们写下了这些美丽的野花此刻正为我诵读的诗篇。是的，作者们已经死了，而我此刻所看见的，是他们风月之恋的花。这些是他们拥抱的子嗣，是他们爱之甘露的结晶。是的，这单独而简单的一桩爱的举动，便生出无穷无尽的花朵，来庆贺那有限的、外在形相的死亡，以及生命那永恒不灭的本质。在彻底的降服之中，方有永恒之物的神性。这便是东方神秘诗人的主调。而我倾向于相信，在所有歌者之中，正是他们把爱之歌唱得最美。与这些梯田的美丽孩子们一道漫游于田野之间，我不知是什么把我引向了法里德（al-Farid）——阿拉伯唯一一位「欲爱与神秘」交融的诗人，他那连绵不绝的韵脚有一种四季常新的魅力。或许是这般诗句——

『一切美者，因她升起而愈美，

　　　　一切甘甜，因她在场而愈甘；
　　　　而她所遇见的每一种美的形相，
　　　　她只消一句低语，便附在其耳旁：

『「啊，莫让你那奇妙的魅力欺骗了你；
　　　　它们不过是向她暂借了片刻；
　　　　你那外在的仪容与可爱反将令你悲伤，
　　　　若她在你灵魂的最深处不曾微笑。」

『一切色彩、形相，彼此交融，
　　　　都织在她那「太一之机」（Loom of Unity）上；
　　　　因为在一切分殊之中，唯她是一，
　　　　唯她绝对，唯她自由。』

「如今，我要带你去看一处最为奇巧、发人深省的景象。看那一小簇雏菊，挤在芋头（colocasia）舒展的叶子底下、围着那株孤零零的银莲花。这是卡西亚科伯爵夫人（Countess Casiacole）府上的一场盛会，这些便是簇拥着当日名媛的初入社交界的新人。可是，倘若它们自觉身价，倘若它们知道它们那位偶像——那炫耀着人缘之朱红冠冕的——其生命之纯粹精华并不比它们之中任何一个更多、甚至更少，它们还会如此簇拥么？可是且让那位名媛站在那里、享受她的时辰罢；明天，那犁田人就要来了。

===Original===
“The sage, with its spikes of greyish blue flowers, its fibrous, velvety leaves, its strong, pungent perfume, which is neither squandered nor repressed, is the stoic of my native terraces. It responds generously to the personal touch, and serves the Lebanonese, rich and poor alike, with a little luxury. Ay, who of us, wandering on foreign strands, does not remember the warm foot-bath, perfumed with sage leaves, his mother used to give him before going to bed? Our dear mothers!”—And here, Khalid goes in raptures and tears about his sorry experience in Baalbek and the anguish and sorrow of his poor mother. “But while I stand,” he continues, “let me be like the sage, a live-oak among shrubs, indifferent as the oak or pine to the winds and storms. And as the sun is setting, find you no solace in the thought, O Khalid, that some angel herb-gatherer will preserve the perfume in your leaves, to refresh therewith in other worlds your dear poor mother?

“My native terraces are rich with faith and love, luxuriant with the life divine and the wondrous symbols thereof. And the grass here is not cut and trimmed as in the artificial gardens and the cold dull lawns of city folk, whose love for Nature is either an experiment, a sport, a business, or a fad. ‘A dilettantism in Nature is barren and unworthy,’ says Emerson. But of all the lovers of Nature, the children are the least dilettanteish. And every day here I see a proof of this. Behold them wading to their knees in that lusty grass, hunting the classic lotus with which to deck their olive branches for the high mass and ceremony of Palm Sunday. But alas, my lusty grass and my beautiful wild flowers do not enjoy the morning of Spring. Here, the ploughman comes, carrying his long plough and goad on his shoulder, and with him his wife lugging the yoke and his boy leading the oxen. Alas, the sun shall not set on these bright, glowing, green terraces, whose walls are very ramparts of flowers. There, the boy with his scythe is paving the way for his father’s plough; the grass is mowed and given to the oxen as a bribe to do the ugly business. And all for the sake of the ugly mulberries, which are cultivated for the ugly silkworms. Come, let us to the heath, where the hiss of the scythe and the ‘ho-back’ and ‘oho’ of the ploughman are not heard.

“But let us swing from the road. Come, the hedges of Nature are not as impassable as the hedges of man. Through these scrub oaks and wild pears, between this tangle of thickets, over the clematis and blackberry bush—and here we are under the pines, the lofty and majestic pines. How different are these natural hedges, growing in wild disorder, from the ugly cactus fences with which my neighbours choose to shut in their homes, and even their souls. But my business now is not with them. There are my friends the children again gathering the pine-needles of last summer for lighting the fire of the silkworm nursery. And down that narrow footpath, meandering around the boulders and disappearing among the thickets, see what big loads of brushwood are moving towards us. Beneath them my swarthy and hardy peasants are plodding up the hill asweat and athirst. When I first descended to the wadi, one such load of brushwood emerging suddenly from behind a cliff surprised and frightened me. But soon I was reminded of the moving forest in *Macbeth*. The man bowed beneath the load was hidden from view, and the boy directly behind was sweating under a load as big as that of his father. ‘*Awafy!*’ (Allah give you strength), I said, greeting them. ‘And increase of health to you,’ they replied. I then asked the boy how far down do they have to go for their brushwood, and laying down his load on a stone to rest, he points below, saying, ‘Here, near the river.’ But this ‘Here, near the river’ is more than four hours’ walk from the village.—Allah preserve you in your strength, my Brothers. And they pass along, plodding slowly under their overshadowing burdens. A hard-hearted Naturalist, who goes so deep into Nature as to be far from the vital core even as the dilettante, might not have any sympathy to throw away on such occasions. But of what good is the love of Nature that consists only in classification and dissection? I carry no notebook with me when I go down the wadi or out into the fields. I am content if I bring back a few impressions of some reassuring instance of faith, a few pictures, and an armful of wild flowers and odoriferous shrubs. Let the learned manual maker concern himself with the facts; he is content with jotting down in his notebook the names and lineage of every insect and every herb.

“But Man? What is he to these scientific Naturalists? If they meet a stranger on the road, they pass him by, their eyes intent on the breviary of Nature, somewhat after the fashion of my priests, who are fond of praying in the open-air at sundown. No, I do not have to prove to my Brothers that my love of Nature is but second to my love of life. I am interested in my fellow men as in my fellow trees and flowers. ‘The beauty of Nature,’ Emerson again, ‘must always seem unreal and mocking until the landscape has human figures, that are as good as itself.’ And ’tis well, if they are but half as good. To me, the discovery of a woodman in the wadi were as pleasing as the discovery of a woodchuck or a woodswallow or a woodbine. For in the soul of the woodman is a song, I muse, as sweet as the rhythmic strains of the goldfinch, if it could be evoked. But the soul plodding up the hill under its heavy overshadowing burden, what breath has it left for song? The man bowed beneath the load, the soul bowed beneath the man! Alas, I seem to behold but moving burdens in my country. And yet, my swarthy and shrunken, but firm-fibred people plod along, content, patient, meek; and when they reach the summit of the hill with their crushing burdens, they still have breath enough to troll a favourite ditty or serenade the night.

‘I come to thee, O Night,

					I’m at thy feet;

					I can not see, O Night,

					But thy breath is sweet.’

“And so is the breath of the pines. Here, the air is surcharged with perfume. In it floats the aromatic soul of many a flower. But the perfume-soul of the pines seems to tower over all others, just as its material shape lifts its artistic head over the oak, the cercis, and the terabinth. And though tall and stately, my native pines are not forbidding. They are so pruned that the snags serve as a most convenient ladder. Such was my pleasure mounting for the green cones, the salted piñons of which are delicious. But I confess they seem to stick in the stomach as the pitch of the cones sticks on the hands. This, however, though it remains for days, works no evil; but the piñons in the stomach, and the stomach on the nerves—that is a different question.

“The only pines I have seen in the United States are those in front of Emerson’s house in Concord; but compared with my native trees, they are scrubby and mean. These pine parasols under which I lay me, forgiving and forgetting, are fit for the gods. And although closely planted, they grow and flourish without much ado. I have seen spots not exceeding a few hundred square feet holding over thirty trees, and withal stout and lusty and towering. Indeed, the floor of the Tent seems too narrow at times for its crowded guests; but beneath the surface there is room for every root, and over it, the sky is broad enough for all.

“Ah, the bewildering vistas through the variegated pillars, taking in a strip of sea here, a mountain peak there, have an air of enchantment from which no human formula can release a pilgrim-soul. They remind me—no; they can not remind me of anything more imposing. But when I was visiting the great Mosques of Cairo I was reminded of them. Yes, the pine forests are the great mosques of Nature. And for art-lovers, what perennial beauty of an antique art is here. These majestic pillars arched with foliage, propping a light-green ceiling, from which cones hang in pairs and in clusters, and through which curiously shaped clouds can be seen moving in a cerulean sky; and at night, instead of the clouds, the stars—the distant, twinkling, white and blue stars—what to these are the decorations in the ancient mosques? There, the baroques, the arabesques, the colourings gorgeous, are dead, at least inanimate; here, they palpitate with life. The moving, swelling, flaming, flowing life is mystically interwoven in the evergreen ceiling and the stately colonnades. Ay, even the horizon yonder, with its planets and constellations rising and setting ever, is a part of the ceiling decoration.

“Here in this grand Mosque of Nature, I read my own Koran. I, Khalid, a Bedouin in the desert of life, a vagabond on the highway of thought, I come to this glorious Mosque, the only place of worship open to me, to heal my broken soul in the perfumed atmosphere of its celestial vistas. The mihrabs here are not in this direction nor in that. But whereso one turns there are niches in which the living spirit of Allah is ever present. Here, then, I prostrate me and read a few Chapters of **my** Holy Book. After which I resign myself to my eternal Mother and the soft western breezes lull me asleep. Yea, and even like my poor brother Muslim sleeping on his hair-mat in a dark corner of his airy Mosque, I dream my dream of contentment and resignation and love.

“See the ploughman strutting home, his goad in his hand, his plough on his shoulder, as if he had done his duty. Allah be praised, the flowers in the terrace-walls are secure. That is why, I believe, my American brother Thoreau liked walls with many gaps in them. The sweet wild daughters of Spring can live therein their natural life without being molested by the scythe or the plough. Allah be praised a hundred times and one.”

===Chinese===
「鼠尾草，以其灰蓝色花穗、纤维质的天鹅绒般叶片、其浓烈辛锐而既不挥霍也不压抑的香气，是我乡梯田上的斯多葛派哲人。它对人的抚摸慷慨回应，并以一点小小的奢华，服务于黎巴嫩人——无论贫富。是啊，我们之中漂泊异乡的人，谁不记得母亲在就寝前为咱们备的那盆漂着鼠尾草叶的温热足浴呢？我们亲爱的母亲们！」——说到此处，哈立德为他自己在巴勒贝克那不堪回首的遭遇、为他那可怜母亲的痛苦与悲伤，一时神驰泪下。「然而我既站立着，」他续道，「便让我像那鼠尾草罢，做灌木中的常青橡，如橡如松，对风雨无动于衷。且趁夕阳西下，想一想这层意思，哈立德啊，岂不令人宽慰：某位采草天使将把你叶中的香气保存下来，好在别的世界里，以此为你那亲爱而可怜的母亲提神？

「我乡的梯田，丰饶于信心与爱，繁茂于神圣的生命及其奇妙象征。这里的草，并不像那些城里人的人工花园与冷清呆板的草坪那样地被修剪裁割——那些城里人对自然的所谓爱，不过是一场试验、一种消遣、一桩买卖、或一时的怪癖。『对自然抱着半吊子的态度，是贫瘠而可鄙的，』爱默生（Emerson）如是说。然而在一切自然的爱好者之中，孩子们最少半吊子气。这一点我每天在这里都看得到明证。看他们没膝地蹚过那片茁壮的青草，寻觅那古典的莲花，用来装饰他们的橄榄枝，好迎接棕枝主日（Palm Sunday）那盛大的弥撒与典礼。然而可叹，我那茁壮的青草、我那美丽的野花，却享受不到春日的早晨。这里，犁田人来了，肩上扛着长犁与刺棍，身旁是他那扛着轭的妻子，还有他那个牵着牛的孩子。可叹，那阳光不会落在这片光彩焕发的绿色梯田之上——它们的石墙简直是用花筑成的城墙。那边，那男孩挥着他的镰刀，为他父亲的犁开路；青草被割倒，喂给了牛，作为它们去干那桩丑活的贿赂。而这一切，全是为了那丑陋的桑葚，而桑葚又是为那丑陋的蚕而种的。来罢，让我们到那荒原上去，那里听不到镰刀的嘶响，也听不到犁田人『嗬—驾』『哦—嗬』的吆喝。

「可让我们从这条道上荡开去罢。来，自然的藩篱并不像人为的藩篱那样不可逾越。穿过这些矮橡与野梨，钻过这片纠结的灌木丛，越过这些铁线莲与黑莓丛——我们便到了松林之下，到了那些高耸而庄严的松树之下。这些野生野长、乱中生序的天然藩篱，与我的邻居们用以圈禁他们的家园、甚至圈禁他们的灵魂的那种丑陋的仙人掌篱笆，有何等不同。但我此刻要说的不是他们。那边又有我的小朋友们，在捡拾去年夏天的松针，好为蚕房生火。而沿着那条窄窄的、绕过巨石、隐没于灌木丛中的小径，看哪，有好大一捆一捆的柴薪正朝我们这边移动。它们底下，是我那些黝黑而强健的农人，汗流浃背、口渴难当地向山上跋涉。我头一次下到那旱谷（wadi）时，有那样一大捆柴薪忽然从一座悬崖背后转出来，倒把我吓了一跳。但随即我便想起了《麦克白》（*Macbeth*）里那座移动的森林。那压在重担之下、弓着身子的汉子完全看不见，紧随其后的男孩，所背的担子与他父亲的一般大，也是大汗淋漓。『*Awafy!*（真主赐你力量！）』我这样向他们招呼道。『愿你康健日增。』他们答道。我于是问那男孩，他们要下到多远的地方才能打到柴；他把担子搁在一块石头上歇息，朝下面一指，说：『这儿，靠河那块儿。』然而这『这儿，靠河那块儿』，离村子足有四个多钟头的脚程。——真主在你们的力量中保佑你们，我的兄弟们。他们就这样缓缓地，在笼罩头顶的重担之下，向前跋涉而去。一位铁石心肠的博物学家，钻研自然钻得那样深，以至于远离了生机之核——与那半吊子并无二致——在这种场合或许并无同情可掷。但那种只由分类与解剖所构成的对自然的爱，又有何益处？我下旱谷或下田野时，并不携带笔记本。我若能带回几则关于某种令人宽慰的信心实例的印象、几幅图景、以及一抱野花与芳香的灌木，便已知足。让那位博学的「手册编纂者」去操心那些事实罢；他满足于在笔记本里草草记下每一只虫、每一株草的名字与谱系。

「可是人呢？在这些科学的博物学家眼中，人又算什么？倘若他们在路上遇见一个陌生人，他们径自走过，眼睛死盯着他们那本大自然的日课经——倒颇有几分像我的那些神父，他们喜欢在日落时的露天之下祈祷。不，我无须向我那班兄弟们证明：我对自然之爱，仅次于我对生命之爱。我对同类的兴趣，一如我对同类树木花朵的兴趣。『自然之美，』又是爱默生之言，『在风景里有了与之同样美好的人物之前，必定始终显得虚幻而嘲弄。』而倘若它们能有那一半的好，也就很好了。于我而言，在旱谷里发现一个樵夫，其乐正与发现一只旱獭、一只林燕、或一株忍冬相同。因为我冥想着，在那樵夫的灵魂里，藏着一支歌，倘若能被唤起，其甜美一如金雀那富有节奏的鸣啭。然而那在沉重的、笼罩头顶的重担之下向山上跋涉的灵魂，哪里还有气息留给了歌唱？那弓身于重担之下的，是人；而弓身于此人之下的，是灵魂！可叹，在我的国度里，我所见的似乎只是会移动的重担。然而我那些黝黑干瘦、却筋骨坚韧的人民，仍这般地跋涉向前——知足、忍耐、柔顺；待他们扛着那压死人的重担攀上山巅之时，他们竟还有足够的气息，高歌一支心爱的小调，或向黑夜献上一支小夜曲——

『我来到你这里，啊，黑夜，
　　　　我在你脚前；
　　　　我看不见，啊，黑夜，
　　　　但你的气息甘甜。』

「而松树的气息也是如此。此处，空气中饱含着香气。许多花的芳香之魂漂浮其中。然而那松树的香魂，似乎凌驾于众香之上，正如同它那有形之躯，把它的艺术之头，昂然地举在橡树、紫荆（cercis）与笃薷香（terabinth）之上。尽管我乡的松树高大庄严，却并不拒人。它们被修剪得那些残桩恰好成了一架最为便利的梯子。我当年之乐，便是攀缘其上去摘那绿色的松果，那盐渍的松子可谓美味。但我得承认，它们似乎粘在胃里，一如松果上的松脂粘在手上。然而这后者，虽能存留数日，却并无大碍；至于前者——松子之于胃，胃之于神经——那便另当别论了。

「我在合众国所见过的唯一的松树，乃是康科德（Concord）爱默生宅前的那几棵；但与我乡的树相比，它们既矮小又寒酸。我如今所卧的这些松树华盖之下，宽恕着、忘却着，它们简直配得上诸神。它们虽种得密，却无须怎样照料便生长繁茂。我见过不过几百平方英尺的一块地，竟能容下三十余株，且株株粗壮、茁壮、高耸。诚然，这座「帐篷」之地有时对其济济的宾客而言似乎过于狭窄；然而地面之下，每一条根都有它的余地，而地面之上，那片天空也足够为众株所共。

「啊，那些透过斑斓立柱望出去的令人目眩的远景——这边一带海，那边一座山峰——有一种迷人的气息，没有任何人为的程式能将一个朝圣者的灵魂从中解脱。它们令我想起——不；它们不能令我再想起任何更为恢宏之物了。然而，当我在开罗（Cairo）参观那些伟大的清真寺（Mosques）之时，我却由它们想起了那些松林。是的，松林便是大自然的大清真寺。对艺术爱好者而言，此处有一种古朴艺术的不朽之美。这些被繁叶拱成穹顶的庄严立柱，撑起一片浅绿色的藻井，松果成双成簇地自其上垂下，透过它能望见奇形怪状的云朵在湛蓝的天空中移动；而到了夜间，代云而起的，是群星——那遥远的、闪烁的、白色与蓝色的群星——古清真寺里的种种装饰，比起它们来又算得了什么呢？在那里，那些怪诞饰、阿拉伯式花纹、华丽的色彩，是死的，至少是无生命的；在这里，它们却因生命而悸动。那流动的、涨涌的、燃烧的、奔涌的生命，神秘地交织在那常绿的穹顶与庄严的柱廊之中。是啊，连那边那道地平线，连同其上升下落不息的行星与星座，也是这片藻井装饰的一部分。

「在这座大自然的大清真寺里，我诵读我自己的《古兰经》（Koran）。我，哈立德，生命沙漠中的一个贝都因人，思想大道上的一个流浪者，我来到这座光荣的清真寺——这是向我敞开的唯一的礼拜之所——好让它那天界远景的芬芳气息，治愈我破碎的灵魂。这里的米哈拉布（mihrabs，即壁龛）不在此一方向，也不在彼一方向。然而无论转向何方，都有真主（Allah）的活灵长存的壁龛。那么，就在这里，我俯伏叩拜，诵读几章**我自己的**圣典。之后，我把自己交托给我那永恒的母亲，让柔和的西风将我哄入梦乡。是啊，便如我那位可怜的穆斯林兄弟，在他那通风的清真寺的一处幽暗角落里，睡在他的鬃毛垫子上，我也做起我的梦——一场知足、顺从与爱的梦。

「看那犁田人昂首阔步地归家，一手执刺棍，一肩扛长犁，仿佛已尽了他的本分。赞美真主，梯田石墙里的花算是保住了。我想，这便是我那位美国兄弟梭罗之所以喜欢缺口多的石墙的缘故了。春日那些甘甜的野女儿，可以在其中过它们天然的生活，免遭镰刀与犁的侵扰。赞美真主，一百次又一遍。」

## IX / IX

Signs of the Hermit / 隐士的征兆

===Original===
Although we claim some knowledge of the Lebanon mountains, having landed there in our journey earthward, and having since then, our limbs waxing firm and strong, made many a journey through them, we could not, after developing, through many readings, Khalid’s spiritual films, identify them with the vicinage which he made his Kaaba. On what hill, in what wadi, under what pines did he ruminate and extravagate, we could not from these idealised pictures ascertain. For a spiritual film is other than a photographic one. A poet’s lens is endowed with a seeing eye, an insight, and a faculty to choose and compose. Hence the difficulty in tracing the footsteps of Fancy﻿—in locating its cave, its nest, or its Kaaba. His pine-mosque we could find anywhere, at any altitude; his vineyards, too, and his glades; for our mountain scenery, its beauty alternating between the placid and the rugged﻿—the tame terrace soil and the wild, forbidding majesty﻿—is allwhere almost the same. But where in these rocky and cavernous recesses of the world can we today find the ancient Lebanon troglodyte, whom Khalid has seen, and visited in his hut, and even talked with? It is this that forces us to seek his diggings, to trace, if possible, his footsteps.

In the K.L. MS., as we have once remarked and more than once hinted, we find much that is unduly inflated, truly Oriental; much that is platitudinous, ludicrous, which we have suppressed. But never could we question the Author’s veracity and sincerity of purpose. Whether he crawled like a zoophyte, soared like an eagle, or fought, like Ali, the giants of the lower world, he is genuine, and ofttimes amusingly truthful. But the many questionable pages on this curious subject of the eremite, what are we to do with them? If they are imaginary, there is too much in this Book against quackery to daunt us. And yet, if Khalid has found the troglodyte, whom we thought to be an extinct species, he should have left us a few legends about it.

We have visited the ancient caverns of the Lebanon troglodytes in the cliffs overhanging the river of Wadi Kadeesha, and found nothing there but blind bats, and mosses, and dreary vacuity. No, not a vestage of the fossil is there, not a skull, not a shinbone. We have also inquired in the monasteries near the Cedars, and we were frankly told that no monk today fancies such a life. And if he did, he would not give his brother monks the trouble of carrying his daily bread to a cave in those forbidden cliffs. And yet, Simeon Stylites, he of the Pillar, who remained for thirty years perched on the top of it, was a Syrian shepherd. But who of his descendants today would as much as pass one night on the top of that pillar? Curious eleemosynary phases of our monkish system, these modern times reveal.

===Chinese===
虽则我们自称对黎巴嫩的群山略有所知——我们降生于世时便落在那里，此后筋骨渐硬、渐壮，又曾数度穿行其间——可是在我们一遍遍诵读、显影了哈立德的那些灵性底片（spiritual films）之后，却仍无从将它们辨认为他所奉为克尔白（Kaaba）的那片乡野。他究竟在哪一座山丘、哪一道旱谷、哪一片松林之下沉思冥想、神游八方，我们单凭这些理想化的画图，无从考订。因为灵性底片不同于照相底片。诗人的透镜，赋有一只观看的眼、一种洞见、一具取舍与组织的才能。故而追寻「想象」的足迹之难——难在寻觅它的洞穴、它的窠巢、或它的克尔白。他的松林清真寺（pine-mosque），无论何处、无论何种海拔，我们都找得到；他的葡萄园，他的林间空地，亦复如是；因为我们这座山的景色，其美在宁静与粗砺之间往复——在驯顺的梯田土与荒蛮逼人的雄浑之间往复——几乎处处相同。然而，在这世界嶙峋多洞的幽深处，今日我们又能去何处寻觅那古老的黎巴嫩穴居人（troglodyte）——那哈立德所见过的、登门拜访过的、甚至与之交谈过的？正是这一点，逼着我们去寻觅他的居处，去追踪——若有可能——他的足迹。

在赫迪夫图书馆手稿（K.L. MS.）里，正如我们曾说过的、又不止一次暗示过的，我们见到许多过度浮夸、确乎东方气派的东西；也有许多陈腐可笑之处，已为我们所删。但我们绝不能怀疑作者的诚实与其用意的真挚。无论他是像一棵植虫似的匍匐，像一头鹰似的翱翔，还是像阿里（Ali）那样与下界的巨人们搏斗，他都是真切的，且往往真得令人发噱。可是关于「隐士」这一古怪题目，那许多可疑的篇幅，我们该拿它们怎么办呢？倘若出自虚构，则本书之中反对招摇撞骗之处已多到足以壮我们的胆。话虽如此，倘若哈立德当真找着了我们本以为早已绝种的穴居人，他总该给我们留下几则关于此物的传说才对。

我们曾探访过瓦迪卡迪沙河（Wadi Kadeesha）上方悬崖间黎巴嫩穴居人的那些古洞穴，所见的不过是失明的蝙蝠、苔藓，与一片凄凉的空寂。不，连这块化石的一点踪影也没有，没有一颗头骨，没有一根胫骨。我们也曾在黎巴嫩雪松（Cedars of Lebanon）附近的修道院里打听，人家坦白相告：今日已无僧侣好此生活。便是有，他也不肯劳烦同门的弟兄，把每日的口粮送到那等禁地的崖穴里去。然而，柱顶修士西缅（Simeon Stylites）——那位在柱顶上一栖就是三十年的——原是叙利亚的一名牧人。可今日他的子孙之中，有谁肯在那柱顶上捱过一夜呢？我们这个僧侣制度里靠布施维生的种种古怪面貌，在这现代时日里一一显露。

===Original===
On our way from a journey to the Cedars, while engaged in the present Work, we passed through a pine forest, in which were some tangled bushes of the clematis. The muleteer stops near one of these and stoops to reach something he had seen therein. No treasure-trove, alas, as he supposed; but merely a book for which he lacerated his hands and which he cursed and handed to us, saying, “This must be the breviary of some monk.”

No, it was an English book, and of American origin, and of a kind quite rare in America. Indeed, here were a find and surprise as agreeable as Khalid’s sweetbrier bush. Henry Thoreau’s *Week*! What a miracle of chance. Whose this mutilated copy of the *Week*, we thought? Who in these mountains, having been in America, took more interest in the Dreamer of Walden Woods than in peddling and trading? We walk our mule, looking about in vague, restless surprise, as if seeking in the woods a lost companion, and lo, we reach a monarch pine on which is carved the name of﻿—Khalid! This book, then, must be his; the name on the pine tree is surely his own; we know his hand as well as his turn of mind. But who can say if this be his Kaaba, this his pine-mosque? Might he not only have passed through these glades to other parts? Signs, indeed, are here of his feet and hands, if not of his tent-pegs. And what signifies his stay? No matter how long he might have put up here, it is but a passage, deeply considered: like Thoreau’s passage through Walden woods, like Mohammad’s through the desert.

===Chinese===
我们自一趟雪松之旅返回、正在从事眼下这部著作的路上，穿过了一片松林，林间丛生着几蓬纠缠不清的铁线莲（clematis）。赶骡人在其中一蓬跟前停下，弯下腰去够他瞧见的东西。唉，并非他所想的那等意外之财，不过是一本书罢了——他为此划破了双手，一面咒骂着一面递到我们手里，说道：「这准是哪位僧侣的日课经（breviary）。」

不，那是一本英文书，出自美国，且是那种在美国也颇罕见的版本。说真的，此处的发现与惊喜，正与哈立德那丛野蔷薇（sweetbrier bush）一样可人。亨利·梭罗（Henry Thoreau）的《一周》（*Week*）！这是何等偶然的奇迹。我们心想：这本残缺的《一周》是谁的呢？在这些山里，有谁曾去过美国，竟对瓦尔登湖林（Walden Woods）的梦者比对自己的货郎营生更上心？我们骑着骡子往前走，满怀一种说不清、坐不安的惊奇，四处张望，仿佛在林中寻觅一位走散的同伴，忽而，我们走到一株王者之松前，那上面刻着一个名字——哈立德！那么，这本书必是他的了；松树上的名字，必是他亲笔；他的笔迹与他的心思，我们都熟。可谁能断言，此处便是他的克尔白，此处便是他的松林清真寺？他岂不也可能只是路过这些林间空地，往别处去了？此处确有他手脚的痕迹，纵然未必有他帐篷的桩子。而他的逗留又算得了什么？无论他在此盘桓多久，深究起来，不过是途经一站：一如梭罗之穿过瓦尔登湖林，一如穆罕默德（Mohammad）之穿过沙漠。

===Original===
This leisure hour is the nipple of the soul. And fortunate they who are not artificially suckled, who know this hour no matter how brief, who get their nipple at the right time. If they do not, no pabulum ever after, will their indurated tissues assimilate. Do you wonder why the world is full of crusty souls? and why to them this infant hour, this suckling while, is so repugnant? But we must not intrude more of such remarks about mankind. Whether rightly suckled or not, we manage to live; but whether we do so marmot-like or Maronite-like, is not the question here to be considered. To pray for your bread or to burrow in the earth for it, is it not the same with most people? Given a missionary with a Bible in his hip-pocket or a peasant with a load of brushwood on his back and the same gastric coefficient, and you will have in either case a resulting expansion for six feet of coffin ground and a fraction of Allah’s mercy. Our poor missionary, is it worth while to cross the seas for this? Marmot-like or Maronite-like﻿—but soft you know! Here is our peasant with his overshadowing load of brushwood. And there is another, and another. They are carrying fuel to the lime-pit ahead of us yonder. What brow-sweat, what time, what fire, what suffering and patient toil, the lime-washing, or mere liming, of our houses and sepulchres, requires. That cone structure there, that artificial volcano, with its crackling, flaming bowels and its fuliginous, coruscating crater, must our hardy peasants feed continually for twenty days and nights.

===Chinese===
这闲暇的一刻，乃是灵魂的乳头。那些不曾被人造地哺育过的，是有福的；无论这一刻多么短促，懂得它的，是有福的；在恰当的时候得到他们的乳头的，是有福的。倘若得不到，此后任是何等养料，他们那已变硬的组织都再也吸收不进了。你可奇怪，这世上为何满是脾气乖戾的人？又为何对他们而言，这婴儿般的一刻、这哺乳的片刻，是如此可厌？但我们不可再多对人类发这等议论了。无论是否被哺育得当，我们总归活得下去；至于我们活得是旱獭式还是马龙派式，则非此处所要讨论的问题。为你的面包祈祷，或在土里刨掘它，于大多数人而言，岂不是一回事？给你一位屁股兜里揣着《圣经》的传教士，或一位背上驮着一捆柴火的农夫，再给他们相同的肠胃系数，那么你在这两种情形之下所得的结果，都不过是六英尺棺地的一处扩展，外加真主（Allah）怜悯的一星半点。我们那位可怜的传教士，漂洋过海就为这个，值得么？旱獭式也罢，马龙派式也罢——可是且慢，你瞧！我们那位农夫来了，背上一捆遮天蔽日的柴火。那边又是一个，又一个。他们正把燃料送到前头那座石灰窑去。我们房屋与坟茔的刷灰——便是单单的抹灰——需要多少汗、多少时日、多少烈火、多少苦难与坚忍的劳作。那边那座锥形的构筑、那座人造的火山，腹中噼啪作响、烈焰熊熊，火山口浓烟滚滚、火星飞溅——我们那些健壮的农人，须得接连二十个日夜不停地喂它。

===Original===
But the book and the name on the pine, we would know more of these signs, if possible. And so, we visit the labourers of the kiln. They are yodeling, the while they work, and jesting and laughing. The stokers, with flaming, swollen eyes, their tawny complexion waxing a brilliant bronze, their sweat making golden furrows therein, with their pikes and pitchforks busy, are terribly magnificent to behold. Here be men who would destroy Bastilles for you, if it were nominated in the bond. And there is the monk-foreman﻿—the kiln is of the monastery’s estate﻿—reading his breviary while the lime is in making. Indeed, these sodalities of the Lebanons are not what their vows and ascetic theologies would make them. No lean-jowled, hungry-looking devotees, living in exiguity and droning in exinanition their prayers﻿—not by any means. Their fleshpots are not a few, and their table is a marvel of ascetism! And why not, if their fat estates﻿—three-quarter of the lands here is held in mortmain by the clergy﻿—can yield anything, from silk cocoons to lime-pits? They will clothe you in silk at least; they will lime-wash your homes and sepulchres, if they cannot lime-wash anything else. Thanks to them so long as they keep some reminiscence of business in their heads to keep the Devil out of it.

The monk-foreman is reading with one eye and watching with the other. “Work,” cries he, “every minute wasted is stolen from the abbey. And whoso steals, look in the pit: its fire is nothing compared with Jahannam.” And the argument serves its purpose. The labourers hurry hither and thither, bringing brushwood near; the first stoker pitches to the second, the second to the third, and he feeds the flaming, smoking, coruscating volcano. “*Yallah!*” (Keep it up) exclaims the monk-foreman. “Burn the devil’s creed,” cries one. “Burn hell,” cries another. And thus jesting in earnest, mightily working and enduring, they burn the mountains into lime, they make the very rocks yield somewhat.﻿—Strength and blessings, brothers.

===Chinese===
但是，那本书与松树上的名字，若有可能，我们还想多知道些这些征兆。于是我们去走访窑上的工人们。他们一边干活一边哼着山歌号子，又说又笑。司炉们，眼里冒着火、胀得通红，那张黄褐色的脸成了一层光亮的青铜，汗水在其上犁出一道道金色的沟，手里忙的是铁钎与干草叉，看上去威猛得可怖。这儿尽是些汉子，倘若契约上写明了，他们为你拆掉巴士底狱（Bastille）都办得到。那边还有那位僧侣工头——这窑原是修道院的产业——石灰烧制之际，他正读着他的日课经。说真的，黎巴嫩这些修道会（sodalities），并非他们所发的愿、他们那套苦行的神学所要造就的样子。绝不是什么两腮瘦削、面带饥色的信徒，住在窘陋里、在一身空乏中嗡嗡地诵他们的经——绝非如此。他们的肉锅不在少数，他们的餐桌简直是苦行主义的一大奇观！又怎能不然呢，倘若他们丰厚的产业——此地四分之三的土地都归教士的永业权（mortmain）所有——能出产一切，从蚕茧到石灰窑？至少，他们能用丝绸来装扮你；他们会给你的房屋和坟茔抹灰，倘若别的东西他们抹不了的话。感谢他们罢，只要他们脑中还留着一点做生意的记忆，能把魔鬼拒之门外。

那位僧侣工头，一只眼读着经，另一只眼盯着工地。「干活，」他喊道，「每浪费一分钟，都是从修道院偷来的。谁要偷，就瞧瞧那窑里罢：它的火比起火狱（Jahannam）来，算不得什么。」这番道理果然奏效。工人们东奔西走，把柴火送到近前；头一个司炉递给第二个，第二个递给第三个，第三个便去喂那座烈焰腾腾、浓烟滚滚、火星飞溅的火山。「*Yallah*！（雅拉，加把劲）」僧侣工头嚷道。「烧掉魔鬼的信条，」一个喊。「烧掉地狱，」另一个喊。他们就这样半是认真半是戏谑，一边卖力地干活、坚忍地熬着，一边把山烧成了石灰，逼着顽石也吐出一点东西来。——力量与祝福，弟兄们。

===Original===
After the usual inquiry of whence and whither, his monkship offers the snuffbox. “No? roll you, then, a cigarette,” taking out a plush pouch containing a mixture of the choicest native roots. These, we were told, are grown on the monastery’s estate. We speak of the cocoon products of the season.

“Beshrew the mulberries!” exclaims the monk. “We are turning all our estates into fruit orchards and orangeries. The cultivation of the silkworm is in itself an abomination. And while its income today is not as much as it was ten years ago, the expenditure has risen twofold. America is ruining our agriculture; and soon, I suppose, we have to send to China for labourers. Why, those who do not emigrate demand twice as much today for half the work they used to do five years ago; and those who return from America strut about like country gentlemen deploring the barrenness of their native soil.”

And one subject leading to another, for our monk is a glib talker, we come to the cheese-makers, the goatherds. “Even these honest rustics,” says he, “are becoming sophisticated (*mafsudin*). Their cheese is no longer what it was, nor is their faith. For Civilisation, passing by their huts in some shape or other, whispers in their ears something about cleverness and adulteration. And mistaking the one for the other, they abstract the butter from the milk and leave the verdigris in the utensils. This lust of gain is one of the diseases which come from Europe and America﻿—it is a plague which even the goatherd cannot escape. Why, do you know, wherever the cheese-monger goes these days ptomaine poison is certain to follow.”

===Chinese===
照例问过从何处来、往何处去之后，这位僧侣先生便递过鼻烟壶。「不吸？那你就自己卷一支烟罢，」说着掏出一只长毛绒烟袋，里头装着一种最上等的本地烟丝调制。据说，这都是修道院自家地里种的。我们便谈起本季的蚕茧出产。

「该死的桑树！」那僧侣叹道，「我们正把全部的田产改成果园和橘园。养蚕本身就是一桩可憎的事。今日它的进项还不及十年前，支出却翻了一倍。美国正在毁掉我们的农业；我看不久，我们非得到中国去请劳工不可。可不是么，那些不外移的，今天干的活只有五年前的一半，开口却要双倍的工钱；那些从美国回来的人，倒像乡下绅士一样神气活现地踱着步，慨叹他们故土的贫瘠。」

一个话题引出另一个话题——我们的僧侣本就是个能说会道的人——我们便谈到了做奶酪的、放羊的。「便这些老实巴交的乡下人，」他说，「也变得油滑了（*mafsudin*，即学坏了）。他们的奶酪不复当年，他们的信仰也复如是。因为『文明』以这种那种模样经过他们的茅舍，在他们耳边悄悄说了些关于精明与掺假的道理。于是把两者混为一谈，他们便把黄油从牛奶里抽走，却把铜绿留在器具里。这种贪欲，是从欧洲和美国传来的一种病——是一场连放羊人也逃不过的瘟疫。你可知道，如今卖奶酪的人走到哪里，尸碱毒素（ptomaine poison）就准跟到哪里。」

===Original===
“And why does not the Government interfere?” we ask.

“Because the Government,” replies our monk in a dry, droll air and gesture, “does not eat cheese.”

And the monks, we learned, do not have to buy it. For this, as well as their butter, olive oil, and wine, is made on their own estates, under their own supervision.

===Chinese===
「那么政府为何不干涉？」我们问。

「因为政府，」我们的僧侣以一副干巴巴、滑稽的口气与手势答道，「是不吃奶酪的。」

而我们得知，僧侣们也不必去买它。因为这东西，连同他们的黄油、橄榄油和酒，都是在他们自家的田产上、在他们自家的监督之下做出来的。

===Original===
“Yes,” he resumes, placing his breviary in his pocket and taking out the snuffbox; “not long ago one who lived in these parts﻿—a young man from Baalbek he was, and he had his booth in the pine forest yonder﻿—bought some cheese from one of these muleteer cheese-mongers, and after he had eaten of it fell sick. It chanced that I was passing by on my way to the abbey, when he was groaning and retching beneath that pine tree. It was the first time I saw that young man, and were I not passing by I know not what would have become of him. I helped him to the abbey, where he was ministered to by our physician, and he remained with us three days. He ate of our cheese and drank of our wine, and seemed to like both very much. And ever since, while he was here, he would come to the abbey with a basket or a tray of his own make﻿—he occupied himself in making wicker-baskets and trays﻿—and ask in exchange some of our cheese and olive oil. He was very intelligent, this fellow; his eyes sometimes were like the mouth of this pit, full of fire and smoke. But he was queer. The clock in him was not wound right﻿—he was always ahead or behind time, always complaining that we monks did not reckon time as he did. Nevertheless, I liked him much, and often would I bring him some of our cookery. But he never accepted anything without giving something in exchange.”

===Chinese===
「是啊，」他接着说，一面把日课经塞进口袋，一面掏出鼻烟壶；「不久前有一位住在这带地方的人——他是巴勒贝克来的年轻人，在远处那片松林里搭了个棚子——从一个赶骡卖奶酪的人那里买了些奶酪，吃下去就病倒了。恰巧我正往修道院去，路过那里，见他正在那棵松树下呻吟呕吐。那是我头一回见那年轻人，倘若我未曾路过，真不知他会落得什么下场。我把他扶到修道院，由我们的医师照看，他在我们这里住了三天。他吃了我们的奶酪，喝了我们的酒，似乎两样都很对他的胃口。自那以后，他在此地时，总带着一只篮子或一只盘子——都是他自己编的——到修道院来，求换一点我们的奶酪和橄榄油。他平日做的便是编柳条篮子和盘子。这人很聪明；他的眼睛有时像这座窑的口，满是火与烟。可是他古怪。他里头的那架钟上得不准——他不是走得太前就是落得太后，总抱怨我们这些僧侣计时不与他相同。尽管如此，我很喜欢他，常给他送些我们的饭菜。可他从不白受东西，总要拿点甚么来交换。」

===Original===
Unmistakable signs.

“And his black turban,” continues the monk, “over his long flowing hair made him look like our hermit.” (Strange coincidence!) “On your way here have you not stopped to visit the hermit? Not far from the abbey, on your right hand coming here, is the Hermitage.”

We remember passing a pretty cottage surrounded by a vineyard in that rocky wilderness; but who would mistake that for a troglodyte’s cave? “And this young man from Baalbek,” we ask, “how did he live in this forest?”

“Yonder,” points the monk, “he cleared and cleaned for himself a little space which he made his workshop. And up in the pines he constructed a platform, which he walled and covered with boughs. And when he was not working or walking, he would be there among the branches, either singing or asleep. I used to envy him that nest in the pines.”

===Chinese===
毫无疑问的征兆。

「还有他那条黑头巾，」那僧侣接着说，「裹在一头披散的长发上，叫人瞧着活像我们的隐士。」（何等奇巧的巧合！）「你们来的路上，可曾停下看过那位隐士？离修道院不远，你们来时右手边，便是那隐庐（Hermitage）。」

我们记起曾在那片乱石荒野里路过一所可爱的小屋，四周围着葡萄园；可谁会把它错认作穴居人的洞穴呢？「那么这位巴勒贝克来的年轻人，」我们问道，「在这林子里是怎么过活的？」

「在那边，」僧侣指着说，「他给自己清出一小块地，扫得干干净净，当作他的作坊。在松树高处他又搭起一座平台，用树枝圈起墙来、盖上顶。他不干活、不散步的时候，便在那枝桠间，不是唱歌便是睡觉。我从前常羡慕他在松林里的那个窠。」

===Original===
“And did he ever go to church?”

“He attended mass twice in our chapel, on Good Friday and on Easter Sunday, I think.”

“And did he visit the abbey often?”

“Only when he wanted cheese or olive oil.” (Shame, O Khalid!) “But he often repaired to the Hermitage. I went with him once to listen to his conversation with the Hermit. They often disagreed, but never quarrelled. I like that young man in spite of his oddities of thought, which savoured at times of infidelity. But he is honest, believe me; never tells a lie; and in a certain sense he is as pious as our Hermit, I think. Roll another cigarette.”

===Chinese===
「他可曾去过教堂？」

「他在我们的小堂里望过两次弥撒，一次是耶稣受难日（Good Friday），一次是复活节（Easter Sunday），若我没记错的话。」

「他常到修道院来么？」

「只在他想要奶酪或橄榄油的时候。」（羞哉，哈立德！）「不过他常往那隐庐去。我曾陪他去过一回，听他与那位隐士交谈。他们常常意见相左，却从不争吵。我喜欢这个年轻人，不顾他思想上那些有时近乎不敬神的古怪。但他是诚实的，信我；从不撒谎；从某种意义上说，我想他和我们的隐士一样虔诚。再卷一支烟罢。」

===Original===
“Thank you. And the Hermit, what is your opinion of him?”

“Well, h’m﻿—h’m﻿—go visit him. A good man he is, but very simple. And between us, he likes money too much. H’m, h’m, go visit him. If I were not engaged at present, I would accompany you thither.”

We thank our good monk and retrace our steps to the Hermitage, rolling meanwhile in our mind that awful remark about the Hermit’s love of money. Blindness and Plague! even the troglodyte loves and worships thee, thou silver Demiurge! We can not believe it. The grudges of monks against each other often reach darker and more fatal depths. Alas, if the faith of the cheese-monger is become adulterated, what shall we say of the faith of our monkhood? If the salt of the earth﻿—but not to the nunnery nor to the monkery, we go. Rather let us to the Hermitage, Reader, and with an honest heart; in earnest, not in sport.

===Chinese===
「多谢。至于那位隐士，您对他作何观感？」

「这个么，嗯——嗯——你们去看看他罢。他是个好人，只是十分单纯。咱们私下说，他太爱钱了。嗯，嗯，去看看他罢。若不是我此刻走不开，我便陪你们一同去了。」

我们谢过这位好僧侣，转身折回那隐庐去，一路上心里却翻来覆去地转着那句关于隐士贪财的可怖评语。盲目与瘟疫啊！便是穴居人也爱与崇拜你，你这银色的造化之神！我们不能相信。僧侣彼此间的嫌隙，往往能沉到更深、更致命的幽暗里去。唉，倘若卖奶酪的人的信仰都已掺了假，我们又该说我们僧侣界的信仰什么呢？倘若世上的盐都——但我们要去的，既不是女修道院，也不是男修道院。读者啊，不如让我们走向那隐庐去罢，怀着一颗诚实的心；认真地，而非游戏地。

## X / X

The Vineyard in the Kaaba / 克尔白中的葡萄园

===Original===
This, then, is the cave of our troglodyte!
