Allah be praised, even the hermits of the Lebanon mountains, like the prophets of America and other electric-age species, are subject to the laws of evolution. A cottage and chapel set in a vineyard, the most beautiful we have yet seen, looms up in this rocky wilderness like an oasis in a desert. For many miles around, the vicinage presents a volcanic aspect, wild, barren, howlingly dreary. At the foot of Mt. Sanneen in the east, beyond many ravines, are villages and verdure; and from the last terrace in the vineyard one overlooks the deep chasm which can boast of a rivulet in winter. But in the summer its nakedness is appalling. The sun turns its pocket inside out, so to speak, exposing its boulders, its little windrows of sands, and its dry ditches full of dead fish spawn. And the cold, rocky horizon, rising so high and near, shuts out the sea and hides from the Hermit the glory of the sundown. But we can behold its effects on Mt. Sanneen, on the clouds above us, on the glass casements in the villages far away. The mountains in the east are mantled with etherial lilac alternating with mauve; the clouds are touched with purple and gold; the casements in the distance are scintillating with mystical carbuncles: the sun is setting in the Mediterranean﻿—he is waving his farewell to the hills.

===Chinese===
原来这便是我们那位穴居人的洞穴！赞美真主（Allah），便是黎巴嫩（Lebanon）群山里的隐修士，也像美国的先知们以及其他电气时代的物种一样，逃不过进化的法则。一座小屋与一座小教堂，安座于一座葡萄园中——这是我们至今所见最美的葡萄园——在这片岩石嶙峋的荒野里浮现出来，宛如沙漠中的一片绿洲。方圆数里之内，四下呈现一片火山的面貌，荒蛮、不毛、凄凉得令人想嗥叫。东面桑宁山（Mt. Sanneen）脚下，越过一道道深沟，方有村落与青翠；从葡萄园最末一层梯田望去，可俯瞰一道深谷，那深谷在冬天倒还勉强有一条细流可夸。可一到夏天，它的赤裸便令人骇然。太阳，可以说，把它整个口袋翻了过来，露出它那些巨石、那一缕缕被风吹成的细沙垄，还有它那些塞满死鱼卵的干沟。而那冷峻的、岩石嶙峋的地平线升得又高又近，挡住了大海，也把日落的荣光从这位隐修士眼前遮去。但我们可以看见那荣光落在桑宁山上、落在我们头顶的云上、落在远处村落里的玻璃窗扉上的效果。东面的群山披上了空灵的淡紫与锦葵色相间的氅衣；云朵被染上紫与金；远处的窗扉则闪烁着神秘的炭火宝石般的光：太阳正在地中海（Mediterranean）上落下——正向这些山峦挥手作别。

===Original===
We reach the first gate of the Hermitage; and the odour peculiar to monks and monkeries, a mixed smell of mould and incense and burning oil, greets us as we enter into a small open space in the centre of which is a Persian lilac tree. To the right is a barbed-wire fence shutting in the vineyard; directly opposite is the door of the chapel; and near it is a wicket before which stands a withered old woman. Against the wall is a stone bench where another woman is seated. As we enter, we hear her, standing at the wicket, talking to someone behind the scene. “Yes, that is the name of my husband,” says she. “Allah have mercy on his soul,” sighs an exiguous voice within; “pray for him, pray for him.” And the woman, taking to weeping, blubbers out, “Will thirty masses do, think your Reverence?” “Yes, that will cheer his soul,” replies the oracle.

===Chinese===
我们走到隐修院的头一道门前；一进门，修士与修道院所特有的那股气味——一股霉味、乳香与燃油混在一起的气味——便迎面扑来，我们走进一小片空地，空地正中长着一棵波斯丁香。右边是一道铁丝篱笆，围着那座葡萄园；正对面是小教堂的门；门旁有一道小窗，窗前站着一个形容枯槁的老妇。墙边有一条石凳，上头坐着另一个妇人。我们一进来，便听见那个站在小窗前的妇人，正在与窗后什么人说话。「是的，那便是我丈夫的名字，」她说。「愿真主怜悯他的灵魂，」里头一个细如游丝的声音叹道，「为他祈祷罢，为他祈祷罢。」那妇人于是哭了起来，抽抽搭搭地说：「做三十台弥撒，够了吗，神父您看？」「够了，那会使他的灵魂得安慰的，」那神谕般的回答说道。

===Original===
The old woman thereupon enters the chapel, pays the priest or serving-monk therein, one hundred piasters for thirty masses, and goes away in tears. The next woman rises to the gate. “I am the mother of﻿—,” she says. “Ah, the mother of﻿—,” repeats the exiguous voice. “How are you? (She must be an old customer.) How is your husband? How are your children? And those in America, are they well, are they prosperous? Yes, yes, your deceased son. Well, h’m﻿—h’m﻿—you must come again. I can not tell you anything yet. Come again next week.” And she, too, visits the chapel, counts out some money to the serving-monk, and leaves the Hermitage, drying her tears.

===Chinese===
那老妇随即进了教堂，把三十台弥撒的价钱——一百皮阿斯特——付给里头的神父，亦即侍候的修士，含泪而去。下一个妇人起身走到门前。「我是某某的母亲，」她说。「啊，某某的母亲，」那细如游丝的声音重复道。「你身子可好？（她想必是个老主顾了。）你丈夫可好？你的孩子们可好？那些在美国的，他们可平安，可发达？是，是，你那个已亡故的儿子。呃，嗯——嗯——你下回再来罢。这会儿我还什么都不能告诉你。下星期再来罢。」于是她也进了教堂，数出一些钱给那侍候的修士，拭干眼泪，离开了隐修院。

===Original===
The Reader, who must have recognised the squeaking, snuffling, exiguous voice, knows not perhaps that the Hermit, in certain moments of *inkhitaf* (abstraction, levitation) has glimpses into the spirit-world and can tell while in this otherworldliness how the Christian souls are faring, and how many masses those in Purgatory need before they can rejoin the bosom of Father Abraham. And those who seek consolation and guidance through his occult ministrations are mostly women. But the money collected for masses, let it here be said, as well as the income of the vineyard, the Hermit touches not. The monks are the owners of the occult establishment, and they know better than he what to do with the revenue. But how far this ancient religious Medium can go in the spirit-world, and how honest he might be in his otherworldliness, let those say who have experience in spookery and table-rapping.

===Chinese===
读者想必已经认出那又尖又闷、细如游丝的声音了，却未必知道：这位隐修士在某些 *inkhitaf*（出神、升玄）的时刻，竟能窥见灵界，并且能在这种超然出神之中道出基督徒灵魂的境况如何、那些在炼狱（Purgatory）里的灵魂还需几台弥撒方能重归亚伯拉罕祖（Father Abraham）的怀抱。而借他这等秘术的法事来求安慰、求指引的，大半是妇人。但这里须得说一句：为弥撒所收的钱，一如葡萄园的入息，这位隐修士是分文不沾的。这所秘术馆的主人是那些修士，他们比他更懂得这笔进项该如何处置。至于这位古老的宗教灵媒在灵界里究竟能走多远，他在那种超然出神之中究竟有几分诚实，那便留给那些在降神术与叩桌招魂上头有经验的人去评说罢。

===Original===
Now, the women having done and gone, the wicket is open, and the serving-monk ushers us through the dark and stivy corridor to the rear, where a few boxes marked “Made in America”﻿—petroleum boxes, these﻿—are offered us as seats. Before the door of the last cell are a few potsherds in which sweet basil plants are withering from thirst. Presently, the door squeaks, and one, not drooping like the plants, comes out to greet us. This is Father Abd’ul-Messiah (Servitor of the Christ), as the Hermit is called. Here, indeed, is an up-to-date hermit, not an antique troglodyte. Lean and lathy, he is, but not hungry-looking; quick of eye and gesture; quick of step, too. He seems always on the alert, as if surrounded continually with spirits. He is young, withal, or keeps so, at least, through the grace and ministration of Allah and the Virgin. His long unkempt hair and beard are innocent of a single white line. And his health? “Through my five and twenty years of seclusion,” said he, “I have not known any disease, except, now and then, in the spring season, when the sap begins to flow, I am visited by Allah with chills and fever.﻿—No; I eat but one meal a day.﻿—Yes; I am happy, Allah be praised, quite happy, very happy.”

===Chinese===
此时妇人们既已了事而去，那小窗便开着了，侍候的修士引我们穿过一条又暗又闷的走廊，来到后头，那里有几只印着「美国制造」（Made in America）的箱子——都是些煤油箱——权当我们的座位。末一间静室门前，摆着几片碎陶，里头栽的甜罗勒正渴得发蔫。不一会儿，门吱呀一响，出来一个并不像那些罗勒一样蔫头蔫脑的人，迎向我们。这便是阿卜杜勒·麦西哈神父（Father Abd’ul-Messiah，意为「基督之仆」）——人们就是这样称呼这位隐修士的。这的确是一位时髦的隐修士，不是什么古旧的穴居人。他瘦而颀长，却不像挨饿的样子；目光与手势都敏捷；步履也敏捷。他似乎总在警醒之中，仿佛时刻被众灵环绕。他年纪尚轻，至少凭着真主与圣母（the Virgin）的恩典与法事保持着年轻。他那长发长须蓬乱不修，却找不出一缕白线。至于他的身子骨？「在我二十五年隐修的岁月里，」他说，「我不曾害过什么病，只是每逢春日树汁流转之时，真主偶尔会打发寒热来探访我。——不；我一日只进一餐。——是；我是快乐的，赞美真主，很快乐，非常快乐。」

===Original===
And he lifts his eyes heavenward, and sighs and rubs his hands in joyful satisfaction. To us, this Servitor of the Christ seemed not to have passed the climacteric. But truly, as he avowed, he was entering the fifth lustrum beyond it. Such are the advantages of the ascetic life, and of such ascetics the Kingdom of Heaven. A man of sixty can carry twenty years in his pocket, and seem all honesty, and youth, and health, and happiness.

===Chinese===
说着他举目向天，叹息一声，喜滋滋地搓着双手。在我们看来，这位基督之仆似乎尚未度过那个要紧的关捩之年。但说实话，照他自己所承认的，他已步入那关捩之后的第五个五年了。苦行的生活便有这等好处，而天国也正是由这样的苦行者所构成。一个六十岁的人，可以把二十岁揣在口袋里，看上去满身的诚实、青春、健康与快乐。

===Original===
We then venture a question about the sackcloth, a trace of which was seen under his tunic sleeve. And fetching a deep sigh, he gazes on the drooping sweet basils in silence. No, he likes not to speak of these mortifications of the flesh. After some meditation he tells us, however, that the sackcloth on the first month is annoying, torturing. “But the flesh,” he continues naively, “is inured to it, as the pile, in the course of time, is broken and softened down.” And with an honest look in his eyes, he smiled and sighs his assurance. For his Reverence always punctuates his speech with these sweet sighs of joy. The serving-monk now comes to whisper a word in his ear, and we are asked to “scent the air” a while in the vineyard.

===Chinese===
于是我们大着胆子，问起他那件粗毛苦衣——方才从他修士袍的袖口下露出一角来。他深深叹了一口气，默默望着那几株蔫垂的甜罗勒。不，他不爱谈这些克苦肉身的事。沉思了一会儿，他却告诉我们：头一个月穿那苦衣，确实烦人、折磨人。「可是肉体，」他天真地接着说，「也会习以为常，一如那粗毛日久之后便被磨软、压伏下去。」他眼里透着诚实的神色，微笑着，又叹一口气，以此向我们保证。原来神父讲话，总爱用这些甘美的喜悦之叹来作标点。这时那侍候的修士过来，在他耳畔低语了一句，我们便被请到葡萄园里去「透透气」。

===Original===
This lovely patch of terrace-ground the Hermit tills and cultivates alone. And so thoroughly the work is done that hardly a stone can be seen in the soil. And so even and regular are the terrace walls that one would think they were built with line and plummet. The vines are handsomely trimmed and trellised, and here and there, to break the monotony of the rows, a fig, an apricot, an almond, or an olive, spreads its umbrageous boughs. Indeed, it is most cheering in the wilderness, most refreshing to the senses, this lovely vineyard, the loveliest we have seen.

===Chinese===
这一片可爱的梯田，是这位隐修士独自耕作打理的。活儿做得那样彻底，土里几乎看不见一块石头。梯田的石墙又是那样平整规整，人会以为是用准绳与线锤砌起来的呢。葡萄藤修剪搭架得十分齐整好看，而为了打破行列的单调，这里那里，一棵无花果、一棵杏树、一棵扁桃，或一棵橄榄，舒展开它们浓荫的枝桠。说真的，在这片荒野之中，再没有比这座可爱的葡萄园——我们所见过最可爱的葡萄园——更叫人振奋、更悦人感官的了。

===Original===
Father Abd’ul-Messiah might be a descendant of Simeon of the Pillar for all we know; but instead of perching on the top of it, he breaks it down and builds with its stones a wall of his vineyard. Here he comes with his serving-monk, and we resume the conversation under the almond tree.

===Chinese===
阿卜杜勒·麦西哈神父，据我们所知，说不定还是柱头修士西面（Simeon of the Pillar）的后裔呢；只是他并不蹲在那柱头顶上，反倒把柱子推倒，用它的石头给自己的葡萄园砌了一道墙。他便带着那侍候的修士来到这里，我们在扁桃树下重新接起话头。

===Original===
“You should come in the grape season to taste of my fruits,” says he.

“And do you like the grape?” we ask.

“Yes, but I prefer to cultivate it.”

“Throughout the season,” the serving-monk puts in, “and though the grapes be so plentiful, he tastes them not.”

“No?”

The Hermit is silent; for, as we have said, he is reluctant in making such confessions. Virtue, once bragged about, once you pride yourself upon it, ceases to be such.

===Chinese===
「你们该在葡萄熟的时候来，尝尝我的果子，」他说。

「那么您爱吃葡萄么？」我们问。

「爱，但我更爱种它。」

「整个一季里，」那侍候的修士插嘴道，「哪怕葡萄结得再多，他也不尝一颗。」

「当真？」

隐修士默然不语；因为，如我们所言，他不肯做这样的自白。德行一旦被人夸耀、一旦引以自矜，便不再是德行了。

===Original===
In his vineyard the Hermit is most thorough, even scientific. One would think that he believed only in work. No; he does not sprinkle the vines with holy water to keep the grubs away. Herein he has sense enough to know that only in *kabrit* (sulphur) is the phylactery which destroys the phylloxera.

“And what do you do when you are not working in your vineyard or praying?”

“I have always somewhat to do, always. For to be idle is to open the door for Iblis. I might walk up and down this corridor, counting the slabs therein, and consider my time well spent.” Saying which he rises and points to the sky. The purple fringes of the clouds are gone to sable; the lilac tints on the mountains are waxing grey; and the sombre twilight with his torch﻿—the evening star had risen﻿—is following in the wake of day; ’tis the hour of prayer.

===Chinese===
在他的葡萄园里，这位隐修士最为彻底，简直可说是科学的。人会以为他只信劳作二字。不；他并不往葡萄藤上洒圣水来驱虫。这一点上他倒有足够的见识，知道唯独在 *kabrit*（硫磺）之中，才有那灭除葡萄根瘤蚜的灵符。

「那么您不在葡萄园里做工、不祈祷的时候，做些什么呢？」

「我总有事可做，总有。因为懒惰，便是给伊布力斯（Iblis）开门。我尽可以在这条走廊里踱来踱去，数一数那里的石板，也觉得这时间花得不亏。」说罢他便站起身来，指向天空。云朵那紫色的镶边已转为玄黑；山上的淡紫色正渐渐化作灰白；那幽暗的暮色执着火把——长庚星已经升起了——正尾随着白昼而来；这是祈祷的时辰了。

===Original===
But before we leave him to his devotion, we ask to be permitted to see his cell. Ah, that is against the monastic rules. We insist. And with a h’m, h’m, and a shake of the head, he rubs his hands caressingly and opens the door. Yes, the Reader shall peep into this eight by six cell, which is littered all around with rubbish, sacred and profane. In the corner is a broken stove with a broken pipe attached﻿—broken to let some of the smoke into the room, we are told. “For smoke,” quoth the Hermit, quoting the Doctor, “destroys the microbes﻿—and keeps the room warm after the fire goes out.”

===Chinese===
但在把他留给他的祈祷之前，我们请求准许看一看他的静室。哎呀，那是犯了修院规矩的。我们一再坚持。他便「嗯，嗯」地应着，摇了摇头，爱抚地搓着双手，开了门。好罢，读者可以朝这间八尺见方、六尺宽的静室里窥上一眼——室内四处乱堆着杂物，神圣的与世俗的杂处一室。一个角落里有一只破炉子，连着一截破烟囱——据说破得恰到好处，好让一部分烟跑进屋里来。「因为烟，」隐修士引那位博士（the Doctor）的话说，「能杀微生物——而且炉火熄了之后，还能让屋子保温。」

===Original===
In the corner opposite the stove is a little altar with the conventional icons and gewgaws and a number of prayer books lying pell-mell around. Nearby is an old pair of shoes, in which are stuck a few candles and St. Anthony’s Book of Contemplations. In the corner behind the door is a large cage, a pantry, suspended middleway between the floor and ceiling, containing a few earthen pots, an oil lamp, and a jar, covered with a cloth. Between the pantry and the altar, on a hair-mat spread on the floor, sleeps his Reverence. And his bed is not so hard as you might suppose, Reader; for, to serve your curiosity, we have been rude enough to lift up a corner of the cloth, and we found underneath a substantial mattress! On the bed is his book of accounts, which, being opened, when we entered, he hastened to close.

“You keep accounts, too, Reverence?”

“Indeed, so. That is a duty devolved on everyone with mortal memory.”

===Chinese===
炉子对面的角落里，是一座小祭坛，上面摆着那一套惯见的圣像与花哨的小摆设，周围乱七八糟地摊着一些祈祷书。旁边是一双旧鞋，鞋里插着几支蜡烛与一本《圣安东尼默想录》（St. Anthony’s Book of Contemplations）。门背后的角落里，挂着一只大笼子——一个食物橱——悬在地板与天花板之间，里头有几只陶罐、一盏油灯，还有一只坛子，上头蒙着布。在食物橱与祭坛之间，地上铺着一张马毛垫子，神父便睡在那里。他的床并不像读者您所想象的那样硬；因为，为了满足您的好奇心，我们无礼地掀起那布的一角，竟发现底下有一床厚实的褥子！床上放着他的账本——我们一进门时它是摊开的，他连忙把它合上。

「您还记账么，神父？」

「当真，是记的。这责任，落在每一个尚有凡人记忆的人身上。」

===Original===
Let it not be supposed, however, that he has charge of the crops. In his journal he keeps the accounts of his masses? And here be evil sufficient for the day.

This, then, is the inventory of Abd’ul-Messiah’s cell. And we do not think we have omitted much of importance. Yes; in the fourth corner, which we have not mentioned, are three or four petroleum cans containing provisions. From one of these he brings out a handful of dried figs, from another a pinch of incense, which he gives us as a token of his love and blessing. One thing we fain would emphasise, before we conclude our account. The money part of this eremitic business need not be harshly judged; for we must bear in mind that this honest Servitor of Christ is strong enough not to have his will in the matter. And remember, too, that the abbey’s bills of expenses run high. If one of the monks, therefore, is blessed with a talent for solitude and seclusion, his brother monks shall profit by it. Indeed, we were told, that the income of the Hermitage, that is, the sum total in gold of the occult and the agricultural endeavours of Abd’ul-Messiah, is enough to defray the yearly expenditures of the monkery. Further, we have nothing to say on the subject. But Khalid has. And of his lengthy lucubration on *The Uses of Solitude*, we cull the following:

===Chinese===
然而切莫以为他管的是庄稼收成。他在那本簿子里，记的是他弥撒的账？这一日的恶也就够了。

这便是阿卜杜勒·麦西哈静室的清单。我们想，要紧的东西并未漏掉多少。是的；在我们未曾提到的第四个角落里，放着三四个煤油罐，里头装着干粮。他从一只罐里摸出一把无花果干，又从另一只里拈出一小撮乳香，送给我们，作为他爱与祝福的记号。在结束这篇记述之前，有一件事我们很想强调。这隐修营生里跟钱有关的部分，不必苛责；因为我们须得记住，这位诚实的基督之仆，在这件事上是坚毅到不肯由着自己做主的。也请记住，修道院的开销账目是很可观的。因此，倘若修士中有一位蒙天赋、擅长独居与清修，他的师兄弟们便可从中取利。说真的，我们被告知，这隐修院的进项——亦即阿卜杜勒·麦西哈在秘术与农事两方面努力所换来的那笔金子的总和——便足以支付这修院一年的开销。除此以外，我们对这桩事再无可说。但哈立德却有话要说。从他论《孤独之用》（The Uses of Solitude）那一篇冗长的冥想之中，我们摘出如下几段：

===Original===
“Everyone’s life at certain times,” writes he, “is either a Temple, a Hermitage, or a Vineyard: everyone, in order to flee the momentary afflictions of Destiny, takes refuge either in God, or in Solitude, or in Work. And of a truth, work is the balm of the sore mind of the world. God and Solitude are luxuries which only a few among us nowadays can afford. But he who lives in the three, though his life be that of a silk larva in its cocoon, is he not individually considered a good man? Is he not a mystic, though uncreative, centre of goodness? Surely, his influence, his Me alone considered, is living and benign, and though it is not life-giving. He is a flickering taper under a bushel; and this, *billah*, were better than the pissasphaltum-souls which bushels of quackery and pretence can not hide. But alas, that a good man by nature should be so weak as to surrender himself entirely to a lot of bad men. For the monks, my brother Hermit, being a silk worm in its cocoon, will asphyxiate the larva after its work is done, and utilise the silk. Ay, after the Larva dies, they pickle and preserve it in their chapel for the benefit of those who sought its oracles in life. Let the beef-packers of America take notice; the monks of my country are in the market with ‘canned hermits!’

===Chinese===
「每个人的生命在某几个时刻，」他写道，「要么是一座神殿，要么是一所隐修院，要么是一座葡萄园：每一个人，为了逃避命运一时的苦厄，所投奔的，不是真主，便是孤独，便是劳作。而诚然，劳作乃是这世界酸痛心智的镇痛之膏。真主与孤独是奢侈品，今日我们之中只有少数人消受得起。但那活在这三者之中的人——纵使他的生命不过是茧中的一条蚕——就其自身而论，岂不是一个好人么？岂不是一个虽无创造力、却是善的神秘中心么？诚然，他的影响——单就他的那个『我』而言——是活生生而良善的，哪怕它并不能赋予生命。他是斗底下一点摇曳的微烛；而这，*billah*（凭真主起誓），已胜过那些沥青灵魂（pissasphaltum-souls）——那斗，再多的庸医与虚伪也遮盖不住的。然而可叹啊，一个天性良善的人，竟软弱到把自己整个交付给一群恶人。因为那些修士——我的隐修士兄弟啊——既是一条茧中之蚕，待它做完它的工作，便会把那蚕儿闷死，而取用它的丝。是啊，那蚕儿一死，他们便把它腌制起来，保存在他们的小教堂里，好让那些生前曾求问它神谕的人得益。让美国的牛肉罐头商们注意了罢：我们这国的修士们，也把『隐修士罐头』拿到市上卖了！

===Original===
“And this Larva, be it remembered, is not subject to decay; a saint does not decompose in the flesh like mortal sinners. One of these, I have been told, dead fifty years ago and now canonised, can be seen yet in one of the monasteries of North Lebanon, keeping well his flesh and bones together﻿—divinely embalmed. It has been truly said that the work of a good man never dies; and these leathery hermits continue in death as in life to counsel and console the Faithful.

“In the past, these Larvae, not being cultivated for the market, continued their natural course of development and issued out of their silk prisons full fledged moths. But those who cultivate them today are in sore need. They have masses and indulgences to sell; they have big bills to pay. But whether left to grow their wings or not, their solitude is that of a cocoon larva, narrow, stale, unprofitable to the world. While that of a philosopher, a Thoreau, for instance, might be called Nature’s filter; and one, issuing therefrom benefited in every sense, morally, physically, spiritually, can be said to have been filtered through Solitude.”

===Chinese===
「而且请记着，这蚕儿是不会腐坏的；一位圣徒并不像凡世的罪人那样在肉身上朽烂。我听说，其中一位，五十年前去世、如今已然封圣，至今仍可在北黎巴嫩的一座修院里见到，筋骨皮肉一一完好——是属天地经过了防腐处理的。常言道：一个善人所做的工永不朽；而这些皮革般的隐修士，在死后一如生前，仍继续为信众们出谋、慰解。

「从前，这些蚕儿并不为着市上的买卖而饲养，便顺其自然地发育下去，从它们那丝织的牢狱里钻出来，成了羽翼丰满的蛾。可今日养蚕的人却窘迫得很。他们有弥撒与赦罪券要卖；他们有大笔账单要付。但无论是否让它们长出翅膀，它们的孤独总归是茧中蚕的孤独——狭隘、陈腐、于世无益。而一位哲人——比如梭罗（Thoreau）——的孤独，倒不妨称之为大自然的过滤器；一个人从其中出来，道德、身体、灵性各方面都得了益处，便可以说：他是经过了孤独这一道过滤的。」

===Original===
“The study of life at a distance is inutile; the study of it at close range is defective. The only method left, therefore, and perhaps the true one, is that of the artist at his canvas. He works at his picture an hour or two, and retires a little to study and criticise it from a distance. It is impossible to withdraw entirely from life and pretend to take an interest in it. Either like my brother Hermit in these parts, a spiritual larva in its cocoon, or like a Thoreau, who during his period of seclusion, peeped every fortnight into the village to keep up at least his practice of human speech. Else what is the use of solitude? A life of fantasy, I muse, is nearer to the heart of Nature and Truth than a life in sackcloth and ashes.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===
「远远地研究人生，是徒劳的；凑近了研究它，又是有缺陷的。因此剩下的唯一法子，或许也是真正的法子，便是画家在画布前的那种法子：他在画前工作一两个钟头，便退后几步，从远处端详它、批评它。人不可能整个从人生中抽身，却又假称对它怀有兴趣。要么像我这一带的隐修士兄弟——茧中的一条灵性的蚕——要么像梭罗那样：他在隐居期间，每两周便溜进村子里窥一眼，好歹把自己说人话的功夫维持下去。否则孤独又有何用？我默想：一种幻想的生活，比披麻蒙灰的生活，更近于自然与真理之心。……」

===Original===
“And yet, deeply considered, this eremitic business presents another aspect. For does not the eremite through his art of prayer and devotion, seek an ideal? Is he not a transcendentalist, at least in the German sense of the word? Is not his philosophy above all the senses, as the term implies, and common sense included? For through Mother Church, and with closed eyes, he will attain the ideal, of which my German philosopher, through the logic-mill, and with eyes open, hardly gets a glimpse.

“The devout and poetic souls, and though they walk among the crowd, live most of their lives in solitude. Through Mother Sorrow, or Mother Fancy, or Mother Church, they are ever seeking the ideal, which to them is otherwise unattainable. And whether a howler of Turabu or a member of the French Academy, man, in this penumbra of faith and doubt, of superstition and imagination, is much the same. ‘The higher powers in us,’ says Novalis, ‘which one day, as Genii, shall fulfil our will, are for the present, Muses, which refresh us on our toilsome course with sweet remembrances.’ And the jinn, the fairies, the angels, the muses, are as young and vivacious today as they were in the Arabian and Gaelic Ages of Romance.

===Chinese===
「然而深加思量，这隐修的营生还有另一面。因为那位隐修士，借着他那祈祷与虔诚的艺术，岂不也是在追求一个理想么？他岂不是一个超验论者——至少在德文那种意义上？他的哲学岂不是凌驾于一切感官之上（一如其名所示），连常识也包括在内？因为借着母教会，闭上双眼，他将达到那理想；而我那位德国哲学家，透过逻辑的磨盘、睁着双眼，却几乎连一瞥也难得。

「那些虔敬而富于诗意的灵魂，纵使他们行在人丛之中，他们一生大部分光阴仍是活在孤独里。他们借着忧愁之母、或幻想之母、或教会之母，永远在追寻那个理想——对他们而言，舍此别无他途可达。无论是一个为图拉布（Turabu）哀号的人，还是法兰西学院（French Academy）的一员，人处在这信仰与怀疑、迷信与想象的半明半暗之中，原是差不多的。『我们心中那些更高的力量，』诺瓦利斯（Novalis）说，『有朝一日将作为精灵（Genii）来成全我们的意志，而眼下它们还是缪斯（Muses），以甘美的回忆，在我们艰辛的途程上使我们苏醒振作。』而那些精灵（Jinn）、仙女、天使、缪斯，今日仍与当年阿拉伯与盖尔人的浪漫时代一样年轻、一样活泼。

===Original===
“But whether Mother Church or Poetry or Philosophy or Music be the magic-medium, the result is much the same if the motive be not religiously sincere, sincerely religious, piously pure, lofty, and humane. Ay, my Larva-Hermit, with all his bigotry and straitness of soul, stands higher than most of your artists and poets and musicians of the present day. For a life sincerely spent between the Temple and the Vineyard, between devotion and honest labour, producing to one man of all mankind some positive good, is not to be compared with the life which oscillates continuously between egoism and vanity, quackery and cowardice, selfishness and pretence, and which never rises, do what it may, above the larva state.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===
「然而无论那施法的媒介是教会之母、是诗、是哲学、还是音乐，结果都差不多——倘若那动机不是宗教上的诚恳、诚恳的宗教、虔敬的纯洁、高尚而仁慈的话。是啊，我的那位蚕儿隐修士，纵使他满心的偏执与心地狭隘，却比你们今日大多数的艺术家、诗人、音乐家站得更高。因为一种真诚地消磨在神殿与葡萄园之间、消磨在虔诚与诚实的劳作之间的生命——它能给全人类中的某一个人产出某种切实的善——是不能与另一种生命相提并论的：那另一种生命，永远在自我与虚荣、庸医与怯懦、自私与虚伪之间来回摆动，无论怎样挣扎，也超不过那条蚕的阶段。……」

===Original===
“Let everyone cultivate with pious sincerity some such vineyard as my Hermit’s and the world will not further need reform. For through all the vapour and mist of his ascetic theology, through the tortuous chasm of his eremitic logic, through the bigotry and crass superstition of his soul, I can always see the Vineyard on the one side of his cell, and the Church on the other, and say to myself: Here be a man who is never idle; here be one who loves the leisure praised by Socrates, and hates the sluggishness which Iblis decks and titivates. And if he crawls between his Church and his Vineyard, and burrows in both for a solution of life, nay, spins in both the cocoon of his ideal, he ought not to be judged from on high. Come thou near him; descend; descend a little and see: has he not a task, and though it be of the taper-under-the-bushel kind? Has he not a faith and a sincerity which in a Worm of the Earth ought to be reckoned sublime? ‘If there were sorrow in heaven,’ he once said to me, ‘how many there would continuously lament the time they wasted in this world?’

“O my Brothers, build your Temples and have your Vineyards, even though it be in the rocky wilderness.”

===Chinese===
「让每一个人都以虔敬的诚意，去耕种一座像我这隐修士那样的葡萄园罢，这世界便不必再求改革了。因为透过他那苦行神学的种种蒸气与迷雾，透过他那隐修逻辑的曲折深渊，透过他灵魂里的偏执与粗陋的迷信，我总能看见：他那静室的一边是葡萄园，另一边是教堂；我便对自己说：这里有一个从不闲散的人；这里有一个喜爱苏格拉底（Socrates）所称颂的那种闲暇、而憎恶那被伊布力斯粉饰装扮的怠惰的人。倘若他在自己的教堂与葡萄园之间匍匐，并在两者之中都挖掘人生的解答，不，在两者之中都纺出他那理想的茧，那他就不该被人从高处来论断。你且走近他些；下来；下来一点儿看看：他岂不是有一项任务么，纵然那是属于斗底微烛那一类的？他岂不是有一种信仰与诚实，在一条地里的虫子身上，也该算作崇高的么？『倘若天上有忧愁，』他有一次对我说，『那里该有多少人要不住地哀叹自己在这世上虚掷的光阴呢？』

「我的弟兄们啊，去建造你们的神殿，去拥有你们的葡萄园罢，哪怕那是在岩石嶙峋的荒野之中。」

## Book the Third / 第三卷

### In Kulmakan / 在库尔玛堪

### To God / 致上帝

===Original===
In the religious systems of mankind, I sought thee, O God, in vain; in their machine-made dogmas and theologies, I sought thee in vain; in their churches and temples and mosques, I sought thee long, and long in vain; but in the Sacred Books of the World, what have I found? A letter of thy name, O God, I have deciphered in the Vedas, another in the Zend-Avesta, another in the Bible, another in the Koran. Ay, even in the Book of the Royal Society and in the Records of the Society for Psychical Research, have I found the diacritical signs which the infant races of this Planet Earth have not yet learned to apply to the consonants of thy name. The lisping infant races of this Earth, when will they learn to pronounce thy name entire? Who shall supply the Vowels which shall unite the Gutturals of the Sacred Books? Who shall point out the dashes which compound the opposite loadstars in the various regions of thy Heaven? On the veil of the eternal mystery are palimpsests of which every race has deciphered a consonant. And through the diacritical marks which the seers and paleologists of the future shall furnish, the various dissonances in thy name shall be reduced, for the sake of the infant races of the Earth, to perfect harmony.

===Chinese===
在人间种种宗教体系里，主啊，我寻觅你，徒然；在他们那机器制造的教义与神学里，我寻觅你，徒然；在他们的教堂、神庙、清真寺里，我寻觅你良久，寻觅你良久而徒然；可是在世间的诸圣典中，我又寻见了什么呢？主啊，你名号里的一个字母，我在《吠陀经》（Vedas）中辨出；又一个，在《阿维斯塔经》（Zend-Avesta）里；又一个，在《圣经》（Bible）之中；又一个，在《古兰经》（Koran）之内。是啊，甚至在皇家学会（Royal Society）的典册里、在心灵研究协会（Society for Psychical Research）的记录里，我也找见了那些变音符号——而这地球上年幼的诸族，尚不懂得把它们标注到你名号的那些辅音上去。这地球上口齿不清的年幼诸族啊，他们何时才能学会把你名号整个念出来？谁来补上那些元音，把诸圣典里的那些喉音联成一体？谁来指明那些短划，将你诸天不同区域里彼此对立的指路明星合而为一？在永恒奥秘的帷幕之上，有一层层的重写羊皮纸，每一个民族都从其上辨出了一个辅音。而经由未来的先见者与古文书学者所提供的变音符号，你名号里种种不谐之音，为了地球上年幼诸族的缘故，终将归于完美的和鸣。

> 哈立德

## I / I

The Disentanglement of the Me / 自我之解缠

===Original===
“Why this exaggerated sense of thine importance,” Khalid asks himself in the K.L. MS., “when a little ptomaine in thy cheese can poison the source of thy lofty contemplations? Why this inflated conception of thy Me, when an infusion of poppy seeds might lull it to sleep, even to stupefaction? What avails thy logic when a little of the Mandragora can melt the material universe into golden, unfolding infinities of dreams? Why take thyself so seriously when a leaf of henbane, taken by mistake in thy salad, can destroy thee? But the soul is not dependent on health or disease. The soul is the source of both health and disease. And life, therefore, is either a healthy or a diseased state of the soul.

“One day, when I was rolling these questions in my mind, and working on a reed basket to present to my friend the Hermit as a farewell memento, his serving-monk brings me some dried figs in a blue kerchief and says, ‘My Master greets thee and prays thee come to him.’ I do so the following morning, bringing with me the finished basket, and as I enter the Hermitage court, I find him repairing a stone wall in the vineyard. As he sees me, he hastens to put on his cloak that I might not remark the sackcloth he wore, and with a pious smile of assurance and thankfulness, welcomes and embraces me, as is his wont. We sit down in the corridor before the chapel door. The odorous vapor of what was still burning in the censer within hung above us. The holy atmosphere mantled the dread silence of the place. And the slow, insinuating smell of incense, like the fumes of gunga, weighed heavy on my eyelids and seemed to brush from my memory the cobwebs of time. A drowsiness possessed me; I felt like one awaking from a dream. I asked for the water jug, which the Hermit hastened to bring. And looking through the door of the chapel, I saw on the altar a burning cresset flickering like the planet Mercury on a December morning. How often did I light such a cresset when a boy, I mused. Yes, I was an acolyte once. I swang the censer and drank deep of the incense fumes as I chanted in Syriac the service. And I remember when I made a mistake one day in reading the Epistle of Paul, the priest, who was of an irascible humour, took me by the ear and made me spell the words I could not pronounce. And the boys in the congregation tittered gleefully. In my mortification was honey for them. Such was my pride, nevertheless, such the joy I felt, when, of all the boys that gathered round the lectern at vespers, I was called upon to read in the *sinksar* (hagiography) the Life of the Saint of the day.

===Chinese===
「你这一身自视甚高的夸张感觉，所为何来？」哈立德在赫迪夫图书馆手稿（K.L. MS.）里这样自问，「当你干酪里那一点点尸碱（ptomaine），便能毒化你那高远沉思的源头？你这一副把『自我』吹胀了的观念，所为何来？一剂罂粟籽的浸液便足以将它催眠，乃至昏昏然人事不知。你那一套逻辑又有何用？一点点曼陀茄（Mandragora）便能将这物质宇宙融化成金色的、层层展开的梦的无穷。你又何苦把自己看得这般郑重其事？一片误入你沙拉里的莨菪叶，便能将你毁去。然而灵魂并不依附于健康或疾病。灵魂乃是健康与疾病两者的源头。所以，生命也无非是灵魂的一种健康或疾病的状态罢了。

「某日，我正一面在心头翻滚着这些问题，一面编一只芦苇篮，要送给我那位隐士朋友作临别纪念，他那侍奉的修士却用一方蓝巾兜着些无花果干给我送来，说道：『我主人向你请安，请你到他那里去。』次日清晨我便去了，带着那只编好的篮子，及至走进隐庐的院落，我看见他正在葡萄园里修一道石墙。他一瞧见我，便连忙披上外氅，免得我留意到他所穿的那件粗毛布衣；带着他那惯有的、虔诚笃定而满怀感恩的微笑，迎上前来拥抱我。我们在小礼拜堂门前的那道廊下坐下。堂内香炉里尚在焚着的那缕芳馨之气，仍缭绕在我们头顶。那一片圣洁的氛围，笼罩着此地那可畏的寂静。那缓慢而暗暗袭人的香气，一如甘贾烟（ganga）之气，沉甸甸压在我的眼皮上，仿佛要将记忆里那时间的蛛网一并拂去。一阵困倦攫住了我；我觉着自己恍如一个刚从梦中醒来的人。我要了水罐来，隐士连忙取了去。我透过礼拜堂的门望进去，只见祭坛上一盏燃着的灯盏明灭不定，好似十二月清晨的那颗水星。我少时曾多少次点燃这样一盏灯啊——我在心底沉吟。是的，我曾是辅祭。我曾摇着香炉，深吸那香烟，一面用叙利亚语（Syriac）吟诵着礼仪。我还记得，某日我读保罗的书信（Epistle of Paul）念错了一处，那位性情暴躁的神父便揪住我的耳朵，叫我把我念不出的字一个一个字母地拼出来。会众里的孩子们咯咯地笑了。我的羞辱便是他们的蜜。然而我的骄傲亦复如是——我的欢喜亦复如是——当日晡时经课，孩子们围在经台前，独独叫我上去，在那部《辛克萨尔》（sinksar，即圣徒传记）里，诵读当日那位圣人的生平。

===Original===
“I knew then that to steal, for instance, is a sin; and yet, I emptied the box of wafers every morning after mass and shared them with the very boys who laughed at my mistakes. One day, in the purest intention, I offered one of these wafers to my donkey and he would not eat it. I felt insulted, and never after did I pilfer a wafer. Now, as I muse on these sallies of boyish waywardness I am impressed with the idea that the certainty and daring of Ignorance, or might I say Innocence, are great. Indeed, to the pure everything is pure. But strange to relate that as I sat in the corridor of the Hermitage and saw the light flickering on the altar, I hankered for a wafer, and was tempted to go into the chapel and filch one. What prevented me? Alas, knowledge makes sceptics and cowards of us all. And the pursuit of knowledge, according to my Hermit, nay, the noblest pursuit, even the serving of God, ceases to be a virtue the moment we begin to enjoy it.

“ ‘It is necessary to conquer, not only our instincts,’ he continued, ‘but our intellectual and our spiritual passions as well. To force our will in the obedience of a higher will, to leave behind all our mundane desires in the pursuit of the one great desire, herein lies the essence of true virtue. St. Anthony would snatch his hours of devotion from the Devil. Even prayer to him was a struggle, an effort not to feel the joy of it. Yes, we must always disobey our impulses, and resist the tyranny of our desires. When I have a strong desire to pray, I go out into the vineyard and work. When I begin to enjoy my work in the vineyard, I cease to do it well. Therefore, I take up my breviary. Do that which you must not do, when you are suffering, and you will not want to do it again, when you are happy. The other day, one who visited the Hermitage, spoke to me of you, O Khalid. He said you were what is called an anarchist. And after explaining to me what is meant by this﻿—I never heard of such a religion before﻿—I discovered to my surprise that I, too, am an anarchist. But there is this difference between us: I obey only God and the authority of God, and you obey your instincts and what is called the authority of reason. Yours, O Khalid, is a narrow conception of anarchy. In truth, you should try to be an anarchist like me: subordinate your personality, your will and mind and soul, to a higher will and intelligence, and resist with all your power everything else. Why do you not come to the Hermitage for a few days and make me your confessor?’

===Chinese===
「那时我便晓得，比方说，偷盗是一桩罪；然而，我每日清晨弥撒之后，仍把那盒圣饼（wafer）掏个精光，与那些嘲笑我念错的孩子们分食。一日，我怀着最纯净的心意，把其中一片圣饼拿给我的驴子吃，它竟不肯吃。我觉得受了侮辱，从此再不曾偷过一片圣饼。如今，当我回味这些少年顽劣的种种行径时，我心头不禁生出一个念头：无知——或者我可以说，天真——所特有的那份笃定与胆量，是了不起的。诚然：对纯洁的人，一切皆是纯洁。然而说来也奇，正当我坐在隐庐廊下、望着祭坛上那明灭的灯火之时，我竟又贪恋起一片圣饼来，竟动了进堂去偷一片的念头。是什么阻住了我？唉，知识把我们一个个都变成了怀疑论者和懦夫。而据我那位隐士所言，对知识的追求——不，乃至最高贵的追求，便是对真主的侍奉——一旦我们开始乐在其中，便也不再是德行了。

「『我们必须征服的，不仅是我们的本能，』他继续说道，『还有我们心智上、灵性上的种种激情。把我们的意志，强按着去顺服一个更高的意志；把我们一切世俗的欲望，都抛在身后，去追求那唯一的大欲望——真正德行的精髓，即在于此。圣安东尼（St. Anthony）连他那祈祷的时辰，也要从魔鬼那里夺过来。便是对他，祈祷也是一场争斗，一番尽力不去感受其中之乐的努力。是的，我们必须时时违抗自己的冲动，抵抗我们欲望的暴政。每当我生出强烈的祈祷之愿，我便到葡萄园里去做工。一旦我开始享受我在园中的劳作，我便做不好它了。于是，我拿起我的日课经（breviary）。在你受苦之时，去做那你不该做的事，待你快乐之时，你便不会再想做了。前几日，有一位到隐庐来的人，向我提起你，哈立德啊。他说你是一个所谓『无政府主义者』（anarchist）。他向我解释了此词何谓——我先前从未听过这样一种宗教——我竟惊讶地发觉：我自己也是个无政府主义者。只是你我之间有这点不同：我只顺服真主，顺服真主的权柄；而你顺服的是你的本能，以及那所谓理性的权柄。哈立德啊，你那个无政府主义，未免狭隘了些。其实，你应当试着像我这样去做个无政府主义者：把你的人格、你的意志、心智与灵魂，统归于一个更高的意志与智慧之下，并以全力抵抗其他一切。你何不到隐庐来住几日，让我做你的听告解者呢？』

===Original===
“ ‘I do not confess in private, and I can not sleep within doors.’

“ ‘You do not have to do so; the booth under the almond tree is at your disposal. Come for a spiritual exercise of one week only.’

“ ‘I have been going through such an exercise for a year, and soon I shall leave my cloister in the pines.’

“ ‘What say you? You are leaving our neighbourhood? No, no; remain here, O Khalid. Come, live with me in the Hermitage. Come back to Mother Church; return not to the wicked world. O Khalid, we must inherit the Kingdom of Allah, and we can not do so by being anarchist like the prowlers of the forest. Meditate on the insignificance and evanescence of human life.’

“ ‘But it lies within us, O my Brother, to make it significant and eternal.’

“ ‘Yes, truly, in the bosom of Mother Church. Come back to your Mother﻿—come to the Hermitage﻿—let us pass this life together.’

“ ‘And what will you do, if in the end you discover that I am in the right?’

“Here he paused a moment, and, casting on me a benignant glance, makes this reply: ‘Then, I will rejoice, rejoice,’ he gasped; ‘for we shall both be in the right. You will become an anarchist like me and not against the wretched authorities of the world, but against your real enemies, Instinct and Reason.’

“And thus, now and then, he would salt his argument with a pinch of casuistic wit. Once he was hard set, and, to escape the alternatives of the situation, he condescended to tell me the story of his first and only love.

===Chinese===
「『我不私下告解，我也不能在屋内就寝。』

「『你不必如此；扁桃树下的那间小棚，任凭你用。只来过一周的灵修便好。』

「『这样的灵修我已行了整整一年，不久我便要离开我在松林里的那间修室了。』

「『你说什么？你要离开我们这一带了？不，不；留下来罢，哈立德。来，与我同住在隐庐里。回到母会（Mother Church）来罢；莫再回到那邪恶的世间去。哈立德啊，我们必须承受真主（Allah）的国，可我们若是像林中那些游荡者那样做无政府主义者，便承受不了。你且默念人生的渺小与无常。』

「『可它正在我们之内，我的兄弟啊，可以使它变得重大而永恒。』

「『是的，诚然，在母会的怀抱之中。回到你母亲那里来罢——到隐庐来罢——让我们一同度过此生。』

「『那么，倘若末了你发觉我是对的，你将如何？』

「说到此处，他停了一停，向我投来一道慈和的目光，作答道：『那么，我便欢喜，欢喜，』他喘着气说，『因为我们两人都将是对的。你将变得像我一样的无政府主义者——不是去反对世间那些可悲的权威，而是去反对你真正的仇敌：本能与理性。』

「于是，他间或也会在自己的议论里撒上一撮诡辩的机锋。有一回他被逼到墙角，为逃出那两难的境地，竟肯放下身段，给我讲了他那初恋、也是他唯一一次恋爱的故事。

===Original===
“ ‘In my youth,’ said the Hermit, ‘I was a shoemaker, and not a little fastidious as a craftsman. In fact, I am, and always have been, an extremist, a purist. I can not tolerate the cobblings of life. Either do your work skilfully, devotedly, earnestly, or do it not. So, as a shoemaker, I succeeded very well. Truth to tell, my work was as good, as neat, as elegant as that of the best craftsman in Beirut. And you know, Beirut is noted for its shoemakers. Yes, I was successful as any of them, and I counted among my customers the bishop of the diocese himself. One day, forgive me, Allah! a young girl, the daughter of a peasant neighbour, comes into the shop to order a pair of shoes. In taking the measure of her foot﻿—but I must not linger on these details. A shoemaker can not fail to notice the shape of his customer’s foot. Well, I measured, too, her ankle﻿—ah, forgive me, Allah!

“ ‘In brief, when the shoes were finished﻿—I spent a whole day in the finishing touches﻿—I made her a present of them. And she, in recognition of my favor, made a plush tobacco bag, on which my name was worked in gold threads, and sent it to me, wrapped in a silk handkerchief, with her brother. Now, that is the opening chapter. I will abruptly come to the last, skipping the intermediate parts, for they are too silly, all of them. I will only say that I was as earnest, as sincere, as devoted in this affair of love as I was in my craft. Of a truth, I was mad about both.

“ ‘Now the closing chapter. One day I went to see her﻿—we were engaged﻿—and found she had gone to the spring for water. I follow her there and find her talking to a young man, a shoemaker like myself. No, he was but a cobbler. On the following day, going again to see her, I find this cobbler there. I remonstrate with her, but in vain. And what is worse, she had sent to him the shoes I made, to be repaired. He was patching my own work! I swallowed my ire and went back to my shop. A week later, to be brief, I went there again, and what I beheld made my body shiver. She, the wench. Forgive me, Allah! had her hands around his neck and her lips﻿—yes, her lying lips, on his cheek! No, no; even then I did not utter a word. I could but cry in the depth of my heart. How can woman be so faithless, so treacherous﻿—in my heart I cried.

===Chinese===
「『在我年轻时，』隐士说，『我是个鞋匠，而且作为一个匠人，我颇有些挑剔。事实上，我一向是、并且始终是一个走极端的人，一个纯粹主义者。我不能容忍人生中那些敷衍了事的修补活儿。要么手巧地、专心地、认真地去做你的活计，要么便干脆别做。所以，做鞋匠这一行，我做得相当成功。说实话，我做的活儿，与贝鲁特（Beirut）最好的匠人一样好，一样干净，一样精致。你们也知道，贝鲁特是以其鞋匠闻名的。是的，我与他们之中任何人一样成功，连教区的那位主教大人，也在我的主顾之列。有一日——宽恕我罢，真主！——一个少女，是我一户农家邻居的女儿，进店来定一双鞋。给她量脚的尺寸之时——可是这些细节，我不能再多停留了。一个鞋匠，不可能不留意他主顾那只脚的形状。嗯，我也量了量她的脚踝——啊，宽恕我罢，真主！

「『简而言之，待那双鞋做好——我足足花了一整日做最后的修整——我便把它当作礼物送给了她。而她，为答谢我这番好意，做了一个长毛绒的烟荷包，用金线在上面绣了我的名字，用一方丝巾包着，托她兄弟送了来。这便是开篇第一章。我且径直跳到最后那一章，中间的部分一概略去，因为它们都太傻了，无一例外。我只能说，我在这一桩恋爱之事上，与在我的手艺上一样认真、一样诚恳、一样专心。说真的，我对这两样都同样地入了迷。

「『如今是收尾的那一章。某日我去看她——我们已订了婚——发觉她到泉边打水去了。我跟随她到那里，看见她正与一个青年男子说话，那是个像我一样的鞋匠。不，他不过是个修鞋匠。次日，我再去访她，看见那修鞋匠又在那里。我向她规劝，却徒劳。更糟的是，她竟把我亲手做的那双鞋送到他那里去修理。他正在补我自己做的活计！我咽下怒火，回到自己的铺子里。一周之后，长话短说，我又去了那里，我所看见的，令我的身子打了个寒噤。她，那丫头。宽恕我罢，真主！她的两手搂着他的脖子，她的嘴唇——是的，她那说谎的嘴唇，贴在他的脸颊上！不，不；即便在那时，我也未曾吐露半个字。我只能在心底深处哭泣。女人怎能如此负心，如此背信——我在心底呼喊。

===Original===
“ ‘It was a terrible shock; and from it I lay in bed for days with chills and fever. Now, when I recovered, I was determined on pursuing a new course of life. No longer would I measure women’s feet. I sold my stock, closed my shop, and entered the monastery. I heard afterwards that she married that young cobbler; emigrated with him to America; deserted him there; returned to her native village; married again, and fled with her second husband to South Africa. Allah be praised! even He appreciates the difference between a shoemaker and a cobbler; and the bad woman He gives to the bad craftsman. That is why I say, Never be a cobbler, whatever you do.

“ ‘But in the monastery﻿—draw near, I will speak freely﻿—in the monastery, too, there are cobblers and shoemakers. There, too, is much ungodliness, much treachery, much cobbling. Ah me, I must not speak thus. Forgive me, Allah! But I promised to tell you the whole story. Therefore, I will speak freely. After passing some years in the monastery, years of probation and grief they were, I fell sick with a virulent fever. The abbot, seeing that there was little chance of my recovery, would not send for the physician. And so, I languished for weeks, suffering from thirst and burning pains and hunger. I raved and chattered in my delirium. I betrayed myself, too, they told me. The monks my brothers, even during my suffering, made a scandal of the love affair I related. They said that I exposed my wounds and my broken heart before the Virgin, that I sinned in thought and word on my deathbed. Allah forgive them. It may be, however; for I know not what I said and what I did. But when I recovered, I was determined not to remain in the monastery, and not to return to the world. The wicked world, I disentangled myself absolutely from its poisoned meshes. I came to the Hermitage, to this place. And never, since I made my second remove until now, have I known disease, or sorrow, nor treachery, which is worse than both. Allah be praised! One’s people, one’s brothers, one’s lovers and friends, are a hindrance and botheration. We are nothing, nothing: God is everything. God is the only reality. And in God alone is my refuge. That is my story in brief. If I did not like you, I would not have told it, and so freely. Meditate upon it, and on the insignificance and evanescence of human life. The world is a snare, and a bad snare, at that. For it can not hold us long enough in it to learn to like it. It is a cobbler’s snare. The world is full of cobblers, O Khalid. Come away from it; be an ideal craftsman﻿—be an extremist﻿—be a purist﻿—come live with me. Let us join our souls in devotion, and our hearts in love. Come, let us till and cultivate this vineyard together.’

“And taking me by the hand, he shows me a cell furnished with a hair-mat, a *masnad* (leaning pillow), and a chair. ‘This cell,’ says he, ‘was occupied by the Bishop when he came here for a spiritual exercise of three weeks. It shall be yours if you come; it’s the best cell in the Hermitage. Now, let us visit the chapel.’ I go in with him, and as we are coming out, I ask him childlike for a wafer. He brings the box straightway, begs me to take as much as I desire, and placing his hand on my shoulder, encircles me with one of his benignant glances, saying, ‘Allah illumine thy heart, O Khalid.’ ‘Allah hear thy prayer,’ I reply. And we part in tears.”

===Chinese===
「『那是一次沉重的打击；自此我卧床数日，时寒时热。及至我痊愈，便决心改走新的人生道路。我再也不量女人的脚了。我盘掉了存货，关了铺子，进了修道院。后来我听说，她嫁给了那个年轻的修鞋匠；随他一起移民去了美洲（America）；在那里又抛弃了他；回到她的本村；再嫁，与第二个丈夫一同逃往南非（South Africa）。真主当受赞美！便连祂也懂得鞋匠与修鞋匠之间的分别；坏女人，祂把她交给坏的匠人。是以我说：无论你做什么，绝不可做一个修鞋匠。

「『可在修道院里——走近些，我要畅所欲言——修道院里也有修鞋匠与鞋匠之分。那里头也有许多不敬神的事，许多背信弃义，许多敷衍了事的修补活儿。哎呀，我不该这样说的。宽恕我罢，真主！可我答应过你，要把整件事的原委都讲出来。所以，我要畅所欲言。在修道院过了几年——那是些试炼与悲愁的年头——我染上了一场凶险的热病。院长见我痊愈之望甚微，便不肯去请医师。于是，我缠绵病榻数周，受尽口渴、灼痛与饥饿的折磨。我在谵妄之中胡言乱语。他们告诉我，我也把自己泄漏了出去。我那些修士兄弟们，竟在我受苦之时，把我所讲的那桩情事当作一桩丑闻。他们说我曾在圣母（the Holy Virgin）面前暴露我的伤口与我破碎的心，说我曾在临终的病榻上在心思与言语中犯了罪。真主饶恕他们罢。或许有之；因为我既不知自己说了什么，也不知自己做了什么。可待我痊愈之后，我便下定决心，既不留在修道院，也不回到世间去。那邪恶的世间，我已把它那染毒的罗网，从我身上彻底解开。我来到这隐庐，来到此地。而自第二次迁居以来，直至如今，我再不曾认识疾病，也不曾认识忧愁，更不曾认识那比这两者都更糟的背信弃义。真主当受赞美！一个人的族人、兄弟、情人、朋友，都是妨碍与烦扰。我们什么也不是，什么也不是：真主才是一切。真主是唯一的真实。唯在真主之中，才是我的避难所。这便是我故事的梗概。若不是喜欢你，我绝不会把它讲出来，更不会讲得这般畅快。你且把它，连同人生的渺小与无常，一同默念罢。这世上网罗一张，而且是一张坏的网罗。因为它不能把我们留住到足够久，久到学会喜欢它。那是一张修鞋匠的网罗。这世上满是修鞋匠，哈立德啊。离它远去罢；做一个理想的匠人——做一个走极端的人——做一个纯粹主义者——来与我同住罢。让我们的灵魂在虔诚中合一，让我们的心在爱中合一。来罢，让我们一同耕耘栽培这座葡萄园。』

「说着他牵起我的手，领我看一间小室，里头陈设着一张毛毡垫、一方马斯纳德（*masnad*，即靠枕），和一把椅子。『这间小室，』他说，『是那位主教大人来这里做三周灵修时所住过的。你若肯来，它便是你的了；这是隐庐里最好的一间。如今，让我们去礼拜堂走走。』我随他进去，待我们要出来时，我孩气地向他讨一片圣饼。他随即取来了那只盒子，请我随心意取用，并把一只手搭在我肩上，用他那慈和的目光将我环视一周，说道：『真主光照你的心，哈立德啊。』『真主听允你的祷告，』我答道。我们便含泪而别。」

===Original===
Here Khalid bursts in ecstasy about the higher spiritual kingdom, and chops a little logic about the I and the not-I, the Reality and the non-Reality.—“God,” says the Hermit. “Thought,” says the Idealist, “that is the only Reality.” And what is Thought, and what is God, and what is Matter, and what is Spirit? They are the mysterious vessels of Life, which are always being filled by Love and emptied by Logic. “The external world,” says the Materialist—“Does not exist,” says the Idealist. “ ’Tis immaterial if it does or not,” says the Hermit. And what if the three are wrong? The Universe, knowable and unknowable, will it be affected a whit by it? If the German Professor’s Chair of Logic and Philosophy were set up in the Hermitage, would anything be gained or lost? Let the *I* deny the stars, and they will nevertheless roll in silence above it. Let the not-I crush this I, this “thinking reed,” and the higher universal I, rising above the stars and flooding the sidereal heavens with light, will warm, remold, and regenerate the world.

“I can conceive of a power,” writes Khalid in that vexing Manuscript, “which can create a beautiful parti-colored sunflower of the shattered fragments of Idealism, Materialism, and my Hermit’s theology. Why not, if in the New World—” And here, of a sudden, to surprise and bewilder us, he drags in Mrs. Eddy and the Prophet Dowie yoked under the yoke of Whitman. He marks the *Key to Scripture* with blades from *Leaves of Grass*, and such fuel as he gathers from both, he lights with an ember borrowed from the chariot to Elijah. And thus, for ten whole pages, beating continually, now in the dark of Metaphysics, now in the dusk of Science; losing himself in the tangled bushes of English Materialism, and German Mysticism, and Arabic Sufism; calling now to Berkeley, now to Hackel; meeting with Spencer here, with al-Ghazali there; and endeavoring to extricate himself in the end with some such efforts as “the Natural being Negativity, the Spiritual must be the opposite of that, and both united in God form the Absolute,” etc., etc. But we shall not give ourselves further pain in laying before the English reader the like heavy and unwieldy lumber. Whoever relishes such stuff, and can digest it, need not apply to Khalid; for, in this case, he is but a poor third-hand caterer. Better go to the Manufacturers direct; they are within reach of everyone in this Age of Machinery and Popular Editions. But there are passages here, of which Khalid can say, ‘The Mortar at least is mine.’ And in this Mortar he mixes and titrates with his Neighbour’s Pestle some of his fantasy and insight. Of these we offer a sample:

===Chinese===
到此，哈立德便欣然忘我地谈起那更高的属灵国度，并对「我」与「非我」、那「真实」与「非真实」略略来一番逻辑推敲。——「真主，」隐士说。「思想，」唯心论者说，「那才是唯一的真实。」可是思想是什么？真主是什么？物质是什么？精神又是什么？它们都是生命那神秘的器皿，永远被爱所注满、又被逻辑所倒空。「外在的世界，」唯物论者说——「并不存在，」唯心论者说。「它存在与否，无关紧要，」隐士说。倘若这三者都错了呢？那可知与不可知的宇宙，会因此受一丝一毫的影响吗？纵使把那位德国教授的逻辑哲学之讲席，搬到隐庐里来，又能得到什么、失去什么？任凭那「我」去否认群星，群星仍将默然在它头顶滚动。任凭那「非我」碾碎这个我、这根「会思想的芦苇」，那更高的、普遍的我，自会升起于群星之上，以光灌注那星辰之天，去温煦、重铸、再造这个世界。

「我能设想一种力量，」哈立德在那部令人头痛的手稿里写道，「它能以唯心论、唯物论与我那位隐士的神学之种种破碎片段，造出一朵美丽的、色彩斑斓的向日葵来。为何不能呢？倘若在新世界——」而在此处，他忽地，为要叫我们又惊又惑，竟把艾迪夫人（Mrs. Eddy）与先知道伊（the Prophet Dowie）一同套在惠特曼（Whitman）的轭下拖了进来。他用《草叶集》（*Leaves of Grass*）的刀叶，在《圣经之钥》（*Key to Scripture*）上做下记号，又从两者那里搜罗来的这等燃料，他用一枚借自以利亚之车（the chariot to Elijah）的余烬点燃。就这样，一连整整十页，他不断地挥舞：时而在形而上学的黑夜里，时而在科学的暮色中；迷失在英国唯物论、德国神秘主义与阿拉伯苏菲派（Sufism）的纠结灌木之中；时而呼唤贝克莱（Berkeley），时而呼唤海克尔（Hackel）；在此处遇见斯宾塞（Spencer），在彼处遇见安萨里（al-Ghazali）；而末了，又以诸如此类的努力去设法脱身——「既自然者为否定性，则精神者必为其反面，两者合于真主，即构成绝对」云云。然而，我们不愿再自寻烦恼，把这等沉重累赘的木料陈在英国读者面前。凡是喜好此物、又能消化它的人，尽可不必求诸哈立德；因为在这种情况下，他不过是个可怜的、第三手的承办者罢了。不如径直去找那些原厂家；在这机械与廉价普及本的年代，人人都触手可及。但此处确有若干段落，是哈立德可以夸口的：「至少，那研钵是我自己的。」而在这只研钵里，他用邻人的杵（Pestle），把自己的一点幻想与洞见研和、滴定。我们且从中摘出一份样品如下：

===Original===
“I say with psychologists, as the organism, so is the personality. The revelation of the Me is perfect in proportion to the sound state of the Medium. But according to the Arabic proverb, the jar oozes of its contents. If these be of a putridinous mixture, therefore, no matter how sound the jar, the ooze is not going to smell of ambergris and musk. So, it all depends on the contents with which the Potter fills his jugs and pipkins, I assure you. And if the contents are good and the jar is sound, we get such excellence of soul as is rare among mortals. If the contents are excellent and the jar is cracked, the objective influence will then predominate, and putrescence, soon or late, will set in. Now, the Me in the majority of mankind comes to this world in a cracked pipkin, and it oozes out entirely as soon as it liquifies in youth. The pipkin, therefore, goes through life empty and cracked, ever sounding flat and false. While in others the Me is enclosed in a sealed straw-covered flask and can only be awakened by either evaporation or decapitation, in other words, by a spiritual revolution. And in the very few among mortals, it emerges out of the iron calyx of a flower of red-hot steel, or flows from the transparent, odoriferous bosom of a rose of light. In the first we have a Caesar, an Alexander, a Napoleon; in the second, a Buddha, a Socrates, a Christ.

“But consider that Science, in the course of psychological analysis, speaks of Christ, Napoleon, and Shakespeare, as patients. Such exalted states of the soul, such activity of the mind, such exuberance of spiritual strength, are but the results of the transformation of the Me in the subject, we are told, and this transformation has its roots in the organism. But why, I ask, should there be such a gulf between individuals, such a difference in their Mes, when a difference in the organism is a trifle in comparison? How account for the ebb and flow in the souls, or let us say, in the expression of the individualities, of Mohammad the Prophet, for instance, and Mohammad the camel-herd? And why is it in psychological states that are similar, the consciousness of the one is like a mountain peak, so to speak, and that of the other like a cave?

“A soldier is severely wounded in battle and a change takes place in his nervous organism, by reason of which he loses his organic consciousness; or, to speak in the phraseology of the psychologist, he loses the sense of his own body, of his physical personality. The cause of this change is probably the wound received; but the nature of the change can be explained only by hypotheses, which are become matters of choice and taste﻿—and sometimes of personal interest among scientists. Now, when the question is resolved by hypothesis, is not even a layman free to offer one? If I say the Glass is shattered and the Me within is sadly reflected, or in a more tragic instance the light of the Me runs out, would I not be offering thee a solution as dear and tenable as that of the professor of psychology?”

===Chinese===
「我与心理学家们一道说：有怎样的肌体，便有怎样的人格。『自我』的显现，与那「介质」之健全程度成正比。然而依照阿拉伯谚语所言：罐子里装的什么，渗出来的便是什么。是故，倘若所盛的是一团腐臭的混合物，那么任凭罐子本身如何完好，那渗出的东西也绝不会带有龙涎香与麝香的气味。所以，我可以告诉你，一切都看那陶匠（the Potter）往他的坛坛罐罐里填的是什么。倘若内容物是好的，罐子又是完好的，我们便得着一种世间罕有的灵魂之卓越。倘若内容物极好而罐子有裂，则外在的影响便要占上风，腐烂迟早会生出来。如今，大多数人中的『自我』，来到这世上时，都是盛在一只带裂的小罐里，待它在青春里一液化，便渗漏殆尽了。于是那只小罐便一生空着、裂着，发出的声响永远是瘪的、是假的。而在另一些人身上，『自我』被封在一只以稻草裹封的瓶里，非经蒸发、非经斩首——换言之，非经一场灵性的革命——便不能被唤醒。而在极少数的人身上，它是从一朵炽红钢铁之花的铁质花萼中绽出，或从一朵光之玫瑰的澄澈芳馨的胸怀中流泻而出。前者之中，我们有的是恺撒（Caesar）、亚历山大（Alexander）、拿破仑（Napoleon）；后者之中，我们有的是佛陀（Buddha）、苏格拉底（Socrates）、基督（Christ）。

「但请留意，科学在心理分析的过程中，竟把基督、拿破仑、莎士比亚（Shakespeare）当作病例来谈论。我们被告知，如此崇高的灵魂状态，如此活跃的心智，如此丰沛的灵性之力，都不过是主体内『自我』转化之结果，而此转化之根，扎在肌体之中。可是我要问，既然肌体之差别相比之下不过是小事，个体之间为何竟有如此鸿沟，他们的『自我』竟如此不同？又如何解释，比方说，先知穆罕默德（Mohammad the Prophet）与赶骆驼的穆罕默德（Mohammad the camel-herd），在灵魂、或者说在其个性之表现上的那潮汐涨落？又为何在彼此相似的心理状态之中，前者的意识好比一座山峰，后者的意识却好比一个洞穴？

「一名士兵在战场上身受重伤，他的神经肌体发生了变化，他因此丧失了他的肌体意识；或者用心理学家的术语来说，他丧失了对自身肉体、对自身物质人格的感觉。这一变化的原因，多半是他所受的伤；但这一变化的性质，却只能用假设来解释，而这些假设如今已成了选择与口味的问题——在科学家之中，有时甚至是个人利益的问题。既然问题既已由假设来裁断，那么连一个门外汉，难道不也可以自由地提出一个假设么？倘若我说：那玻璃已经碎了，其内的『自我』所映出的尽是悲凉；或者更可悲些，那『自我』的光已经流尽了——我所给你的，难道不是与那位心理学教授同样可贵、同样站得住脚的解答么？」

## II / II

The Voice of the Dawn / 黎明之声

===Original===
Breathless but scathless, we emerge from the mazes of metaphysics and psychology where man and the soul are ever playing hide-and-seek; and where Khalid was pleased to display a little of his killing skill in fencing. To those mazes, we promise the Reader, we shall not return again. In our present sojourn, however, it is necessary to go through the swamps and Jordans as well as the mountains and plains. Otherwise, we would not have lingered a breathing while in the lowlands of mystery. But now we know how far Khalid went in seeking health, and how deep in seeking the Me, which he would disentangle from the meshes of philosophy and anchoritism, and bring back to life, triumphant, loving, joyous, free. And how far he succeeded in this, we shall soon know.

On the morning of his last day in the pines, meanwhile, we behold him in the chariot of Apollo serenading the stars. He no longer would thrust a poker down his windpipe; for he breathes as freely as the mountain bears and chirps as joyously as the swallows. And his lungs? The lungs of the pines are not as sound. And his eyes? Well, he can gaze at the rising sun without adverting the head or squinting or shedding a tear. Now, as a sign of this healthy state of body and mind, and his healthier resolve to return to the world, to live opposite his friend the Hermit on the other antipode of life, and furthermore, as a relief from the exhausting tortuosities of thought in the last Chapter, we give here a piece of description notably symbolical.

===Chinese===
气喘未定、幸而无伤，我们从形而上学与心理学的迷宫里钻将出来——在那迷宫之中，人与灵魂永远在捉迷藏；而哈立德也曾乐于一展他那一手致命的剑术。我们向读者诸君保证，那迷宫，断不再返。然而此番驻留，沼泽与约旦河是要涉的，山岳与平原也是要过的。否则，我们便不会在那神秘的低地里，多作这一口呼吸的盘桓。可如今我们既已晓得，哈立德在寻健康这一途上走了多远，在寻那「自我」一途上又掘得多深——他要把那「自我」从哲学与隐修主义的网罗里解出来，再唤回人世，使之凯旋、有爱、喜乐、自由。至于他这一番究竟成了几分，我们不久便知。

与此同时，且看他在松林最后一日的清晨，乘着阿波罗（Apollo）的日辇，向群星奏着小夜曲。他再也不肯把一根拨火棍捅进自己的气管了；因为他呼吸之舒畅，一如山里的熊；他啁啾之欢悦，一如燕子。至于他的肺？松林的肺都不及他的健全。他的眼睛呢？嘿，他尽可凝望初升的旭日，不必侧头、不必眯眼、不必落一滴泪。如今，为了印证他这身心两健的状态，又为了印证他那更健的决心——决心回到人世去，与他的隐士朋友分居生命的两极而对门相望——再者，也为了替上一章那叫人疲竭的迂曲思绪稍作纾解，我们于此特地给出一段意蕴显然象征的描写。

===Original===

“I slept very early last night; the lights in the chapel of the abbey were still flickering, and the monks were chanting the complines. The mellow music of a drizzle seemed to respond sombrely to the melancholy echo of the choir. About midnight the rain beat heavily on the pine roof of the forest, and the thunder must have struck very near, between me and the monks. But rising very early this morning to commune for the last time with the pensive silence of dawn in the pines, I am greeted, as I peep out of my booth, by a knot of ogling stars. But where is the opaque breath of the storm, where are the clouds? None seem to hang on the horizon, and the sky is as limpid and clear as the dawn of a new life. Glorious, this interval between night and dawn. Delicious, the flavour of the forest after a storm. Intoxicating, the odours of the earth, refreshed and satisfied. Divine, the whispers of the morning air, divine!

“But where is the rain, and where are the thunderbolts of last night? The forest and the atmosphere retain but the sweet and scented memories of their storming passion. Such a December morning in these mountain heights is a marvel of enduring freshness and ardour. All round one gets a vivid illusion of Spring. The soft breezes caressing the pines shake from their boughs the only evidence of last night’s storm. And these are more like the dew of Summer than the lees of the copious tears of parting Autumn. A glorious morning, too glorious to be enjoyed by a solitary soul. But near the rivulet yonder stands a fox sniffing the morning air. Welcome, my friend. Welcome to my coffee, too.

===Chinese===

「昨夜我睡得极早；修道院小礼拜堂里的灯火仍在摇曳，修士们正吟诵着夜课经（compline）。那蒙蒙细雨的柔润乐音，仿佛在阴郁地应和着唱诗班那忧伤的回响。约莫半夜时分，雨势猛烈地敲打着松林那松针覆顶的穹庐，雷声想必就劈在近处——劈在我与修士们之间。可今日清晨我早早起身，要与这松林里黎明那忧思的寂静作最后一场神交；我刚一探出我那棚屋，便有一簇顾盼生辉的繁星迎上前来。可是那风暴不透明的气息在哪里呢，那云又在何处？天边一缕也无；这天空，清澄澄、明净净，竟如新生之黎明一般。荣耀啊，这黑夜与黎明之间的间隙。甘美啊，这风暴过后的林间气息。醉人啊，这大地的气味——它既得了清润，便心满意足。属天啊，这清晨空气的低语，属天啊！

「可昨夜的雨在何处，昨夜的雷火又在何处？森林与大气，所留住的不过是那一场狂飙之情的甜美芳馨的记忆。在这样高的山间，十二月的这样一个清晨，是一种历久弥新、历久弥烈的奇观。四围之中，人尽可得一种栩栩如生的春之幻觉。那轻抚松枝的软风，从枝头摇落的，乃是昨夜风暴仅存的证据。而这些，倒更似夏日的露珠，不似那辞行秋日的滂沱泪痕。一个荣耀的清晨——荣耀得不容一颗孤寂的灵魂独自享用。然而那边，小溪之畔，立着一只狐狸，正在嗅那清晨的空气。欢迎啊，我的朋友。也欢迎你来分享我的咖啡。

===Original===
“I gather my mulberry sticks, kindle them with a handful of dried pine needles, roast my coffee beans, and grind them while the water boils in the pot. In half an hour I am qualified to go about my business. The cups and coffee utensils I wash and restore to the chest﻿—and what else have I to do today? Pack up? Allah be praised, I have little packing to do. I would pack up, if I could, a ton of the pine air and the forest perfume, a strip of this limpid sky, and a cluster of those stars. Never at such an hour and in this season of the year did I enjoy such transporting limpidity in the atmosphere and such reassuring expansiveness on the horizon. Why, even the stars, the constellations, and the planets, are all here to enjoy this with me. Not one of them, I think, is absent.

“The mountains are lost in the heavens. They are seeking, as it were, the sisters of the little flowers sleeping at their feet. The moon, resembling a crushed orange, is sinking in the Mediterranean. The outlines of earth and sky all round are vague, indistinct. Were not the sky so clear and the atmosphere so rare, thus affording the planets and the constellations to shed their modicum of light, the dusk of this hour would have deprived the scene of much of its pensive beauty of colour and shade. But there is Pegasus, Andromeda, Aldebaran, not to mention Venus and Jupiter and Saturn﻿—these alone can conquer the right wing of darkness. And there is Mercury, like a lighted cresset shaken by the winds, flapping his violet wings above the Northeastern horizon; and Mars, like a piece of gold held out by the trembling hand of a miser, is sinking in the blue of the sea with Neptune; the Pleiades are stepping on the trail of the blushing moon; the Balance lingers behind to weigh the destinies of the heroes who are to contend with the dawn; while Venus, peeping from her tower over Mt. Sanneen, is sending love vibrations to all. I would tell thee more if I knew. But I swear to thee I never read through the hornbook of the heavens. But if I can not name and locate more of the stars, I can tell thee this about them all: they are the embers of certainty eternally glowing in the ashes of doubt.

===Chinese===
「我收拢些桑枝，用一把干松针引火点燃，焙炒我的咖啡豆，趁着壶中水沸之际把它们碾碎。半个时辰之后，我便有资格去办我今日的事了。杯盏与咖啡器具，我一并洗净，归入箱中——今日我还有何事可办呢？打点行装么？感赞真主（Allah），我几乎无甚可打点。倘若能够，我真想打点上一吨的松间空气与林中芬芳，裁下这一带清澄的天空，再摘下一簇那样的星辰。一年之中这样的时辰、这样的季节里，我从未享受过这般令人神往的澄明大气、这般叫人安心的开阔天涯。哎，便是那些恒星、那些星座、那些行星，也全都来此与我同享。我想，没有一个缺席的。

「群山已迷失在天宇之中。它们仿佛在寻觅——寻觅睡在它们脚下的那些小花的姐妹。那月亮，形如一只压扁的香橙，正沉入地中海（Mediterranean）之中。四围天与地的轮廓，皆朦胧难辨。若非天空如此澄澈、大气如此稀薄，让行星与星座得以洒下它们那一点微光，这一时辰的薄暮，便会夺去此景大半那沉思的色与影之美。然而这里有飞马座（Pegasus）、仙女座（Andromeda）、毕宿五（Aldebaran），更不必提金星、木星、土星——单单这些，已足以征服黑暗的右翼。又有水星，如一盏被风摇撼的燃灯，在东北方的天际之上拍着它紫罗兰色的翅膀；火星，则如一块由吝啬人颤抖的手伸出来的金子，正与海王星一同沉入那海的蔚蓝之中；昴宿星团正踏着那含羞的月亮的余晖而行；天秤座则落在后头，要来称量那些将与黎明争雄的英雄们的命运；而金星，从她在桑宁山（Mt. Sanneen）之上的塔楼里探出头来，向众人频送着爱的震颤。倘若我晓得，我本可告诉你更多。但我向你发誓，我从不曾把那天穹的角书（hornbook）从头读完。然而，纵使我不能为更多的星辰一一命名、一一定位，关于它们全体，我却可以告诉你这样一句：它们乃是那在怀疑之灰烬中永远灼灼发光的、确信之余烬。

===Original===
“The Eastern horizon is yet lost in the dusk; the false dawn is spreading the figments of its illusion; the trees in the distance seem like rain-clouds; and the amorphous shadows of the monasteries on the mountain heights and hilltops all around, have not yet developed into silhouettes. Everything, except the river in the wadi below, is yet asleep. Not even the swallows are astir. Ah, but my neighbour yonder is; the light in the loophole of his hut sends a struggling ray through the mulberries, and the tintinnabulations of his daughter’s loom are like so many stones thrown into this sleeping pond of silence. The loom-girl in these parts is never too early at her harness and shuttle. I know a family here whose loom and spinning wheel are never idle: the wife works at the loom in the day and her boy at the wheel; while in the night, her husband and his old mother keep up the game. And this hardly secures for them their flour and lentils the year round. But I concern not myself now with questions of economy.

“There, another of my neighbours is awake; and the hinges of his door, shrieking terribly, fiendishly, startle the swallows from their sleep. And here are the muleteers, yodeling, as they pass by, their

‘Dhome, Dhome, Dhome,

					O mother, he is come;

					Hide me, hide me quickly,

					And say I am not home.’

===Chinese===
「东方的天际，犹自迷失在薄暮里；那伪黎明（false dawn）正摊开着它幻觉的种种虚影；远处的树木，望去竟似雨云；四围山巅与峰顶上那些修道院的无定形阴影，也还未显出它们的剪影。万物，除了下面干谷（wadi）里的那条河，都还在睡梦之中。便是燕子也还未醒来。啊，可是我那边那位邻居却醒了；他棚屋洞窗里的灯火，穿过桑林送来一缕挣扎的光线，而他女儿那架织机的叮当声，便如一颗颗石子投进这一池沉睡的寂静。这几方的织女，操起她的综丝与梭子，从来不嫌太早。我识得这里一户人家，他们的织机与纺轮从不曾闲着：白日里妻子在织机前做工，男孩在纺轮前做工；到了夜里，丈夫与他的老母又接着这场游戏。可这样辛劳，也仅勉强够他们一年到头的面粉与扁豆罢了。不过，经济一类的问题，此刻与我无干。

「那边，又一位邻居醒了；他家门上的铰链，发出骇人的、近乎凶煞的尖叫，把燕子们从睡梦里惊起。而这里，赶骡的人们正唱着岳得尔歌，打从这儿经过——

『咚，咚，咚，
　　　　哎呀妈妈，他来啦；
　　　　快快把我藏起来，
　　　　就说我没在家。』

===Original===
“Lo, the horizon is disentangling itself from the meshes of darkness. The dust of haze and dusk on the scalloped edges of the mountains, is blown away by the first breath of dawn. The lighter grey of the horizon is mirrored in the clearer blue of the sea. But the darkness seems to gather on the breast of the sloping hills. Conquered on the heights, it retreats into the wadi. Ay, the darkest hour is nearest the dawn.

“Now the light grey is become a lavender; the outlines of earth and sky are become more distinct; the mountain peaks, the dusky veil being rent, are separating themselves from the heaven’s embrace; the trees in the distance no longer seem like rain-clouds; and the silhouettes of the monasteries are casting off the cloak of night. The lavender is melting now into heliotrope, and the heliotrope is bursting here and there in pink; the stars are waning, the constellations are dying out, and the planets are following in their wake. The darkness, too, which has not yet retreated from the wadi, must soon follow; for the front guard of the dawn is near. Behold the shimmer of their steel! And see, in the dust of the retreating darkness, the ochre veins of the lime cliffs are now perceptible. And that huge pillar, which looked like the standard-bearer of Night, is transformed into a belfry; and a monk can be seen peeping through the ogive beneath it. Mt. Sanneen, its black and ochre scales thrown in relief on a coat of grey, is like a huge panther sleeping over the many-throated ravine of Kisrawan. Ah, the pink flower of dawn is bursting in golden glory, thrilling in orange and saffron, flaming with the ardency of love and hope. The dawn! The glow and glamour of the Eastern dawn!﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===
「看哪，那地平线正从黑暗的网罗里挣脱出来。山峦那扇贝形边缘上那层霾与暮的尘翳，被黎明的第一缕气息吹散。地平线上那较浅的灰色，倒映在海水那较清的蓝色之中。然而黑暗似在坡岭的胸膛上聚拢起来。它在高处既被征服，便退入干谷之中。是啊，最暗之时，去黎明最近。

「如今那浅灰已化作淡紫；天与地的轮廓变得更分明了；山峰，既撕破了那层薄暮的面纱，便从苍天的拥抱里脱身出来；远处的树木不再像雨云了；修道院的剪影也正褪去夜的斗篷。那淡紫此刻正融作天芥菜色（heliotrope），而那天芥菜色又这里那里地绽成粉红；群星在凋敝，星座在熄灭，行星也随其后而去。那尚未从干谷退去的黑暗，不久也必将随之而去；因为黎明的前锋已近。且看他们兵刃的寒光！再看，在那退却的黑暗之尘里，石灰岩悬崖的赭黄脉络已隐约可辨。那一根巨柱，方才望去还像是黑夜的掌旗官，此刻已化作一座钟楼；可以看见一位修士正从它下方的尖拱（ogive）里向外探望。桑宁山，它那黑与赭的鳞甲，在一件灰色的大氅上凸显出来，宛如一头巨豹，伏卧在基斯鲁万（Kisrawan）那众口同啸的深壑之上。啊，黎明的粉红之花，正以金色的荣光绽放，颤动在橙黄与橘红之间，炽燃着爱与希望的热烈。黎明啊！东方黎明的那光华与旖旎！……

===Original===

“The dawn of a new life, of a better, purer, healthier, higher spiritual kingdom. I would have its temples and those of the vast empire of wealth and material well-being, stand side by side. Ay, I would even rear an altar to the Soul in the temple of Materialism, and an altar to Materialism in the temple of the Soul. Each shall have its due, each shall glory in the sacred purity and strength of life; each shall develop and expand, but never at the expense of the other. I will have neither the renunciation which ends in a kind of idiocy dignified with a philosophic or a theologic name, nor the worldliness which ends in bestiality. I am a citizen of two worlds﻿—a citizen of the Universe; I owe allegiance to two kingdoms. In my heart are those stars and that sun, and the **light** of those stars and that sun.

“Yes, I am equally devoted both to the material and the spiritual. And when the two in me are opposed to each other, conflicting, inimical, obdurate, my attitude towards them is neither that of my friend the Hermit nor that of my European superman. I sit down, shut my eyes, compose myself, and concentrate my mind on the mobility of things. If the clouds are moving, why, I have but to sit down and let them move away. I let my No-will, in this case, dominate my will, and that serves my purpose well. To be sure, every question tormenting us would resolve itself favourably, or at least indifferently, if we did not always rush in, wildly, madly, and arrogate to ourselves such claims of authority and knowledge as would make Olympus shake with laughter. The resignation and passiveness of the spirit should always alternate equitably with the terrible strivings of the will. For the dervish who whirls himself into a foaming ecstasy of devotion and the strenuous American who works himself up to a sweating ecstasy of gain, are the two poles of the same absurdity, the two ends of one evil. Indeed, to my way of thinking, the man on the Stock Exchange and the demagogue on the stump, for instance, are brothers to the blatant corybant.”

===Chinese===

「那是新生命的黎明，是一个更善、更纯、更健、更高的属灵国度的黎明。我愿它的殿宇，与那庞大的财富与物质福祉之帝国的殿宇并肩而立。是啊，我甚至愿在唯物主义（Materialism）之庙里，为灵魂立一座坛；又在灵魂之庙里，为唯物主义立一座坛。各得其所，各以其生命之神圣的纯洁与力量为荣；各自发育、舒展，却永不以对方的牺牲为代价。我所不取的，是那种以哲学或神学的美名来粉饰、终归沦为某种白痴的弃世；我所不取的，也是那种沦为兽性的世俗。我是两个世界的公民——是宇宙的公民；我效忠于两个国度。我心里有那些星辰、那轮太阳，也有那些星辰、那轮太阳的**光**。

「是的，我对物质与属灵两者，等同地倾心。当二者在我之内彼此对立、冲突、相仇、相持不下之时，我对它们的态度，既不取我那位隐士朋友的态度，亦不取我那位欧洲超人的态度。我坐下来，闭上眼，平心静气，把心念凝注于万物的流动之上。倘若云在移，哎，我尽可坐着，由它移去便是。此际我便让我的「不意志」（No-will）凌驾我的意志，这正合我意。说真的，凡一切扰我们的难题，倘若我们不是一味地狂躁、癫狂地冲将进去，僭称那般足以叫奥林匹斯（Olympus）笑得发颤的权柄与知识，那些难题自会顺遂地化解——至少也会淡然地了之。灵性的顺服与无为，与意志之可怖的奋争，理当公允地交替而行。因为那旋舞入一种口吐白沫的虔诚之狂的德尔维希（dervish），与那拼命工作至汗流浃背的获利之狂的健美美国人，乃是同一种荒谬的两极，同一种邪恶的两端。诚然，照我想来，证券交易所里的人、树桩上的煽动家——举例而言——都是那喧嚣的科律班特（corybant，即疯狂祭司）的兄弟。」

## III / III

The Self Ecstatic / 出神之我

===Original===
To graft the strenuosity of Europe and America upon the ease of the Orient, the materialism of the West upon the spirituality of the East—this to us seems to be the principal aim of Khalid. But often in his wanderings and divagations of thought does he give us fresh proof of the truism that no two opposing elements meet and fuse without both losing their original identity. You may place the bit of contentment in the mouth of ambition, so to speak, and jog along in your sterile course between the vast wheat fields groaning under the thousand-toothed plough and the gardens of delight swooning with devotion and sensuality. But cross ambition with contentment and you get the hinny of indifference or the monster of fatalism. We do not say that indifference at certain passes of life, and certain stages, is not healthy, and fatalism not powerful; but both we believe are factors as potent in commerce and trade as pertinacity and calculation. “But is there not room in the garden of delight for a wheat field?” asks Khalid. “Can we not apply the bow to the telegraph wires of the world and make them the vehicle of music as of stock quotations? Can we not simplify life as we are simplifying the machinery of industry? Can we not consecrate its Temple to the Trinity of Devotion, Art, and Work, or Religion, Romance, and Trade?”

This seems to be the gist of Khalid’s gospel. This, through the labyrinths of doubt and contradiction, is the pinnacle of faith he would reach. And often in this labyrinthic gloom, where a gleam of light from some recess of thought or fancy reveals here a Hermit in his cloister, there an Artist in his studio, below a Nawab in his orgies, above a Broker on the Stock Exchange, we have paused to ask a question about these glaring contrarieties in his life and thought. And always would he make this reply: “I have frequently moved and removed between extremes; I have often worked and slept in opposing camps. So, do not expect from me anything like the consistency with which the majority of mankind solder and shape their life. Deep thought seems often, if not always, inconsistent at the first blush. The intensity and passiveness of the spirit are as natural in their attraction and repulsion as the elements, whose harmony is only patent on the surface. Consistency is superficial, narrow, one-sided. I am both ambitious, therefore, and contented. My ambition is that of the earth, the ever producing and resuscitating earth, doing the will of God, combatting the rasure of time; and my contentment is that of the majestic pines, faring alike in shade and sunshine, in calm and storm, in winter as in spring. Ambition and Contentment are the night and day of my life-journey. The day makes room for the fruits of solacement which the night brings; and the night gives a cup of the cordial of contentment to make good the promise of day to day.

===Chinese===
把欧美的奋发嫁接到东方的闲适之上，把西方的唯物论嫁接到东方的灵性之上——在我们看来，这便是哈立德的首要宗旨。然而他在思想的漫游与歧出之中，常常给我们以新的证据，证明一条不言自明的道理：任何两种对立的元素相遇相融，都必双双丧失其本来的属性。你尽可把「知足」这一嚼子，套进「野心」的口中，这么说罢，然后在贫瘠的道上颠簸前行——一边是无垠的麦田，在千齿犁下呻吟；一边是欢乐之园，在虔诚与肉欲之间晕眩。然而以野心与知足相交配，你得到的，或是冷漠的驴骡，或是宿命的怪物。我们并非说在人生的某些关头、某些阶段，冷漠不健康、宿命不有力；而是相信此二者之于商贸，也如同固执与算计一般，是同样强有力的因子。「可是，」哈立德问道，「在那欢乐之园里，难道容不下一片麦田么？难道我们不能把琴弓搭上世界的电报线，使它既载股价行情、也载乐曲么？难道我们不能像简化工业的机器那样，去简化人生么？难道我们不能把它那座神殿，奉献给『虔信、艺术与劳作』的三位一体，或者『宗教、浪漫与贸易』的三位一体么？」

这似乎便是哈立德福音的要旨。穿过那疑虑与矛盾的迷宫，这便是他要攀登的信仰之巅。而每每在这迷宫般的幽暗之中——自思想或想象的某一深处射来的一缕微光，在这里照见一位修道密室中的隐士，在那里照见一位画室中的艺术家，在下面照见一位纵酒作乐的纳瓦卜（Nawab），在上面照见一位证券交易所的经纪人——我们便曾驻足发问：他这般醒目的矛盾，其生活与思想中如此刺目的相违，究竟从何而来？而他总作如是答：「我时常往返于两极之间，迁移不止；我常常在对立的营垒里做工、安眠。所以，不要指望我能像世间大多数人那样，把他们的人生焊结塑造成前后如一的样子。深思在初看之下，纵非总是，亦常常显得自相矛盾。精神之激越与精神之恬淡，其相吸相斥之自然，一如元素之于元素；而元素之间的和谐，不过显于表面罢了。一致性是肤浅的、狭隘的、片面的。故而我既雄心勃勃，又知足常乐。我的雄心，是大地之雄心——那永远在生产、永远在复活的大地，奉行真主（God）的旨意，对抗时间的磨灭；而我的知足，是苍松之知足——在荫翳与阳光之下一样度日，在风平与狂飙之中一样安身，在严冬亦如在阳春。雄心与知足，乃是我人生旅程的夜与昼。白昼为黑夜所带来的慰藉之果腾出地方；而黑夜则奉上一杯知足的醇酒，以兑现白昼对白昼许下的诺言。

===Original===
“Ay, while sweating in the tortuous path, I never cease to cherish the feeling in which I was nourished; the West for me means ambition, the East, contentment: my heart is ever in the one, my soul, in the other. And I care not for the freedom which does not free both; I seek not the welfare of the one without the other. But unlike my Phoenician ancestors, the spiritual with me shall not be limited by the natural; it shall go far above it, beyond or below it, saturating, sustaining, purifying what in external nature is but a symbol of the invisible. Nor is my idea of the spiritual developed in opposition to nature, and in a manner inimical to its laws and claims, as in Judaism and Christianity.

“The spiritual and natural are so united, so inextricably entwined around each other, that I can not conceive of them separately, independently. And both in the abstract sense are purportless and ineffectual without Consciousness. They are blind, dumb forces, beautiful, barbaric pageants, careering without aim or design through the immensities of No-where and No-time, if they are not impregnated and nourished with Thought, that is to say, with Consciousness, vitalised and purified. You may impregnate them with philosophy, nourish them with art; they both emanate from them, and remain as skidding clouds, as shining mirages, as wandering dust, until they find their exponent in Man.

“I tell thee then that Man, that is to say Consciousness, vitalised and purified, in other words Thought—that alone is real and eternal. And Man is supreme, only when he is the proper exponent of Nature, and spirit, and God: the three divine sources from which he issues, in which he is sustained, and to which he must return. Nature and the spiritual, without this embodied intelligence, this somatic being, called man or angel or ape, are as ermine on a wax figure. The human factor, the exponent intelligence, the intellective and sensuous faculties, these, my Brothers, are whole, sublime, holy, only when, in a state of continuous expansion, the harmony among themselves and the affirmative ties between them and Nature, are perfect and pure. No, the spiritual ought not and can not be free from the sensuous, even the sensual. The true life, the full life, the life, pure, robust, sublime, is that in which all the nobler and higher aspirations of the soul **and the body** are given free and unlimited scope, with the view of developing the divine strain in Man, and realising to some extent the romantic as well as the material hopes of the race. God, Nature, Spirit, Passion—Passion, Spirit, Nature, God—in some such panorama would I paint the life of a highly developed being. Any of these elements lacking, and the life is wanting, defective, impure.

===Chinese===
「是啊，虽在曲折的路上汗流浃背，我却从未停止珍视那哺育我成长的心境：西方于我意味着雄心，东方于我意味着知足；我的心永远在前者之中，我的灵魂永远在后者之内。而我所不取的自由，是那种不能使此二者同得解放的自由；我所不求的福祉，是舍此一者而单取彼一者的福祉。但我不同于我的腓尼基人（Phoenicians）祖先，那属灵之事在我这里，绝不为其所限；它要远远凌驾于其上，超乎其外、或入乎其下，去浸润、去支撑、去净化那外在自然中本不过是不可见者的象征之物。我对属灵之事的见解，也并非在反乎自然之中发展起来、并以与其法则与诉求相敌对的方式发展起来——一如在犹太教与基督教中所见。

「属灵之事与自然之事，结合得如此紧密、相互缠绕得如此难解难分，以至于我无法将它们分别地、独立地加以设想。而此二者在抽象意义上，若无意识（Consciousness）为之点化，便都是无谓而无力的。它们是盲目的、喑哑的力量，是华美而蛮荒的巡游队伍，若无思想——亦即被赋以生命、被加以净化的意识——为之受胎、为之滋养，便漫无目的、漫无章法地疾驰于那无限的乌有之乡、乌有之时。你尽可以哲学为之受胎，以艺术为之滋养；二者皆自其中流出，却仍如飘忽的云、如闪烁的蜃景、如游荡的尘埃，直至在『人』身上找到它的代言者。

「那么我告诉你：人——亦即被赋以生命、被加以净化的意识，换一个说法，亦即思想——唯有此者，才是真实而永恒的。而人之所以为至尊，只在他恰为自然、为精神、为真主（God）之合宜代言者之时：这三种神圣之源，他从之而出，因之而存，亦必归之而返。自然与属灵之事，若无这种具身的智慧、这种有躯的存在——无论名之为人、为天使、为猿——则犹蜡偶上之貂皮，徒有其表。人之因子、那代言的智慧、那理智与感官的诸般官能——我的兄弟们——唯当其处于不断舒展的状态之中、其彼此间的和谐及与自然之间的肯定性纽带皆臻完美而纯净之时，方为整全的、崇高的、神圣的。不，属灵之事不当、也不能脱离感官之事，乃至肉欲之事。真正的人生、丰盈的人生、那纯净而强健而崇高的人生，乃是灵魂**与身体**一切更高尚更崇高的抱负皆得以自由无碍地施展的人生——其目的，乃为发扬人身上那属神的血脉，并在某种程度上实现这种族之浪漫的与物质的期望。真主、自然、精神、激情——激情、精神、自然、真主——我愿以这样一幅全景图，绘出一个高度发展之生命。此诸元素缺其一，则人生便有所亏、有所残、有所不洁。

===Original===
“I have no faith in men who were conceived in a perfunctory manner, on a pragmatical system, so to speak; the wife receiving her husband in bed as she would a tedious guest at an afternoon tea. Only two flames uniting produce a third; but a flame and a name, or a flame and a sponge, produce a hiff and nothing. Oh, that the children of the race are all born phoenix-like in the fire of noble and sacred passion, in the purgatory, as it were, of Love. What a race, what a race we should have. What men, what women! Yes, that is how the children of the earth should be conceived, not on a pragmatical system, in an I-don’t-care-about-the-issue manner. I believe in evoking the spirit, in dreaming a little about the gods of Olympus, and a little, too, about the gods of the abysmal depths, before the bodily communion. And in earnest, O my Brother, let us do this, despite what old Socrates says about the propriety and wisdom of approaching your wife with prudence and gravity.…”

And thus, if we did not often halloo, Khalid, like a huntsman pursuing his game, would lose himself in the pathless, lugubrious damp of the forest. If we did not prevent him at times, holding firmly to his coattail, he would desperately pursue the ghost of his thoughts even on such precipitous paths to those very depths in which Socrates and Montaigne always felt at home. But he, a feverish, clamorous, obstreperous stripling of a Bedouin, what chance has he in extricating his barbaric instincts from such thorny hedges of philosophy? And had he not quoted Socrates in that last paragraph, it would have been expunged. No, we are not utterly lost to the fine sense of propriety of this chaste and demure age. But no matter how etiolated and sickly the thought, it regains its colour and health when it breathes the literary air. Prudery can not but relish the tang of lubricity when flavoured with the classical. Moreover, if Socrates and Montaigne speak freely of these midnight matters, why not Khalid, if he has anything new to say, any good advice to offer. But how good and how new are his views let the Reader judge.

===Chinese===
「我不信那些敷衍了事地被孕育出来的人——可以说，他们是循着一套实用主义的法子孕育出来的；其妻在床上接纳丈夫，一如在午后茶会上接待一位乏味的客人。唯有两团火焰相合，方能生出第三团火来；而一团火焰与一个虚名相合，或一团火焰与一块海绵相合，则只生出一缕烟气与一片虚无。啊，愿这种族的子女，都能如凤凰（phoenix）一般，在那高贵而神圣的激情之火中诞生——在那可以说是爱的炼狱之中诞生。我们将拥有怎样一个种族，怎样一个种族啊！怎样的男人，怎样的女人！是的，大地的子女就该这样被孕育——而不是循着一套实用主义的法子，以一种『管他结果如何』的态度去孕育。我所信的，是在肉身的合一之前，先唤起精神，先做一做关于奥林帕斯诸神的梦，也稍做一做关于深渊诸神的梦。我的兄弟啊，且让我们认真地这样做罢，尽管老苏格拉底（Socrates）曾谈及应以谨慎与庄重之态走近你的妻。……」

如此说来，倘若我们不常常大声呼唤，哈立德的情形便如同一猎人追猎他的猎物，会在那无径可循、阴湿凄迷的林中迷失了路。倘若我们不时而拦住他、紧紧拽住他的衣襟后摆，他便会不顾一切地追着他思想的幽灵，一直追到那样陡峭的路上、追到那样深的深处——而那深处，正是苏格拉底与蒙田（Montaigne）一向如归故里之处。但他，一个躁热、喧嚷、桀骜的贝都因少年，要从哲学这般荆棘丛中抽出他那蛮荒的本能，又有几分胜算呢？倘若他在上一段里不曾援引苏格拉底的话，那一段便要被删去了。不，对这个贞洁而端肃的时代那份雅正的分寸感，我们尚未全然麻木。然而无论一念如何苍白、如何病弱，一旦它呼吸到文学的空气，便能重获它的颜色与健康。假道学也无不乐于品尝那淫靡的滋味——只消它调和以古典的风味。再者，倘若苏格拉底与蒙田能对这些夜半之事畅所欲言，哈立德又何尝不可，只要他有什么新话可说、有什么良言可献。至于他的见解究竟有多好、有多新，且由读者自判罢。

===Original===
’Tis very well to speak “of evoking the spirit before the bodily communion,” but those who can boast of a deeper experience in such matters will find in Socrates’ dictum, quoted by Montaigne, the very gist of reason and wisdom. Those wise ones were as farsighted as they were far gone. And moderation, as it was justly said once, is the respiration of the philosopher. But Khalid, though always invoking the distant luminary of transcendentalism for light, can not arrogate to himself this high title. The expansion of all the faculties, and the reduction of the demands of society and the individual to the lowest term;—this, as we understand it, is the aim of transcendentalism. And Khalid’s distance from the orbit of this grand luminary seems to vary with his moods; and these vary with the librations and revolutions of the moon. Hallucinated, moonstruck Khalid, your harmonising and affinitative efforts do not always succeed. That is our opinion of the matter. And the Reader, who is no respecter of editors, might quarrel with it, for all we know.

Only by standing firmly in the centre can one preserve the equilibrium of one’s thoughts. But Khalid seldom speaks of equilibrium: he cares not how he fares in falling on either side of the fence, so he knows what lies behind. Howbeit, we can not conceive of how the affinity of the mind and soul with the senses, and the harmony between these and nature, are possible, if not exteriorised in that very superman whom Khalid so much dreads, and on whom he often casts a lingering glance of admiration. So there you are. We must either rise to a higher consciousness on the ruins of a lower one, of no-consciousness, rather, or go on seeming and simulating, aspiring, perspiring, and suffering, until our turn comes. Death denies no one. Meanwhile, Khalid’s rhapsodies on his way back to the city, we shall heed and try to echo.

---

===Chinese===
说什么「在肉身的合一之前，先唤起精神」，话说得固然动听；但凡能自诩于此道阅历更深之人，必能在蒙田所引苏格拉底那句箴言里，觅得理性与智慧之精核。那些智者目光之远，正不亚于他们走得之深。而节制，正如有人曾恰切所言，乃是哲学家的呼吸。然而哈立德虽总是援引超验主义那颗遥远的星辰以借光，却无由把这一崇高的名号加诸己身。据我们之理解，超验主义的宗旨乃是：舒展人的一切官能，而把社会与个人的需求约减至最低之项。而哈立德距这颗大星之轨道的远近，似乎随他的心境而变；而这心境，又随月亮的盈亏与环行而变。中了魔、中了月疾的哈立德啊，你那调和与亲和的种种努力，并非总能奏效。这便是我们对这件事的看法。至于读者——他向来不把编者放在眼里——说不定还要与之争上一争，这也未可知。

唯有稳立于中央，方能保思想的均衡不坠。然而哈立德罕言均衡：只要知道篱笆背后藏着什么，他并不在意自己跌落在哪一边。纵然如此，心智与灵魂之于感官的亲和、此者与自然之间的和谐，倘若不外化于哈立德所深惧、而又每每投以一缕流连赞许之目光的那位超人（superman）身上，我们实难设想它们如何可能。如此而已。我们要么在一层较低意识——毋宁说是在无意识——的废墟之上，升华到一种更高的意识；要么就继续去装作、去假装，去渴慕、去流汗、去受苦，直至轮到我们为止。死亡不拒任何人。在此期间，哈立德在归城路上所发的那些狂想曲，我们自当留意，并尽力为之作一回响。

---

===Original===

“On the high road of the universal spirit,” he sings, “the world, the whole world before me, thrilling and radiating, chanting of freedom, faith, hope, health and power, and joy. Back to the City, O Khalid—the City where Truth, and Faith, and Honesty, and Wisdom, are ever suffering, ever struggling, ever triumphing. No, it matters not with me if the spirit of intelligence and power, of freedom and culture, which must go the rounds of the earth, is always dominated by the instinct of self-interest. That must be; that is inevitable. But the instinct of self-interest, O my Brother, goes with the flesh; the body-politic dies; nations rise and fall; and the eternal Spirit, the progenitor of all ideals, passes to better or worse hands, still chastening and strengthening itself in the process.

“The Orient and Occident, the male and female of the Spirit, the two great streams in which the body and soul of man are refreshed, invigorated, purified—of both I sing, in both I glory, to both I consecrate my life, for both I shall work and suffer and die. My Brothers, the most highly developed being is neither European nor Oriental; but rather he who partakes of the finer qualities of both the European genius and the Asiatic prophet.
